# INTRODUCTION.

### OF THE TOPOGRAPHICAL POEMS OF O’DUBHAGAIN AND O’HUIDHRIN.

There are two copies of these poems in the Library of the Royal Irish Academy: one in the handwriting of Cucocriche O'Clery, the other in the transcript of Duald Mac Firbis's genealogical work, made for the Academy by Mr. Eugene Curry. The original of this latter copy is in the handwriting of Michael O'Clery, the chief of the Four Masters, and is bound with the autograph of Mac Firbis's Genealogies in the volume from which Mr. Curry transcribed it—a manuscript in the possession of the Earl of Roden. The various readings of these copies are provided after the notes in this volume. No vellum copy of these poems has yet been discovered, nor is it likely that any exists. 

In a modern paper copy of them preserved in the *Leabhar Branach*, in the Library of Trinity College, Dublin, the authorship is attributed to Ferganainim Mac Eochadha (Mac Keogh, now Keogh), chief poet to the O'Byrnes of Wicklow; but as this copy is modern and carries little authority, it has not been used in this edition. It is probable that a copy of O'Dubhagain's poem was originally contained in the Book of O'Dubhagain, called *Leabhar Ui Maine* (the Book of Hy-Many), a large part of which is now in the possession of Lord Ashburnham. However, no reference to such a poem occurs in O'Reilly's description of the contents of that manuscript as it stood when in the possession of Sir William Betham, nor is it found in the detached fragment of the same manuscript now in the Library of the British Museum, Egerton 92 (Plut. clxviii.).

The first printed notice of these poems, as far as the editor has been able to determine, is the abstract given by Dr. John Lynch in chapter 3 of his *Cambrensis Eversus*, published in 1662. In that work, the author, in the following passage, attributes the

entirely to O’Dubhagain alone, and makes no mention whatever of O’Huidhrin:

> “Nec stirpium Hiberniam, ante arma illuc ab Anglis illata, incolentium nomenclaturam aliunde meliùs haurire poterimus, quàm ex illo insigni Joannis O’Duvegani poëmate, cui melioris notæ stemmata, quæ suo ambitu antiquitùs Hibernia complexa est inseruit. Illius autem Hibernici scripti initium est: *Triallam timcheall na Fodhla*, &c., quæ verba hunc sensum referunt, ‘O socii pulchræ fines obeamus Iernes.’”

Which the Rev. M. Kelly translates as follows:—

> “Nor can we obtain the naming of the tribes who inhabited Ireland before the English carried their arms there from any better source than that remarkable poem by John O’Dubhagain, in which he has inserted the more notable families which Ireland anciently comprised within its borders. The beginning of that poem, which is written in Irish, is ‘*Triallam timcheall na Fodhla*,’ etc., which words convey this meaning: ‘O, companions, let us traverse the territories of beautiful Ierne.’”

Dr. Lynch’s abstract of the poems was annotated by the Editor of the present volume in the edition of *Cambrensis Eversus* edited by Rev. M. Kelly for the Celtic Society in 1848–52.

Nearly opposite the quotation, “*Triallam timcheall na Fodhla*,” Dr. Lynch has written in the margin of page 25, “<margin>In ejus libro, 221</margin>” <note>In his book, 221</note>, from which it appears that he took his abstract of the poem from O’Dubhagain’s book. The O’Clerys attribute the authorship of the first poem to O’Dubhagain and of the second to O’Huidhrin; and it is very clear from the first two quatrains of the second poem attributed to O’Huidhrin that O’Dubhagain had left his work unfinished—not through ignorance—and that O’Huidhrin undertook to complete a task which this learned man had not lived to accomplish.

At the conclusion of his abstract of these poems, the author of *Cambrensis Eversus* (Kelly’s Ed., vol. i., p. 278) observes:

> “Non sum nescius optimo poemati me decus omne detraxisse, quòd insignis fragmenti, compage soluta, partes tumultuariè dissipavi, sicut teretem fabricam lapidum distractio venustate spoliat. Missum tamen illud facere non volui, ut ex tam locupleti monumento constaret, qui, ante

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the topographical poems of O'Dubhagain and O'Huidhrin, focusing on Dr. Lynch's analysis and Edward O'Reilly's historical account of the authors.</meta>

<header>The Topographical Poems of O'Dubhagain, &c.</header>

Anglos huc ingressos, Hiberniæ regiones incoluerunt. Pleræque autem è memoratis in isto poemate gentibus ; sub initio nuperi belli, non solum in rerum naturâ extiterunt, sed etiam aliæ in aliquo pristinæ ditionis angulo perstiterunt, aliæ latissimis latifundiis potiti sunt.

“I am aware that the merit of the original, excellent poem cannot be appreciated from the hurried summary which I have given of this remarkable fragment; just as all beauty and order leave a stone structure when the connection of its component parts has been broken. Nevertheless, I did not wish to miss an opportunity of providing, from such a valuable monument, an account of the families who inhabited the various territories of Ireland before the invasion of the English. Most of the families which the poem mentions were not only in existence at the beginning of the late war, but some of them were even then occupying portions of their old territories, and others possessed very extensive estates.”

In his chapter on these poems, Dr. Lynch has strangely confused tribes and families, evidently due to translations made for him from the originals. It appears there were then different copies in existence, which had been altered in various places by unskilled hands using other topographical writings.

Edward O’Reilly, in his “Catalogue of Irish Writers,” pages 99 and 100, gives the following account of this poem and its author, under the year A.D. 1372:—

“<term>John O’Dugan</term>, chief poet of O’Kelly of Ibh Maine, died this year. He was the author of ‘A Topographical and Historical Poem’ of eight hundred and eighty verses, beginning ‘Triallam timċeall na Foḋla:’ ‘Let us go around Fodhla (Ireland).’ This poem gives the names of the principal tribes and districts in Meath, Ulster, and Connacht, and the chiefs who presided over them at the time Henry II, King of England, was invited to this country by Dermod Mac Morogh, King of Leinster.

“From the first line of this poem, and from the few verses (ranns) that this author has left us on the districts of the province of Leinster, it would seem that it was his intention to have given a complete account of all the districts and chief tribes in Ireland. It would be a cause of much regret that he left such an interesting work unfinished, if it had not afterwards been taken up and completed by his contemporary, Giolla-na-naomh O’Huidhrin, who died, an old man, in the year 1420.

<page-type>preface</page-type>
<page-num>[ 4 ]</page-num>
# Introduction.

> “The work of O’Huidhrin has sometimes been joined to O’Dugan’s poem, so as to appear as one entire piece of one thousand six hundred and sixty verses, and the merit of the whole is given to the latter, though he really wrote only thirty-eight <term>ranns</term> <note>stanzas</note>, or one hundred and fifty-two verses on Meath; three hundred and fifty-four verses on Ulster; three hundred and twenty-eight verses on Conaght; and fifty-six verses on Leinster, making in total eight hundred and eighty verses. For the account of the ancient families of Leath-Mogha (Leinster and Munster), we are indebted to O’Huidhrin.
>
> “Copies of this poem are numerous, but few of them are perfect. The copy used by the author of *Cambrensis Eversus* must have been incomplete, or he did not translate it fully. A complete copy in the handwriting of Cucoigcriche O’Clery, one of the *Four Masters*, is in the collection of the Assistant Secretary,” that is, of O’Reilly himself.

This copy is now in the Library of the Royal Irish Academy. Again, under the year 1420, p. cxix., O’Reilly writes:

> “GIOLLA-NA-NAOMH O’HUIDHRIN, a learned historian, died this year, according to the *Annals of the Four Masters*. He was the author of a Topographical Poem, intended as a supplement to John O’Dugan’s *Triallam timcheall na Fodhla*. We have seen, under the year 1372, that John O’Dugan had given an account of the chief tribes and territories of Leath Cuinn (Meath, Ulster, and Conaght) at the time of the Anglo-Norman invasion. O’Huidhrin’s work gives an account of the principal families of Leath Mogha (Leinster and Munster), and the districts occupied by them at the same period. The poem consists of seven hundred and eighty verses, beginning *Tuille feasa ar Erinn oigh*: ‘An addition of knowledge on sacred Erin.’
>
> “A very valuable copy of this poem, in the handwriting of Cucoigcriche O’Clery, is in the collection of Manuscripts belonging to the Assistant Secretary to this [the Iberno Celtic] Society.”

Various extracts from these Topographical Poems of O’Dubhagain and O’Huidhrin have been already given by Doctor Lynch, in his *Cambrensis Eversus*, and by the Editor in the notes to his edition of the *Annals of the Four Masters*, and in various other works edited by him for the Irish Archaeological and Celtic Societies; but the entire original text, or a complete translation, has never been published, and the present edition is the only perfect one that has

<summary>This page clarifies that the topographical poem is a collaborative work, with John O'Dugan covering the northern half of Ireland (Leath Cuinn) and Giolla-na-naomh O'Huidhrin covering the southern half (Leath Mogha). It also highlights that while fragments have been published previously, this edition represents the first complete publication of the text based on authoritative manuscripts like those of O'Clery.</summary>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the topographical poems, focusing on the technical meter and poetic style used by the authors, and notes the inclusion of modern discoveries in the commentary.</meta>

<page-num>5</page-num>
->The Topographical Poems of O'Dubhagain & O'Huidhrin.<-

yet appeared. All the discoveries and identifications of ancient territories and tribes made by investigators up to the present day will be found embodied in the notes.

These poems are written in the meter called by the Irish <term>Dan direach</term>, which O’Molloy pronounces the most difficult under the sun. Each quatrain should consist of four lines, each line generally of seven syllables; every line must exhibit alliteration, and the lines should end so as to form a kind of rhyme with each other successively or alternately. Every quatrain should also exhibit union and head.

O’Molloy’s observations on this meter are as follows:—

> “Carmen apud Hibernos est triplex, scilicet metrum, vulgo <term>dan direach</term>, et <term>bruilingeacht</term>, et <term>oglachas</term>, de quibus infra. Maximè autem de metro, omnium quæ unquam vidi, vel audivi ausim dicere, quæ sub sole reperiuntur, difficillimo; quo nimirùm benè semel cognito, nulla in reliquis cognoscendis supererit difficultas. Carmen hoc ut evadat metrum, Hibernis <term>dan direach</term> vel <term>rann direach</term> septem necessariò expostulat, certum scilicet syllabarum numerum, quartorum numerum, concordiam, correspondentiam, extrema, seu terminos, unionem et caput, quæ vulgò dicuntur <term>nuimhir, cheathromhan cinteacht shiolladh in gach ceathromhain, uaim, comhardadh, rinn, agus asroirin uaitne, agus ceann</term>.”—*Grammatica Latino-Hibernica, authore Rev. P. Fr. Francisco O’Molloy, Romæ 1677, pag. 143-4.* See also the Editor’s *Irish Grammar*, pp. 412, 419.

The style of the poems is necessarily very stiff, in some instances defective, and in others redundant. The adjectives, sometimes lavishly used, are neither descriptive of the families nor their territories, except in very rare instances, but merely introduced for the sake of filling up the meter, and to complete the peculiarly mechanical structure of the verse. This will be evident from a comparison of the two copies used, which exhibit very different epithets. These epithets have been closely translated, which gives the English version, in many instances, a rude appearance, but this could not have been avoided without abandoning the attempt to give a literal translation.

The orthography is in general that of the seventeenth century,

<summary>This page describes the technical requirements of the Irish poetic meter Dan direach and explains that the poems' stiff style and repetitive adjectives are due to the rigid rules of the verse structure.</summary>
<keywords>Dan direach, O'Molloy, Irish prosody, quatrains, poetic meter, translation style, Irish Grammar, topographical poems</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the orthography used by the O’Clerys and transitions into a general explanation of ancient Irish tribal and territorial naming conventions.</meta>

the age in which the O'Clerys lived; sometimes, however, they have introduced very ancient forms of spelling, and they mostly use the aspirations and eclipses common in their own times, as ff, pp, $\tau\tau$. They adhere, however, to no regular rule, but write sometimes the ancient, and sometimes modern orthography in the most capricious manner.

### OF THE ANCIENT NAMES OF TRIBES AND TERRITORIES IN IRELAND.

To save useless repetition in the notes, some general explanations are given here of the names of tribes and territories which are of most frequent occurrence throughout these poems; it has also been considered necessary to add some notices of the manner in which Irish names and surnames have been disguised by the custom adopted of reducing both to English forms.

It is now universally admitted that the ancient names of tribes in Ireland were not derived from the territories which they inhabited, but from certain of their distinguished ancestors. In nine cases out of ten, names of territories and of the tribes inhabiting them are identical. The tribe names were formed from those of their ancestors by prefixing the following words:—

1. *Cinel*, kindred, race, descendants; as Cinel Eoghain, the race of Eoghan—*genus Eugenii*. Cinel Conaill, the race of Conall; and this prefix is still retained in the baronies of Kinelarty, Kinelmeaky, and Kinelea.
2. *Clann*, children, race, descendants; as Clann Colmain, the race of Colman, the tribe name of the O'Melaghlins of Meath.
3. *Corc*, *Corca*, race, progeny; as Corca Bhaiscinn, the race of Baiscinn, in the county of Clare; Corca-Duibhne, the race of Duibhne, in the county of Kerry.
4. *Dal*, tribe, progeny; as Dal-Riada, Dal-Araidhe, Dal-Mesincorb, Dal Cais, etc.
5. *Macu*. This prefix appears in very ancient manuscripts in the sense of *filiorum* <note>Of the sons</note>, as Dubthach Macu Lugair, "Dubthach of the sons of Lugar."
6. *Muintir*, family, people; as Muintir Maoilmordha, the tribe

<summary>This page explains the inconsistent orthography of the O'Clery family and introduces the six primary prefixes used to form ancient Irish tribe names from ancestral figures. It clarifies that Irish territorial names are typically identical to the names of the tribes that inhabited them.</summary>

# Ancient Names of Irish Tribes and Territories.

name of the O’Reillys, of East Brefney; Muintir Murchadha, the tribe name of the O’Flahertys, of West Connaught.

7. *Siol*, seed, progeny; as Siol-Muireadhaigh, the tribe name of the O’Conors and their relatives, in the present county of Roscommon; Siol-Anmchadha, the tribe name of the O’Maddens, of Hy-Many; Siol-Maoelruanaidh, the tribe name of the Mac Dermots, of Moylurg.

8. *Tealach*, family; as Tealach Eachdhach, the tribe name of the Magaurans, in County Cavan; Tealach Dunchadha, the tribe name of the Mac Kernans, in the same county.

9. *Sliocht*, progeny; as Sliocht Aedha Slaine, the progeny of Aedh Slaine, in Meath; Sliocht Aineslis, the progeny of Stanislaus, the tribe name of a sept of the O’Donovans, in the parish of Kilmeen, in County Cork.

10. *Ua*, grandson, descendant; plural *Ui*; dative or ablative *Uibh*. This word, which is evidently cognate with the Greek υἱός, *filius*, appears in the names of Irish tribes more frequently than any of the preceding terms, as Ui-Neill, the descendants of Niall, the tribe name of the families descended from Niall of the Nine Hostages, monarch of Ireland in the beginning of the fifth century; Ui-Briuin, that is, the descendants of Brian, the tribe name of the descendants of Brian, the eldest brother of the same monarch.

Some have supposed that the word *Ui*, in such names, signifies land or territory; but that this is an error is very clear from the ancient writers. Adamnan, Abbot of Hy <note>Hy is an ancient name for the island of Iona.</note>, in the seventh century, in his *Vita Columbæ*, published by this Society in 1856, invariably renders *ua*, *ui*, *uibh*, by *nepos*, *nepotes*, *nepotibus*, his habit being to substitute Latin equivalents for Irish proper names as often as practicable. Thus in book ii., chapter xvi., he renders Ua Briuin as *nepos Briuni*; in book iii., chapter v., he translates Ua Ainmirech as *nepos Ainmirech*, retaining the Irish genitive of the name Ainmire; in book iii., chapter xvii., Ua Liathain as *nepos Liathain*; in book i., chapter xlix., Ui-Neill as *nepotes Neilli*, that is, the descendants of Niall; and in book i., chapter xxii., Ui Tuirtre as *nepotes Tuirtre*. The same interpretation of this word, *ua*, *ui*, *uibh*, is supported by the authority of the annalist

<header>Introduction.</header>

Tighernach, and by that of the compiler of the *Annals of Ulster*, and various writers of the lives of Irish Saints: it is therefore unnecessary to provide more examples here; but it may be observed that Colgan, Lynch, O’Flaherty, and all those who treated of Irish history in Latin have understood the word exclusively in this sense. However, although *Ui* does not originally mean land or territory, the tribe name beginning with this word is often used to signify the territory inhabited by the tribe, in the same way as the names of tribes on Ptolemy’s map of Ireland and in Caesar’s *Commentaries on the Gallic War*. Accordingly, while the editor, in his edition of the *Annals of the Four Masters*, has classified all words beginning with *Ua* or *O* under the *Index Nominum* <note>Index of Names</note>, he has assigned those beginning with *Ui* to the *Index Locorum* <note>Index of Places</note>.

In addition to the words listed above, which were prefixed to the names of ancestors to form tribe names, there are others occasionally encountered that are followed by the names of territories, such as *aes* (people), *fir* (men), *aicme* (tribe), and *pobul* (people). Examples include *Aes-Greine*, a people situated in the northeast of the present County Limerick; *Aes-tri-maighe*, meaning the people of the three plains in the same county; *Fir Maighe Feine*, now Fermoy; *Fir-Rois*, the men of Ross, the name of a people in the present County Monaghan; *Fir-Arda*, a tribe settled in and giving its name to the barony of Ferrard, in the present County Louth; and *Pobul Droma*, in the present County Tipperary.

Many other Irish tribe names are formed by adding suffixes, such as *raighe*, *aighe*, *ne*, or *acht*, to the names of their ancestors, such as Caenraighe, Muscraighe, Dartraighe, Calraighe, Ciarraighe, Tradraighe, Partraighe, Osraighe, Orbhraighe, Greagraighe, Ernaidhe, Mairtine, Conmaicne, Olnegmacht, Connacht, Cianacht, Eoghanacht, and others. These are the usual forms of tribe names among the descendants of the *Aithech Tuatha*, or Attacotic families, which are listed in the *Books of Lecan* and *Leinster* as existing in Ireland during the first century. It is likely that the tribe names given on Ptolemy’s map of Ireland are partly imaginative translations and partly modifications of these terms.

The earliest dissertation on the subject of surnames that we know of is the one given by Plutarch in his *Life of Caius Marcius Coriolanus*, but the names he refers to bear more resemblance to nicknames than to hereditary surnames.

It is stated by Ware, Keating, and Dr. John Lynch that family names or hereditary surnames first became established in Ireland during the reign of Brian Borumha, A.D. 1002–1014. This assertion has been repeated by all subsequent Irish writers, but none of them have attempted to question or prove it. The most ancient authority on this subject is found in a manuscript fragment in the Library of Trinity College, Dublin (H. 2. 15.), supposed to be a part of Mac Liag’s *Life of Brian Borumha*, which states:—

->Is e Brian tuc .vii. mainistreaċa eitir aiðme 7 eallaċ, 7 fearonn<-
->amaċ ; 7 dá ċloicteaċ triċat ; 7 is lais ro dainġneað an t-ord posda ;<-
->7 is ri a linn tucað sloinnte ar tus, 7 duthaða do na sloinnte, 7<-
->do rinne criċaireċt caċa tuaite, 7 gaca triċa ced.<-

> “It was Brian who endowed seven monasteries with furniture, cattle, and land; and thirty-two <term>cloictheachs</term> [or round towers]; and it was by him the marriage ceremony was confirmed; and it was during his time that surnames were first given, and territories were [allotted] to the surnames, and the boundaries of every lordship and <term>cantred</term> were fixed.”

That this statement is more rhetorical than accurate will appear from the following alphabetical list, showing the periods at which the ancestors of various important native families flourished or died, according to the Irish Annals. The dates have been added for the most part from the *Annals of Ulster*, or the *Annals of the Four Masters*.

MacEgan of Ui-Maine, flourished 940.
MacEochy, or Keogh, of Ui-Maine, 1290.
MacGillapatrick of Ossory, slain 995.
MacMurrough of Leinster, died 1070.
MacNamara of Thomond, flourished 1074.
O’Boyle of Tirconnell, flourished 900.

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the genealogical list provided on the previous page, explaining the historical process of how Irish families adopted hereditary surnames based on their ancestors.</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>Ancient Names of Irish Tribes and Territories. [ 11 ]</header>

From this list it is evident that in the formation of surnames at this period, the several families adopted the names of their fathers with the prefix Mac, or of their grandfathers, or more remote ancestors, with the prefix O’. The O’Neills of Ulster took their surname from Niall Glundubh, Monarch of Ireland, who was killed by the Danes in the year 919; the O’Briens of Thomond took theirs from Brian Borumha, Monarch of Ireland, who was slain at the battle of Clontarf in the year 1014; and it will be seen that the ancestors of the most distinguished Irish families, whose names have been preserved in the surnames of their descendants, flourished from the year 900 to 950, or 1000. A few exceptions will, however, be found, as in the family of O’Dowda of Tireragh, whose progenitor, Dubhda, flourished about the year 876; in that of O’Kelly of Ui-Maine, whose ancestor, Ceallach, flourished as early as the year 874; and in that of O’Ruarc, of Breifny, whose progenitor, Ruarc, flourished from about the year 820 until 893.

There are also instances to be met with of surnames, established in the tenth century, having been changed to others which were taken from ancestors who flourished at a considerably later period, such as O’Mulrony of Moylurg, who assumed the surname of Mac Dermott from Dermott, chief of Moylurg, who died in the year 1159; <note>The year 1159.—Memoirs of Charles O’Conor, page 305.</note> and O’h-Eochy of Ulidia, who changed the family name to Mac Donlevy.

There are also instances of minor branches of great tribes having changed the original prefix O’ to Mac, or Mac O’, or I, when, having acquired new territories for themselves, they became independent or separate families. Examples include O’Brien changing to Mac I-Brien and Mac Brien, in the instances of Mac I-Brien Ara, Mac Brien Coonagh, and Mac Brien Aharlagh, all offshoots from the great O’Brien family of Thomond; and O’Neill to Mac I-Neill Buidhe, in the instance of a branch of the Tyrone family who settled in the fourteenth century in the counties of Down and Antrim. These surnames having been rejected in modern times, the original surnames of O’Brien and O’Neill have been restored.

<summary>This page describes the historical development of Irish surnames using Mac and O' prefixes, noting that while most originated between 900 and 1000 AD, some were established earlier or changed in later centuries as branches of tribes became independent.</summary>
<keywords>Irish surnames, Mac, O', genealogy, Niall Glundubh, Brian Borumha, O'Neill, O'Brien, nomenclature, Thomond, Ulster, Mac Dermott, family history</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the evolution of Irish surnames, specifically focusing on branches of families that adopted new names and the linguistic roots of the prefixes O’ and Mac.</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>
<header>Introduction.</header>

A branch of the O’Kellys of Ui Maine, in Connaught, took the name of Mac Eochy (now Keogh) from an ancestor named Eochy O’Kelly, who flourished about the year 1290. A branch of the O’Conors of Connaught took the name of Mac Manus from Maghnus (son of Turlogh O’Conor, King of Ireland), who died in the year 1181; and a branch of the Maguires of Fermanagh also took the surname of Mac Manus from Maghnus, the son of Don Maguire, chief of Fermanagh, who died in 1302. A branch of the O’Kanes of Ulster took the name of Mac Bloscaidh (now Mac Closkey) from Bloscadh O’Kane, who flourished in the thirteenth century. Branches of the O’Dohertys of Inishowen took the surnames of Mac Devitt and Mac Connell Og at a relatively late period.

It is, therefore, clear that Irish family names, or hereditary surnames, are formed from the genitive case singular of the names of ancestors who flourished in the tenth or beginning of the eleventh century, or at least from the year 850 until 1290, by prefixing *O’* or *Mac*, such as O’Neill, Mac Carthy, and Mac Murrough. The prefix *O’*, otherwise written *Ua*, literally signifies *nepos*, or grandson—a sense in which it is still used in the province of Ulster—and in a broader sense, any male descendant, like the Latin *nepos*. *Mac* literally signifies *son*, like the Anglo-Norman prefix *Fitz*, and in a broader sense, any male descendant. The word *O’* or *Ua*, as has already been observed, is translated as *nepos*, and *Mac* as *filius* by Adamnan and various other writers; the latter word is evidently related to the Welsh *Map* or *Ap*, and is equivalent to the Anglo-Norman *Fitz*, which is a corruption of the Latin *filius*.

Giraldus Cambrensis, in his *Hibernia Expugnata* (book 1, chapter 6), Latinizes the name of the King of Leinster, Dermod Mac Murchadh, as *Dermitius Murchardides*; from this, it is evident that he regarded the prefix *Mac* as equivalent to the Greek patronymic termination ιδης. The only difference, therefore, to be observed between O’ and Mac in surnames is that the families who took the prefix Mac called themselves after their father, and those who took the prefix O’ formed their surname from the name of their grandfather or a more remote ancestor.

<summary>This page details how various branches of Irish families adopted new surnames between the 12th and 13th centuries and explains the linguistic origins and meanings of the prefixes O' and Mac.</summary>
<keywords>Irish surnames, O'Kelly, Mac Eochy, Mac Manus, O'Kane, Mac Closkey, Mac Devitt, genealogy, nepos, filius, Giraldus Cambrensis, patronymics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the formation of Irish surnames, specifically addressing the prefix used by women and the social perceptions associated with the prefixes O' and Mac.</meta>

<header>Ancient Names of Irish Tribes and Territories.</header>
<page-num>[ 13 ]</page-num>

*Ni*, a contraction of *inghen* (a daughter), was used in the surnames of women instead of Mac, Ua, or O'. Thus, a female of the O'Brien family was called Ni-Brien; one of the O'Donovans, Ni-Donovan. However, this is now obsolete among the English-speaking part of the Irish population, although it is strictly followed by those who speak the Irish language.

It is perhaps not an improbable conjecture that at the period when surnames first became hereditary, some families went back several generations to select an illustrious ancestor from whom to take a surname. A very extraordinary instance of this method of forming Irish surnames occurred in our own time in the province of Connaught, where John Geoghegan (or more correctly MacEochagain, in English Mageoghegan), Esq., of Bunowen Castle, in the west of County Galway, applied to George IV for a license to reject the surname which his ancestors had borne for about 800 years—from their progenitor, Eochagan, son of Cosgrach, chief of Cinel-Fiacha in Westmeath in the tenth century—and to take a new name from his more remote and more illustrious ancestor, Niall of the Nine Hostages, monarch of Ireland in the fourth century. His Majesty granted this license, and the sons and grandsons of this John Geoghegan now bear the name of O'Neill. The other branches of the Mageoghegan family, however, still retain the surname that was established in the tenth century as the distinguishing name of the chief family of the race of Fiacha, a younger son of the monarch Niall of the Nine Hostages.

From the similarity and practical meaning attached to the words O' and Mac in surnames, it might be expected that they would generally be considered as conferring the same respectability on the bearer; yet this is far from being the case. It is popularly believed in every part of Ireland that the prefix O' was a kind of title among the Irish, while Mac was a mark of no distinction whatever; they believe that any common Irishman may bear the prefix Mac, while one must have some claim to royalty, nobility, or high birth before he can presume to prefix O' to his name.

<summary>This page explains the use of the feminine surname prefix "Ni" and details a 19th-century instance where a family changed its name from Mageoghegan to O'Neill to claim a more ancient lineage. It also describes the popular Irish belief that "O'" denotes higher social status than "Mac."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the prefixes O' and Mac, specifically addressing the misconception that O' is inherently more prestigious than Mac and noting the historical use and modern changes of these prefixes in Ireland and Scotland.</meta>

<header>Introduction.</header>
<page-num>14</page-num>

This is the universal feeling in the province of Connaught, where the gentry of Milesian descent call themselves O'Conor, O'Flaherty, O'Malley, O'Dowda, O'Hara, O'Gara, etc., and the peasantry, their collateral relatives, are called Connor, Flaherty, Malley, Dowd, Hara, Gara, etc. All this, however, is a popular error, for the prefix O' is in no way more respectable than Mac, nor is either the one or the other an index to any specific respectability, since every family of Firbolgic, Milesian, and even Danish origin in Ireland is entitled to use either O' or Mac as the first part of its surname. This popular error is of comparatively modern growth. It has been generally known that O'Neill was King of Ulster, O'Conor king of Connaught, and O'Brien king of Munster, and therefore it is assumed that the prefix O' must be a mark of great distinction. But some of the humblest people in Irish history have used the prefix O', and some of the noblest have used Mac, such as Mac Murrogh, king of Leinster; Mac Carthy, king of Desmond; Mac Mahon, king of Oriel; and Magenis, chief of Iveagh in Ulster. The Patent Rolls from the reign of James I show that the O' was prefixed to the surnames of the most obscure and humble families of the native Irish as well as to those of the highest; and that the O' is much more common than the Mac in Irish surnames of that period.

It is, therefore, certain that the prefixes O' and Mac<margin>
The prefixes O' and Mac.—It has been alleged that names beginning with Mac are, generally speaking, much more modern than those starting with O', and for the most part belong to branches that broke off long after the O' had been established in the name. It must be admitted, however, that the descendants of the Irish in the Highlands of Scotland never adopted the O', for which no reason has been given. It appears from the Census of Ireland for 1851 that the O's have nearly all been dropped, except among the gentry, while the Macs have increased, particularly in Ulster, likely due to Scottish colonization and influence. Many families of Highland descent have Anglicized their names, such as MacDonald to Donaldson, MacAedha to Hughson or Hewson, MacEan to Johnson, etc. Even some of the descendants of the historical family of O'Brollaghan, who emigrated from Ulster to the Highlands, have changed their name to Brodie. This change was evidently made to disguise their Irish origin. In Ireland, the name O'Brollaghan is always Anglicized as Bradley; and, as might be expected, it is popularly believed that Bradley is an English translation of O'Brollaghan.
</margin> are of equal importance, both meaning male descendant, and that neither indicates—

any kind of respectability, unless the pedigree is proved and the history of the family is distinguished.

As examples, the names of O’Donovan and Mac Carthy may be cited. The former, before the Revolution of 1688, always had the O’ prefixed as an indication of descent from Donovan, chief of the plains of <term>Ui Fidhgeinte</term> in what is now the county of Limerick, who was slain by the monarch Brian Borumha in the year 977; but the Mac prefixed in the latter name is a mark of superior descent, namely, from Carthach, the great-grandson of Ceallachan Cashel, King of Munster. His descendants held royal power in Desmond before the English invasion and, after the fall of the Geraldines, enjoyed the highest rank in that same territory under the English government until the Revolution of 1688.

This popular error seems to gain some support from the fact that the ancient Irish, for reasons we cannot now understand, never prefixed the O’ to any surname derived from an art, trade, or science (with the exception, perhaps, of O’Gowan, from *gobhan*, "a smith"), the prefix *Mac* having always been used in such instances. For we never encounter, as derivatives of *saor* ("a carpenter"), *bard* ("a poet"), or *filidh* ("a poet"), the forms O’ an tSaoir, O’ an Bhaird, or O’ an Fhilidh, but rather Mac an tSaoir, Mac an Fhilidh, and Mac an Bhaird; and surnames formed this way never ranked as high as those which were formed from the names of kings or chieftains.

It may also be noted that the O’ was never prefixed to names beginning with the word *gilla* (meaning a youth, attendant, or servant), the cause of which is also unclear.

Another very strange error prevails in the North of Ireland regarding these prefixes O’ and Mac: the belief that every surname in the province of Ulster in which Mac forms the first syllable is of Highland Scottish origin, while those beginning with O’ are of Irish origin; for example, that O’Neill and O’Kane are Irish, while Mac Loughlin and Mac Closkey are of Scottish descent. This error originates from the fact that Scottish families never prefix the O’ to their names, while the Irish use the O’ far more frequently than the Mac; it happens, however, that in the two in-

# Introduction.

instances cited, the family of Mac Loughlin is the senior branch of that of O'Neill, and Mac Closkey is a well-known offshoot of that of O'Kane. The prevalence of the O' prefix in the surnames of Irish families over the Mac appears from the Irish Genealogical books and from the Patent Rolls of James I, in which there are at least two surnames beginning with O' for every one beginning with Mac. The same fact also appears from the Index to the *Annals of the Four Masters*. At the present day, however, the very opposite will be found to be the case—nearly all the O' prefixes are rejected and the Macs retained.

An idea likewise popular among the Irish of every class is that only five Irish families are entitled to have the O' prefixed to their surnames, while it is universally admitted that any Irish family, from Mac Carthy and Mac Murrough down to Mac Gucken and Mac Phaudeen, has a full title to the prefix Mac. This notion may have arisen from the fact that for some centuries after the English settlement, only five families of purely Irish blood were admitted to the privileges of English law. These were O'Brien, O'Neill, O'Conor, O'Melaghlin, and Mac Murrogh.

Another extraordinary error prevailed among the Irish gentry of Milesian blood—namely, that the chief of the family alone was entitled to have the O' prefixed to his surname; but there is not a single passage in the authentic Irish Annals, in the Anglo-Irish records, or in the Irish Genealogical Books which even suggests that such a custom ever existed among the ancient Irish at any period of their history; for every member of the family used the O' prefix as well as the chief himself. However, a distinction was made between the chief and the members of his clan in the following manner: In all official documents, the chief used the surname only—*Misi O'Neill*, "*I am O'Neill;*" *Misi O'Domhnaill*, "*I am O'Donnell;*" like the King of Spain's signature, "*Yo El Rey.*" In conversation, also, the surname alone was used, but the definite article was frequently prefixed, such as *the O'Neill*, *the O'Conor*, or *the O'Brien*; while in annals and other historical documents, in which it was necessary to dis-

<summary>This page refutes the modern misconceptions that the O' prefix was reserved for only five families or exclusively for family chiefs, explaining that historical records show it was widely used by all family members.</summary>

...distinguish a particular chief from his predecessors or ancestors, the chief of a family was designated by giving him the family name first, and the Christian or baptismal name after it in parentheses. But the subordinate members of the chief’s family and sept always had their Christian names prefixed, as is done at the present day, and the O’ was always retained, as in *Brian O’Neill*, *Con O’Donnell*, and *Turlogh O’Brien*.

---

### OF ANCIENT IRISH AGNOMINA.

Besides the surnames, or hereditary family names, which the Irish people assumed from the names of their ancestors by prefixing O or Mac, it appears from the Irish Annals, Genealogical Books, etc., that most, if not all, of their chieftains had certain agnomina attached to their Christian names, and sometimes to their surnames, by which they were distinguished from one another. These agnomina—or, as they may in many instances be called, sobriquets—were in several cases given to them because of some accomplishment, personal physical trait, disposition, or quality of mind, or from their places of fosterage, and very frequently from the places where they died or were killed. Of the greater number of these agnomina, the pedigree of the royal Irish family of *O’Neill* furnishes examples, such as *Niall Ruadh*, that is, Niall the Red, who flourished about the year 1225 and was so called because he had red hair; *Aedh Toinleasc* <note>podice-segnis</note>, who died in 1230 and was so called by antiphrasis because of his restless activity; *Niall Mor*, Niall the Great or the Large-bodied, who died in 1397; and *Conn Bacach*, Con the Lame, who was created Earl of Tyrone in 1542. Of the same family were *Henry Aimhreidh*, Henry the Contentious; *Shane an-diomais*, John of the pride or ambition; *Aedh Balbh*, Aedh the Stammering; *Aedh Ballach*, Aedh the Speckled; *Aedh Buidhe*, Aedh the Yellow; *Aedh Reamhar*, Aedh the Fat; and *Aedh Geimhleach*, Aedh of the Fetters.

Regarding the agnomina derived from the places where and the families by whom they were fostered, the same tribe of *O’Neill*

Various agnomina given to Irish chieftains from the place or territory in which they were fostered are also frequently found in family pedigrees, such as in that of O'Brien of Thomond, *Donogh Cairbreach*, who was so called because he was fostered in the territory of *Cairbre Aebhdha*, in the present county of Limerick. In the family of Mac Murrough of Leinster, *Donnell Cavanagh* was so named because he was fostered by the *comharba* <note>ecclesiastical successor</note> of St. Cavan, at Kilcavan, near Gorey, in Odea, in the present county of Wexford. The agnomen of this Donnell has been adopted for many centuries as a surname by his descendants, a practice very unusual among Irish families. In the family of Mac Donnell of Scotland, which is of Irish descent, *John Cahanach* was so called because he was fostered by O'Cahan, or O'Kane, in the present county of Londonderry.

The genealogical histories of other Irish families record various instances of agnomina having been applied by posterity to chieftains from the place of their death: as, in the family of O'Kelly, Tadhg, chief of Ui-Maine, is called *Catha Bhriain* ("of the Battle of Brian"), because he was killed in the battle of Clontarf, fought by Brian Borumha against the Northmen in the year 1014. This battle is also called the "Battle of Brian" in the Danish Sagas, and the ancestors of Danish families who fought in it are similarly designated by Danish genealogists. In the family of O'Neill, *Brian Catha Duin* ("of the Battle of Down")^c was so called by posterity because he was killed in a battle fought at Downpatrick in the year 1260. [ 19 ]

# Ancient Irish Agnomina.

*Siudaine* was so called in later times because he was killed at the wood of Siudain in the year 1267; and in the Mac Carthy family, the noted *Finghin Reanna Roin* was so surnamed because he was slain by the English at the castle of Rinn Roin (*Ringrone*) in the year 1261.

On this subject of agnomina and nicknames among the Irish, Sir Henry Piers speaks as follows in the year 1682, in his *Chorographical Description of the County of Westmeath*. This was written in the form of a letter to Anthony Dopping, Bishop of Meath, and published about a century later in the first volume of *Vallancey's Collectanea*:—

> "Every Irish surname or family name has either O or Mac prefixed. Concerning this, I have found some make this observation—though I do not dare to guarantee that it shall hold universally true—that those who have O prefixed were formerly superior lords or princes, such as O'Neal, O'Donnell, O'Melaghlin, etc., and those who have Mac were only great men, namely lords or <term>thanes</term>, such as Mac Gennis, Mac Loghlin, Mac Doncho, etc. But however this observation may hold, it is certain they take much liberty, and seem to do it with delight, in giving nicknames; if a man has any imperfection or bad habit, he is sure to hear of it in his nickname. Thus, if he is blind, lame, squint-eyed, gray-eyed, a stammerer in speech, or left-handed, he will certainly have one of these added to his name. This is also true of his hair color, such as black, red, yellow, brown, etc.; and from his age, such as young or old; or from what he devotes himself to or takes great delight in, such as draining, building, fencing, and the like. So it is that no man whatsoever who lives among them or converses with them can escape a nickname. And sometimes they are so unrestrained in this kind of mockery that they will give nicknames <term>per antiphrasim</term>, or by contrariety of speech. Thus a man of excellent qualities, beloved by all men, shall be called *grana*, that is, naughty or fit to be complained of; if a man has a beautiful face or lovely eyes, they will call him *Cuiegh*, that is, squint-eyed; if he is a great housekeeper <note>meaning a generous host</note>, he shall be called *Ackerisagh*, that is, greedy."—(*Collectanea*, vol. I., p. 113.)

In the fourteenth and fifteenth centuries, when the Irish families increased and their territories were divided into two or more parts among rivals of the same family, each of

Thus, among the O'Conors of Connacht we find *O'Conor Don*, that is, O'Conor the Brown-haired, and *O'Conor Ruadh*, that is, Red-haired. The distinction in this case was first made in the year 1384, when *Turlogh Don* and *Turlogh Ruadh*, who had been for some time competing with each other for the chieftainship of Sil-Murray, agreed to have it divided equally between them; on which occasion it was arranged that the former should be called *O'Conor Don*, and the latter *O'Conor Ruadh*.—*Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1384, p. 702. It is now wrongly supposed by some that the epithet *Don* added to the name of the chief of this sept is of Spanish origin.

In Connaught we also find the Mac Dermots, of Moylurg, divided into three distinct families, the head of whom was styled *the* Mac Dermot, and the other two, who were tributary to him, called Mac Dermot *Ruadh*, the Red, and Mac Dermot *Gall*, or the Anglicized. In Thomond the Mac Namaras split into two distinct families, distinguished by the names of Mac Namara *Finn*, the Fair-haired, and Mac Namara *Reagh*, or the Swarthy.

In Desmond, the family of Mac Carthy separated into three great branches, known by the names of Mac Carthy *Mor*, the Great; Mac Carthy *Reagh*, the Swarthy; and Mac Carthy *Muscraigheach*, of Muskerry; and there were various minor branches of the same family, known as Mac Carthy *Glas*, the Green; Mac Carthy *Cluasach*, of the long ears; Mac Carthy *Duna*, Mac Carthy *Muckalagh*, and various others. ### CONCERNING THE IRISH NAMES ANCIENTLY ADOPTED BY THE ENGLISH IN IRELAND.

The preceding notices are sufficient to indicate the nature of the surnames and agnomina in use among the Scotic or Milesian Irish families. A few observations may now be made on the effect which the Anglo-Norman invasion, and the introduction of English laws, language, and names, have had in changing or modifying them; and on the other hand, the influence which the Irish may have had in changing or modifying English surnames.

After the murder of the Great Earl of Ulster, William de Burgo, the third Earl of that name, in 1333, and the consequent decline of English power in Ireland, many, if not all, of the distinguished Anglo-Norman families settled in Connaught and Munster became Hibernicized—*Hibernis ipsis Hiberniores*—spoke the Irish language, and adopted surnames like those of the Irish by prefixing Mac to the Christian names of their ancestors, but never O' in any instance; no reason has been assigned for this latter fact. Thus the De Burgos in Connaught adopted the name Mac William, from their great ancestor, William Fitz-Adelm De Burgo, and became divided into two great branches, called Mac William *Uachtar* and Mac William *Iochtair*,^d that is, Mac William Upper, and

---

<note>^d *Mac William Iochtair.*—It is worth noting here that Sir Henry Docwra, in his account of the services of Sir Richard Bingham in the province of Connaught during the reign of Queen Elizabeth (printed in the *Miscellany of the Celtic Society*), does not appear to have known that the Lower Mac William Bourkes, of whom the Earl of Mayo is the current chief, were of Anglo-Norman descent; and that the compiler of the *Book of Howth* thought that Mac William of Clanrickard, the ancestor of the Marquis of Clanrickarde, was merely Irish, and not of English or British descent. The truth is, the Bourkes of Connaught had become so Irish that the nobility of the English Pale in Ireland chose to regard them as being of purely Irish descent.</note>

They alleged, as the main cause for having revolted from their original loyalty, "that some of her Majesty’s officers had been too much charmed by the beauty and profitability of the said barony, and therefore had sought to take many of their lives through indirect and unjust means." They proceed to say that, lacking a good leader from their own tribe, they have chosen Thomas Staunton of Wolverton, county of Warwick, as their chieftain.</note> The chief of the Barretts of Tirawley in Connaught took the surname of Mac Wattin, and minor branches of the same family called themselves Mac Andrew, Mac Tomin, and Mac Robert; the first was located in the Bacs territory, situated between Lough Con and the River Moy, and the others at Dundonnell in Erris. The names of Mac Damore and Mac Vaddock are at present unknown in the county of Wexford, the former being disguised under the anglicized form of Davis, and the latter under that of Maddock.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the adoption of Irish surnames by English families, then shifts to the legal mandates requiring the native Irish within the English Pale to adopt English surnames and customs.</meta>

<page-num>25</page-num>
<header>English Names assumed by the Irish.</header>

Sir Henry Piers of Tristernagh, in the county of Westmeath, who wrote about a century later than Spenser, complained as follows regarding the custom among the families of English descent of changing their surnames:

> "In the next place, I rank the degeneracy of many English families as a great hindrance to the reduction of this people to civility, occasioned not only by fostering—that is, having their children nursed and bred during their tender years by the Irish—but much more by marriages with them. By these means, our English, in too many great families, became in a few generations one both in manners and interest with the Irish, so much so that many of them have not hesitated to assume Irish names and appellations; instances of this are all too many even to this very day: thus a Birmingham is called by them Mac Yores, Fitz-Simmons Mac Ruddery, Weysly Mac Falrene, etc., and from men thus metamorphosed, what could be expected?"—*Vallancey’s Collectanea*, Vol. I., p. 105.

---

->OF THE ASSUMPTION OF ENGLISH NAMES BY THE NATIVE IRISH.<-

THE Irish families who lived within the English Pale and its vicinity gradually conformed to English customs and assumed English surnames; a practice which was deemed to be of such political importance that it was thought worthy of the interference of the Parliament of the English Pale. Accordingly, it was enacted by the Statute of 5 Edward IV (1465), that every Irishman dwelling within the Pale—then comprising the counties of Dublin, Meath, Uriel <note>Louth</note>, and Kildare—should take an English surname. This Act, which curiously illustrates the history of Irish family names, was as follows (*Rot. Parl.*, c. 16):

> "An Act, that the Irishmen dwelling in the counties of Dublin, Meath, Uriel, and Kildare, shall go appareled like Englishmen, and wear their beards after the English manner, swear allegiance, and take English surnames."
> "At the request of the Commons it is ordained and established by authority of the said Parliament, that every Irishman who dwells between or among Englishmen in the county of Dublin, Meath, Uriel, and Kil-"

<summary>Sir Henry Piers critiques the cultural assimilation of English families into Irish customs, while the text introduces the 1465 Statute requiring Irish residents of the Pale to adopt English names and styles.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the citation of the Statute of 5 Edward IV (1465), which required the Irish living in the Pale to adopt English dress and surnames, and examines the historical accuracy of how these names were translated or adopted.</meta>

<page-num>26</page-num>
<header>Introduction.</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

dare, shall dress like an Englishman in his apparel, and shave his beard above the mouth, and shall be within one year sworn the liege man of the king in the hands of the lieutenant or deputy, or such as he will assign to receive this oath, because of the multitude that is to be sworn; and shall take to himself an English surname of a town, such as Sutton, Chester, Trim, Skryne, Cork, or Kinsale; or a color, such as White, Black, or Brown; or a trade or profession, such as Smith or Carpenter; or an office, such as Cook or Butler; and that he and his descendants shall use this name under pain of forfeiting his goods yearly until these requirements are fulfilled, to be levied twice a year for the king’s wars, according to the discretion of the king’s lieutenant or his deputy.” —*5 Edward IV., c. 3. (Statutes at Large, Ireland, Vol. I., p. 29.)*

“In obedience to this law,” says Harris (*Works of Sir James Ware*, vol. ii., p. 58), “the Shanachs took the name of Foxes; the Mac-an-gabhans, of Smiths; Geals, of Whites; the Brannachs, of Walshes; and many others; the said words being only literal translations from the Irish into the English language.”

Harris, however, was very much mistaken in supposing that the Brannachs (<term>Breatnaiġ</term>, i.e., Britons), of the English Pale in Ireland, are an Irish family, or that any ancient Irish family had borne that name before the Anglo-Norman and Welsh families settled in Ireland towards the end of the twelfth century; he was also wrong in assuming that the Irish word for *Geal*, white, was by itself ever used as the name of any family in Ireland. In the other two instances he is correct; for the head of the O’Caharnys of Teffia, who was usually styled the Shinnagh (*An Sionach*), Anglicized his name into Fox, and the Mac-an-Gowans and O’Gowans translated their names into Smith.

The importance attached by this Act to the bearing of an English surname soon induced many of the less distinguished Irish families of the English Pale and its vicinity to translate or disguise their Irish names, so as to make them appear English; thus *Mac an t-saoir*, Mac Intire, was altered to Carpenter; *Mac Speallain*, Mac Spallane, to Spenser; *Mac Con-cogry*, Mac Cogry, to L’Estrange, etc.; but the more eminent families of the Pale and its vicinity, as

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the 1465 Statute of Edward IV regarding surnames and its impact on Irish families within the Pale, transitioning into the observations of Edmund Spenser in the late 16th century.</meta>

<page-num>[ 27 ]</page-num>
<header>English Names assumed by the Irish.</header>

Mac Murrogh, O'Brennan, O'Toole, O'Byrne, O'Murchoe, Mac Gillapatrick, Mac Damore, O'Nolan, O'More, O'Dunn, O'Ryan, O'Dempsey, O'Conor Faly, O'Kelly, and others, retained their original Irish names unaltered. It is certain, however, that the translation and assimilation of Irish surnames to English was carried out to a great extent in the vicinity of Dublin and throughout Leinster; hence it may at this day be safely concluded that many families bearing English surnames throughout what was formerly the English Pale, are undoubtedly of Milesian, or of Danish origin.

It appears, however, that the Statute referred to did not have the intended effect to any great extent; for about a century after it had passed, we find Spenser recommending a revival of it, since the Irish had then become as Irish as ever. His observations on this point are highly interesting, as they throw light on the history of Irish surnames towards the close of the sixteenth century. They are as follows:—

> “Moreover, for the better breaking up of these heads and <term>septs</term>, which (as I told you) was one of the greatest strengths of the Irish, it seems to me it would be very good to renew that old Statute which was made in the reign of Edward the Fourth in Ireland. By this law it was commanded—whereas all men used to be called by the name of their <term>septs</term>, according to their various nations, and had no surnames at all—that from henceforth each one should take for himself a distinct surname, either of his trade and profession, or of some quality of his body or mind, or of the place where he lived, so that everyone should be distinguished from the others, or for the most part. By this means they shall not only not depend upon the head of their <term>sept</term>, as they do now, but also in time learn to completely forget their Irish nation. And along with this, I would also wish all the O’s and Mac’s, which the heads of <term>septs</term> have taken to their names, to be utterly forbidden and extinguished. For since that was an ordinance (as some say) first made by O’Brien [meaning Brian Borumha] for the strengthening of the Irish, the abolishing of it will weaken them just as much.”—*View of the State of Ireland, A.D. 1596, p. 108 (Dublin, 1633.)*

In the month of September, 1585, sessions were held at Dunnamona, in the county of Mayo, presided over by Sir Richard

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the efforts of the English administration to suppress Irish surnames and clans, moving from the recommendations of Edmund Spenser to the practical implementation of these policies in Connaught under Sir Richard Bingham.</meta>

<page-num>28</page-num>
<header>Introduction.</header>

Bingham, Chief Commissioner and Governor of the Province of Connaught, together with Sir Nicholas White, Master of the Rolls, “for the perfecting of the last composition made within the said Province.” Sir Henry Docwra<gloss>m</gloss> states that the “plot of this composition was devised by Sir Richard, on purpose to take away the greatness of the Irish lords, with their names, Macs, and Os, so that the inferior subjects might be freed from their Irish customs, <term>cuttings</term>, and unreasonable exactions, and (by knowing what was their own), be drawn to depend ever after upon the state, and not on those Irish lords, or gentlemen; which also might not only much benefit her Majesty in times of any stirs or revolts, by drawing the common people from following the great chief lords, but also bring a more certain yearly rent or revenue into her Highness's coffers than was previously accustomed.”

About the same period, various natives who were employed as clerks, interpreters, and spies for the State successfully changed and concealed their Irish names. Of these, the most deserving of notice were John Mac Laighid (Lye or Leigh);<gloss>n</gloss> William O’Duinne (Doyne); Sir Patrick Fox; Sir Thomas Shaen;<gloss>o</gloss> and Patrick Mac Crossan (Crosbie).

In a tract in the State Paper Office, dated July 3, 1600, it is stated that Patrick Crosbie, or Crossan, was a mere Irishman by birth, and that his father had been a rhymer or bard to the O’Moores. The aged Earl of Ormonde, in a letter written on December 2, 1601, to Sir Robert Cecil regarding the bad conduct of the subordinate Government officials of the day, observes that Crosby’s real surname was Mac-y-Crossane, and that his ancestors had been chief rhymers to the O’Moores and O’Connors.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the suppression and alteration of Irish surnames, shifting from 16th-century administrative policies to 17th and 18th-century social pressures and the voluntary Anglicization of names among both lower and upper classes.</meta>

<page-num>29</page-num>
<header>English Names assumed by the Irish.</header>

Towards the close of the seventeenth century, Sir Henry Piers of Tristernagh, in his account of the county of Westmeath, made the following observations on the less distinguished Irish families then beginning to take English surnames:

> “These, I suppose, may be counted among the causes of the slow progress this nation has made toward civility and accommodation to our English laws and customs; yet, notwithstanding these, this people—especially in this and the adjoining counties—have in our days become more polite and civil than in former ages, and some are very eager to accommodate themselves to English fashions, particularly in their clothing, language, and surnames, which by all possible ways they strive to make English or English-like; I say this of the lower ranks among them. Thus you have the surname Mac Gowne calling himself Smith; Mac Killy, Cock; Mac Spollane, Spencer; Mac Kegry, Lestrange, etc., in this making small amends for our degenerate English mentioned before.” — *Vallancey’s Collectanea, i., 108.*

Many others, even of the most distinguished Irish family names, were similarly Anglicized, such as O’Conor to Conyers, O’Brien to Brine, O’Reilly to Ridley, O’Donnell to Daniel, O’Sullivan to Silvan and Silvers, O’Murchoe to Morpie, Mac Carthy to Carter, etc.

This change from Irish into English names continued to increase after the Revolution of 1688, when the natives who remained in Ireland were completely subjected. About this period, numbers of the oppressed native Irish reduced their names as much as possible to the level of English pronunciation, rejecting in almost every instance the O’ and Mac, and making various other changes in their names so as to give them an English appearance. These changes did not fail to draw the censure of native rhymers; a specimen of whose satires on this subject is extant in the following epigram,<note>p</note> written by the Rev. Christopher Mac Conway (Mac Conmhuidhe) in the last century, regarding a gentleman of Tyrone altering his old name of Phelim O’Neill to Felix Neele:

### OF THE IRISH FAMILIES WHO RETAINED THEIR ANCIENT NAMES ON THE CONTINENT AND IN IRELAND.

The respectability of the native Irish was maintained and increased abroad by the distinguished careers of numerous members of the old Gaelic families of Ireland, who became exiles as a result of the Penal Laws.

In those countries where they were allowed to exercise their abilities, “we will find them,” wrote Dr. O’Conor,<note>s *Dr. O’Conor.*—Memoirs of C. O’Conor, p. 154.</note> “whether in an ecclesiastical, military, or mercantile capacity, triumphing over

Every sign indicating their Irish origin was preserved with pride by those distinguished exiles; and, perhaps, nothing can more strikingly display the high regard in which the members of the historic Irish families were held on the Continent than the fact that Henry O’Donnell, third son of Charles Duff (*Dubh*) O’Donnell, of Murresk, in the county of Mayo, received in marriage, in 1754, a near relative of the Empress Maria Theresa, a Princess of the illustrious house of Cantacuzene, descended from John Cantacuzene, the Byzantine emperor and historian, who reigned from 1347 to 1355. In the following extract from the patent to Count Maximilian O’Donnell—who was Aide-de-camp to the Emperor Francis Joseph I., whom he saved from assassination in February 1853—the various distinguished representatives of the O’Donnell family in Austria are noted, with a general allusion to the nobility of that branch of the race which settled in Spain:

> “He [Count Maximilian] is descended from the exceedingly ancient and very illustrious race, the Chiefs of Donegal and <term>Dynasts</term> of the former Tyrconnell, in Ireland. History speaks of them in early ages, when Christianity was first introduced into that country, and praises the zeal with which they founded churches and monasteries to assist in the spreading of the true faith. In later times, they exercised princely power in the land of their descent and enjoyed widely-extended military fame. Shortly before the final incorporation of Ireland with the Royal Crown of Great Britain, Roderick, one of this ancient princely race, was invested with the dignity of Count” <note>meaning Earl</note> “of the above-named province; as we have satisfactorily confirmed by the original document of King James I., with the seal of Ireland attached to it, dated the 10th day of February, in the first year of his reign in England, France, and Ireland, and the thirty-seventh year of his reign in Scotland. Various events in ecclesiastical and political affairs, unnecessary to list now, compelled the above-named” Earl “to leave his native land and seek refuge in a Catholic foreign country, as his elder brother, Hugh, had previously done. As early as 1849, as a mark of our confidence in his zeal and abilities, we appointed him as an aide-de-camp to our person. At all times he has fulfilled the high expectations we formed of him; and this was most fully demonstrated when, at the risk of his own life, he warded off from our imperial person the murderous attack of an assassin on February 18th of the current year, by which he rendered to us, to our royal house, and to our realm an unforgettable service. We rewarded him by conferring upon him the Cross of our Order of Saint Leopold. But so that he may enjoy a lasting and prominent sign of our just recognition, which can be passed down to his descendants, we grant him, further, all the rights and privileges of an Austrian Count; and, as a further proof of imperial and royal grace and favor, we henceforth increase his hereditary and family coat of arms by the addition of our own initials, the shield of our Most Serene ducal house of Austria, and finally, the double-headed eagle of our empire, to remain as a visible and everlasting memorial of his proven and devoted services.”

against Napoleon. The first, Henry, was one of the ablest and most popular Spanish commanders; he distinguished himself greatly against the French Generals or Marshals Duhesme, Saint-Cyr, Augereau, Suchet, Macdonald, and others in Catalonia, where he was appointed Captain-General. By destroying or capturing the force of General Schwartz at the village of La Bisbal (where he received his sixteenth musket wound), he obtained the title of Count of La Bisbal. He was afterwards a member of the Regency of the kingdom, and the organizer and Commander-in-Chief of the Army of Reserve, known as the Army of Andalusia.

His brother Charles was appointed Captain-General of the Canary Islands by popular election in 1808. As a General of Division in the army of the Marquis of La Romana, he opposed the French General Reynier (or Regnier) in the south; he later joined Lord Wellington’s forces at Torres Vedras and was again opposed to Reynier. While acting as Commander-in-Chief *ad interim* in the kingdom of Valencia, he cooperated with Commodore Adams of the *Invincible* and other vessels in harassing the enemy's maritime posts. Then, under Don Joaquín Blake, he distinguished himself in the skirmishes and the battle connected with the siege of Saguntum (or Morviedro) by Suchet. Subsequently, as a Major-General (or Camp-Marshal), he participated in the defense of Valencia by Blake; after becoming a prisoner of war when that place fell, he was sent to France.

After the general peace treaty and his release, he was appointed Commander-in-Chief of Old Castile.

His brother Joseph served as Colonel of the Princess's Regiment under the Marquis of La Romana and Don Francisco Ballesteros (or Valesteros) in the north. He was a General of Division in the third Spanish army under Don Manuel Freire in the south and became Chief of Staff for that army. He then served as General-in-Chief of that army and the second Spanish army opposed to Marshal Suchet; he was afterwards appointed by the Spanish Regency to command the reserve force organized on the Isle of Leon.

The youngest brother of these three officers, named Alexander, held political views opposite to theirs; he was a Colonel of a Spanish regiment under King Joseph <note>Joseph Bonaparte</note> that accompanied Emperor Napoleon to Russia in 1812.

The most recent prominent representative of the Spanish O’Donnells (a grandson of their patriarch, Joseph, through his second son, Charles) is Leopold. Because of his notable service to the Christina party as General of its forces against the Carlists, he has been created Count of Lucena and Governor of Cuba.

Leopold O’Donnell has since attained even higher honors, as Prime Minister of Spain, Conqueror of Morocco, and Duke of Tetuan.

In the same work, a biographical sketch will be found of the most remarkable gentleman of the name in the service of France, and the preserver of the clan's famous battle-reliquary, or the *Cathach*, Daniel O’Donnell. After having attained the rank of Brigadier, he died in July 1735 at St. Germain-en-Laye, in his seventieth year. Other O’Donnells are also mentioned in the French service as officers in the Irish regiments of O’Donnell, Berwick, Clare, and Dillon, some of whom were Chevaliers or Knights of St. Louis. Among those officers, not all were indeed of the great northern or Ulster sept <note>clan</note> of O’Donnells; some were of the Munster sept of Corcobaskin in County Clare. Yet, as members of an ancient Irish race serving abroad, they are entitled to be noticed here, though they are of much less fame than their Tirconnell namesakes. Many of the O’Donnells in Ireland, especially in Munster, had changed their name to Daniel, imitating the Protestant Archbishop of Tuam, who had adopted this form of the name as early as the reign of James I. However, they have now nearly all resumed the original name, with the "O" prefix.

Of the O’Neills, there have been general officers in Spain from the century following the fall of their Princes or Chieftains of Tyrone until the great war against Napoleon. Of those officers, it is only necessary to mention Major-General Owen Roe O’Neill, the brave defender of Arras and conqueror of Benburb, and his nephew, Major-General Hugh Duff O’Neill, the strong opponent of the Cromwellians at Clonmel and Limerick. One of the name was enrolled among the Spanish nobility in 1679 with the title of "Marqués de la Granja." This title has continued in that branch of the race to our time, and its representative visited Ireland some years ago. In France, up to the rank of Chef-de-Brigade, and including Chevaliers of St. Louis and the Legion of Honor, O’Neills were to be seen in all the infantry regiments belonging to the Irish Brigade, in the Garde-du-Corps, etc. Of these, some, however, owing to

their connection with the regiment of Clare, as apparently the Lieutenant-Colonel of that corps, who fell at Fontenoy, should not be confused with those of Ulster, but considered as most probably belonging to the less noted Munster O'Neills, of Tradry, in Clare; of whom the Creaghs also, who have been distinguished by military rank in France, Spain, and the united army of Great Britain and Ireland, are a branch. Of the Macgennises—compared with whom, as heads of the old heroic race of Ir in Ulster, the O'Neills and O'Donnells were both only modern intruders in that province—there were several officers in France, in the regiments of Galmoy, Dillon, Bulkeley, Lee, Rothe, etc.; some of whom attained the posts of Colonel and *Chef-de-Bataillon*, and three were Knights of St. Louis. The most remarkable of the name was Bernard Macgennis, Colonel of a regiment of French dragoons, killed at the battle of Spire in November 1703, and father of four sons slain in the same service.

The family of O'Brien of Thomond were as distinguished in the service of France as the O'Donnells were in that of Austria; for, besides the veteran Major-General Murrogh O'Brien and his descendants (or son and grandson), Earls of Lismore and Viscounts of Tallow by Stuart creation, and the successive noble representatives of the title of Clare, one of whom was a Marshal of France, there were in that service five officers named O'Brien who became *Knights of St. Louis.*<sup>t</sup>

Of the family of Kavanagh, two officers were Counts of the Holy Roman Empire and Generals in the Austrian service, and another is referred to with honor in the Polish-Saxon service. Of the O'Reillys, several were officers of the National Brigade in France, as well as in other regiments in its service, some of whom were Knights of St. Louis. In Spain, Alexander O'Reilly, born at Baltrasna in 1722 and deceased in 1794, rose to be a Count, Governor of New Orleans in Louisiana, Grand Commander of the Order of Calatrava, Governor of Madrid, and Captain-General of Andalusia,

Civil and Military Governor of Cadiz, Inspector-General of the Spanish Infantry, President of the Military School at Port St. Mary, Generalissimo of the Spanish Forces; one of his sons, Don Dominic O’Reilly, being a Lieutenant-General, and the other, Don Nicholas O’Reilly, a Brigadier General. In Austria, also, Andrew O’Reilly, of Ballinlough, born in 1742 and deceased in 1832, was a most illustrious officer, a Knight Commander of the Military Order of Maria Theresa, General of Cavalry, etc. Of these two highest representatives of their name, in Spain and Austria respectively, *both* were nobly connected in marriage. Of the O’Rourkes, various officers are referred to with honor in the armies of Spain, France, and Russia, in which great empire one has attained the rank of Prince. Of the O’Dwyers, one in the service of Austria was Governor of Belgrade, during the war, under the famous Prince Eugene of Savoy, against the Turks, early in the last century; others served in France, either in the Brigade, including some who were Knights of St. Louis, or with French corps, the name being found among the officers of the French army to our own times; and towards the close of the same century, or in the reign of the Empress Catherine II, there was an Admiral O’Dwyer in the Russian service. Of the Macguires, the noble representatives of the title of Baron of Enniskillen, were officers in France from the reign of Louis XIV to that of Louis XVI; and, during the same period, gentlemen of that old sept were to be found there in the national Brigade, or the regiments of Lee, Dorrington, Dillon, O’Donnell, FitzJames, Bulkeley, and Lally; the most eminent representative, however, of the name having been in the Austrian service, in the person of John Sigismund Macguire, Colonel of a regiment of Infantry of four battalions, a General of Artillery, and Lieutenant-General, Governor of Carinthia, Imperial Chamberlain, and Grand Cross Knight of the Military Order of Maria Theresa, and of the White Eagle of the King of Poland. He, too, was married to a lady of very high rank.

Of the O'Lallys, or O'Mullallys, of Tullachnadaly, near Tuam, Sir Gerard Lally—a Baronet by Stuart creation—died a Brigadier in France in 1737. His son, Thomas Arthur, Count Lally, who was so distinguished at Ettlingen, Dettingen, Fontenoy, Lafelt, Bergen-op-Zoom, Maestricht, *etc.*, was Colonel of an Irish Regiment of Infantry specifically raised for him, a Lieutenant-General, Grand Cross Knight of St. Louis, and Commander-in-Chief of the French forces in the East Indies; and his nephew, Michael Lally, died a Brigadier at Rouen in 1773. 

Of the O'Mahonies of Desmond, or South Munster, the chief officer under Louis XIV and Philip V in France and Spain was Daniel, most celebrated at Cremona, Almanza, Saragossa, and Villaviciosa. He was Colonel of an Irish Regiment of Dragoons, a Lieutenant-General, and a Count of Castile, *etc.* His elder son, James, was a Lieutenant-General in the service of Naples, and his younger son, Demetrius, was a Lieutenant-General and Count in Spain and Ambassador from that power to the Court of Vienna, where he died in 1770. The name of O'Mahony, in other branches, remained prominent in the French army until the present century, when one of its representatives—an officer of the old Brigade—was a Lieutenant-General and Commander of the Order of St. Louis, and another was a Major-General and Commandant of the Legion of Honor. 

Of the O'Farrells or O'Ferralls, there were, in the days of the Old Brigade, officers in the national regiments of FitzJames, Lally, Dillon, Berwick, and Walsh; and, since the restoration of the Bourbons, or from 1814 to 1846, others have been in the King’s Bodyguard (*Garde du Corps du Roi*), the Hohenlohe Legion, the Artillery, the Sappers, the Hussars, *etc.*, including a Colonel of the 7th Regiment of the Line, a Chevalier of the Legion of Honor and of the Order of Charles III of Spain.

O'Neny, more correctly MacNeny, of Tyrone, became a Count of the Holy Roman Empire, Councilor of State to Her Imperial Majesty Maria Theresa, and Chief President of the Privy Council at Brussels. To these may be added O'Sullivan, O'Callaghan, O'Naghten, O'Murphy, etc., to note whose various distinguished descendants abroad would occupy so much more space than is at our disposal here that we shall only observe: never were the old chieftain races of the north and south of Erin more nobly represented in military service upon the Continent than at present—in Spain, by O'Donnell, Duke of Tetuan, and in France, by MacMahon, Duke of Magenta.

The list of Milesian Irish officers who distinguished themselves in foreign service, if fully compiled, would be found to embrace members of all the great Irish families and most of the lesser ones. (in the edition of the *Macariae Excidium*, printed by the Irish Archaeological Society) has contributed largely to our information on the same topic. Count Charles Mac Donnell, private secretary to Marshal Nugent of Austria, is also collecting materials for a work on the history of the Irish officers in the service of Poland and Austria; so that, in a few years, we may expect a complete account of the illustrious deeds of the scattered Irish race, from the year 1600 down to the present day.

The respectability derived from the renown of the Irish officers abroad led some of their relatives at home to resume the O’ and Mac prefixes. Some have been prevented from doing so by the patents of their estates, such as Kelly of Castlekelly, in the county of Galway, and Dunne of Iregan, in the Queen’s County, who are ordered by distinct clauses to reject the O’, and not to take any form of name indicating clanship of any kind.

Other Irish families, however, who were not bound by patents of this kind, have resumed their ancient names. Thus, the late Owen O’Conor, M.P. for the county of Roscommon, assumed the title "Don" upon the extinction of the senior branch, although he was the sixth in descent from the last ancestor who had borne it. O’Grady of Kilballyowen has also prefixed the O’ and assumed the chieftainship of the O’Gradys, after that title, or mark of seniority, had been out of use for at least six generations. Morgan William O’Donovan, Esq., of Montpelier, in the county of Cork, has not only re-assumed the O’, which his ancestors had rejected for many generations, but has styled himself “the O’Donovan,” chief of his name, being the next of kin to the last acknowledged head of that family, the late General Richard O’Donovan of Bawnlahan, whose family became extinct in the year 1841. His example in resuming the O’ has been followed by Timothy O’Donovan, Esq., of O’Donovan’s Cove, in the county of Cork, head of a very ancient branch of the same family, and by William John O’Donnavan, a junior member of the Wexford Clan-Donovan.

There are other heads of Irish families who retain their Irish names in full with pride, such as Sir Richard O’Donnell of Newport, Bart.; General Sir Charles O’Donnell of Trughe, near Limerick,
