<page-num>[ 42 ]</page-num>
->Introduction.<-

### OF IRISH FAMILY NAMES ANGLICIZED AND ALTERED.

Among the less distinguished Irish families, however, the translation and anglicization of names have gone on to such a great degree that there is no doubt that in the course of half a century it will be difficult, if not impossible, to distinguish many families of Irish name and origin from those of the English race—unless, indeed, researchers are enabled to do so with the assistance of history, family documents, or physical characteristics. The principal cause of the change of these names was the ridicule thrown upon them by English magistrates and lawyers, who were ignorant of the Gaelic language. This made the Irish ashamed of all such names as were difficult for English speakers to pronounce, and they were thus led to change them by degrees, either by translating them into what they conceived to be their meanings in English, or by adapting them to local English surnames of respectable families, or by shortening them.

The families among the lower ranks who have translated, anglicized, or totally changed their ancient surnames are very numerous, and are daily becoming more so. Besides the cause already mentioned, two reasons may be assigned for this desire which prevails at present among the lower classes for the continued adoption of English surnames: first, the English language is becoming the one universally spoken among these classes, who now believe that many Irish surnames do not sound very pleasant in that tongue; secondly, the names translated or totally changed are, with very few exceptions, of no celebrity in Irish history, and when they do not sound well in English, the owners wish to change them to respectable English or Scottish names in order that they may obtain English or Scottish armorial bearings and cease to be considered as being of common Irish blood. As this change is going on rapidly in every part of Ireland, it appears desirable to give some notices here of the <term>Milesian</term> or <term>Scotic</term> names that have thus become transformed.

First, of names which have been translated correctly or incor-

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Irish family names being anglicized, providing specific examples of how these names were translated—often incorrectly—into English based on phonetic similarities or misinterpreted meanings.</meta>

<page-num>43</page-num>
<header>Irish Family Names altered to English.</header>

directly. In the county of Sligo, the ancient name of *O'Mulclohy* has been transformed into *Stone*, from an idea that *clohy*, the latter part of it, signifies a stone; but as this is an incorrect translation in this instance, these persons may be said to have taken a new name. In the county of Leitrim, the ancient and by no means obscure name of *MacConnava* has been rendered *Forde*, from an erroneous notion that *ava*, the last part of it, is a corruption of *atha*, meaning "of a ford." This is also an instance of false translation, for *MacConnava*, chief of *Munter Kenny* in the county of Leitrim, took his name from his ancestor *Cusnava*, who flourished in the tenth century. In Kerry and Thomond, the ancient name of *O'Cnavin* is now often anglicized as *Bowen*, because *Cnavin* signifies a *small bone*. In Tirconnell, the ancient name of *O'Mulmoghery* is now always rendered *Early*, because *moch-eirghe* signifies *early rising*. This version, however, is excusable, though not altogether correct. In Thomond, *O'Marcachain* is translated *Ryder* by some (*Marcach* signifying a horseman), but anglicized *Markham* by others; and in the same territory, *O'Lahiff* is made *Guthrie*, which is altogether incorrect. In Tyrone, the ancient name of *MacRory* is now invariably made *Rogers*, because *Roger* is assumed to be the English Christian name corresponding to the Irish *Ruadhri* or *Rory*. In Connemara, in the west of the county of Galway, the ancient name of *MacConry* is now always made *King*, because it is assumed that *ry*, the last syllable of it, is from *righ*, a king; a gross error, for this family, who are of Dalcassian origin, took their surname from their ancestor *Curoi*, a name which forms *Conroi* in the genitive case, and has nothing to do with *righ*, a king. The townland of *Ballymaconry*, situated near Streamstown in Connemara, has also been changed to *Kingston*. Sir Thomas King, one of this race, was the first who made this change. He settled in Dublin about a century ago, made a fortune, and was knighted; and his poor relatives at home adopted his name, thinking that they too might get rich if they rejected their old surname.

These examples, selected out of a long list of Irish surnames erroneously translated, are sufficient to show the false process by

by which the Irish are getting rid of their ancient surnames. A few specimens may next be presented of Irish surnames which have been assimilated to English or Scotch ones, based on a fancied resemblance in the sounds of both.

In Ulster, MacMahon—the name of the chiefs of Oriel, which, as we have already seen, the poet Spenser attempted to prove was an Irish form of Fitzursula—is now very frequently anglicized as Matthews. MacCawell, the name of the ancient chiefs of Kinel Ferady, is anglicized as Camphill, Campbell, Howell, and even Caulfield.<sup>w</sup> In Thomond, the name O'Hiomhair is anglicized to Howard among the peasantry, and to Ivers among the gentry. In the same county, the ancient Irish name of O'Beirne is metamorphosed into Byron; while in the original locality of the name, in Tir-Briuin na Sinna in the east of County Roscommon, it is anglicized as Bruin among the peasantry. However, among the gentry who know the historical respectability of the name, the original form O'Beirne is retained. In the province of Connaught, a family named O'Heraghty has anglicized their old Gaelic name to Harrington. In the city of Limerick, the ancient name of O'Shaughnessy is metamorphosed into Sandys, perhaps to disguise the Irish origin of the family; but it is retained by the more respectable branches of the family, such as by Sir William O'Shaughnessy of Calcutta. In the county of Londonderry, the old name O'Brollaghan is made to look English in Ireland and America by being transmuted to Bradley, while in Scotland it is changed to Brodie. In the county of Fermanagh, the O'Creighans have changed their name to Creighton, for no other apparent reason than because it is the family name of the Earl of Erne. In the county of Leitrim, O'Fergus—the descendant of the ancient <term>Erenachs</term> of Rossinver—has lately changed his name to Ferguson. Throughout the province of Ulster generally, very extraordinary changes have been made in the names of the original inhabitants: such as MacTeige to Montague;

Various similar instances might be given. It could indeed be shown that in the neighborhood of the principal Irish towns the farmers and cottiers have two names—a country name and a town name. Thus in the vicinity of Cork, O'Leyne of the country becomes Lyons in the city; O'Houlahan of the country is made Holland in the city. In the neighborhood of Enniskillen, MacGilfinnen of the country becomes Leonard in the town. In the neighborhood of Sligo, O'Sumaghan of the country becomes Somers in the town, etc.; but the number of these changes shown here is sufficient to demonstrate the manner in which the lower Irish are assimilating their names with those of the English.

MacNebo changed to Victory; and to Victoria by emigrants to America.

# Irish Family Names altered to English.

O’Clery, to Clarke, and Clerkin.
O’Cindellan, to Cunningham.
O’Drum, to Drummond (Drum, in Fermanagh).
Tackney, to Tackit and Sexton.
Murtagh, in America, to Mortimer.

Examples have now been given of the process occurring throughout the various provinces of Ireland among the general population, where people are changing their original names into names that appear English or Scottish; there are also some among the higher classes in Ireland who have altered their old Milesian names in such a manner as to give them a French or Spanish appearance. These, it is true, are few in number, but some of them hold a respectable rank. We shall therefore show a few instances of the method supposed to make Irish names respectable by giving them a foreign aspect. The most remarkable of these changes was made by the family of O’Dorcy in the west of County Galway, who have assumed not only the name of D’Arcy, but also the coat of arms of the Anglo-Norman D’Arcys of Meath. It is well known, however, that the D’Arcys of Galway are all descended from James Reagh Darcy, a merchant from Galway, whose pedigree is traced by Duald MacFirbis—not to the D’Arcys of Meath, who are unquestionably of Anglo-Norman origin, but to the Milesian O’Dorcys (*Ua Dorchaidhe*, now called *Darkey*) of West Connaught. They were the ancient chiefs of Partry, a well-known territory extending westward from the lakes of Lough Mask and Lough Carra toward Croagh Patrick.

Another instance is found in Thomond, where a gentleman of the O’Mulronies, following the common corruption of that name, has transformed it into Moroni, by which he pretends to be of Spanish descent; but his neighbors persist in calling him *O’Murruana* when they speak the native language. In that part of Ireland, where the Irish language is very correctly pronounced in most other instances, when the prefix *maol* is followed by *r*, the *l* itself is pronounced as *r*, as in the instance under consideration.

The next instance deserving notice is in the province of Connaught, where the O’Mulaville family have all changed their name to Lavelle, and where those who know nothing of the history of that family are beginning to think that they are of French descent. However, it is a constant, though false, tradition in the County of Mayo that they are of Danish origin, and that they have been located in Iarowle since the ninth century. The late editor of the Dublin *Freeman’s Journal*—a man of great ability and extensive learning who possessed a good knowledge of the ancient Irish language—was of this name. In the province of Ulster, the name O’Mulaville is Scotticized as MacPaul.

Another name which some people suppose to be French or Anglo-Norman is Delany, as if it were De Lani; but the Irish origin of this family cannot be questioned, for the name is called O’Dubhlaine, or O’Dulany, in the Gaelic language, and they were originally located at the foot of Sliabh Bladhma, in Upper Ossory. Another instance is found in the change of O’Dowling to Du Laing; but this is seldom done, and never by anyone except people of no consequence.

Some individuals with the name Magunshinan or Magilsinan, upon leaving their original locations in Cavan and Meath, have assumed the name Nugent, and others the name Gilson. Charles Gilson, the founder and benefactor of the public school of Oldcastle, belonged to this family; upon his removal to London, he shortened his name to Gilson.

Other persons of Irish name and origin, upon settling in London and other parts of England, have changed their surnames altogether. This last change entirely disguises the origin of the family, which was moved from Munster to Connacht by Oliver Cromwell under the name of O'Houlahan. The true Nolans of Ireland are of Leinster origin and were the ancient chiefs of the barony of Forth in what is now County Carlow (formerly called Fotharta Fea), where they are still numerous. However, those called Nolans in Connacht are actually O'Houlahans, a family who held the rank of chieftains in ancient times. It happens that, either through ignorance of their history or a dislike for the sound of the name, they have assumed the name of a Leinster family, which seems to them to be somewhat more acceptable to modern ears. In Munster, however, O'Houlahan is beginning to be Anglicized as Holland. In the province of Ulster, the name MacGillaroe has been shortened to Gilroy and Kilroy; MacBrady to Brady; O'Kelaghan to Callaghan; MacGilla Brighde to MacBride; MacGillacuskly to Cuskly, Cosgrove, and Costello; MacGillafinen to Linden and Leonard; MacGennis to Ennis and Guinness; and MacBlosky to Closky. The most distinguished of these was MacMurrough, but there is not an individual of that name now known in Leinster, as all the families of that race have, without exception, adopted the name Kavanagh.

The name now generally anglicized as Murphy is not MacMurrough, but O’Murchoe, which was the name of a branch of the royal family of Leinster. They became chiefs of the territory of South Hy-Felimy, now called the Murroos, or the barony of Ballaghkeen, in the east of the county of Wexford; their chief seat was at Castle Ellis in that barony. All the families of the name Murphy now in Ireland are called in Irish *O’Murchadha*, pronounced O-Murraghoo, and it is believed that they are originally from Leinster. Regarding the difference between these two families of MacMurrough and Murphy, Roderic O’Flaherty has the following observation in his critique of Peter Walsh:

> “Cognominibus Hibernicis, quæ semper sunt unius è majoribus propria nomina O vel Mac præponitur Cognominatos illius, ex quo cognomen, natos, nepotes, vel posteros significans ; nec licet unum pro alio promiscuè usurpari, quemadmodum ille O’Morphæum regem Lageniæ pro MacMorphæum (seu potius MacMurchadh), scribit : ab hac enim diversa est et longe inferior O’Murchadh (quam Anglicè *Morphy* dicunt), familia.” — *Ogygia seu Rerum Hibernicarum Chronologia*, 1685, page 210.

> “An O or a Mac is prefixed to Irish surnames, which are always the proper names of one of their ancestors, indicating that they were of the same name—the sons, grandsons, or descendants of the person whose name they adopted; but it was not proper to use the one interchangeably in the place of the other” <note>that is, O for Mac or vice versa</note>, as he <note>namely, Peter Walsh</note> writes

There are, however, a few instances to be found in which "O" has been changed to "Mac," and vice versa, as in the case of O’Melaghlin, chief of the southern Hy-Niall race, to MacLoughlin; and in the following instances: O’Dubhdierma to MacDermot; O’Donoghy to MacDonough; O’Cnavin to MacNevin; O’Heraghty to MacGeraghty; and some few others.

These latter changes were not intended to disguise the Irish origin of the families who made them, but they tend to confuse the tribe and locality of the respective families.

Similar changes have been made in family names among the Welsh: such as Ap-John into Jones; Ap-Richard into Pritchard and Richards; Ap-Owen into Owens; Ap-Robert into Probert and Roberts; Ap-Gwillim into Williams; Ap-Rody into Brody; Ap-Hugh into Pughe and Pew, and more recently into Hughes, etc.

### OF ANCIENT IRISH CHRISTIAN OR BAPTISMAL NAMES OF MEN, AND THEIR MODERNIZED FORMS.

Having thus addressed the alterations the Irish have made to their surnames, or family names, for the purpose of giving them an English appearance, the changes which they have likewise made to their Christian or baptismal names, with the same intention, may next be considered. Many of their original names they have altogether rejected, as they were not easily reducible to any modern English forms; but others have been retained, though altered in such a manner as to make them appear English. From authentic Irish annals and genealogical books, an extensive list could be compiled of proper names for men in use during the reign of Queen Elizabeth which have long been laid aside, but the limits of this work do not allow room for such a catalog. It must, therefore, suffice to point out the original forms of such names as have been retained in an anglicized shape. These changes in

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the modernization of Irish names, moving from surnames to a specific critique of how Irish Christian names have been erroneously equated with English ones.</meta>

<page-num>[ 52 ]</page-num>
<header>Introduction.</header>

The Christian names have been changed by the families who have adopted English surnames, as well as by those who have retained the *Milesian O and Mac*; but these families have assumed that the English forms they have given to this class of names are perfectly correct. This was considered to be true as early as the year 1689, when Sir Richard Cox wrote on the subject as follows in the introductory discourse to his *History of Ireland*:—

> “The Christian names of the Irish are as in England: Hugh; Mahoone, i.e., Matthew; Teige, i.e., Timothy; Dermond, i.e., Jeremy; Cnoghor, i.e., Cornelius; Cormuck, i.e., Charles; Art, i.e., Arthur; Donal, i.e., Daniel; Goron, i.e., Geoffrey; Magheesh, i.e., Moses.”

Now, these names are by no means identical, though at present they are universally accepted and used as such. In the first place, the name *Aedh*, which has been transformed into Hugh, is not synonymous with it; for the word *Aedh* means *fire*, but Hugh, which has been borrowed from the Saxon, signifies *high* or *lofty*. Since, then, they do not carry the same meaning and are not composed of the same letters, it is quite obvious that they have nothing in common with each other. In the second place, *Mahon* (*Mathghamhain*)—or as Sir Richard Cox writes it, Mahoone—is not *Matthew*; for according to Spenser and others, *Mahon* signifies a *bear*, and therefore cannot be identical, synonymous, or related to the Scriptural name *Matthew*, which signifies a *gift* or *present*. In the third instance, the Irish name *Teige* (*Tadhg*), which according to all the Irish glossaries signifies a *poet*, is not synonymous with Timothy, which means "God-honoring." *Teige* was first anglicized as Thady, and the editor is acquainted with individuals who have rendered it as Thaddaeus, Theophilus, and Theodosius.

In the fourth instance, *Dermot*—or as Sir Richard Cox wrote it, *Dermond*—is not identical or even related to *Jeremy*. Regarding this name, which was at first very incorrectly anglicized as *Darby*, the learned Dr. O’Brien wrote as follows:—

> “*Diarmaid*, the proper name of several great princes of the old Irish. This name is a compound of *Dia*, God, and *armaid*, the genitive plural of [unclear]”

<summary>The author critiques the historical practice of incorrectly equating Irish Christian names with English ones, providing etymological evidence that names like Aedh and Mahon are not equivalent to Hugh and Matthew.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the critique of Sir Richard Cox’s list of Irish names, specifically addressing the names Dermod, Conchobhar, and Cormac, and correcting false etymologies provided by earlier scholars.</meta>

the Irish word *arm*, Latin, *arma, armorum*: so that *Dia-armaid* literally signifies the same as *Deus-armorum*, the god of arms. Such is the exalted origin of this Irish name, which does not shield it from being, at times, a subject of ridicule to some of our 'pretty gentlemen' of the modern English taste.”—*Focalóir Gaoidhilge Sax-Bhéarla, or an Irish-English Dictionary.* Paris: 1768, page 179.

It must, however, be acknowledged that this is not the meaning of the name Dermod, and that Dr. O’Brien was incorrect in this explanation. He believed it gave respectability to a name common in his own ancient family, which was regarded as vulgar by those in power in Ireland during the period in which he wrote. We have the authority of the Irish glossaries to show that *Diarmaid*, which was adopted at a remote period of Irish history as the proper name of a man, signifies a *freeman*.

In the fifth instance, Conchobhar, or, as Sir Richard Cox writes it, Cnogher, is not identical, synonymous, or even related to Cornelius; for though it has been customary for some families to Latinize it as Cornelius, we know from the roots of both names that they bear not the slightest analogy to each other. The Irish name is compounded of *conn*, strength, and *cobhair*, aid or assistance; while the Latin Cornelius is differently derived. It is, then, evident that there is no reason for changing the Irish Conchobhar, or Conor, to Cornelius, except for a fancied and very remote resemblance between the sounds of both.

In the sixth instance, the name Cormac has no analogy to Charles (which means noble-spirited), for it is explained by all our glossographers as signifying “Son of the chariot.” It is added “that it was first given as a nickname in the first century to a Lagenian <note>Leinster</note> prince who happened to be born in a chariot while his mother was going on a journey, but that it later became honorable as the name of many great personages in Ireland.” After the accession of Charles I to the throne, however, many Irish families of distinction changed the name Cormac to Charles, thinking the latter more dignified as the name of the reigning

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the origins and meanings of Irish names, specifically addressing the names Art, Domhnall, and others, while refuting the connections made by Sir Richard Cox between Irish and Scriptural or Anglo-Norman names.</meta>

<page-num>[ 54 ]</page-num>
<header>Introduction.</header>

monarch—a practice which has since been very generally followed in Ireland.

In the seventh instance, Sir Richard is probably correct, as *Art* may be synonymous with Arthur; indeed, they both appear to be words of the same original family of language, for the Irish word *Art* signifies *noble*; and if we can rely on the British etymologists, Arthur bears a very similar meaning in *Cymraig*, or *Old British*.

With respect to the eighth instance, given by Sir Richard Cox, it appears certain that the Irish proper name Domhnall, which was originally anglicized as Donnell and Donald, is not the same as the Scriptural name Daniel, which means *God is my judge*. The ancient Irish glossographers never viewed it as such, for they always wrote it *Domhnall*, and understood it to mean a great or proud chieftain. This explanation may, however, be possibly incorrect; but the *m* in the first syllable shows that the name is formed from a root very different from the one from which the Scriptural name Daniel is derived.

As to the names Goron (which is but a mistake for Searon), Jeoffry, and Magheesh or Moses—the last two instances mentioned by Sir Richard Cox—they were never in use among the old Irish, but were borrowed from the Anglo-Normans, and therefore do not require notice in this place. The foregoing remarks sufficiently show that the Christian names borne by the ancient Irish are not identical, synonymous, or even cognate with those substituted for them in the time of Sir Richard Cox. The baptismal, or Christian names of the ancient Irish were variously formed, but were chiefly composed of adjectives denoting colors or qualities of the mind or body; they also consisted of names of animals, with various adjectives prefixed or suffixed. Thus, we have Aedh, now Hugh, denoting fire; Art (now Arthur), which means noble stone, or rock; Brian, from *bri*, strength; Becan, from *beg*, little; Beoan, from *beo*, lively. We have also Bran, a raven, and its diminutive Branán; Brocán, from *broc*, a badger; Buadhach, from *buadh*, victory; Caemhán and Caeimhghin, from *caemh*, comely, or handsome; and Blathmac, a blooming son, from *blath*, a blossom.

It explains that native Irish names were typically derived from adjectives describing personal traits, colors, or animals.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the formation of Irish proper names, transitioning from the qualities of noble character to the use of adjectives, suffixes, and religious prefixes in naming conventions.</meta>

<page-num>55</page-num>
<header>Modernized Ancient Irish Christian Names of Men. [ 55 ]</header>

Names formed from adjectives denoting colors are very numerous, such as Banán, from *bán* (white); Corcran, from *corcair* (ruddy); Ciarán and Ceirin, from *ciar* (black); Cronán and Croinin, from *cron* (dark); Donnán, from *donn* (dun); Deargan, from *dearg* (red); Dubhan, from *dubh* (black); Fionnan and Fionnagan, from *fionn* (fair); Gormán and Gormóg, from *gorm* (blue); Glasan and Glaisin, from *glas* (green); Liathan, from *liath* (gray); Lachtnan, from *lachtna* (green); Odhran and Uidhrin, from *odhar* (pale); Riabhán, from *riabhach* (grayish); Ruadhan, from *ruadh* (red); and Uaithnin, from *uaithne* (green).

Irish proper names of men were also formed by adding the suffix *gal* (valor) and *gus* (virtue), such as Ferghal, Donnghal, Tuathghal, Donnghus, Cuangus, and Aenghus.

Names of men were likewise created by prefixing *gilla* (youth or servant) to the name of God or of some saint, such as Gilla-Dé (servant of God); Gilla-Chomghaill (servant of St. Congall); Gilla-Choluim (servant of St. Columba); Gilla-Brighde (servant of St. Brigid); and so on. Some are of the opinion that the ancient Irish borrowed this word *gilla* from the Scandinavians, who added it as a suffix to the names of their gods to form names of men, as in *Thorghils*, and that Irish history does not show any name beginning with *gilla* before the invasion of the Northmen in 792. Be this as it may, very little doubt can exist that the Irish had, in early times, the word *gilla* for a youth, servant, boy, or lackey; and the name *Gilla*, or Gildas, on its own, is certainly more ancient than the Danish invasions.

The word *mael* (bald, shorn, or tonsured) is also prefixed to the names of saints to form proper names of men, such as *Mael-Patraic* (that is, Patrick’s servant, or one tonsured in his name); *Mael-Eoin* (servant of John); and *Mael-Suthain* (“*calvus perennis*”). When this word *mael* is followed by an adjective, it is synonymous with *mal* and signifies a chief or king, as in *Maeldearg* (the red or ruddy chief) and *Maeldubh* (the black chief).

The word *ceile* (companion or vassal) is also sometimes, though rarely, prefixed, as in *Ceile-Petair* (the vassal of Peter). In Scotland

<page-num>56</page-num>
<header>Introduction.</header>

...we find *cara*, friend, similarly prefixed, as in *Cara Michil*, the friend of St. Michael.

Some proper names of men were formed by prefixing the word *cu*, a hound or dog, to the name of a *place*, or some substantive, such as *Cu-Uladh*, hound of Ulster (or *Canis Ultoniæ* as it is sometimes rendered in the Annals of Ulster); *Cu-Mumhan*, hound of Mumhain, or Munster; *Cu-Chonnacht*, hound of Connaught; *Cu-Chaisil*, hound of Cashel; *Cu-Bladhma*, hound of Sliabh Bladhma; *Cu-Cuailgne*, hound of Cuailgne; *Cu-Sionna*, hound of the Shannon; *Cu-mhaighe*, hound of the plain; *Cu-sleibhe*, hound of the mountain; and *Cu-gan-mathair*, hound without a mother.

Other names are formed by prefixing *dubh*, black, to the names of places, such as *Dubhdothra*, the black man of the <note>river</note> Dodder; *Dubh-da-inbher*, the black man of the two rivers; *Dubh-da-thuath*, the black man of the two territories, etc.

At the present day, very few of the original Irish names remain without being translated into or assimilated with those borne by the English. Thus, while among the O'Conors of Connaught, Cathal and Calbhach were changed into Charles (with which, it will be readily granted, they have nothing in common, either in meaning or sound); among the O'Conors of Offaly in Leinster, Cathir, which signifies *warrior*, was also similarly metamorphosed. At the same time, the name of Charles was substituted by the Mac Carthys of Desmond for their Cormac, and by the O'Hagans and other northern families sometimes for their Turlogh, which, however, is more usually made Terence.

In the families of Mac Carthy, O'Sullivan, and O'Driscoll, Finghin <note>Fineen</note>, a name very general among them and which signifies "the fair offspring," has been anglicized to Florence. The famous Finghin Mac Carthy, who was imprisoned in the Tower of London for thirty-six years, was the first who translated this name into Florence, and some of his enemies tried to make it appear that he had a sinister motive in thus anglicizing the Irish name. Among the same southern families, the name Saerbrethach, which prevails among the Mac Carthys in par-

In the family of O’Donovan, as the writer has had every opportunity to know, the name Murrogh has been changed to Morgan; Dermod to Jeremiah; Teige to Timothy; Conchobhar, or Conor, to Cornelius; Donogh to Denis; and Donnell to Daniel. In the family of O’Brien, the hereditary name of Turlogh has been changed to Terence; Mahon to Matthew; Murtogh, or Moriertagh, to Mortimer (but this only very recently); and Lachtna and Laoiseach to Lucius. Among the O’Gradys, the name Aneslis is rendered Stanislaus and Standish. In the families of O’Donnell, O’Kane, and others in the province of Ulster, Manus—a name borrowed by those families from the Danes—is now often rendered Manasses. In the families of Mac Mahon and Mac Kenna in Ulster, the name Ardghal, or Ardal, signifying “of high prowess or valor,” is always anglicized Arnold. In the family of O’Madden of *Sil Anmchadha*, in the southeast of the county of Galway, the hereditary name of Anmcha, which is translated Animosus by Colgan, is now always rendered Ambrose, to which it does not bear the slightest analogy. Among the families of O’Doyle, Kavanagh, and others in the province of Leinster, the name Maidoc, or Mogue, which they adopted from St. Maidoc, or Aidan, the patron saint of the diocese of Ferns, is now always rendered Moses among the Roman Catholics and Aidan among the Protestants. Among the O’Neills in the province of Ulster, the name Feidhlim, Felim, or Felimy, explained as meaning the *ever good*, is now made Felix; Con, signifying *strength*, is made Constantine; and Ferdoragh, meaning *dark-visaged man*, is rendered Frederic or Ferdinand. Among the O’Conors of Connacht, the name Ruaidhri, or Rory, is now anglicized Roderic; but the O’Shaughnessys and most other families render it Roger. In the O’Conor family, Tomaltach is rendered Thomas; Aedh, Hugh; and Eoghan, Owen. In the families of MacDonnell and others in Scotland and in the north of Ireland, the name Aenghus, or Angus, is always rendered Æneas, and Feradhach, Frederic. # Introduction.

The name Berach, which they have adopted from their patron saint, and which is translated by Colgan as *directe ad scopum collimans* <note>aiming directly at the mark</note>, is now always, and correctly enough, rendered Barry. Throughout Ireland, the old name of Brian is now rendered Bernard and popularized as Barney; the latter is more properly an abbreviation of Barnaby. Among the O’Haras and O’Garas in County Sligo, the name Cian—which they have adopted from their great ancestor, Cian, the son of Olioll Olum, King of Munster—has long been rendered Kean, and sometimes, though rarely, changed to King. In the Maguire family, Cuconnacht is rendered Constantine, while in other families, Cosnava undergoes exactly the same change. In the O’Kane family, the name written *Cu-mhaighe* in the original language (pronounced Cooey) and signifying "dog or hound of the plain," is now rendered Quintin. In the O’Dowda family, the ancient name of Dathi—which they have adopted from their great pagan ancestor, a king of Ireland—is now rendered David, a name with which it is supposed to be synonymous. 

In the north and west of Ireland, the names *Dubhaltach*, *Dubhdalethe*, and *Dubhdara* are all Anglicized as Dudley. In the Mac Sweeny family, the very ancient name of Heremon is Anglicized as Irwin, but it is now nearly obsolete as a first name. In the families of O’Hanlon, O’Haran, and O’Heany in the province of Ulster, the name *Eochaidh*—signifying "horseman," and which was Latinized as Eochodius, Achaius, Euthichius, and Equitius—is Anglicized as Auhy and Atty. However, this is also almost obsolete as a first name, the editor never having met more than one person who bore it in all his travels through Ireland. Among the O’Mulconrys, now Conrys, the names Flann, Fithil, and Flaithri have been Anglicized as Florence. In the O’Daly family, the name Baothghalach, which was formerly Latinized as Boethius, is now always rendered Bowes; and in that of O’Clery, the name Lughaidh is Anglicized as Lewy and Lewis. Among the O’Reillys of Cavan, the hereditary name of Maelmordha, which signifies "majestic chief," is now invariably rendered Myles; and among the O’Kellys of Hy-Many, the name Fachtna is rendered Festus. In every part of Ireland, Mael-seachlainn, or

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the modernization and Anglicization of Irish names, moving from male names like Melaghlin and Gilla-Patraic to the history of female names found in the Ban-Seanchus.</meta>

<page-num>59</page-num>
<header>Ancient Irish Female Names and their Changes.</header>

Melaghlin, which means "servant of St. Sechnall" or "Secundinus," has been changed to Malachy, to which it bears no relation at all, except for some distant resemblance in sound. The name Gilla-Patraic has been universally changed to Patrick; and it is interesting to observe that as common as the name Patrick has now become in Ireland—especially among the lower classes—it was never used among the ancient Irish. They used to call their children by the name of the Irish Apostle by styling them his servants, believing it to be more respectful and auspicious; and therefore we find the ancient Irish calling their children not Patrick, but Mael-Patraic or Gilla-Patraic. They Latinized these names as Patricianus, not Patricius. The name Patrick (Patricius) was one of the most honorable names in all antiquity, as the reader will see in the work on the British Churches by Archbishop Ussher, pages 841 and 1046 (4to, Dublin, 1639).

### OF ANCIENT IRISH FEMALE NAMES AND THEIR CHANGES.

Irish names for women have also been significantly transformed, and many of the most unique ones have been entirely rejected. The editor possesses a list of women's names compiled from authentic Irish annals and from the *Ban-Seanchus*, or "History of Remarkable Women"—an interesting tract in the Book of Lecan, folio 193. However, since the limits of this Introduction do not allow for the inclusion of this entire catalog, it will suffice to provide the names still in use, along with a selection of the most interesting rejected ones, including their meanings where they are certain. The following are the ancient Irish names for women that are still retained, as determined by the editor after examining the provinces of Ulster, Connacht, Leinster, and most of Munster.

1. *Aine*, now Hannah.
2. *Brighid*, now Anglicized as Bridget, due to its resemblance to the name of the celebrated Swedish saint of that name. Brighid is a woman’s name of pagan origin in Ireland; it has been explained as "fiery dart" by Irish glossographers, particularly by Cormac, king

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the transformation of ancient Irish names, specifically focusing on the origins and Anglicization of female names such as Brighid and the transition of others like Sorcha into modern equivalents like Sarah.</meta>

[ 60 ]
<header>Introduction.</header>

and bishop of Cashel, who distinctly states in his Glossary that it was the name of the muse who was believed to preside over poetry in pagan times in Ireland. *Brighid* is now very common in Ireland as a woman's name because it was the name of the most celebrated of the female saints of Ireland, the patroness of Kildare, and anciently of all Ireland; she was well known throughout Europe as the most illustrious of the female saints of the West.

3. *Finola* (*Finnghuala*, "of the fair shoulders") has become nearly obsolete since the beginning of the eighteenth century, but some few people still retain it in the abbreviated form of Nuala.

4. *Graine*, now Grace.

5. *Lasairfhina*, or Lassarina, was also in use not long ago but has recently become obsolete.

6. *Meadhbh*, pronounced Meave. This is still preserved and anglicized as Maud, Mab, and Mabby; the editor is acquainted with several old women of the Milesian race who still retain it. Meadhbh was the name of a celebrated queen of Connacht who flourished in the first century and who is now known in the legends of the mountainous districts of Ireland as the queen of the fairies.

7. *Mor*, pronounced More, and anglicized as Martha. The editor believes that there are very few women with this name now living in Ireland, though it was the name of many ladies in the reign of Queen Elizabeth and for a century later. In our own times, however, it has been almost invariably anglicized as Mary, with which it is neither synonymous nor related.

8. *Sadhbh*, pronounced Soyv, is still the name of several women of the native Irish race. It is now almost invariably anglicized as Sally, to which it bears no analogy.

9. *Sorcha* is still the name of several women in Ireland, especially in the province of Ulster; but the rising generation are beginning to change it to Sarah or Sally. The editor is acquainted with families in which this name is hereditary, and among whom the mother is always called Sorcha and the daughter Sally. The

10. *Una.* This name is still in constant use among the women of Ireland, but when speaking English they invariably anglicize it to Winifred or Winny.

The editor is not aware of any other names used in ancient Irish times that are still retained.

The names Catherine, Eleanor, Isabella, Mary, Honora, Judith, Joane, Sighile, Sheela (Celia), and many others now in use and supposed to be of Irish origin, do not occur in the “Account of Remarkable Women” referred to above, and there is no reason to believe that they were ever used in ancient Ireland.

# CONCLUDING OBSERVATIONS.

The reader has now seen the process by which so many of the Irish people have assimilated their names and surnames to those of the English. Some of the families who have thus anglicized their names wish to conceal their Irish origin; and one result of these changes must soon be that statistical writers will be apt to infer, from the small number of ancient Irish surnames retained in Ireland, that all the old Irish race were supplanted by the English.

These notices of the surnames of the Irish people may be closed by the remark that no ancient Irish surname is perfect unless it has O or Mac prefixed, except in those instances where the nickname or cognomen of the ancestor is used as the surname, such as Kavanagh, etc. Accordingly, nine-tenths of the surnames at present borne by the Irish people are incorrect, as they are mere mutilations of their original forms.

->“Per Mac atque O, tu veros cognoscis Hibernos :
His duobus demptis, nullus Hibernus adest :”<-

->“By Mac and O
You’ll always know
True Irishmen, they say ;
But if they lack
Both O and Mac,
No Irishmen are they.”<-

The truth of these well-known lines may now be questioned, though it was undeniable a few centuries ago.

Spenser <note>Referring to the poet Edmund Spenser, who wrote on the state of Ireland in the 16th century.</note>, while he advised that the Irish should be compelled by England to reject their Os and Macs and to adopt English surnames, dissuaded his own countrymen from adopting Irish names, as some of them had done, in the following words, which an Irish writer might now adopt as his own:—

“Is it possible that any should so far grow out of frame, that they should in so short a space quite forget their country and their own names:"

<summary>This page discusses the anglicization of Irish surnames and emphasizes that the prefixes "O" and "Mac" are the traditional markers of authentic Irish names. It notes that the loss of these prefixes makes modern Irish surnames technically "incorrect" and mentions Edmund Spenser's historical role in encouraging the adoption of English names.</summary>

<keywords>Irish surnames, anglicization, O, Mac, ethnic identity, Edmund Spenser, Kavanagh, Irish genealogy, surname prefixes</keywords>

# Introduction.

> “That is a most dangerous lethargy, much worse than that of Messala Corvinus, who, being a most learned man, through sickness forgot his own name.” — *View of the State of Ireland. Dublin: 1633, page 45.*

And again: —

> “Could they ever conceive any such dislike of their own natural countries, as that they would be ashamed of their name, and bite at the dug from which they sucked life.” — *Ibid, page 46.*

The Society is indebted to the Council of the Royal Irish Academy for having, with its accustomed liberality, given the editor access to the Academy’s valuable collection of Irish Manuscripts, for the purpose of transcribing and collating the texts of the following poems.

The editor desires here to express his obligations to the Rev. J. H. Todd, D.D., and J. T. Gilbert, Esq., the Honorary Secretaries of the Society, for the assistance rendered him in passing this volume through the press. To the Rev. William Reeves, D.D., Secretary of the Royal Irish Academy, he is also indebted for his careful revision of the work in its progress, and for many valuable suggestions, which, together with the copious index which he has contributed, eminently entitle him to the thanks of the Society.

J. O’D.

DUBLIN, *1st of December, 1861.*

# O’DUBHAGAIN.

Muintir Siorthachain; and Mag Amalgadha, lord of Callraighe; Mag Carrghamhna over Muintir Maoiltrionda; O’Daly, lord of Corca Adam; and O’Muireadhaigh over Cenel Tlamain; O’Scolaidhe over West Delvin; O’Comraidhe, lord of Ua Mac Uais; O’Hea over Tir Teathra; O’Carroll and O’Dunne over Tara; and Mac Giollaseachlainn over South Breagh; and O’Ronan over Cairbre Ghabhra; O’Hennessy over Little Gaileanga.

That is the portion of Meath, except for two lords of Fingal, namely Mac Giolla Mocholmog and O’Dunchadha, and three territories, namely, Tuilen; O’Muircheartaigh, lord of O’Maine; and O’Modhairn over Cenel nEoghain and over the Britons.

It was concerning these that O’Dubhagain spoke—namely Seaan Mór, a learned historian and chief poet of O’Maine; the year of Christ when he died was 1372.

->Let us travel around Ireland,<-
->Men set out at the summons,<-
->On the roads where we are,<-
->Let us visit the provinces.<-

---

->Let us go first to Tara,<-
->To the green of the smooth-formed court,<-
->A constant search that does not fail <insert>[by the ground]</insert>,<-
->Let everyone come to meet us.<-

---

->The nobles of Ireland move<-
->To Tara of the lordly bonds,—<-
->Your journey is not before the division,<-
->No man shall be without a country.<-

---

->The noble host says there:<-
->Name our nobles for us on one side,<-
->A prosperous host of every line that has not decayed,<-
->The prince of every land let us enumerate.<-

Amhalghadha, lord of Callraighe; MagCarrghamhna, over Muinter-Maoiltsionna; O'Daly, lord of Corca Adam; and O'Muireadhaigh, over Cenel Tlamain; O'Scolaidhe, over West Delvin; O'Comhraidhe, lord of Ui MacUais; O’Hea, over Tir Teathra; O'Carroll and O'Dunne, over Tara; and MacGiolla-sechlainn, over South Breagh; and O'Ronan, over Cairbre Gabhra; O’Hennessy, over Little Gaileanga.

Is king of the very fine Corca-Raeidhe.<note>Now the barony of Corkaree in Westmeath.</note>

Host of the girdles, handsome is their complexion,
Over the manly Cinel-Fiachach.<note>A territory in Westmeath belonging to the Southern Ui Neill.</note>

# O’DUBHAGAIN.

Mag Rourke is over the tribe of Enda,<note>Aicme Enda, a territory in ancient Westmeath.</note>
Who never gave a refusal to a poetic company;
O’Carberry is over the sweet and victorious Tuath,<note>Tuath-buadha, a district in Teffia.</note>
There are hosts over the territory, as I record.

O’Haughey is over the great household,
Over the noble Kinel-Aengusa;<note>Cenel n-Aenghasa.</note>
Over Dealbhna Beg,<note>Now the barony of Fore in Westmeath.</note> brave is his clan,
Is O’Maol-caomchadla of Callann.

Let us approach the lords of Teffia,<note>Teathbha, a large territory comprising parts of modern Westmeath and Longford.</note>
It is not right for us to pass over their royal history;
The Dondairbhe of the horses of the glens,
The protection and hospitality of Ireland.

The high king of Teffia, the plunderer,
Is O’Kearney<note>O’Catharnaigh, also known as An Sionnach, or "The Fox."</note> of the plundering weapons;
A branch who found for us success,
With Mac Quinn and O’Confiacla.

O’Loughnan, who causes no small slaughter,
And O’Murigan of the great pride;
They well deserved the queens,
Those are the sub-kings of the region.

The good kings of Comar of the drinking-horns,
Is O’Flanagan, the princely plunderer;
They will all be by my side,
And sweet O’Breen over Brawney.<note>Breaghmhuine, now the barony of Brawney in Westmeath.</note>

MacConmeadha of the swine-pens,
Is over the fierce Muintir-Laodhuicain;
Mag Aedha, who is called beloved,
Is over the fruitful Muintir-Tlamain.<note>A tribe located in the barony of Magheradernon, Westmeath.</note>

# O'DUBHAGAIN.

Mag Rourke is over the tribe of Enda,<note>The sept of Enda was a territory in ancient Westmeath.</note>
Who never gave a poetic company a flat refusal;
O’Carberry is over sweet Tuath Buadha,<note>Tuath-buadha, a district in Teffia.</note>
With armies over the district, as I reckon.

O’Haughey of the great showers,
Is over high Kinel-Aengusa;<note>Cenel n-Aenghusa.</note>
Over Dealbhna Beg,<note>Now the barony of Fore in Westmeath.</note> brave are his children,
Is O’Maelcallann, the fair and hardy.

Let us approach the lords of Teffia,<note>Teathbha, a large territory comprising parts of modern Westmeath and Longford.</note>
We ought not always to avoid them,
The brown oaks of the valleys,
The protection and bounty of Ireland.

The chief king of Teffia, of whom robbers are afraid,
Is O’Kearney of the wounding weapons,
A branch who left ploughed divisions;
Along with Mac Quinn<note>Mag Cuinn.</note> and O’Confiacla.<note>O’Confiacla.</note>

O’Loughnan,<note>O’Lachtnain.</note> of no small farming,
And O’Murigan,<note>O’Muireagan.</note> the very bountiful,
They have well ordered the seasons,<note>A poetic way of saying they governed so well that the land was productive.</note>
These are the sub-kings [of Teffia].

The goodly kings of the festive Comar,<note>Meeting of the waters; likely referring to a place near Clonard.</note>
Is O’Flanagan, the plundering chief—
Let them all be by my side—
And O’Breen, the melodious, over Brawney.<note>Breaghmhuine, now the barony of Brawney in Westmeath.</note>

MacConmeadha of the swine-pens,
Is over the fierce Muintir-Laodhuicain.
Mag Aedha, to whom the title is given,
Is over the fruitful Muintir-Tlamain.

O’Carroll is near Tara;
The land of the men has gone under a cloud,
That people did not follow their heritage.<note>This suggests the family had been dispossessed or had declined by the time of the writing.</note>

Let us select more concerning Tara,
Of kings with great spirit;
His yoke smoothed every battle,
O’Duinn is over the lands of Tara.

Mac Gillareachloinn, the very heart,
Is over southern Breagh of dewy flowers;
The king over pure Cairbre Ghabhrain
Is O’Ronan, the brave champion.

# O'DUBHAGAIN.

The fair Mag Amhalghadha, over all

O'Dalaigh of lasting renown;

O'Cearbhail<note>53</note> over the south of Teamhair;
The land of the men has gone into bondage,

His yoke has tamed every battalion,
O'Duinn<note>54</note> over the districts of Teamhair.

MacGillaseachlainn the peaceable
Over Southern Breagh<note>55</note> of dewy flowers;
King over the fine Cairbre Gabhrain<note>56</note>
Is O'Ronain, the brave hero.

# O’DUBHAGÁIN.

->Over the Little Gaileanga of Breagh,<-
->O’Hennessy is counted;<-
->Seeking their protection without doubt,<-
->The beautiful men of Meath I have enumerated.<-

->Of the Meath-men of Meath,<-
->For a long period of time,<-
->They are the branches of the planted trees,<-
->The two kings of Fingal, bright and pure.<-

->Mac Giollamocholmog the fair,<-
->And O’Dunchadha of goodly mien,<-
->To them the reigns were assigned,<-
->It was proved that they were good kings.<-

->The three districts of Tuilen without fault,<-
->In Meath, though they are not of Meath;<-
->The men of Eoghan were distinguished for them,<-
->And the British Maine of lasting affection.<-

->An early estimation these men make,<-
->For they are the congregation of Cairneach.<-
->The gifts of the race of bright-eyed Domhnall,<-
->The king of Ui Maine is O’Muircheartaigh.<-

->O’Modhairn, a very handsome king,<-
->Over the noble Cenel Eoghain;<-
->Under the distinction they themselves achieved,<-
->Their own Britons were under them.<-

->Let us put an end to their stories,<-
->The choice of Meath of the smooth grass;<-
->From the Brugh, from the plain of Breagh of the deeds,<-
->To go to Tara, let us journey. Let us journey.<-

The portion of Meath regarding this subject and this poem ends here.

<page-num>15</page-num>
# O’DUBHAGÁIN.

> Over Lesser Gailenga, of Breagh, <note>5</note>
> O'Hennessy is counted;
> Seek his protection without hesitation,
> He is the most splendid Meathman of all I have named.
>
> Of the Meathmen of Meath,
> For a long period of time,
> There have been tribes of distinction,
> The two kings of bright Fingal: <note>58</note>
>
> MacGiolla-Mocholmog, the fair,
> O’Dunchadha, <note>59</note> of good appearance,
> By them the seasons were ruled,
> To prove that they were good kings.
>
> The three clans of Tuilen <note>60</note> without blemish,
> In Meath, though they are not Meathmen themselves,
> Are the Fir-Eoghain, distinguished among them,
> The Maini, and the Britons of lasting fame.
>
> Early these men drink their mead;
> They are the congregation of Cairneach.
> Valiant are the Siol-Domhnaill of fine eyes,
> The king of Ui-Maine is O’Muircheartaigh.
>
> O’Modhairn, a peaceful king is he,
> Over the noble Cenel Eoghain,
> Who have flourished under him,
> With their own Britons subject to them.
>
> Let us cease our stories
> Of the smooth-grassy Meath,
> From the Brugh, from the plain of Breagh of laws,
> To go to Tara, let us pass.

This concludes the portion of the subject and the poem that relates to Meath.

## The following is the portion of the province of Ulster, and its produce to Oileach of the kings.

O’Neill, high-king of Oileach, and MagLachlainn, another high-king; O’Cahan and O’Connor, the two high-lordships of Keenaght; O’Duvdirma, lord of the Bredach; O’Hogan over Tulach Og; and O’Gormley over Cenel Moen; O’Farrell, and O’Donnellan, and O’Donegan, and MacMurchada, and MacDuinnchuain, and MacRory over Tellach Anveith and over Muintir Birn; the chief of Corca Eachach is O’Kelly; O’Tierney and O’Kiernan over Farney; O’Mulvressal and O’Boyle, O’Quinn and O’Kenny over Magh Itha; O’Donnell over Cenel Binny of the Glen, and over Cenel Binny of Tuath Rois, and over Cenel Binny of Loch Drochait; O’Duvduany, and O’Hamill, and O’Etigan over the three Tellachs, namely: Tellach Cathalain, Tellach Duvrailve, and Tellach Mvrainan; O’Mulfogarty, and O’Hosey, and O’Hogan over Cenel Tierney; O’Cuanach and O’Baethgalach over Clann Fergus; O’Brouder, and O’Mulfavill, and O’Hogan over Carrickbraghy; O’Murphy and O’Mellan over Siol Aedha Eanaigh; and MacFiachrach over Cenel Ferady.

Siol Arnin, and Siol Mulfavill, and Clann MacCawell on the north side; the two noblest districts in Cenel Ferady are Tellach Maoil-geimridh and Tellach Maoil-pattraic.

## The portion of Oirghiall here.

O’Carroll, O’Duvdara, and O’Larkin, tributary kings <note>literally "hand-kings"</note> of Oirghiall, and MacMahon thereafter; O’Flaherty high-king of Ulster; O’Flynn and O’Donnellan, lords of O’Tuirtre; O’Hirk over Ui Fiachrach Finn; O’Creedan, lord of the Machaire <note>the Plain</note>; O’Hea over the men of Farney; and O’Kevan lord of Magh Lemhna; and O’Macaden lord of Mourne; O’Heer and O’Hanlon, the two lords of Orior; O’Cosgrave lord of Fear Rois; O’Hanratty, lord of Ui Meith Macha; O’Boylan, lord of Dartree; Muintir Taichligh and Muintir Muldoon, the chiefs of Laeghaire; and MacTiernan over Clann Fergail.

<header>O’DUBHAGAIN.</header>

Let us travel into the lands of Ulster,
From Tailtin of the lordly heroes,
From Bregia, out from Meath,
From the industrious tribe of Tara.

There will be no stopping until we reach Aileach,
To the race of Eoghan of the spirited arms;
A pledge they received from a king without treachery,
The noble pillars of Ireland.

This saying is no hidden saying,
As it is spread by historians;
The light of the princely houses and the feasts,
The resort of everyone is to Eoghan.

The royal O’Neills of great prosperity,
And the brave MacLaughlins;
It is hereditary to the fierce progeny,
The two tribes of the sovereignty.

Ten cantreds—no difficult division—
The ten sons of Eoghan of the red arms;
Pleasant indeed is what I have seen of their lands,
Which they hold as a true inheritance.

Of the race of Eoghan of the battles,
Is the fair king of Cianachta, O’Cathain;
A host in every direction, easily...;
Of the seed of Tadhg, son of Cian of Cashel.
The family of the bright fertility,
O’Conor was their first king.

O’Duibhdhiorma, high and proud,
Lord of the ever-noble Bredach,

<summary>This page identifies the noble lineages of the Cinel Eoghain in Ulster, specifically mentioning the O'Neills, MacLaughlins, and O'Cathains. It situates their ancestral authority between the ancient seats of Tara and Aileach.</summary>

<page-num>21</page-num>
<header>O'DUBHAGAIN.</header>

Let us pass into the lands of Ulster, <note>61</note>
From Tailtin <note>62</note> of lordly champions,
From Bregia, <note>63</note> out from Meath,
From the spreading tribe of Tara.

We shall not halt until we reach Aileach, <note>64</note>
To the race of Eoghan <note>65</note> of valiant arms,
Who have obtained the prize for greatness without fraud,
The pinnacle of the nobility of Ireland.

This saying is no hidden saying
Circulated by the historians;
The abundance of princely houses and banquets,
Everyone flocks to Eoghan.

Royal O'Neill <note>66</note> of great prosperity,
And the very proud MacLaughlins,
A race of no hereditary tameness,
Two tribes of the kingship. <note>67</note>

Ten cantreds—no difficult partition—
The ten sons <note>68</note> of red-armed Eoghan got;
Delightful, too, was what they saw beneath them,
And which they hold as a true patrimony.

Of the race of Eoghan of valor,
The fair king of Cianachta <note>69</note> is O'Cathain;
His host in every quarter are mild toward you;
Of the race of Tadhg, son of Cian of Cashel,
A tribe of abundant fruit, with brilliance,
O'Conor <note>70</note> was its first king.

O'Duibhdhiorma <note>71</note> of high pride,
Chief of ever-noble Bredach. <note>72</note>

<page-num>22</page-num>
<header># O’DUBHAGAIN.</header>

Well has the king found the race of his ancestors,
The race of highest nobility among Eoghan's descendants.
A progeny that flourished without a rival,
The tribe of the noble Bredach <note>Bredach was a territory in Inishowen</note>.

A strong chief over Tulaigh Óg <note>Tullaghogue</note>,
Is O’Hagan, lord of the fair roads;
Every oak wood throughout the district is governed
By another O’Hagan surrounding it.

Many are their warriors under spears,
From the active O’Gormleys;
A brilliant, illustrious stronghold without sorrow,
Over the noble Cenel Moen.

The O’Farrells of healthy vigor,
And the ruddy-faced O’Donnelans;
Over the great kindling at the Learga <note>The borders or slopes</note> with you,
Are the O’Donnagans of Meigh Mhurchadha.

The Mac Duinnchuans and the smooth Mac Rorys,
Over the formidable Teallach nCinbich;
They are never heard of being "dry" <note>Lacking in hospitality or drink</note> at their house,
And over the victorious Muintir Birn.

The chiefs of high Cenel Eachach
Are the just-judging Muintir Kelly;
The O’Kierans are great over Fermuigh,
And the substantial Sil Tierney.

The noble men of Magh Iotha,
Who defended the borderlands;
Delightful is the skill in every church,
Of the O'Mulkerrins <note>Ui Maoilbhreasail</note> and the O’Boyles.

# O'DUBHAGAIN.

Well has it found the strength of its ancestors,
The noblest clan of the race of Eoghan <note>Cinel Eoghain</note>;
A tribe which has thrived even without peace,
The clan of the chieftaincy comes from Bredach.

A strong chief over Tulach Og <note>Tullaghogue</note>,
Is O’Hagan, chief of the white roads;
The plow has passed through every wood for them,
And another O’Hagan is near it.

Many are the heroes with spears
Among the active O'Gormleys,
A stronghold of flaming girdles without misfortune,
Over the majestic race of Moen <note>Cenel Moen</note>.

The O'Farrells of healthy vigor,
And the ruddy-faced O'Donnelans;
Great kindling on the hill slopes belongs to you,
The O'Donnagans and the MacMurchadas.

The MacDuinnchuains and the gentle MacRorys,
Over the formidable Teallach Ainbhith;
They are never heard to be "dry" <note>lacking in hospitality or drink</note> at their house,
And they are over the victorious Muintir Birn.

The chieftains of noble Cinel Eachach
Are the just-judging Muintir Kelly.
The O'Kierans are great over the Fearamaigh,
And the substantial Siol Tierney.

The men of noble Magh Iotha,
Who defended the borders;
Delightful are their customs in every church,
They are the O'Maoilbreasails and the O'Boyles.

# O'DUBHAGAIN.

A great abode for every hero's ship,
The brave O’Quinns and the O’Kennys.

The Cinel Binnigh <note>Cenel mBinnigh</note> of the clear valley,
Princes who adore the truth.
The Cinel Binnigh of Tuath Rois,
A race that is a pillar against injustice.

The Cinel Binnigh of short-lived captivity,
Of the deep-waved Loch Drochaid.
A pillar who shared out every spear,
O’Donnell is a good chieftain.

In one tribe are the O’Duibhduanas;
What poet has not truly heard of them?
Their words are spoken without hesitation,
The O’Hamills and the O’Etigans.

Three households <note>teallaigh</note> are in the eastern territory:
The household of Cathalan of the clergy,
Whose purchase of a beautiful poem is well-known,
And the fine household of Braonan.

The household of the law-giving Dubhroilbhe,
Whose heritage is well-maintained.
Illustrious men of fame in the place,
Dividing these three households.

Over the powerful Cinel Tierney <note>Cenel Tighearnaigh</note>,
Let us mention O’Maolfothartaigh;
Great is their knowledge and their valor:
The O’Hoseys and the O’Hagans.

The clans of the gentle Fergus,
Known for the strength of their prince.

It also identifies the leaders of Cinel Tierney and the Clann Fergus.</summary>

<page-num>25</page-num>

# O'DUBHAGAIN.

Their dwellings are over the house of every hero:
The brave O’Quinns and O’Kennys.

The fine Cinel Binnigh <note>Cenel mBinnigh</note> of the Glen,
Chieftains who adore the truth.
The Cinel Binnigh of Tuath Rois,
You may escape from it in its absence.

The Cinel Binnigh of no lasting servitude,
Of the rapid-waved Loch Drochaid.
Towers who have shattered every spear,
O’Donnell is a goodly chieftain here.

In one tribe are the O’Duibhduannas;
What poet has not truly heard of them?
Speech without hesitation is what I say:
The O’Hamills and the O’Etigans

Are over the three tribes in the eastern heath,
The household of Cathalan <note>Teallach Cathalain</note> of the troops.
How polished the poem for their patronage,
And the majestic household of Braonan <note>Teallach Braonain</note>.

The righteous household of Dubhroilbhe <note>Teallach Dubhroilbhe</note>,
They cling well to their patrimony.
Bright men of fame in their home—
These are the three tribes.

Over the powerful Cinel Tierney <note>Cenel Tighearnaigh</note>,
I reckon O’Maolfothartaigh.
Great is their knowledge and their luck—
The O’Hoseys and the O’Hagans.

Behold the clans of Fergus,
And know their vigorous chieftains.

# O'DUBHAGAIN.

Victorious yonder on every hill,
Are the Clann Chuanach and the Clann Baoithghulaigh.

Over the enduring Carraic Brachaidhe <note>Carrickabraghy in Inishowen</note>,
Over the red-armed Clann Fergus.
They have gone to the sea on every side:
The O'Broders <note>Uí Bhruadair</note> and the O'Mulfoyles <note>Uí Maoilfhabhuill</note>.
The O'Conneys and many O'Hagans,
A choice and populous assembly.

Mention the Sil Aedha of Eanach <note>Síol Aedha Eanaigh</note>,
Their princes and their tribes;
For them the gathering was not weak—
The O'Murphys <note>Uí Mhurchadha</note> and the O'Mellans <note>Uí Melláin</note>.

The festive Cenel Fearadhaigh <note>Cinel Farry</note>,
Noble is the custom of their lineage;
The O'Fiachras are on the strong southern side—
In the heat of battle we do not mourn them.

The Sil Airnin are on the northern side,
And the red-armed Sil Mulfoyle;
A clan not impoverished by their weapons,
And the battle-armed Clann Cathmhaoil <note>MacCawell</note>.

Two tribes to the east of every troop
Belong to the high Cenel Fearadhaigh:
The household of Mulgevery <note>Teallach Maoilgeimhridh</note>, free from theft,
And the fair-fingered household of Mulpatrick <note>Teallach Maoilphatraigh</note>.

Let us leave the mead-drinking progeny;
Let us stay with the tribe of Fearadhach.
I seek the lineage of every learned man;
From the noble race of Eoghan let us proceed. Let us proceed.

# O'DUBHAGAIN.

Victorious over enemies on every hill,
Are the Clann-Cuanach and the Clann-Baothghalaigh.

Over the enduring Carraic Brachaidhe <note>Carrickabraghy in Inishowen</note>,
Over the red-armed Clann Fergusa.
On each side they extended to the sea <note>Literally "the wave"</note>,
The O'Bruadairs and the O'Maoilfhabhaills,
The O'Coinnes and the O'Hagans here,
A distinguished and populous people.

Speak of the Siol Aedha of Eanach <note>Síol Aedha Eanaigh</note>,
Their chieftains and their tribes;
For them the gathering was not small,
The O'Murchadhas <note>O’Murphys</note> and the O'Mellains.

In the festive Cinel Fearadhaigh <note>Cinel Farry</note>,
Their genealogies are consistently noble;
The O'Fiachras are on the strong south side,
I do not lament their heroic fight.

The Siol-Airnin are on the north side,
And the red-armed Siol-Maoilfhabhaill;
A clan without disgrace from their weapons,
And the warlike Clann Cathmhaoil <note>MacCawell</note>.

The two eastern septs are over every tribe,
In the high Cinel Fearadhaigh:
Teallach Maoilgeimhridh <note>The household of Mulgevery</note>, free from theft,
And the white-fingered Teallach Maoilpatraig <note>The household of Mulpatrick</note>.

Let us leave the mead-drinking tribe;
Let us stop discussing the sept of Fearadhach.
Let us ask for the lineage of every learned man;
From the noble race of Eoghan, let us pass. Let us pass.

# O’DUBHAGAIN.

## OIRGHIALLA HERE.

Move quickly from there,
Find the assembly of the nobility,
Take their tribute along with their hostages;
Do not delay in going to the Oirghialla <note>Airgialla or Oriel</note>.

O'Carroll and O'Duibhdara,
High kings without kin-slaying,
Men who served every member of the clergy,
Ruling over Oirghialla without reproach.

The high kings of those places
Are MacMahon and Maguire;
Good for you is the duty of their rule,
The descendants of the nobility of Oirghialla.

Lordship was hereditary to the family of
O'Lairgnen, the full king of Oirghialla;
Do not overlook the fierce, pure power
Of O'Flaithri, high king of Ulaidh <note>Ulster</note>.

The kings of Ui Tuirtre <note>A territory north of Lough Neagh</note> of the heavy slaughters
Are O'Flynn, and among them is O'Donnellan;
O'Herc is over the fair Ui Fiachrach <note>Ui Fiachrach Finn in Ardstraw</note>,
Without concealing battle-arrays or conflict.

The king of the smooth, mead-abounding plain
Is O'Creighan over the tribes;
O'Hea is over another band,
Noble over the men of Fearmaighe <note>Farney</note>.

O'Keevan, the head of the battle,
Is king over Magh Leamhna <note>The plain of the Lemhan, near Clogher</note> of heroic prosperity;
Noble is the battle-chief of the drinking horns,
O'Mochoidhein, king of Mughdhorn <note>Mourne</note>.

<summary>This page introduces the kingdom of Oirghialla (Oriel), identifying its major ruling families such as the O'Carrolls, MacMahons, and Maguires. It further lists the local chieftains of sub-territories including Ui Tuirtre, Farney, and Mourne.</summary>

<page-num>29</page-num>

# O'DUBHAGAIN.

## OIRGHIALLA HERE.

Move forward quickly,
Leave the assembly of the nobility,
Take their tribute along with their hostages;
Do not stop until you come to the Oirghialla <note>Airgialla or Oriel</note>.

O'Carroll and O'Duibhdara,
High kings without kin-slaying,
Men who have served every poet,
Rule over the Oirghialla without reproach.

High kings in place of these,
Are the MacMahons and Maguires;
Good for you is their mercy and their rule,
They are the noblest races of the Oirghialla.

It is his hereditary right to succeed to the lordship;
O'Lairgnen is the full king of Oirghialla;
He is no weakling, but possesses fine vigor;
O'Flaithri is high king of Uladh <note>Ulster</note>.

The kings of Ui Tuirtre <note>A territory north of Lough Neagh</note> of the heavy slaughters
Are O'Flynn and O'Donnellan;
O'Herc is over the fair Ui Fiachrach Finn <note>Ui Fiachrach Finn in Ardstraw</note>,
Without hiding from battles or conflicts.

The king over the smooth, mead-abounding plain
Is O'Creighan over the tribes;
O'Hea is over another tribe,
Noble over the men of Farney <note>Feara Fearnmaighe</note>.

O'Keevan, head of the battle,
Is king of Magh Leamhna <note>The plain of the Lemhan, near Clogher</note> of the hero-fort;
Noble is the battle-island of the drinking cups,
O'Mochoidhein is king of Mughdhorna <note>Mourne</note>.

<summary>This page continues the account of the kingdom of Oirghialla (Oriel), identifying its major ruling families such as the O'Carrolls, MacMahons, and Maguires. It further lists the local chieftains of sub-territories including Ui Tuirtre, Farney, and Mourne.</summary>

<page-num>30</page-num>

# O'DUBHAGAIN.

Two kings rule over the Oirtheara <note>The territories of Orior</note> through pride:
O'Heer and O'Hanlon;
O'Cosgraigh is king of the smooth Fir Rois,
For whose prowess every triumph opened.

The kings of Ui Meith Macha <note>A territory in County Monaghan</note> without decay
Are the O'Hanrattys of the high reaches;
A rod of division, a tribe of distinction,
Is MacDonnell over Clann Ceallaigh.

A blue-eyed host with white hands
Are the red-mouthed people, the O'Boylans;
Griffins who are neither base nor small,
The bold kings of Dartraighe <note>Dartree</note>.

Over Ui Laoghaire of Loch Lir <note>Near Enniskillen</note>
Are the Muintir Taithligh as chieftains;
Muintir Maolduin, whose track is not weak,
Are deep in their defense in combat.

MacTiernan, a lord of renown,
Is over the Clann Fetha of Fearghal;
A land of forts, peaceful and without strife,
Belongs entirely to O'Flanagan.

Muintir Pheodachain of the stronghold
Are chieftains of noble descent;
Sweet-voiced men from the level plain—it is no secret—
Along with the fortunate MacGillafinnen.

The guiding force of the noble fort
Are the brave chieftains, the O'Connollys;
A beautiful tribe, fierce in battle as a griffin,
And the majestic MacGillamichael.

It details the geographical lordships of Orior, Dartree, and regions near Lough Erne.</summary>

# O’Dubhagáin.

->Plunder is natural to their lords,<-
->To the nobles of the O’Mahonys. <note>168</note><-

->Sub-kings of Iveagh of Cobha, <note>169</note><-
->Who were powerful in ancient times,<-

# O'DUBHAGAIN.

Acquisitions are hereditary to their chieftains;
Of their chieftains are the O'Mahonys;<gloss>168</gloss>

The sub-chiefs of Iveagh Cobha,<gloss>169</gloss>
Who were powerful in ancient times,
How graceful at the meeting in each territory,
Are the active O'Conney<gloss>170</gloss> and O'Garvey.<gloss>171</gloss>

O'Hanvey<gloss>172</gloss> was high king there;
He was not neglected, and we shall not omit him;
Neither his prosperity nor his career has been checked,
Proud was his battalion when marching.

Chief over noble Clan-Aedha
Is the majestic and splendid Magennis;<gloss>173</gloss>
They have chosen the warm hill,
They have taken all of Ulster.

MacArtan has by charter
The steady and stout Kinel-Fogarty,<gloss>174</gloss>
Who never refuse gifts to the poets;
They are the treasury of hospitality.

The MacDevanys<gloss>175</gloss> without plunder,
Are over the noble Kinel-Awley;
The O'Mornas,<gloss>176</gloss> the lineage of victory,
Are the supports of hard-armed Ulster.

The MacDuilechains,<gloss>177</gloss> of the districts,
Are over the red-haired Clan-Brazel.
O'Colteran,<gloss>178</gloss> of the border town,
Dwells over the Dal-Cuirb.

There has been collected within the north-east,
The foundation of the nobility in Ulster,

<page-num>38</page-num>

# O'DUBHAGAIN.

Good men who redden a troop,
The forge of the hospitality of Ireland.

The heroic victory of Conn's Half,<note>Leath Cuinn, the northern half of Ireland.</note>
The summit of restraint and combat;
Many are the hostellers among the peaks,
Ulstermen and Irishmen.

It is difficult to depart from Emain,<gloss>Emain Macha, the ancient capital of Ulster.</gloss>
From the Red Branch of the long weapons.
Even though our possessions and provisions be many,
Our mind is only for a slow journey.

Let us travel from peaked Boirche,<note>The Mourne Mountains.</note>
And from the mantle-rich land of Cuailgne,
From Magh Rath of fierce enmity,
And from the heroic battalion of the O'Lowrys.

From Downpatrick of the mantles,
For it is the royal cemetery of Ireland;
Let it not escape my attention there,
The town where the clay of Columba covered.

In the same grave was placed
Bridget, the glory of our bands of women;
As we leave to them every triumph,
Patrick of Macha lies in that great grave.

The triumph of Ireland is with the Ulstermen,
With the pillar-battalion of the fair company.
The nobles of Ireland, the Eoghanachs,
Are found by the border of the valiant king.

The head of Ireland is great Armagh;
No high lords are nobler.

It highlights Downpatrick as the royal cemetery and resting place of Saints Patrick, Bridget, and Columba, while affirming Armagh's status as the spiritual head of Ireland.</summary>

### THE PORTION OF TIR CONNELL.

> On our journey on a prosperous voyage,
> Let us leave the swift great hosts of Macha;
> Let us not make a good omen for the people
> Until we arrive in Cenél Conaill.

> Arrived on a direct journey,
> Rugged is the land of that tribe
> To meet us at Eas Aoidha,<note>Assaroe, the waterfall at Ballyshannon.</note>
> The advantage of the fair-shaped people.

> The O'Muldorys,<note>Uí Maoldoraidh</note> if they survived,
> They will not come as they once came,
> Without delay and without slow meeting
> To meet us, and the O'Canannans.<note>Uí Chananáin</note>

> They will come, for their lord was strong,
> The Clann Dalaigh<note>Clanna Dálaigh, the O'Donnells</note> of the brown shields.
> By them through conflict was not withered
> The inheritance from the high kings.

> The Clann Chinfaelaidh of the white horses,
> And beautiful Tir Ainmireach,
> I see, as a sword-drawn host,
> The fierce Tir Baghaine.<note>Tír mBaghaine, now the barony of Banagh in Donegal.</note>

> Here is the portion of the proud hosts,
> The land of O'Boyle<note>Tír Ó mBaoighill</note> of the sweet words,
> The handsome host in their house yonder;
> A great portion of the land is theirs.

### THE PART OF TIR CHONAILL.

Our journey is a tour of prosperity,
Let us leave the vigorous host of great Macha,
Let us not refuse good luck to the people,
Let us proceed to Cinel-Conaill.<note>Cenél Conaill, the territory of the O'Donnells and related families.</note>

Let them come on a journey of prosperity;
Rugged is the land of this tribe;
To meet us at the Cataract of Aedh,<note>Eas Aoidha or Assaroe, the waterfall at Ballyshannon.</note>
The prosperity of the splendid-faced people.

The O'Maoildoraidhs,<note>Uí Maoldoraidh</note> if they were living,
Would come (but they will not come),
Without slowness or slow delay,
To meet us, as would the O'Canannains.<note>Uí Chananáin</note>

But others will come, their chief is stout,
The Clanna-Dalaigh<note>Clanna Dálaigh, the O'Donnells</note> of brown shields;
With them, through contest, the inheritance
Of the sovereignty has not withered.

The Clann-Chinnfhaelaidh<note>Clann Cinnfhaolaidh, in the barony of Kilmacrenan</note> of white steeds,
And the beautiful Tir-Ainmirech,<note>Tír Ainmirech</note>
I see the host with swords,
And the fierce *men of* Tir-Baghaine.<note>Tír mBaghaine, now the barony of Banagh in Donegal.</note>

This is the share of the haughty hosts,
The land of O'Baoighill<note>O'Boyle</note> of the ruddy mouth,
The fair host over at their house,
They have a large share of the lands.

It lists specific territories such as Tir Baghaine and Tir Ainmirech, identifying O'Boyle as a significant landholder in the region.</summary>

# O'DUBHAGAIN.

O'Maelmaghna over Magh Seiridh,<note>Magh Seiridh was a plain near Ballyshannon, County Donegal.</note>
To the companies of poets, he was not stingy;
O'Hea on the prosperous Eas Ruaidh,<note>Eas Ruaidh is the waterfall of Assaroe at Ballyshannon.</note>
Active in the battle of every heavy host.

With O'Taircheirt of heavy contracts,
Are the bright-shining Clanna Neachtain;
Mac Dubhain—tales have separated us—
Is over the bright Cenel Enda.<note>Cenél Enda is a territory in the barony of Raphoe.</note>

Glenn Binne—noble is the stewardship—
Is held by the populous Mag Loingseacháin;
Fierce was the strength of their leaping into every fight;
The generous O'Breislein is of Fanad.

A warlike host that is not deceitful,
Over the soft-sloped Ard Miodhair;<note>Ard Miodhair is a district in the barony of Raphoe.</note>
Men who were seen to be powerful
While acting for O'Doherty.

Held by the stout Mac Gille Samhaith
Are Ros Guill and Ros Iorguill, let us reckon;
A host without panic, without weakness,
O'Furadhran over Fiondruis.

Two other chiefs are certain for you,
Over the famous, victorious territory;
One of them is O'Cearnachain with vigor,
A part of his good fortunes I have confirmed.

Another of them is a royal, prosperous host,
The family of Dalachan of the hard spears;
I name for you without bad verses
Their names and their country.

Is over the bright, fine Cinel Enda.<note>Cinel Enda is a territory in the barony of Raphoe.</note>

Gleann Binnigh,<note>Gleann Binnigh is a valley in the barony of Raphoe.</note> noble is the list of chiefs,
Is held by the populous Mag Loingseachain;
He has leaped vehemently into every fight,
The generous O'Breislein in Fanad.<note>Fanad is a peninsula in the north of County Donegal.</note>

A battle-armed host that is not treacherous
Is over Ard Miodhair<note>Ard Miodhair is a district in the barony of Raphoe.</note> of the well-watered slopes;
Men who have been found valiant
Are proving it to O'Doherty.

To the stout Mac Gille Samhais<note>Mac Gille Samhais was the chief of Ros Guill.</note>
Belong Ros Guill<note>Ros Guill is a peninsula in the barony of Kilmacrenan.</note> and Ros Iorguil,<note>Ros Iorguil is a district near Ros Guill.</note> I reckon;
A host without boasting or falsehood,
O'Furadhrain over Fionnros.<note>Fionnros is a district in the barony of Boylagh.</note>

Two other chieftains, you can be certain,
Are over the victorious Tuath Bladhach.<note>Tuath Bladhach, now known as Doe, is in the barony of Kilmacrenan.</note>
One of them is O'Cearnachan<note>O'Cearnachan was a chieftain of the territory of Tuath Bladhach.</note> of valor,
Whose prosperity I have witnessed.

Of them is the royal host of prosperous tribes,
The bountiful Muintir Dalachain.<note>Muintir Dalachain was a family located in the barony of Kilmacrenan.</note>
I mention to you, without elaborate verses,
Their names and their country.

Tir Mac Carthainn<note>Tir Mac Carthainn was a territory in the barony of Raphoe, County Donegal.</note> of the spoils,

Tir Breasail,<note>Tir Breasail was a district in the barony of Raphoe.</note> land of fruit,

The victory and hardiness of Clann Neill
Are in Conall’s triumph;
A host that was not slow to serve me there,
Though from Conall we now depart. Let us depart.

### THE PART RELATING TO BREIFNE.

O’Rourke is high king of Breifne; MacKiernan over Tullyhunco; Magauran over Tullyhaw; and MacConsnamha over Clann Chionaoith; and MacCaughan over Clann Fearmaighe; and MacDorcy over Cenel Luachain; and MacClancy over Dartry; O’Finn and O’Carroll over Calry; O’Reilly over Muintir Maelmordha; O’Quinn over Muintir Gillagan; and MacMaelisa over Magh Breacraighe; and Maginver over Muintir Gearadhain; and MacRannall over Muintir Eolais; and O’Mulvey over Magh Neis; O’Quinn over Muintir Fearghail; O’Mulclohy over the two Carburys; O’Hara, O’Huathmharan, O’Kernaghan, and O’Gara, lords of Leyny; O’Devlin and O’Duncahy, lords of Corran; MacGeoch, MacMaonaigh, and MacReevy were the three ancient chiefs of Moylurg; O’Dowda, lord of northern Ui Fiachrach from the River Robe to the Codnach; O’Murray, O’Gormog, and O’Tierney over Carra; O’Beirne over Muintir Mandachain; MacBrennan and O’Mulvihil over Corca Achlann; O’Hanly over Cenel Dobtha; O’Kearney and O’Kierin over Ciarraighe Maighe; O’Molloy over Clann Taidhg; and O’Flynn over Clann Maoilruain; O’Rothlain over Caille Fothaidh; MacScahill over Corca Mogha; and O’Breen over Loch Gealgosa; O’Malley over the two Umhalls; O’Talcharan over Conmaicne Cuile; and O’Kealy over Conmaicne Mara; MacConry over Gnomore; and O’Hadnaidh over Gnobeg; MacHugh over Clann Cosgraigh; O’Flaherty over Muintir Murchadha; O’Heyne, MacGillacelly, and the O’Clerys over Ui Fiachrach Finn; and O’Dungilla over Cenel Chinnghamhna; and MacFiachra over Ogaibh Beathra; and O’Kane over Cenel Sedna; and O’Maghna over Caenraighe; and O’Shaughnessy and O’Cahill, two chiefs of Cenel Aedha.

<page-num>48</page-num>
# O'Dubhagáin.

## THE PART RELATING TO HY-MANY.

O'Kelly is the high lord of Hy-Many; O'Connell is lord from Grian to the end of the plain; O'Naghten and O'Mullally are the two lords of Moenmoy; O'Mannion, the Clann Ward, O'Scurry, O'Lennon, O'Cassain, O'Gially, and O'Maigin—whichever of them is your lord, he is over the east above the other people during the time of his reign; O'Cahill, O'Muran, and O'Mulrooney are the three lords of Crumthan; O'Leog is the sub-king of the Caladh; O'Madden is over Sil Anmchadha, and O'Hoolahan as well; MacEgan is over Clann Dermot north and south; MacGillafinnagan and O'Kenny are over Clann Flahive; O'Donnellan is over Clann Breasail; O'Donoghue is over Clann Cormac of Moenmoy; O'Deegan is over the twelve townlands of the O'Deegans; O'Docomlan is over the Eidneach; O'Goran is over Dal Druithne; and O'Mulbride is the chief of Moyfinn.

---

> Let us proceed—it is no trifling journey—
> From the great, delightful host of Macha;
> It is not a summons but a journey to be made,
> Across the Srodhair to the plain of Cruachan.

> Let us look around Cruachan of the ramparts,
> To the south, the north, the west, and the east;
> Its tribes in their lands,
> And their kindreds let us visit.

> Let us divide, with a happy omen,
> The Clann Conor around Cruachan.
> Let the bards be with us with every poem they recite,
> From Cill-ard to Tuaim Dreccoin.

> Kings of the hosts who did not flee,
> From great, smooth-grassed Cruachan;
> They were never surpassed there in abundance,
> Their ancestry is of the Clann Conor.
