<header>O'DUBHAGÁIN.</header>

### THE PART RELATING TO HY-MANY.

O'Kelly, chief lord of Hy-Many; O'Connell is lord from the Grian to the head of the plain; O'Naghten and O'Mullally, the two lords of Moenmoy; O'Mannion, and the Mac Wards, and O'Scurry, and O'Lennon, and O'Cassain, and O'Gially, and O'Maigin—whichever of them is lord is sub-king over the others during his reign; O'Cahill, O'Muran, and O'Mulrooney, the three lords of Crumthan; O'Leog is sub-king of the Caladh; O'Madden is over Sil Anmchadha, and O'Hoolahan also; and MacEgan over Clann Dermot north and south; and MacGillafinnagan and O'Kenny over Clann Flahive; and O'Donnellan over Clann Breasail; and O'Donoghue over Clann Cormac of Moenmoy; and O'Deegan over the twelve townlands of the O'Deegans; and O'Docomlan over the Eidneach; and O'Goran over Dal Druithne; and O'Mulbride, chief of Moyfinn.

> Let us pass—it is no pleasant journey—
> From the great and delightful host of Macha;<sup>222</sup>
> It is not wisdom, but delay, not to proceed
> Across the Drowes<sup>223</sup> to the plain of Cruachan.<sup>224</sup>

> At the walled Cruachan let us take a survey,
> South, north, west, and eastwards;
> Their tribes and their lands,
> And their kindreds let us visit.

> Let us divide—may it be a happy project—
> At Cruachan, the Clann Conor;<sup>225</sup>
> Bards with us in every poem shall arise,
> From Kill-ard<sup>226</sup> to Tuaim Dreccoin.<sup>227</sup>

> The kings of the hosts do not deny,
> At the great, smooth-grassed Cruachan,
> Who were not diminished by want of produce,
> Their lineage is the Clann Conor.

Hereditary to the descendants of Fiachra, south and north,
And to their kindreds forever,
Is the circuit of their host around Ath Slisin;
That kingship is hereditary to the O'Rourkes.

The inheritance of this seed, above all others,
Is the Sil-Muireadhaigh of the smooth forts;
A fierce tribe watching over it,
The kingship of branchy Cruachan.

The Clann Duach Teanguma of the bright hair,
The Sil-Flaherty, and Clann Cosgraigh;
Since they were not weak—you will not find it otherwise—
They took the fair province.

The Clann Mulrooney of the choice,
And the Clann Conor of the canon;
The tribe went without a steward;
They are both of one kindred.

Remember Clann Cathail above all others,
For the number of its usual kings;
Recount their bountiful deeds in the east,
The memory of the noble kings of O’Rodiv.

Four levying chiefs
Are over the triumphant Clann Cathail;
A phalanx that was never censured,
These are the four to be counted:

O’Flannagan, lord of the land,
And O’Maolmora, whom I praise.
While they lived, the company was beautiful:
The majestic O’Carthy and O’Mughroin.

Hereditary to the Ui-Fiachrach,<sup>228</sup> north and south,
And to their tribes, through constant victory,
Is the visitation of their hosts at Ath Slisin.<sup>229</sup>
That kingdom is hereditary to the Ui-Ruaire.<sup>230</sup>

Hereditary to this race above all others,
To the Sil-Muireadhaigh<sup>231</sup> of the flat forts—
A heroic tribe watching over it—
Is the kingdom of bushy Cruachan.

The race of Duach Teangumha<sup>232</sup> of the fine hair,
The Sil-Flaithbheartaigh,<sup>233</sup> and the Clann-Cosgraigh,<sup>234</sup>
Since they have not stayed behind, I shall not omit them;
They seized the fine province.

The Clann-Maoilruana,<sup>235</sup> the choicest,
And the Clann-Conchobhair<sup>236</sup> we sing;
The tribe is seen without a steward,
For they are both one tribe.<sup>237</sup>

Remember the Clann-Cathail<sup>238</sup> above all,
With their number of usual kings;
Their deeds of generosity are recounted in the east,
Equal to the noble kings of the O'Roduibhs.

Four levying chieftains
Are over the valorous Clann-Cathail;
A valiant bulwark, never disparaged,
These are the four to be counted:

O'Flannagain, chief of the land,
O'Maoilmordha,<sup>239</sup> whom I praise;
How splendid the tribe is in life—
The majestic O'Carthaigh,<sup>240</sup> and O'Mughroin.<sup>241</sup>

O'Maoilbrenainn of fame,
Over the smooth-plained Clann Conchobhair;
Their race is entitled to rule over every group,
The tribe of Clann Cathail.

O'Cathalain as their captains,
Over the green-grassed Clann Foghartaigh;
Not weak is their heavy flood for you,
Clann Murthuile is under O'Maonaigh.

To Magh Oireachtaigh of the horses,
Muintir Roduibh of the royal judgments;
A territory not yielded for a planting wood,
O'Finaghty over Clann Conmhaigh.

Over Clann Murchadha of the princes,
O'Finaghty, high and complete;
Two who governed the tribe of the two divisions,
Though they are of one race, they are not the same.

The Ui Diarmada in abundance,
True-worded kings of the royal land;
Lords of the territory without shifting their ancestral seats,
The Ui Concannainn are the leaders.

Mag Murchadha of powerful effort,
Over the bright-sided Clann Tomaltaigh;
A deed of their good fortune follows them,
From the high-spirited Sil Muireadhaigh.

The Sil Fallamhain leading every troop,
Over Clann Uadach of the wine-feasts;
The men whose trees have not withered,
Of them are those noble clans.

O'Maoilbhrenainn<note>242</note> with fame,
Over the well-watered plain of Clann-Conchobhair,
Their children are entitled to be above every tribe,
That branch of the Clann-Cathail.

O'Cathalain is established by charter
Over the green-grassed Clann-Foghartaigh,<note>243</note>
Their heavy flood is not weak for you;
The Clann-Murthuile<note>244</note> belong to O'Maenaigh.

To Mag Oireachtaigh<note>245</note> of the horses,
Belong Muintir Roduibh of royal judgments;
A lord who has not weakened over the flourishing wood,
O'Finachta over Clann-Conmhaigh.<note>246</note>

Over Clann-Murchadha<note>247</note> of the chiefs,
O'Finachta, high and perfect;
The two groups are two branches of the royal family,
Though they are one tribe, they are not equal.

Of the Ui-Diarmada,<note>248</note> the worthy,
People of true words, kings of royal men,
Chiefs of the land without difficult contracts,
The O'Conceanainns are in their leadership.

The Mag Murchadhas of brave effort,
Over the fair-sided Clann-Tomaltaigh,<note>249</note>
An act of their good prosperity lives after them,
They are of the spirited Siol-Muireadhaigh.

The Siol-Fallamhain<note>250</note> before every tribe,
Over the Clann-Uadach of wine-filled banquets;
These descendants are men who have not weakened;
From them come the noble clans.

Clann-Maoilruana of prosperity,
I have heard of none who are their equals;
Pure are their companies, handsome each face,
They possess Magh-Luirg<note>Moylurg</note> and Airteach.

Tir-Oilella and Tir-Tuathail,
After going down beyond ancient Cruachan;
It is no loss to the generation to name in verse,
The territory of Fear-Tire and Clann-Chuan.

Tir-Nechtain and Tir-Enda,
They are sages without regret;
Men of generous bows who have won valor,
Belong to Mac Diarmada as his native land.

<margin>Let us journey.</margin>
From the spirited Siol-Muireadhaigh,
Let us journey into the land of ancient Fearghal,
To the host of Breifne of sensible forms;
It is time, though it be no early journey.

The High-king of Breifne of lasting power is
O'Rourke, to whom the tribute of Connacht is due;
Sub-kings of that fame which is not meager,
And his chieftains are all around him.

Mac Tighearnain of the troops,
The protection of the fair Gaels,
The patron of the poets and their friend,
Rules over the fierce Tellach-Dunchadha.<note>Tullyhunco</note>

Mac Samhradhain, the bond of every strength,
Rules over the illustrious Tellach-Eachdhach;<note>Tullyhaw</note>
His land is not made ugly by the wind;
Mac Consnamha is over Clann-Chionaoith.<note>Muintir-Kenny</note>

The Clann-Maoilruana<note>Clan Mulrony</note> of prosperity,

Generous-hearted men who received valor;

Mag Caghadhain, a visit of fame,
Rules over noble Clann-Fearmaighe;
Mac Dorchaidh, whose cause is not condemned,
Is over the heroic Cenel-Luachain.

Three chiefs of Dartraighe I shall name,
And of Calraighe of the tribes;
Whose plunderings destroyed the valley:
The brave O'Finn and O'Carroll.

No common person performs the service of the feasts,
With the magnificent Mag Flannchadha<note>MacClancy</note>.

The royal chieftain of fierce onslaughts,
O'Reilly of the red weapons;
The charm of his gold is heard of
Over the smooth, majestic Muintir-Maoilmordha.

It is better at this time,
To depart for a while from this land. &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; Let us depart.

Let us depart—may it be a journey of profit—
On a visit to the Clann-Fearghusa;
To the other active host,
To the prosperous Clann-Rughraidhe.

Muintir-Giolla-gain of the spoils,
O'Quin is their lord and their chieftain;
Mag Maoiliosa of the fine horses,
His tribute is on Magh-Breacraidhe.

Mag Fionnbairr, pleasant his clear success,
Over the pleasant Muintir-Geradhain.
Mag Raghnaill<note>Reynolds</note> is heard of now,
Over the quick-moving Muintir-Eolais.

Mag Fionnbhairr<note>274</note>, delightful is his great prosperity,

Mag Raghnaill<note>275</note> is heard of now,

<header>O'DUBHAGAIN.</header>

The noble Muintir Maoil-míochair,
Upon Magh Neri of the strong hostages.
Good was the battle-scarred company;
Let us enumerate the lords of the tribes.

Muintir Farrell, of whom we now speak,
Hold sovereignty over the Clann Fergus;
Against every tribe they exert their power,
The O'Quins are their companions there.

Let us travel westward—a strong effort—
Let us make a survey of the Connachta;
To the center of Cairbre, let us not conceal it,
To the plain of plundering let us approach.

It is well for the heavy-haired host of the west,
That our joint journey is toward the Connachta;
In the province of the Clann Neill,
To Cairbre of the smooth-bordered territories.

A plain of games, a path of broad views,
O'Maolcluithe is at their head.
Let us go into the other Luighne;
Let us leave behind the territory of Cairbre.

Let us remember the Clanna Cein,
In active Luighne of the sharp weapons;
The kings of Luighne of great fame
Are O'Hara and O'Huathmharain.

Look upon Luighne of the full lakes;
Remember the descendants of Cearnachan.
Good is every dwelling of that warrior band;
O'Gara is of that noble disposition.

The noble Muintir-Maoilmiadhaigh,<sup>276</sup>
Over Magh-Nisi of the strong hostages.

<header>O'DUBHAGAIN.</header>

O'Dobhailen of good fame,
O'Duinncathaigh of the kerns;
Let me love and pray for them fervently,
The two kings of the level plain of Corann.

The ancient chiefs of full Magh Luirg,
We ourselves must not omit them:
Mag Eoach, the great Mag Maonaigh,
And Mag Riabhaigh of the royal host.

Let us go with spirit to the Ui Fiachrach,
To the sweet-voiced host of fierce habits;
From that brave host yonder,
It is not right to depart, though we proceed. *Let us proceed.*

O'Codhnaigh on a visitation of kings,
At the marker of the boundary,
As far as the river Robe, so it is said,
Over a beautiful and complete inheritance.

There is no territory narrower than that,
Held by O'Dowd as his homeland.
Fourteen kings of that people
Obtained the province without division,

Through deeds of mutual effort and battle,
Of the illustrious seed of Fiachra.
O'Muireadhaigh with spirit,
O'Gormog, and O'Tighearnaigh.
A goodly mind is the portion of this people,
Over rugged and beautiful Ceara.

Let us remember the Three Tuatha,
The helpful host of fair Cruachain.

<header>O’DUBHAGÁIN.</header>

Let us never neglect the pure ones,
Let us name their three lords:

Muintir Birn, brave in battle-ranks,
The illustrious O’Manachain;
Through conflict, through power, through threats,
Theirs is the land into which they came.

Clann Branan, powerful in their might,
And the descendants of Maoilmhordha of Michael.
The authority of that host, which is not weak, extends
Over Corca Shealbha of Seachlann.

The heritage of that sharp-armed troop
Is Cenel Dobhtha, dense and rugged;
Let the assembly of O’Cinlighe <note>O'Hanly</note>
Be held in my heart's affection.

Let us leave Cenel Fechin for a while,
Let us turn our backs on them immediately;
They were divided for a time,
They were cursed by a chief.

Over Ciarraighe of the smooth plain
Is the prudent Mac Ceithearnaigh;
A claim of their right is due from you,
Of that tribe is Clann Ceirin.

Clann Taidhg, the prosperous Siol Maoilruain,
A steady host, fierce and prompt;
O’Maoilmuaidh and the generous O’Floinn,
Those two groups received a difficult submission.

Over another little pillar there,
Let us not omit Caille Fothaidh.

# O'DUBHAGAIN.

Let us not spoil their untarnished splendor,
Let us name their three lords.

The Muintir-Birn,<sup>302</sup> brave in the battle-ranks,
In the strongholds of the O'Mannachains.
Through conflict, through vigor and threats,
Theirs is the country into which they came.

The Clann-Branain, powerful in their vigor,
And the majestic O'Maoilmhichils.
The influence of this tribe, which is not weak,
Extends over the wealthy Corca Sheachlann.<sup>303</sup>

The inheritance of the sharp-armed tribe of O hAinlighe,
Is Cinel-Dobhtha,<sup>304</sup> the dense and rugged.
I have an affection in my heart,
For the sept of the O hAinlighes.

Let us leave Cinel-Fechin<sup>305</sup> for a while,
Let us turn our backs on them at once;
They have been weakened for some time,
They have degenerated from their ancestors.

Over the smooth Ciarraighe of the plain,<sup>306</sup>
Is the prudent Mac Ceithearnaigh,<sup>307</sup>
We proclaim their right to you;
Of that tribe is Clann Cheirin.<sup>308</sup>

The Clann-Taidhg,<sup>309</sup> the prosperous Siol-Maoilruana,<sup>310</sup>
A steady, fierce, and active host.
O'Maoilmuaidh and the generous O'Floinn,
The two tribes have gained rule over them.

There is another small corner,
Caille-Fothaidh,<sup>311</sup> let us not omit it,

Lord of white stone cups and spears,
O'Rothlan is their royal chieftain.

Mac Sgaithghil of the beautiful troops,
Is over the populous Corca Mogha.
That flower of honorable beauty,
O'Braoin, is over Loch Gealgora.

Eochaidh of the lineage of the fine Ua Briains,
Your grandfather and your ancestor.
Not slow is the collection of your tribute,
The Clann Maille of the sea's wealth.

Every country is against you there,
Or else stay in the two Umhalls.

There was never a good man
Of the O'Malleys who was not a mariner;
You are prophets of the weather,
A tribe of affection and brotherhood.

Over Conmaicne Chuile, it is heard,
O'Talcharain is mentioned;
Over Conmaicne of the great sea
Is O'Cadhla, a friend of feasting.

The Conmaicne of spirited Dunmore,
Their chieftains are gentle;
A smooth land for the clergy above all others,
From Sidhlinn east to the Shannon.

Meg Conroi is easily found
Over Gno Mor of the small harbors;
O'Hadhnaidh is over the enduring Gno Beag,
A nest that is neither poor nor unstable.

<header>O’Dubhagáin.</header>

The race of Mac Aodha on the eastern side,
Over the wide-plained Clann Cosgraigh;
A gentle host who desires mead,
Beautiful and generous are their tribes.

Clann Murchadha of the beloved sea
Belongs to the valiant Muintir Flaithbheartaigh;
It is necessary to flee from their combat;
They are the guardians of the fair harbors.

Let us approach Aidhne of the horses,
With its nobility and its hospitality;
Let us follow its kings who are not few;
Let us join with the race of noble clans.

Let us mention Aidhne—a duty without exception—
Let us leave the other tribes of Connacht;
Let us speak of its great men;
Let us commemorate the princes of the Ui Fiachrach.

The clan of the holy Mac Giollacheallaigh,
And the O’Heynes <note>Uí Eidhin</note> of the slender, sleek horses;
The protection of their pride rests on their weapons,
From the seed of Guaire of the bright brows <note>Guaire Aidne mac Colmáin</note>.

Good are the warriors where feasts are plentiful,
The O’Clerys and their descendants;
Over pure Cenel Chinngamna
Are the O’Duibhghiollas and their homeland.

The profit of the shore and the tide
Belongs to O’Maghna on the plain of Caonruighe;
Two kings of Cenel Aodha are there;
Let us not pass over O’Shaughnessy.

<meta>This page continues Seán Ó Dubhagáin's topographical poem, moving from the territories of the Hy-Fiachrach into the extensive lands of the Ui Maine, specifically focusing on the regions of Maonmhagh and the families of O'Neachtain, O'Mullally, and the Sodhans.</meta>

<header>O'DUBHAGAIN.</header>

Over the O'Cathails of the learned,
Smooth is their field and their green mountain.

Let us approach Echtge <note>Slieve Aughty</note> of the glens,
Let us visit the extensive land.
Let us place a residence in every townland,
Let us settle in the smooth Maonmhagh,
On the plain of Cara, where the sun is brief;
Our staying is better than our departing.

The great third of Connacht is that plain,
The Ui Maine of that great assembly,
From the Shannon of the royal streams
To Meadha; it is no small kingdom.

O'Connell's portion of that territory,
Of that beautiful, rugged land,
From Grian to the head of the great plain,
A host obeying the royalty.

The kings of Maonmhagh of the tributes,
Whose inheritance is the dark plain;
Two who have possessed that side
Are O'Neachtain and O'Mullally.

Their conflict is heavy in the battles,
For the land as far as the Hy-Fiachrach is theirs.
Let us not pass by the Sodhans,
Their kings are without great error.

Good is the host of the plundering dwellings,
To whom the spear-armed Sodhan is natural:
O'Cathail, the swift O'Mudhroin,
And O'Maolruanaidh of the royal feasts.

Of the Ui-Maine, of great assemblies,
From the Shannon <note>Sionainn</note> of the fairy flood,
To Meadha hill <note>Knockmaa</note>, it is no small kingship.

The share of O'Conaill <note>O'Connell</note> of that territory,
Of that beautiful, uneven land,
Extends from Grian <note>the river Graney</note> to the head of the great plain;
A host obeys the royal chieftain.

The kings of Maonmhagh of the chiefs
To whom the brown plain is hereditary,
Two who have possessed that side,
Are O'Neachtain <note>O'Naghten</note> and O'Maolalaidh <note>O'Mullally</note>.

Their fight is overpowering in conflicts,
Theirs is the land as far as the Ui-Fiachrach <note>Hy-Fiachrach</note>;
The six Sodhans <note>Soghain</note> let us not shun,
Their kings shall not be neglected.

Good is the host of plundering incursions,
To whom the spear-armed array is due:
O'Cathail, the swift O'Mudhroin,
And O'Maoilruanaidh <note>O'Mulrooney</note> of royal banquets.

<summary>This page outlines the tribal territories within the Ui Maine kingdom of Connacht, specifically identifying the rulers of Maonmhagh and the six Sodhans. It names prominent families including O'Neachtain, O'Maolalaidh, O'Conaill, and O'Maoilruanaidh.</summary>

<page-num>70</page-num>
<header>O’DUBHAGAIN.</header>

The protective trees of the fertile marsh-land
Are the kings of Crumhthann <note>Cruffon</note>, a woody territory;
O’Laodhog is a hero who does not retreat,
The King of the broad Caladh <note>a riverside district along the Shannon</note>.

Men by whom were occupied, in their vicinity,
The Caladh of the clear-streamed Shannon;
Renowned and enduring sub-kings
Over the territory of the Anmchadha <note>the Siol Anmchadha or O'Maddens</note>.

The lord of heavy fetters and bright triumphs
Is O’h-Uallachain <note>O'Houlihan or Cuolahan</note> of the deadly weapons;
Speak briefly of those distinguished ones,
Their princes and their primary chiefs.

As the streams of the lands are hereditary,
Mention every district diligently;
The precedence for gentleness and for fame
Belongs to the noble Mac Eitteagain <note>MacEgan</note>.

Describe him for the skill of his warriors,
And for the prosperity of his royal dignity.
The Clann Diarmada <note>the MacDermots of Ui Maine</note>, north and south,
Should be properly included in my poem.

Mac Giolla Fionnagain <note>O'Finnegan</note> the gentle,
And the brave Clann Cionaoith <note>the O'Kennys</note>,
Two parties of beautiful followers
Over the brilliant Clann Laitheamhain.

Noble is the blood in their service,
The handsome O’Domhnallain <note>O'Donnellan</note>,
Who advanced with the force of a flood
Over the yellow-haired Clann Breasail.

<header>O'DUBHAGAIN.</header>

O'Donnchadha without gloom,
Of Ui Cormaic of great Maonmagh,
On the edge of the marshland outside,
With those lords is that territory.

Stationed over brown Inis Duibhginn,
Are the twelve townlands of Ui Duibhginn;
He fought for the land of the chieftains,
The royal leader, brother of a king.

O'Docomlain, I declare,
Is over the high point of the Eirinnach;
No shameful or weak lord is he,
O'Gabhran of Dal Druithne.

Chieftain of strong Magh Finn,
To whom Brigid gave a blessing;
Noble is his active host to this day,
O'Maoil-buain-feardha of Brigid.

Good were the deeds he did for every person,
The fine lord of the Breudach.

<header>O'DUBHAGÁIN.</header>

# THE PART OF OSSORY HERE.

Mac Giollaphátraic <note>Fitzpatrick</note>, and O'Cearbhaill <note>O'Carroll</note>, and O'Donnchadha <note>O'Donoghue</note>, kings of Ossory; O'Bruadair <note>O'Broder</note>, and Mac Brain <note>MacBreen</note>, and O'Braonáin <note>O'Brennan</note>, over the three Cantreds <note>Triucha</note>, namely: the Clanna, and the Comar, and Uí Eirc.

> Let us travel quickly into Leinster,
> A broad land not poor in heroes;
> Ever-famous is the assembly of the men,
> The deed-renowned cemetery of the Gaels.

> I shall reckon now, now,
> The generous princes of the province in one prose,
> A scion with whom we find no enmity,
> We find [him] at Mac Murchadha <note>MacMurrough</note>.

> The king of Ui Enechglais now,
> O'Fiachrach of the vast land,
> Who stood firm through the slaughter of the foreigners,
> And O'Cosgraigh <note>O'Corccraigh</note> on the plain of Cualann.

> O'Riain <note>O'Ryan</note>, king of Ua nDróna,
> More active than the great waves;
> Over Ui Muireadhaigh out there,
> No mere flood is O'Tuathail <note>O'Toole</note>.

> The race of Eochaidh of high valor,
> Over the tribes of the O'Faoláin,
> Mac Gormáin with vigor hither,
> Over the fair land of the Ui Bairche.

> We proceed westward into Ui Failghe <note>Offaly</note>,
> Where the heroic oaks bend,
> Of their laws I speak,
> Of their tribes I remember.

It describes the territories of various clans and their standing during periods of conflict with foreigners.</summary>

<page-num>75</page-num>
<header>O'DUBHAGAIN.</header>

### THE PART RELATING TO OSSORY.

Mac Giollaphatraic <note>Fitzpatrick</note>, O'Cearbhaill <note>O'Carroll</note>, and O'Donnchadha <note>O'Donoghue</note>, kings of Ossory <note>Osraighe</note>; O'Bruadair <note>O'Broder</note>, Mac Brain <note>MacBreen</note>, and O'Braonain <note>O'Brennan</note>, over the three cantreds <note>Triuchas</note>, namely: the Clanns, the Comar, and Ui Eirc.

Let us travel swiftly into Leinster,
The broad land that is not poor in heroes;
Ever-famous is the career of the men,
The cemetery of the courageous Gaels <note>Gaeidhil</note>.

We shall now count in the east
The hospitable chiefs of the province in one outpouring;
A scion with whom we shall find no ill-will,
We shall stay with Mac Murchadha <note>MacMurrough</note>.

King of Ui n-Enechlais in the east,
O'Fiachrach of the vast land;
Held firm through the slaughter of the foreigners,
And O'Cosgraigh on the plain of Cualann.

O'Riain <note>O'Ryan</note>, king of Ui Drona,
Is swifter than the great waves;
Over the Ui Muireadhaigh outside,
The O'Tuathails <note>O'Tooles</note> are not illegitimate.

The Siol-Eochadha <note>the race of Eochaidh</note> of high prosperity
Over the tribes of Ui Faolain;
Mac Gormain with wealth here
On the fair-surfaced land of Ui Bairche.

Let us proceed westwards to Ui Failghe <note>Offaly</note>,
To whom the heroic oaks bend;
I speak of their rights,
And I commemorate their tribes.

<meta>This page begins the supplement by O’Huidhrin to O’Dubhagáin’s topographical poem, shifting the focus to the southern half of Ireland and Leinster after O’Dubhagáin’s treatment of the north.</meta>

<page-num>80</page-num>
<header>O'huidrin.</header>

# O'HUIDRIN.

Additional knowledge of youthful Ireland;
No historian is good who is not an elder.
A correct history from me for the host,
The people from the bright-swan Boyne.

Regarding what other authors have omitted
Of the nobles of fair-grassed Fodhla <note>Ireland</note>,
I shall sing the knowledge of the tribes
From the warm mansion of every lord.

The territory which Conn <note>Conn of the Hundred Battles</note> divided,
O'Dugan <note>John O'Dugan</note> of the dark-brown hair,
He has composed the history of its men;
It is no small task to recount them.

It was not ignorance that caused him
Not to compose the whole from the first day;
However, he has left to me my share,
And he left this race of Cathaoir <note>Cathaoir Mor, ancestor of the kings of Leinster</note>.

Conn's Half <note>Leath Cuinn, the northern half of Ireland</note> of the spirited heart,
That he composed from old books;
He revealed it all, west and east,
Every person as they belong to their native land.

Mogh's Half <note>Leath Mogha, the southern half of Ireland</note>, the rampart of fair Eber,
The two southern thirds of Ireland,
And the plain of Leinster <note>Laighean</note> at that time,
And every good man around the waters of Limerick <note>Luimneach</note>.

<summary>O’Huidhrin introduces his supplement to O’Dugan’s topographical poem, stating his intent to complete the history of Ireland’s noble tribes. He explains that while O’Dugan chronicled the northern half of Ireland (Leath Cuinn), O’Huidhrin will now document the southern half (Leath Mogha) and Leinster.</summary>
<keywords>O'Huidrin, O'Dubhagain, Ireland, Leath Cuinn, Leath Mogha, Leinster, Boyne, Cathaoir Mor, topography, Irish history</keywords>

--- Page 159 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to O’Huidhrin’s supplement, where he establishes his authority as an elder historian and defines his task: to document the territories of the southern half of Ireland (Leath-Mhogha) and Leinster, which were omitted in O’Dugan’s previous work.</meta>

<page-num>81</page-num>
<header>O'HUIDHRIN.</header>
<sig>G</sig>

## O'HUIDHRIN.

An addition of knowledge regarding sacred Ireland <note>Erin</note>,
No historian is good who is not an elder; <note>390</note>
A true history [is provided] by me for the tribes,
The people of the Boyne <note>Boinn, 391</note>, of the fair cattle.

Those whom another author has omitted
Of the nobles of green-grassed Ireland <note>Fodhla, 392</note>
I shall sing:—the knowledge of every tribe
From the warm fort of every lord.

Of this country, which Conn <note>Conn of the Hundred Battles, 393</note> divided,
O'Dugan <note>O'Dubhagain, 394</note>, the bright-eyed and brown-haired,
Composed the history of the men;
It is not a small matter to be mentioned.

It was not ignorance that caused him
Not to compose it from the first day;
Yet, he left to me [to tell of the land] around the Deel <note>Daoil, 395</note>,
He has neglected this race of Cathaoir <note>Cathaoir Mor, 396</note>.

The Half of Conn <note>Leath Cuinn</note> of cheerful heart
He described from ancient books; <note>397</note>
He has shown it all, west and east,
Every man belonging to his ancestral land.

Mogh's Half <note>Leath-Mhogha, 398</note>, the portion of Eber Finn <note>Ebhir Finn</note>,
The two southern thirds of Ireland,
And the plain of Leinster belongs to us,
And every brave man around the Limerick <note>Luimneach, 399</note>.

<summary>O’Huidhrin explains that he is completing the historical record of Ireland’s tribes by focusing on the southern half (Leath-Mhogha) and Leinster. He acknowledges the work of O'Dugan, who chronicled the northern half (Leath Cuinn) but left the history of the southern territories and the race of Cathaoir Mór to be finished by O’Huidhrin.</summary>
<keywords>O’Huidhrin, O'Dugan, Ireland, Leath-Mhogha, Leath Cuinn, Leinster, Boyne, Cathaoir Mor, genealogy, topography</keywords>

<meta>This page begins the detailed topographical survey of the southern half of Ireland, starting with the province of Leinster (the race of Cathaoir Mor) and moving from Dublin westward, identifying the primary lords and chieftains of the region.</meta>

<page-num>82</page-num>
<header>O'HUIDHRIN.</header>

The two provinces of the plain of the Gaels <note>Gaoidhil</note>,
And the province of the race of Cathaoir <note>the descendants of Cathaoir Mor</note>,
The hosts of the Sabhrann <note>likely the River Lee or a poetic name for the southern region</note> from sea to sea,
Let us give them their ancestral land.

The final portion of fair Ireland <note>Fodhla</note>,
Let us set out from the fort of Dublin <note>Dun Duibhlinne</note>,
Westward to the stone-walled Burren <note>Boirinn</note>
Of the fair-landed, victorious territory.

Let us give precedence, according to the chief of the Gaels,
To the royalty of the race of Cathaoir;
Let us speak of them by law in the eastern land,
Every man of them upon his patrimony.

Heir of Nar <note>Arthri Nair, a poetic title for the King of Leinster</note>, the noble tree of the Brog <note>the Brug, a royal seat</note>,
The lord of Leinster is Mac Murrough <note>Mac Murchadha</note>;
The province is held in his bright hand,
The charter of the division belongs to the hero.

The lord of Offaly <note>O’Failghe</note> of the cattle-rich land—
It is not unknown to the poets—
Is O'Connor <note>O'Conchobhair</note>, the leader of the plain,
Upon the green hill of Cruachan <note>Cruachain</note>.

The people of the territory of the broad plain—
Let us pass through their chieftains;
What host is wealthier than those
On the pure and prosperous border of Offaly?

Over Hy-Regan <note>Ui Riagain</note> of the heavy raids,
A swift, fierce, and battling people,
Is O'Dunne <note>O'Duinn</note>, chieftain <note>taoiseach</note> of the siege,
The leader of the battle-spears.

<summary>O’Huidhrin begins his topographical poem of Leinster, identifying Mac Murrough as the chief lord of the province and O'Connor and O'Dunne as the lords of Offaly and Hy-Regan, respectively.</summary>

# O'HUIDHRIN.

In these two provinces of the plain of the Gaels <note>Gaoidhil</note>,
And the province of the race of Cathaoir <note>Cathaoir Mór</note>,
Of the hosts of the Sabhrann <note>likely the River Lee or a poetic name for the southern region</note> from sea to sea,
We shall detail for them their patrimonies.

Toward the south of fair Ireland <note>Fodhla</note>,
Let us pass from the fort of Dublin <note>Dubhlinn</note>,
Westward to the ruggedly fenced Burren <note>Boirinn</note>,
Of white stone fields and active men.

Let us give first place among the chiefs of the Gaels <note>Gaoidhil</note>
To the kings of the race of Cathaoir;
Let us mention separately in the eastern country
Each one of them over his patrimony.

The chief king of Naas <note>Nas</note>, tree of the fort,
The lord of Leinster is Mac Murrough <note>Mac Murchadha</note>;
He holds the sovereignty of the province in his fair hand;
The charter of the lands is held by the hero.

The lord of Offaly <note>Ui-Failghe</note>, the land of cattle—
It is not unknown to poets;
O'Connor <note>O'Conchobhair</note> is the hero of the plain
On the green round hill of Croghan <note>Cruachan</note>.

The tribes of the territory of the extensive plain,
Let us treat of their sub-chiefs <note>tributary lords</note>;
What host is richer than they,
Over the people of Offaly <note>Ui-Failghe</note> of the fair land?

Over Hy-Regan <note>Ui Riagain</note> of heavy routs,
A vigorous tribe who conquer in battle,
Is O'Dunne <note>O'Duinn</note>, chief of demolition,
Hero of the golden battle-spears.

# O'HUIDHRIN.

Another chieftain known to me
Is O'Hennessy <note>O' hAenghusa</note> of Clan Colgan <note>Clár Colgan</note>;
His heritage is pure across the land of Ireland <note>Féin Fháil</note>,
And lies close to the side of Croghan <note>Cruacháin</note>.

Over Tuath-da-mhuighe <note>the territory of the two plains</note> of the white fortress
Is O'Moylan <note>O' Maoilchein</note>, of the wealthy heart;
Delightful is the smooth district of the plain,
At the border, like a promised land.

The bright district of Geashill <note>Géirille</note> has taken
A chieftain from the border of Leinster <note>Laighean</note>;
His fame travels as a strong and swift fame;
O'Amergin <note>O' hAimirgin</note> is his name.

Over Magh Aoife of the warm slopes,
O'Murchain <note>O Murcháin</note> is the chieftain of the district;
The man who dwells in green Figile <note>Fidh Gaibhle</note>—
Where is there a stronger native inheritance?

The Clan Malughra <note>Clann Maoilughra</note> over every troop,
Noble is the rank of their tribe;
A smooth plain is the harbor he protected,
A land hereditary to O'Dempsey <note>O' Díomasaigh</note>.

Delightful is the territory—it has long been heard of—
The district of Lea <note>Tuath Léghe</note> of the bright slopes.
O'Kelly of Lea <note>O' Ceallaigh Leighe</note>, from the eastern shore,
Is the lord of the indented, yew-covered plain.

After Offaly <note>O Fhfailghe</note> of the ancient lands,
Let us move toward Leix <note>Laoighis</note> of Leinster;
Brown-haired heroes who are generous men,
Let us spend a while on their history <note>seanchas</note>.

<header>O'HUIDHRIN.</header>

Another chieftain <note>dynast</note> who is known to me
Is O'Hennessy <note>O'hAenghusa</note> of Clan Colgan <note>Clar-Colgan</note>;
Fine is his country, surpassing even the land of the Fians of Ireland <note>Fians of Fail</note>,
Which borders the greenery of Croghan <note>Cruachan</note>.

Over Tuath-da-mhuighe <note>the territory of the two plains</note> of the fair fortress
Is O'Moylan <note>O'Maoilchein</note> of the generous heart;
Delightful is the smooth district <note>cantred</note> of the plain,
Its border is like the land of promise.

The bright district <note>cantred</note> of Geashill <note>Geisill</note> is possessed
By a chieftain from the border of Leinster,
Whose progress is rapid and strong;
He is named O'Amergin <note>O'hAimirgin</note>.

Over Magh Aoife of the warm slopes
Is O'Murchan <note>O'Murchain</note>, as chieftain <note>dynast</note> of the district <note>cantred</note>,
The hero of the green Figile <note>Fidh-gaibhle</note>;
Against plundering, what country is stronger?

The Clan Malughra <note>Clann-Maoilughra</note> is over every tribe;
Noble is the rank of their race.
This clan <note>sept</note> has defended a smooth plain;
The land is hereditary to O'Dempsey <note>O'Diomosaigh</note>.

Delightful is the territory—it was heard of long ago—
The district <note>cantred</note> of Lea <note>Leghe</note> of the bright slopes;
O'Kelly of Lea <note>O'Ceallaigh of Leghe</note>, of the eastern bank,
Is the sub-chief of the plain of valleys and yews.

After Offaly <note>Ui-Failghe</note> of the ancient lands,
Let us approach Leix <note>Laoighis</note> of Leinster—
Brown-haired heroes for whom the rains fall;
We shall devote some time to their history.

->The great territory of Leix <note>Laoighse</note> of the slender blades,<-

->A fair-haired host of hospitality.<-

->Is like the fruitful land of promise.<-

->Is not foreign to O’Kelly;<-
->The tribe is heavy in the hunt<-
->On the sunny land of Gailine.<-

->By the soft, pool-filled Barrow <note>Bearbha</note>;<-

->Of the race of the melodious Daire Barrach;<-

->Who was quick in the meeting of battle.<-

# O'HUIDHRIN.

Pass across the Barrow <note>Bearbha</note> of the cattle borders,
From the land of corn and rich honey,
From Dinn Righ <note>Dinnrigh</note> to Maistin <note>Maistin</note> the strong,
My journey is rewarded by their nobility.

O'Toole <note>O'Tuathail</note> of the fort of mead,
Is over the active Hy-Murray <note>Ui-Muireadhaigh</note>
To Allen <note>Almhuin</note> of the thrilling music,
Of the fair-topped fruitful grass.

Lord of the green grassy sod
Is the fair Mac Gilla Mo-Cholmóg <note>Mac Giolla Mocholmog</note>,
Free men over men who are not weak,
Over the fair men of Cualann <note>Feara-Cualann</note>.

Other kings obtained the land,
The mast-bearing territory of the plain of Cualann,
O'Cosgrave <note>O'Cosgraigh</note> of the flowing tresses,
Of the triumphant saffron-speckled tribe.

The plain of the Liffey <note>Lifè</note> of the black ships,
A great land of beautiful fruit,
Westward beyond Tara <note>Teamhair</note>, of the house of Conn,
O'Galvan <note>O'Gealbrain</note> is the old tree of the fair land.

O'Teige <note>O'Taidhg</note> found a land under fruit,
Hy-Mail <note>Ui-Mail</note>, a land without eclipse;
O'Kelly <note>O'Ceallaigh</note> is over eastern Hy-Teigh <note>Ui-Teigh</note>,
Which he purchased for his fair-haired tribe.

Over Carbury <note>Cairbre</note> of Leinster of the plains
Is O'Keary <note>O'Ciardha</note> of the red-bladed swords;
The scion of Allen <note>Almhain</note> without scarcity in the east,
By whom battles were kindled around Cruachan <note>Cruachan</note>.

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>90</page-num>
<header>O'hUidrin.</header>

Over the Forthuatha of Leinster <note>Forthuaṫa Laiġean</note> of the slopes,
And over Carbury <note>Cairpre</note> of the red-bladed hosts,
Is the scion from the Boyne <note>Bóinn</note> of Colla and Conn,
Upon whom it is right to share our attention.

Over all the Ui-Inechris <note>Uíḃ Ineċris</note>
Is O'Fiachra <note>O'Fiaċra</note>, prince of Allen <note>Alṁuine</note>;
O'Hea <note>O' hAoḋa</note> is over the Ui-Deaghaidh <note>Uíḃ Deaḋaiġ</note> for me,
For whom the branches whiten as they bend.

O'Muirthe <note>O'Muirte</note> was of great mirth,
Over the fair Cinel-Flaithamhain <note>Cenel finn Flaiṫeaṁain</note>;
Over the Ui-Mealla <note>Uíḃ Mealla</note> of the swift ships,
The man O'Fintire <note>O'Finntiġearn</note> holds a grip.

O'Murchadha <note>O'Murċaḋa</note> obtained a profitable, great lordship
Over a smooth, bright land;
That man obtained the territory of O'Felme <note>Críoċ O Felme</note>,
At the time of the possession of his ancestors.

The northern land of the Ui-Felme <note>huí Felme</note> was found—
A bright land which O'Garvey <note>O'Gairḃíṫṫ</note> took;
The warriors of Tullow <note>Fian Tolċa</note> uniting the tribes,
A host without decay around the region.

Another noble high-tribe,
The Siol-Brain <note>Síol mBrain</note>, the people of the Duffry <note>Duḃṫoire</note>;
They took no share of the Plain of Corc <note>Ċlár Ċuirc</note>,
The trees from the center of the garden.

From the Barrow <note>Ḃearḃa</note> eastward to the Slaney <note>Sláine</note>,
Is the portion of the territory of the Clan-Cosgraigh <note>Ċloinne Cosccraiġ</note>;
The host of Bantry <note>Ḃeanntraiġe</note> of the curling locks,
A law-abiding, very stately company of warriors.

<summary>This page continues the catalog of Leinster's noble families and their territories, identifying chieftains such as O'Fiachra, O'Hea, O'Murchadha, and O'Garvey across regions like Carbury, Tullow, and Bantry. It highlights the ancestral lands and characteristics of these tribes as they lived between the Barrow and Slaney rivers.</summary>

# O'HUIDHRIN.

The Fortuatha <note>Forthuaṫa Laiġean</note> of Leinster of the slopes,
Over Carbury <note>Cairpre</note> of the red-speared hosts,
The tribe from the Boyne <note>Bóinn</note> of Colla and Conn,
For them, the division is rightful.

Over all Ui-Inechrais <note>Uíḃ Ineċris</note>
Is O'Fiachra <note>O'Fiaċra</note>, chief of Allen <note>Alṁuine</note>;
O'Hea <note>O' hAoḋa</note> is over Ui-Deaghaidh <note>Uíḃ Deaḋaiġ</note> for me,
For whom the trees blossom after bending.

O'Muirthe <note>O'Muirte</note> of great mirth
Is over the fair Cinel-Flaitheamhain <note>Cenel finn Flaiṫeaṁain</note>;
Over Ui-Mealla <note>Uíḃ Mealla</note> of swift ships,
The hero O'Fintire <note>O'Finntiġearn</note> holds sway.

A profitable, weighty lordship
Has O'Murchadha <note>O'Murċaḋa</note> of smooth, fair land;
The hero has obtained the territory of Ui-Felme <note>Críoċ O Felme</note>,
In his turn of ancestral possession.

The cold northern tract of Ui-Felme <note>huí Felme</note>—
A fair land has O'Garvey <note>O'Gairḃíṫ</note> obtained;
The warriors of Tullow <note>Fian Tolċa</note> to unite the tribes,
All are without decay throughout the region.

Another high noble tribe
Is the Siol-Brain <note>Síol mBrain</note>, the people of the Duffry <note>Duḃṫoire</note>;
They have not obtained a portion of the plain of Corc <note>Ċlár Ċuirc</note>,
The scions from the middle of the garden.

From the Barrow <note>Ḃearḃa</note> to the Slaney <note>Sláine</note> eastward
Is the extent of the territory of the Clan-Cosgraigh <note>Ċloinne Cosccraiġ</note>;
The host of Bantry <note>Ḃeanntraiġe</note> of curling locks,
The hawk-like, slow-eyed, warlike host.

<summary>This page identifies the prominent chieftains and noble families of Leinster, such as the O'Fiachra, O'Murchadha, and O'Garvey, delineating their ancestral territories. The geographical focus remains on the lands between the Boyne, Barrow, and Slaney rivers.</summary>

# O'HUIDHRIN.

The king of the fierce southern land—
To count him is not difficult—
Is O’Duibhginn <note>O'Duibhghinn</note>, to whom it is due,
Of the host from dark-red Dublin <note>Duibhlinn</note>.

A warrior of Fothart <note>Fotharta</note> of the cairn,
A noble, modest, and gentle leader,
A hero of good deeds with spears,
Is the wealthy prince O’Lorcain <note>O'Lorcáin</note>.

The territory of the tribes, a fair land,
In the region of the brown-surfaced roads;
A harbor of pure hospitality under the sun,
O’Hartghoile <note>O'hArtghoile</note> holds it by right.

Due to O’Riaghain <note>O'Riaghain</note> is a smooth land,
A cantred <note>Tríocha céd</note>—vast is the territory—
Of Ui Drona <note>Uí Drona</note> of the rapid wheels;
Fairer than any neighboring land.

O’Nuallain <note>O'Nualláin</note>, a hero without fault,
The noble, pure high-king of Fotharta <note>Fotharta</note>;
O’Neill <note>O'Néill</note> of fair Moyacomb <note>Magh dá chon</note>,
A rank he has attained from the race of the Gael.

Siol Elaigh <note>Síol Elaigh</note>, the tribe of treasures—
It is right for O’Gaitin <note>O'Gaitín</note> to guard it;
A prince of that noble group, of no foolish affection,
Is O’Dunlaing <note>O'Dunlaing</note>, hero of the Lagan <note>an Lagáin</note>.

Let us pass across the Barrow <note>Bearbha</note> of the ancient streams,
After leaving the warriors of Leinster <note>Laighean</note>,
To the harbor of the level land of my heart,
To the beautiful host of Ossory <note>Osruighe</note>.

# O'HUIDHRIN.

Lord of the fine Fearann-deisertach <note>Fearann-deisertach</note>
Which is not unworthy to be mentioned,
To O'Duibhginn <note>O'Duibhghinn</note> it is hereditary,
The host from the black pool of fair bushes.

Hero of Fothart <note>Fotharta</note> of the cairn <note>Carn</note>,
A stately, modest, and polished youth;

Land of the sod of brown berries,

Hereditary to O'Riaghain <note>O'Riaghain</note> of smooth land

O'Neill <note>O'Néill</note> of fair Magh dá chon <note>Moyacomb</note>,

# O'HUIDHRIN.

Mac Giollaphadraig <note>Mac Giollapaṫruicc</note> of the fort of Bregia,
The land of Ossory <note>Osraiġe</note> is his by right,
From Slieve Bloom <note>Blaḋma</note> out to the sea,
Brave is his battle over the battalions.

The sub-kings and mighty chiefs
Let us speak of under the hero of Liathdruim <note>Liaṫdroma</note>,
From the Barrow <note>Bearḃa</note> to the smooth part of Munster <note>Muṁan</note>,
United with the king of Tara <note>Teaṁra</note>.

High chief of the fruitful territory,
From the pleasant woods of Oughteragh <note>Coill Uaċtoraiġ</note>,
Is O’Delany <note>O'Duḃṡláine</note>, a generous man,
From the mountain of the most beautiful estuary.

O’Carroll <note>O'Cearḃaill</note> for whom trees were reddened,
O’Donoghoe <note>O'Donnċaḋa</note> of the stately face,
Mighty hosts upon the fruitful land,
They are two kings of the same path.

Beside the Barrow <note>Bearḃa</note> of the fruitful bank,
The king of the territory of whom you have heard,
A manly choice over Magh Mail <note>Maiġ Mail</note>,
Is the pure O’Donoghoe <note>O'Donnċaḋa</note> of Gowran <note>Gaḃráinn</note>.

From Kilkenny <note>Cill Chainniġ</note> of the limestones
To Slieve O’Caithle <note>Sliaḃ O'Caiṫle</note> of the fair-surfaced hill,
Is Cluain-Ui-Cearbhaill <note>Cluain Uí Cearḃaill</note>, a white smooth plain,
Land of the blue-headed, winding water.

Of Ui-Duach <note>Uí Duaċ</note> of Ossory of the warm land,
The fair wide plain of the Nore <note>An Ḟeoir</note>,
The wood of the plain is not to be divided;
Its guarantor is O’Brennan <note>O'Braonáin</note>.

### O'HUIDHRIN.

To Mac Giollaphadraig <note>Mac Giollaphatraic</note> of the Bregian fort,
The land of Ossory <note>Osraighe</note> is due,
From Slieve Bloom <note>Bladhma</note> out to the sea,
Brave is his battle over the battalions.

Sub-chiefs and mighty chiefs
I mention under the hero of Liathdruim,
From the Barrow <note>Bearbha</note> to the plain of Munster,
To the king of Tara it belongs to unite them.

The high chief of the fruitful <term>cantred</term>,
Of the delightful Coill Uachtorach,
Is O'Delany <note>O'Dubhshlaine</note>, a hospitable man,
From the mountain of the most beautiful rivers.

O'Carroll <note>O'Cearbhaill</note> for whom trees are red,
O'Donoghoe <note>O'Donnchadha</note> of honest appearance,
Whose rock-like hosts possess the fruitful land,
Are two kings of the same territory.

Near the Barrow <note>Bearbha</note> of the fruitful border,
The king of the district you have heard of,
It is he who is elected over Magh Mail,
O'Donoghoe of fine Gowran <note>Gabhran</note>.

From Kilkenny <note>Cill Chainnigh</note> of the limestones
To Sliabh gCaithle of the fine sloping hill
Is the plain of O'Carroll <note>O'Cearbhaill</note> for whom the sea is smooth,
Land of the green, rich, grassy carpet.

Ui Duach of Ossory <note>Osraighe</note> of the warm soil,
The fair wide plain of the Nore <note>Feoir</note>,
The wood of the plain is not easily passable,
Its protecting chief is O'Brennan <note>O'Braonain</note>.

### O'HUIDHRIN.

Mac Broin of the well-ordered land,
Of the clans I remember,
A bright land of long shelter, with pleasant nuts,
From the border on Magh Raoir of Sedna.

In Magh Lacha of the warm slopes
Is O’Phelan <note>O'Faoiláin</note>, a manly tribe;
A great country is their rightful due,
The race of O’Phelan has filled it.

Upon Magh Airbh, a fitting mention,
Is O'Coveney <note>O'Caibhdeanaigh</note> of the wooded plain;
The head of every assembly is that fair chief,
At the head of Coille O'Catharaigh.

O'Gloran <note>O'Gloirinn</note>, a swift branch, obtained
A <term>cantred</term> of sweet territory,
A smooth land around the gentle Callan,
A land without blemish for a royal seat.

Ui Bearchon of the yellow mantle,
The king of the district is O'Kealy <note>O'Caolluidhe</note>;
The plain of the host where the harvest is heavy,
The land by the bright-watered Barrow <note>Bearbha</note>.

The king of Ui Eirc of the slender steeds
Is O'Broder <note>O'Bruadair</note>, a majestic tree;
A sandy territory of heavy floods,
Like the salmon-abounding land of Maonmhagh.

Having explored Ossory <note>Osraighe</note> of the fair land,
And completed the description of the race of Cathaoir,
It is no wonder to travel now to the Suir <note>Siuir</note>,
Westward to the fair, noble plain of Magh Feimin.

<summary>This page lists the subordinate lords of southern Ossory, including O'Phelan, O'Coveney, O'Gloran, O'Kealy, and O'Broder, describing their fertile lands along the Barrow and Callan rivers before turning toward the Suir.</summary>

### O'HUIDHRIN.

Mac Braoin<sup>498</sup> of the firm land
Is over the clans I commemorate;
A fine district of good acorns,
O'Broithe<sup>499</sup> <note>O'Brophy</note> over free Magh Sedna.

In Magh Lacha<sup>500</sup> of the warm hill slopes
Is O'Faolain<sup>501</sup> <note>O'Phelan</note> of a manly tribe;
Extensive is the district due to them,
Which the O'Faolains have filled.

Over Magh Airbh<sup>502</sup> whom I now mention,
Is O'Caibhdeanaigh<sup>503</sup> <note>O'Coveney</note> of the wooded plain;
The head of every assembly is the steady chief
At the head of Coill O'gCathasaigh.<sup>504</sup>

O'Gloiairn,<sup>505</sup> <note>O'Gloran</note> the fruitful branch, has obtained
A <term>cantred</term> of a sweet country,
A smooth land along the beautiful Callann,<sup>506</sup> <note>Callan</note>
A land without a single blemish.

Of Ui Bearchon<sup>507</sup> of the yellow mantle,
The king of the territory is O'Caollaidhe;<sup>508</sup> <note>O'Kealy</note>
The plain of the tribe who return heavily,
Is the land over the bright-flowing Barrow <note>Bearbha</note>.<sup>509</sup>

The king of Ui-Eirc<sup>510</sup> <note>Iverk</note> of slender steeds
Is O'Bruadair,<sup>511</sup> <note>O'Broder</note> scion of the flood;
A sandy territory of heavy floods,
Like the open land of Maonmhagh.<sup>512</sup>

After having visited Ossory <note>Osraighe</note> of beautiful land,
After having encompassed the clans of Cathaoir,
Let us pass (and do not wonder at it) to the Suir,<sup>513</sup>
Westward to the fair, rich Magh Feimhin.<sup>514</sup>

<page-num>98</page-num>
### O'HUIDHRIN.

Our visit is to Cashel of the kings,
Following the track of Corc <note>Corc, King of Munster</note> who loved no misdeed;
The story of our journey is like a doctor's,
Concerning the ancient, the faces, and the adventures.

Let us manfully mention every warrior
Across the plain of Cashel of the hospitable wall,
A territory of hazel and strong embankments;
We are not uninformed about them.

Let us give precedence to its two own territories,
To Cashel of the smooth, level plain;
Corca Athrach is its name,
A bright assembly of fame above the territories.

The chief of the territory who is here,
Near Cashel of the brown-headed lands,
With the new luster of that rank,
Is O'Healaithe <note>O'Healy</note> of the household of Cashel.

The great high-kings of Magh Fail <note>Plain of Tal</note>,
Around Cashel of the white territory,
We shall not hide it from anyone:
Prosperity without flaw, without lack.

High-king over all Ireland was
Brian, the hero of the Boru;
From the territory of Cashel he arose,
A tribe of the blessing of the Tailginn <note>A name for St. Patrick</note>.

The lords of Munster of the Shannon’s wall,
The descendants of Eoghan, son of Oilill;
Mac Carthaigh <note>Mac Carthy</note> is the one who bears the tribute,
Like an adventurous wave of the sea.

<summary>This page describes the royal seat of Cashel and its surrounding territories, acknowledging the high-kingship of Brian Boru and the lineage of the Mac Carthys of Munster.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the topographical description of the territories surrounding Cashel and the leading families of Munster, focusing on the lineage of Brian Boru and the Mac Carthys.</meta>

<page-num>99</page-num>
# O'HUIDHRIN.

Our visit shall be to Cashel of the kings, <note>515: Cashel, the royal seat of Munster</note>
The seat of Corc, <note>516: Corc mac Luigthig, an early King of Munster</note> who practiced no evil deeds:
The story of our adventure, when unfolded,
Will predict prosperity, luck, and success.

Let us mention from now on every hero
On the plain of Cashel of firm ramparts,
A fruitful, wooded country belonging to the chief fortress;
We are not ignorant of them.

Let us give the first place to its own territory,
To Cashel of the smooth, clear plain;
Corca Athrach <note>517: The ancient name for the plain surrounding Cashel</note> is its name;
Fine are its battalion and its march through the districts.

The ruler of the district that is here,
Under Cashel of the territories of brown nuts,
Is a fresh, bright, and gentle scion—
A wreath upon the head of Cashel.

The chief princes of the great plain of Tal, <note>518: Magh Tal, a poetic name for the territory of the Dal gCais or Munster</note>
Around Cashel of the fair territory—
I will not conceal that from anyone;
A cause without flaw or defect.

High king over all Ireland
Was Brian, <note>519: Brian Boru</note> hero of the cattle-tribute,
Who will prevail over the territory of Cashel—
A house built over the relics of the Tailgenn. <note>520: A name for St. Patrick</note>

The chiefs of Munster of the fortress of the Shannon,
Descendants of Eoghan, son of Oilioll, <note>521: Oilioll Olum, ancestor of the Eoghanacht and Dal gCais</note>
Mac Carthaigh <note>522: MacCarthy</note> is the hero of their law,
Like a stormy, inexhaustible sea.

# O'HUIDHRIN.

> The Eoghanacht of Cashel of the plain of Cian, <note>523: A territory of the Eoghanacht lineage in Tipperary</note>
> O’Donoghue is the rightful heir to it;
> Feimin <note>524: Magh Feimin, the great plain in south Tipperary</note> was its name at another time,
> Linked to the harbor of the yellow nuts.

> Sliabh Ardagh <note>525: Now the barony of Slievardagh, County Tipperary</note> of the fair land,
> Is due to O’Deaghaidh <note>526: O'Day or O'Dea</note> as his native territory;
> Mention the people of the head of the plain,
> O’h-Oilella and the sweet O’Brachain.

> Two fair kings, I do not conceal it,
> Rule over the Deisi, <note>527: The Decies, an ancient territory in Waterford and Tipperary</note> I affirm:
> O’Bric, who surpassed all in tribute,
> And the wise O’Faelain.

> O’Mearaidhig, a good king,
> Is lord of O'Fathaidh, <note>528: Now the barony of Iffa and Offa West, Tipperary</note> who obtained a great land;
> The O’Neills of Ui Eoghan Finn—
> All these lions I mention.

> O’Flanagan obtained the land
> Of Uachtar Tire, <note>529: Upperthird, a barony in County Waterford</note> a rich brown land;
> A land of enduring, fruitful appearance,
> Under a varied green cloak.

> Ui Athele <note>530: A territory in County Waterford near the coast</note> as far as the sea,
> He took that by the hardship of combat;
> A smooth-skinned hero in the heat of battle—
> The O’Breisleins are truly mentioned.

> The O’Fodhladhas are to the east for us,
> Among the fresh-foliaged woods;
> O’Cein from the mead-drinking Mahon <note>531: The River Mahon in County Waterford</note>
> Will advance a step beyond other tribes.

# O'HUIDHRIN.

Eoghanacht of Cashel <note>523: A territory of the Eoghanacht lineage in Tipperary</note> of the plain of Cian,
  O'Donoghue is its hereditary chieftain;
It is also called Feimin,
  Uniter of the tribe of yellow nuts.

Sliabh Ardagh <note>524: Now the barony of Slievardagh, County Tipperary</note> of the fine land
  Is hereditary to O'Dea <note>525: O'Deaghaidh, also modernized as O'Day or O'Dea</note> as a patrimony;
Septs of the tribe of the head of the plain
  Are O’h-Oilella <note>526: Modernized as O'Haly or Healy</note> and the melodious O'Brachain. <note>527: Modernized as Bracken</note>

Two fair kings, I do not conceal them,
  Over the Deisi <note>528: The Decies, an ancient territory in Waterford and Tipperary</note> I assert,
Are O'Bric, <note>529: Now Brick</note> who has exceeded every tribe,
  And the fair, wise O'Faelain. <note>530: Modernized as Phelan or Whelan</note>

O'Meara, <note>531: O'Mearadhaigh, modernized as O'Meara</note> the good king,
  Chief of Ui Fathaidh, <note>532: Now the barony of Iffa and Offa West, Tipperary</note> who obtained great land,
The O'Neills of Ui-Eoghain Finn, <note>533: A territory in the barony of Iffa and Offa West</note>
  All these lions I mention.

O'Flanagan obtained the land,
  Uachtar-tire, <note>534: Now Upperthird, a barony in County Waterford</note> a land of brown berries,
A land of most lasting fruitful soil
  Under a clothing of variegated green.

Ui Athele <note>535: A territory in County Waterford near the coast</note> to the sea
  Was obtained by hardihood of conflict,
By scions of smooth skin to fight the battle,
  The O'Breslens south-east to the sea.

The O'Foleys <note>536: O'Fodhladhas, modernized as Foley</note> it is fitting for us
  To mention, of the scions of rich hair;
O'Keane <note>537: O'Cein, modernized as Keane</note> from the mead-abounding Mahon, <note>538: The River Mahon in County Waterford</note>
  They will exceed all tribes in fame.

# O'HUIDHRIN.

The land of Ui Eachach <note>537: A territory belonging to a branch of the southern Ui Eachach, located here within the borders of the Decies</note>, pleasant is that,
  The southern part of the island of Fail <note>538: Inis Fail, a poetic name for Ireland</note> is its boundary;
O'Bric <note>539: Now modernized as Brick</note> chooses it across the wave,
  From Lic Logha <note>540: An unidentified landmark in the Decies</note> to Liathdruim. <note>541: Now Leitrim, a townland in the barony of Decies-without-Drum</note>

The lord of Fir Maighe <note>542: Now the barony of Fermoy, County Cork</note> of the peaked ramparts,
  Is O'Dubhagain <note>543: Now O'Dugan or Duggan</note> of Dun Manann;
A kindred race of prosperous success,
  And O'Caoimh <note>544: Now O'Keeffe</note>, a branch from Gleannomhain. <note>545: Now Glanworth, the ancient seat of the O'Keeffes in Fermoy</note>

The King of Ui Liathain <note>546: Now the baronies of Barrymore and Kinnatalloon, County Cork</note>, a hero of fame,
  A hardy piece of the battles of Munster;
He is head of the Ui Anmchadha <note>547: A sept of the Ui Liathain</note> by right of heredity,
  A thin-armed host of noble excellence.

A fine host in strong pursuit
  Over Ui Mac Caille <note>548: Now the barony of Imokilly, County Cork</note> of the banquets;
Two tribes are in that smooth plain,
  The Ui Breagha and the Ui Gloinn Glaisrin. <note>549: Ancient septs located within the barony of Imokilly</note>

Ciarraighe Chuirche <note>550: Now the barony of Kerrycurrihy, County Cork</note> of the bright harbor,
  Belongs to the race of Torna in that land;
O'Cuirche <note>551: Now modernized as Curcy or Quirk</note> obtained the warm land,
  Occupying it like the smooth plain of Meath.

Over Cineal Aedha <note>552: Now the barony of Kinalea, County Cork</note> of the warm soil
  Is O'Ceallachain <note>553: Now O'Callaghan</note> of the plain of Beare;
A green land of bright-sunlit pools,
  A field of wide-stretching harbors.

Cineal mBeci <note>554: Now the barony of Kinmeaky, County Cork</note> of the land of cattle,
  Around the white-wooded Bandon <note>555: The River Bandon</note>;
A man who is a champion from the swift Muaidh,
  Is O'Mathghamhna <note>556: Now O'Mahony</note> of the white-foamed harbor.

<summary>This page details the Gaelic lords and territories of southern Ireland, specifically focusing on the O'Bric, O'Dugan, O'Keeffe, and O'Mahony families across areas such as Fermoy, Imokilly, and Kinalea.</summary>

# O’HUIDHRIN.

The delightful land of Ui-Eachach <note>539: Now the barony of Ivagha, in the southwest of County Cork</note>,
The south of the woody Inis Fail <note>540: A poetic name for Ireland</note>,
O’Bric <note>541: Now modernized as Brick</note> selects it across the flood,
From Lec Logha <note>542: An unidentified landmark in the territory of the Decies</note> to Liathdruim <note>543: Now Leitrim, a townland in the barony of Decies-without-Drum</note>.

The lord of Feara-muighe <note>544: Now the barony of Fermoy, County Cork</note> of smooth mounds,
O’Dubhagain <note>545: Now O'Dugan or Duggan</note> of Dun-Manann <note>546: Now Dunmahon in the barony of Fermoy</note>,
A tribe of relations of prosperous wealth,
O’Caoimh <note>547: Now O'Keeffe</note>, a branch of Gleannomhain <note>548: Now Glanworth</note>.

The King of Ui-Liathain <note>549: Now the baronies of Barrymore and Kinnatalloon</note>, a hero of renown,
Hardy divisions of the battalion of Munster;
The head of the O'Anmchadhas <note>550: A sept of the Ui Liathain</note> is its rightful chief,
A host of thin-edged arms of the best nobility.

A fine tribe strong in pursuit
Is over Ui Mac Caille <note>551: Now the barony of Imokilly, County Cork</note> of the drinking;
Two tribes are in the smooth plain,
The Ui Breaghdhas <note>552: An ancient sept in Imokilly</note>, and the fine O’Glaisins <note>553: Now O'Glassin or Gleeson</note>.

Ciarraighe Chuirche <note>554: Now the barony of Kerrycurrihy, County Cork</note> of the bright harbor,
To the race of Torna <note>555: The descendants of Torna Eigeas, a celebrated poet</note> this land belongs.
O’Cuirre obtained the warm land,
Of a level like the plain of Meath.

Over Cinel-Aedha <note>556: Now the barony of Kinalea, County Cork</note> of the warm land
Is O’Ceallachain <note>557: Now O'Callaghan</note> of the plain of Bearra <note>558: Now the barony of Bear, though the O'Callaghans were later located in Duhallow</note>,
A land of green pools with white bottoms;
A land of the widest harbors.

Cinel m-Bece <note>559: A territory now in the barony of Kinmeaky</note> of the land of cattle,
Around the Bandain <note>560: The River Bandon</note> of fair woods,
The most warlike man from the rapid Muaidh <note>561: Referring to a river, likely the River Moy used poetically, or a local stream</note>,
Is O’Mathghamhna <note>562: Now O'Mahony</note> of the harbor of white foam.

->To the race of Lugaid <note>557: The Corca Laidhe, descendants of Lugaid mac Itha</note> near the wave,<-
->Here I am traveling across the boundary;<-
->To pass over these people would not be right for me,<-
->But rather to share the fame of the warriors.<-

---

->O’Driscoll, high-king of the land,<-
->Of Corca Laidhe <note>558: An ancient territory in southwest Cork, now roughly the Diocese of Ross</note> I speak,<-
->He took possession of the harbor of Clear <note>559: Cape Clear Island</note>,<-
->A difficult path for the royal line.<-

---

->The O'Flynns of Arda <note>560: Now the parish of Ardfield in the barony of Ibane and Barryroe</note> on fresh woody land,<-
->A band of bright-armed warriors;<-
->Every man of his stock is a noble prince,<-
->The O'Baghamnas <note>561: An ancient sept in the same region, whose name is preserved in the barony of Ibane</note> inhabit there.<-

---

->The king of the swift Middle Cantred <note>562: Now the barony of East Carbery</note>,<-
->O’Coffey of the bright-surfaced harbor,<-
->The land of Cliodhna <note>563: Tonn Chliodhna, the "Wave of Cliodhna" in Glandore Harbor</note>, the plain of O’Coffey,<-
->An enemy in battle to the foreigners.<-

---

->Muintir Baire <note>564: Now the parish of Kilcrohane, also known as Muintirwara</note> of the bright mansion,<-
->Of the descendants of Fothadh Airphtheach <note>565: A legendary 3rd-century High King of Ireland</note>,<-
->O'Baire is over the land of the wave,<-
->What is fairer than the smooth land of Manainn <note>566: A district name within Muintir Baire</note>?<-

---

->O’Driscoll of Beare <note>567: Now the barony of Bear, County Cork</note>, the noble,<-
->Over Beare of the salmon-filled coast,<-
->A harbor that was in the green branched sea,<-
->Under the widespread fame of the wine-fleet.<-

---

->In the manner of the race of Lugaid,<-
->And the loyal land of the West Munstermen,<-
->Let us leave the whole land of Ith <note>568: A poetic name for the territory of Corca Laidhe, named after the uncle of Milesius</note>,<-
->The territories of the hazels with yellow nuts.<-

To the race of Lughaidh <note>563: The Corca Laidhe, descendants of Lughaidh mac Itha</note> near the sea,
Here I pass over the boundary;
It does not befit me to pass these people by,
But to describe the renown of the heroes.

O’Driscoll <note>564: O’h-Eidirsceoil</note>, chief king of the land,
Of Corca Laidhe <note>565: Corca Laighdhe, an ancient territory in southwest Cork</note> I speak,
He took possession of the harbor of Clear <note>566: Cape Clear</note>,
The most steadfast pillar of the kings.

The O’Flynns of Arda <note>567: Ardfield, in the barony of Ibane and Barryroe</note> of the green woods,
A tribe of illustrious lineage;
Every man of their host has the making of a chief;
These are the O'Baghamnas <note>568: Ui-Baghamhna</note>.

King of the vigorous Middle Cantred <note>569: Tricha medhonach, now the barony of East Carbery</note>
Is O’Coffey <note>570: O’Cobbthaigh</note> of the white-stone harbor;
Land of Cliodhna <note>571: Tonn Chliodhna, the Wave of Cliodhna in Glandore Harbor</note>, the plain of O’Coffey,
An enemy in battle to foreigners.

Muintir-Bhaire <note>572: Now the parish of Kilcrohane, also known as Muintirwara</note> of the fair fort,
Of the race of the warlike Fothadh <note>573: Fothadh Airphtheach</note>;
O’Baire <note>574: O'Baire, a sept of the Corca Laidhe</note> is over this land of the sea;
Is the plain of Manainn <note>575: A district within Muintir-Bhaire</note> any fairer?

O’Driscoll of Beare <note>576: Bearra, now the barony of Bear</note>, the good,
Over Beare of the salmon-filled coast;
The harbor of Baoi <note>577: Likely Bantry Bay or the waters near Dunmanus</note>, where the branching sea is green,
Is under his extensive fleet of wine.

After treating the race of Lughaidh <note>578: The Corca Laidhe</note>,
And the rightful land of Desmond,
Let us leave entirely the land of Ith <note>579: The territory of the Corca Laidhe, named for their ancestor Ith</note>,
Territories of yellow hazel nuts.

It marks the transition away from the lands of the race of Ith towards the rest of Desmond.</summary>

->Let us start our pursuit in the west,<-

<page-num>111</page-num>

# O'HUIDHRIN.

The territory of O'Donnagrain is certain,
It is the great Muscraighe of the Three Plains <note>605</note>,
With the host of the flock-abounding Iarann <note>606</note>,
Host of the sunny land of vowed deeds.

Tuath-Saxan <note>607</note> of the fair district,
I mention for O'h-Ionmhainen;
A beautiful territory of abundant crops,
A hardy section of the race of Conaire <note>608</note>.

The mighty Muscraighe Treithirne <note>609</note>
Is hereditary to O'Cuirc, as a just man;
O'Maoilbloghain <note>610</note>, important in the territory,
Has tilled the land of fine sods.

O'Carthaigh's <note>611</note> just share
Is Muscraighe <note>612</note> of the west of Feimhin;
Fort of the chessmen, pleasant until now,
A saying often said of it.

Over the warm Muscraighe-tire <note>613</note>
Are two dynasts of the best nobility;
O'Donghalaigh <note>614</note> and O'Fuirg <note>615</note> also,
Of the fresh plains of the flowery, smooth border.

Two cantreds <note>A territorial unit, from the Irish tricha céd</note>, we remember,
The two flowery Corca-Baiscinns <note>616</note>,
Which are hereditary to the Muinter Domhnaill <note>617</note>,
A host who divide the territory.

Another sub-king of this land of flocks—
Sun-bright is his genealogy—
Is O'Baiscinn <note>618</note>, a tree over the Boinn <note>619</note>,
A tribe who traverse every hosting.

<summary>This page identifies the hereditary rulers of several Muskerry territories and the two cantreds of Corca Baiscinn, naming families such as O’Cuirc, O’Carthaigh, and O’Donnell. It describes these lands through their agricultural richness, noble lineages, and historical associations.</summary>

<keywords>O'Huidhrin, Muscraighe, Corca Baiscinn, O'Cuirc, O'Carthaigh, O'Donnell, O'Baiscinn, Irish topography, Gaelic lordships, genealogy</keywords>

<page-num>112</page-num>

# O'HUIDHRIN.

Lord O’Bracain of the satin robes,
A chief of mighty gatherings;
O’Maolcorcra of swift fame,
From the banks of the two estuaries <note>Likely the Shannon and the Fergus</note>.

The two territories of all Pochla <note>A district in Corca Baiscinn</note>,
Belong to the valiant race of Conaire;
Around the harbor of the moist plain—it is no lie—
It is O’Kelly's right to protect them.

Let us leave the race of Conaire of Cliach,
The kings of the Erainn <note>The Erainn or Ernai tribe</note> of the golden shields;
A flood in our path is the host of Fergus <note>The River Fergus in County Clare</note>,
Stepping forth from the breast of guardianship.

The King of Kerry <note>Ciarraighe Luachra in North Kerry</note> of the children of Ciar,
O’Connor is the rightful lord of it;
Lord of the plain of the swift mead-river,
From the shore to the bright-streaming Shannon.

O’Laoghain, a hero of renown,
We found over the Ui Fearba <note>A territory in the barony of Clanmaurice, Kerry</note>;
O’Caithneanaigh obtained the land,
Hardy beneath the battle-peaks of Cualann.

The Ui Flannannain, broad is their land,
A green country of most beautiful streams;
O’Duibhdhuin is over the warm land
As king, and his heart is set upon it.

All the Alltraighe <note>A tribe in Kerry, ancestors of St. Brendan</note>
Belong to the king of the plain of Kerry;
A tribe of prosperity in the best of hospitality,
The O’Neidhes and the race of Conaire.

# O'HUIDHRIN.

The chief of Ui-Bracain <note>Ibrickan, in County Clare</note> of the satin cloaks,
A chieftain of mighty gatherings,
O’Maolcorcra <note>A family of the Corca Baiscinn</note> of swift fame,
From the banks of the two estuaries <note>The "inbhers" likely refer to the Shannon and the Fergus</note>.

The two clans of all the Fochla <note>A district within Corca Baiscinn</note>,
Of the brave race of Conaire,
Around the harbor of the moist plain—truly—
It is O’Kelly’s <note>O'Ceallaigh of Corca Baiscinn</note> heritage to guard them.

Let us leave the race of Conaire of Cliach <note>A territory in County Limerick</note>,
The kings of the Erainn <note>The Ernai or Erainn tribe</note> of the golden shields;
Let us turn our attention to the race of Fergus <note>The descendants of Fergus Mac Roich, the Ciarraighe</note>,
It is a step required as a just debt.

The King of Kerry <note>Ciarraighe Luachra in North Kerry</note> over the clans of Ciar,
O’Connor—it is right for him to be so—
Is the chief of the land where mead abounds,
From the shore to the fair-streamed Shannon.

O’Laoghain <note>O’Leyne or Lane</note>, a hero of renown,
We have found over the Ui-Fearba <note>A territory in the barony of Clanmaurice, Kerry</note>;
O’Caithneannaigh obtained the land,
Sturdy beneath the battle-peaks of Cualann.

Ui-Flannannain, an extensive land,
A great country of delightful streams;
O’Duibhduin is over the warm land,
He is its king, and his heart is set upon improving it.

All the Alltraighe <note>A tribe in Kerry, ancestors of St. Brendan</note> return
Two kings of the plain of Kerry;
A tribe which is ready in times of difficulty,
The O’Neidhes and the Clann-Conaire.

# O'HUIDHRIN.

Muintir Diocolla <note>The family of Diocolla</note>, it is their right,
In Corcomroe <note>A territory now forming the baronies of Corcomroe and Burren in County Clare</note> of the battle-host masses;
O’Maoileitigh <note>O'Mulleity or O'Melady</note> was a generous protector,
Who never refused to follow his hereditary duty.

The lands around fair Sliabh Eithri <note>Likely Slieve Elva in the Burren</note>,
Over the sweet-streamed Cenel Sedna;
A family that strengthened their dominion,
Over their native land is O’Draighnean <note>O'Drinan</note>.

The cantred of the men of Arda-an-oir <note>“Height of the gold,” possibly Ardnagor in Corcomroe</note>,
In Corcomroe of the battle-host masses;
O’Connor obtained the land,
A hero from the beautiful Cullenagh <note>Likely the river Cullenagh or the surrounding district</note>.

O’Loughlin, a hero in battles,
Rules over the soft, dewy-sheltered Burren;
Over Tealach Chuirc <note>A territory in the Burren</note> through his victories,
A place of cattle and great wealth.

The Dal Meadruaidh <note>The tribal name for the people of Corcomroe</note>, the youths of Macha,
Noble are those high-lords;
Along with the race of musical Ciar <note>Ciar, the ancestor of the Ciarraighe</note>,
They are knights like battle-lions.

From the Clann Tail <note>A name for the Dalcassians or the race of Tal</note> let us turn westward,
To the province of the children of Macnia <note>Munster, or specifically the Desmond region</note>;
From the host that excelled at Cruachan,
Our path is right to go to ancient Luachair <note>Sliabh Luachra, a mountainous district on the borders of Kerry, Cork, and Limerick</note>.

The plain of Luachair is a fertile land,
Belonging to the stern O’Donoghue;
Tribes sturdy in the hardship of battle,
A white-cloaked and well-watered host.

->I 2<-

To Muintir Diocholla <note>The family of Diocolla</note> is due
Corcumruadh <note>Corcomroe, a territory now forming the baronies of Corcomroe and Burren in County Clare</note> of the fiery battle hosts,
O’Maoileitigh <note>O'Mulleity or O'Melady</note> of the hospitable seat,
Who has not refused to contest his right.

The lands around fair Sliabh Eisi <note>Likely Slieve Elva in the Burren</note>
In the sweet-streamed Cinel-Sedna <note>A tribal district in Corcomroe</note>,
A tribe who has united its people;
Of their country is O’Draighnen <note>O'Drinan</note>.

The cantred of Feara Arda <note>“Men of the Heights,” possibly Ardnagor in Corcomroe</note> of gold,
Corcumruadh of the fiery battle hosts,
O’Conchobhair <note>O'Connor of Corcomroe</note> obtained the land,
The hills of beautiful Conach <note>Likely the river Cullenagh or the surrounding district</note>.

O’Lochlainn <note>O'Loughlin, chief of the Burren</note>, a hero over battalions,
Rules over the soft, dew-scattering Boirinn <note>The barony of Burren in North Clare</note>,
Over Tealach Chuirc <note>A territory in the Burren</note> by right,
Of the cattle and wealth-abounding port.

Dal Meadhruaidh <note>The tribal name for the people of Corcomroe</note>, hosts of Macha <note>A reference to the royal site of Emain Macha, signifying heroic youths</note>,
Nobler than the high chieftains,
Together with the race of musical Ciar <note>Ciar, the ancestor of the Ciarraighe or Kerry people</note>,
Are over the knightly host of embattled lions.

From the race of Tal <note>The Dalcassians, or the race of Cas</note> let us turn westwards <note>Turning toward the west of Munster</note>
To the province of the race of Maicniadh <note>The territory associated with the Eoghanacht or specifically Desmond</note>,
From the host who prevailed over Cruachan <note>The royal seat of Connacht, here used to describe the power of the clans</note>
It is right to proceed to old Luachair <note>Sliabh Luachra, a mountainous district on the borders of Kerry, Cork, and Limerick</note>.

The plain of Luachair <note>The fertile district of Sliabh Luachra</note>, land of produce,
Belongs to the beautiful O’Dunadhaigh <note>O'Dunadhaigh, likely O'Donoghoe or O'Dunaigh</note>,
Tribes of hardy battle,
A fair-surfaced, moist district.

# O’HUIDHRIN.

O’Donnchadha <note>O'Donohoe</note> of Loch Lein <note>Lough Leane, Killarney</note>,
King of the Eoghanacht of Ireland <note>Specifically the Eoghanacht Locha Lein</note>,
O’Cearbhaill <note>O'Carroll</note> is that friend of ours,
The fair branch of the bright-stranded territory.

O’Caoimh <note>O'Keeffe</note> of the righteous brown brow,
Lord of Urluachra <note>A district in the barony of Duhallow, County Cork</note> on fresh green soil,
A man who united the warm land,
Which he frequented as if it were the smooth plain of Meath.

O’Ceallachain <note>O'Callaghan</note> of the fair skin,
Of the race of Ceallachan of Cashel <note>Ceallachan Caisil, a 10th-century King of Munster</note>,
Men for whom a flood of fruit was produced
From the black, nut-bearing wood.

Far from the river of generous Ealla <note>The River Allow in Duhallow, County Cork</note>,
Westward across Gleann Salchain <note>Glenshalane</note> of the smooth rods,
The bright land of beautiful harvests,
The territory of the noble Mac Amhlaoibh <note>MacAuliffe</note>.

Who possesses the plain of Corc <note>Referring to the territory of Corc, King of Cashel</note> as his heritage,
On Ealla of the illustrious floor.
A branching tree of Banbha <note>Ireland</note> of the curly hair,
O’Teaghamhna of Dun Duirlais.

Come hither across Luachair <note>Sliabh Luachra, on the borders of Cork, Kerry, and Limerick</note>,
A journey to the east for the poets,
As far as the cold-feasting Claonghlais <note>Clonlish, in the barony of Upper Connello, County Limerick</note>,
Of the green-watered, tree-abounding harbor.

The Ui Conaill <note>The O'Connells</note> of the battles of Munster,
Fruitful is the assembly,
A prosperous household with whom it is not natural to contend,
The battle-ranking host of the O’Cuilein <note>O'Collins</note>.

It describes their lineage and the natural abundance of their lands as the poem moves toward the borders of Cork and Limerick.</summary>

O'Donnchadha<gloss>654</gloss> of Loch Lein,
King of Eoghanacht is he,
O'Cearbhaill<gloss>655</gloss> who is our friend,
Hawk of the lineage of the white strand.

O'Caoimh<gloss>656</gloss> of the righteous, brown brow,
Lord of Urluachair of fresh pastures,
A man who united the warm country,
Which is constantly like the plain of Meath.

O'Ceallachain<gloss>657</gloss> of the fair skin,
Of the race of Ceallachan of Cashel,
Men for whom a flood of fruit burst forth
Over the dark nut-bearing wood.

Far from the bounteous river Ella,<gloss>658</gloss>
To the west of Gleann Salchain<gloss>659</gloss> of smooth rods,
Is a fine land where fair nuts are not hidden;
It is the land of the noble Mac Amhlaoibh.<gloss>660</gloss>

A patrimony of the plain of Corc,
Aes-Ealla of the famous level floor,
Belongs to this stately scion of Banba of curling hair,
To O'Tedgamhna<gloss>661</gloss> of Dun Durlais.<gloss>662</gloss>

Let us proceed across Luachair<gloss>663</gloss> hither,
A journey which is fit for poets,
To the cold and festive Claonghlais
Of the green, well-watered, wooded land.

The Ui-Conaill<gloss>664</gloss> of the battalion of Munster,
Numerous is the gathering;
A great tribe, with whom it is not common to contend,
Are the battle-trooped host of the O'Coilens.<gloss>665</gloss>

# O'HUIDHRIN.

->O’Sullivan,<gloss>683</gloss> who did not love oppression,<-
->Governed the great Eoghanacht of Munster;<-
->Under Knockgraffon he obtained the lands,<-
->After winning battles and conflicts.<-

---

->O’Cuile, who defended his fame,<-
->Over the generous Eoghanacht Aradh;<gloss>684</gloss><-
->Over the land of fair Aolmhagh<gloss>685</gloss><-
->O’Caollaighe is the brave and hospitable man.<-

---

->Green its young corn, green its mounds,<-
->Eoghanacht of the territory of Cathbhuidh;<gloss>686</gloss><-
->Delightful the land of the broad plain,<-
->It is hereditary to the host of O’Duineachair.<gloss>687</gloss><-

---

->To O’Mergdha belongs as his share<-
->The smooth Eoghanacht of Ross-arguid,<gloss>688</gloss><-
->He is lord of every hill of fairy spirits<-
->Around the beautiful Carn Mughaine.<gloss>689</gloss><-

---

->The Siol-Maoilduin<gloss>690</gloss> of Dun gCais<-
->Is over the noble Eoghanacht Indais,<-
->An armed people passing over the waves,<-
->The people of Eachdruim,<gloss>691</gloss> rich in flocks.<-

---

->Eoghanacht of the sunny field of Gabhra,<gloss>692</gloss><-
->Land of sweetest, smooth-round apples,<-
->The gem of every famous band of women,<-
->To O’Cinnfhaeladh<gloss>693</gloss> of red weapons [it belongs].<-

---

->Aes-Greine<gloss>694</gloss> of the fine bright land was obtained<-
->By O’Conaing<gloss>695</gloss> of the territory of Saingel,<gloss>696</gloss><-
->He possessed a cheerful land around fair Grian,<gloss>697</gloss><-
->From his noble descent from Eoghan.<-
