# O'HUIDHRIN.

->Let us speak of the race of Cormac Cas,<-
->Let us journey across the green-streamed Shannon,<-
->From the host of Corp on a path we describe,<-
->To the tribe of Lorc of the lamp.<-

---

->The Small Deis<gloss>698</gloss> of the purple cloak,<-
->A territory for the high-deeded host;<-
->The heroes of Claire are mentioned by us,<-
->From the most beautiful harbor in Ireland.<-

---

->Three tribes of high spirits<-
->Are in the Small Deis of the sacred trees;<-
->Bright on the small plain of the house of Tal,<-
->Is the numerous tribe of O’Luain.<-

---

->The O’Duibhbroga of the hot raids,<-
->The O’Foircheallaigh of the soil of Claire,<-
->And the men of blood of the other tribe,<-
->From whom the great Martini are descended.<-

---

->The Dal gCais in the battles of Claire,<-
->Are as silver of deeds, and likewise<-
->As gold after its pure refining;<-
->The cheerful host is not empty.<-

->Every lord is in his own territory;<-
->Of the Dal gCais, brave is the triumph;<-
->Men of great success who stood with us,<-
->From Collan eastward to the Shannon.<-

---

->Let us give precedence without exaction<-
->To the high upper cantred;<-
->To O’Deaghaidh the land is hereditary,<-
->Around Tealach of the brown-haired troops.<-

--- Page 201 ---
<meta>This page continues the topographical survey by O’Huidhrin, focusing on the territories of the Dal gCais (the descendants of Cormac Cas). The poet transitions from the previous regions across the Shannon, detailing the tribes and lords of the Small Deis and the surrounding plains of Claire.</meta>

# O'HUIDHRIN.

Let us speak of the race of Cormac Cas,<note>698</note>
Let us pass across the Shannon of green waves;
From the sept of Corc, let us point out our way
To the tribe of Lorc of the lamp.<note>699</note>

The Small Deis<note>700</note> of the purple cloak
Is hereditary to the valorous tribe,
The heroes of Claire<note>701</note> mentioned by us,
Of the fairest bay of Ireland.

Three septs of high spirits
Are over the Small Deis of trees,
Fair over the smooth plain of the house of Tal,
The populous tribe of O'Luain.<note>702</note>

The Ui-Duibhrosa<note>703</note> of fierce incursions,
The Ui-Faircheallaigh<note>704</note> of the land of Claire,
True is the blood of the other tribe
By whom the tribe of the Mairtine<note>705</note> were subdued.

The Dal gCais in the battalions of Claire
Have pure silver, and with it,
Gold purely refined;
The pleasant host is not poor.

Each lord fits in his own territory;
Of the Dal gCais, brave is the career;
Men of great prosperity, who are mentioned by us,
From Collan<note>706</note> eastward to the Shannon.

We give first place without force
To the high upper cantred;<note>707</note>
To O'Deadhaigh<note>708</note> the land is due,
At Tealach<note>709</note> of the plains of brown nuts.

<vocab>Cormac Cas, Shannon, Corc, Lorc, Small Deis, Claire, Ireland, house of Tal, O'Luain, Ui-Duibhrosa, Ui-Faircheallaigh, Mairtine, Dal gCais, Collan, cantred, O'Deadhaigh, Tealach</vocab>

<summary>This page details the migration of the topographical survey across the Shannon into the lands of the Dal gCais, specifically identifying the tribes of the Small Deis. The poet lists the noble families and their territories, such as the O'Luains and O'Deas, while praising their wealth and martial success.</summary>
<keywords>Dal gCais, Cormac Cas, Shannon River, Small Deis, O'Luain, O'Deadhaigh, Claire, Irish topography, genealogy</keywords>

# O’HUIDHRIN.

From O'Cuinn of the noble heart,<note>710 O’Cuinn, now O’Quin. The territory was Muintir-Iffernan, around the lake of Inchiquin.</note>
The extensive people of Fearnan;
Fruitful is the land of the bright youth,
Around Coradh-Finne<note>711 Now Corofin, in the county of Clare.</note> of the feasts.

The Ui-Flaithri,<note>712 This tribe was located in the barony of Inchiquin.</note> worthy of praise,
Hold the land in front of Fionnchoradh;
The land of O'Cathail west and east,
The smooth land of the yew-grown field.

Cenel-Baith,<note>713 Now known as the Cineal Baith.</note> no small tribe,
The fine territory of Brentir;<note>714 Now Breintir, a district in the barony of Inchiquin.</note>
The Ui-Maoilmeadha<note>715 Now O’Mulvey.</note> of the fair bright helmet,
Of the wood around pleasant Eidneach.<note>716 Now the River Inagh.</note>

From the seed of Eoghan of the region of Cliach,<note>717 Eoghan of Cliach, a branch of the Dal gCais.</note>
The Ui-Cormaic<note>718 This was the name of the tribe and territory of O’Hehir.</note> are on a fair bright land;
With O'h-Aichir<note>719 Now O’Hehir.</note> is the warm land,
Its likeness is the smooth Meath.

Chiefs who are strong in every house,
A noble tribe are the Ui-h-Aichir;
Over the Ui-Flannchadha<note>720 Now O’Flannery or Clancy; this family held the hereditary office of judges to the O’Briens.</note> of the generous mansion,
The illustrious, thin-weaponed host.

O'Duibhghinn,<note>721 Now O’Dugan.</note> of the countenance like crimson,
Is over the fair Muintir-Connlochtach;
The chief obtained his profit
With hardy, social spears.

O'Grada<note>722 Now O’Grady. Their territory was Cineal Dunghaile.</note> has taken all
The noble-voiced Cenel-Dunghaile;
Their yellow-handled swords are sharp,
The sledge-hammers of the assembly.

<vocab>O’Chuinn, Fearnáin, Coradh Finne, Uí Flaithri, Fionnchoradh, Uí Chathail, Cenel mbaitch, Bréntire, Uí Maoilmeadha, Eidnigh, Eoghan, Cliach, Uí Cormaic, Aichir, Uí Flannchadha, O’Duibhghinn, Connlochtach, O Gráda, Cenel Dunghuile</vocab>

# O'HUIDHRIN.

To O'Cuinn<note>710 O’Cuinn, now O’Quin. The territory was Muintir-Iffernan, around the lake of Inchiquin.</note> of the noble heart
Belongs the extensive Muintir-Ifearnain,<note>711 Muintir-Ifearnain, the tribe of O’Quin.</note>
The fruitful land of the fine youth
Lies around the festive Coradh-Finne.<note>Coradh-Finne is now Corofin, in County Clare.</note>

Ui Flaithri,<note>712 This tribe was located in the barony of Inchiquin.</note> worthy of much praise,
A land close to Fionnchoradh,
Is the land of O'Cathail<note>713 Now O’Cahill.</note> west and east;
A smooth land is this land of the yew.

Cinel Baith, of no small territory,
The fine tribe of Brentir,<note>714 Now Breintir, a district in the barony of Inchiquin.</note>
O'Maoilmeadha of the fair land;
His are the woods around the delightful Eidhneach.<note>715 Now the River Inagh.</note>

Of the race of Eoghan, of the region of Cliach,
The Ui Corbmaic<note>716 This was the name of the tribe and territory of O’Hehir.</note> of beautiful green land;
To O'h Aichir belongs the warm land,
The plain of Meath is similar to it.

A ruler powerful in every house,
A noble clan of the Ui-Aichirs
Is over Ui Flannchadha<note>717 Now O’Flannery or Clancy; this family held the hereditary office of judges to the O’Briens.</note> of hospitable seats,
The thin-edged, illustrious host.

O'Duibhginn<note>718 Now O’Dugan.</note> of the ruddy countenance,
Is over the fair Muintir Connlochtaigh,<note>719 A tribe located in the same region.</note>
The chief gained its profit
By the strength of battle spears.

O'Grada<note>720 Now O’Grady.</note> took all
The bountiful Cinel Dunghaile,<note>721 The territory of the O’Gradys.</note>
His yellow-hilted polished swords,
Weapons that slaughter at encounters.

# O'HUIDHRIN.

Royal chieftain of noble onsets,
Mac Conmara<note>Now MacNamara. Their territory was the barony of Upper Bunratty.</note> of Magh Adhair,<note>Magh Adhair was the place of inauguration for the O’Brien kings of Thomond.</note>
His is the country of smooth lands,
Over the cantred of Clann Chaisin.<note>The tribal name of the MacNamaras.</note>

The host of O’Dobharchon<note>Now O’Douran.</note> of the merry company,
I mention Muintir Liddane;<note>Now Liddane.</note>
These are Clann Sinnill,
Safe are they, men of nobility.

The land of Clann Delbhaoith<note>A tribe of the Dal gCais.</note> of poems,
Under O'Neill,<note>O’Neill of Tradree.</note> prince of Finnluarach;
The host of Tradraighe<note>Now Tradree, a district in the barony of Bunratty Lower.</note> coming into his house,
Under his yellow, wavy, flowing hair.

The cantred of Ui Bloid<note>A large territory in the northeast of County Clare, including the baronies of Tulla and Bunratty.</note> of the satin banners,
The kings of Cliach<note>A district on the borders of Limerick and Tipperary.</note> of the battle-fields;
The tribe of O'Tail with the clear stream,
Throughout the wide plain of the yew-trees.

Over Ui Cearnaigh<note>A territory in the barony of Bunratty.</note>—brave is the career—
Is Ui Echthighern<note>Now O’Ahern.</note> of the land of Maicniadh;
A spirited country under the noble youth,
As far as the bright old stream of the Shannon.

The wood of Ui Ronghaile<note>A territory in the barony of Tulla.</note> of smooth land,
O'Shanahan<note>Now O’Shanahan or Shannon.</note> of the bright eyes possessed;
All the country under Eibhlinn,<note>Slieve Eilim, now the Slieve Phelim mountains.</note>
Like the smooth surface of Maonmagh.<note>A plain in County Galway, used here as a standard of beauty and fertility.</note>

O'Kennedy,<note>Now O’Kennedy.</note> who breaks the spear,
Is over the wide, smooth Glenomra;<note>Now Glenomra, a valley in the parish of Killokennedy.</note>
The race of Duinnchuan,<note>Duinnchuan was the brother of Brian Boru; his descendants, the O’Kennedys, originally held Glenomra before moving into Tipperary.</note> through bravery,
Obtained the lands without dispute.

# O'HUIDHRIN.

Royal chieftain of noble raids
Is MacNamara<note>Mac Conmara. Their territory was the barony of Upper Bunratty.</note> of Magh Adhair;<note>Magh Adhair was the place of inauguration for the O’Brien kings of Thomond.</note>
The lands of wealth are his country;
He is over the cantred of Ui gCaisin.<note>The tribal name and territory of the MacNamaras.</note>

The host of the O’Dobharchons, a pleasant company,
Are the Muintir Lideadha<note>Now Liddane or Liddy.</note> I mention;
These are the clans of Sinnell,
They are ready among the nobles.

The land of the poetic Clann Dealbhaoith<note>A tribe of the Dal gCais.</note>
Is under O’Neill, chief of Fionnluaraigh;<note>O’Neill of Finnluarach.</note>
The host of Tradraighe<note>Now Tradree, a district in the barony of Bunratty Lower.</note> come into his house,
With long, yellow, flowing hair.

The cantred of Ui Bloid<note>O'm-Bloid; a large territory in the northeast of County Clare.</note> of satin banners,
Kings of Cliach<note>A district on the borders of Limerick and Tipperary.</note> of the warring tribes;
The tribe of Ui Tail, by the clear green stream,
Is throughout the wide plain of yew trees.

Over the Ui Cearnaigh,<note>A territory in the barony of Bunratty.</note> of noble career,
Are the O’Echthigherns,<note>Now Ahern.</note> of Maicniadh's land;<note>Maicniadh was a common name among the Dal gCais ancestors.</note>
A spirited territory is under the fine youth,
Extending to the bright old stream of the Shannon.

The wood of Ui Ronghaile<note>A territory in the barony of Tulla.</note> of cleared land,
O’Shanahan<note>O'Seanchain; now Shanahan or Shannon.</note> of the bright eyes possessed;
The land around all Eibhlinn,<note>Slieve Eilim, now the Slieve Phelim mountains.</note>
Like the fine smooth plain of Maonmagh.<note>A rich plain in County Galway used as a point of comparison for beauty.</note>

O’Kennedy,<note>O'Cinneidigh.</note> who reddens the spear,
Over the wide, smooth Gleann Omra;<note>Now Glenomra, a valley in the parish of Killokennedy.</note>
The race of our Donnchuan<note>The brother of Brian Boru and ancestor of the O’Kennedys.</note> who, through valor,
Obtained the lands without dispute.

<meta>This page continues O’Huidhrin’s topographical poem, moving from the central Dalcassian territories toward Killaloe, the Shannon, and the borders of Limerick, identifying the O'Duracks, O'Turloughs, and the chieftains of the district of Limerick.</meta>

# O'HUIDHRIN.

The O'Duracks of Dun Braite,
Chieftains of the district of O'Connolly;
Their forts are around the noble Borumha,<note>Borumha, the ancient seat of Brian Boru near Killaloe.</note>
With hair like woven golden brass.

The O'Turloughs of the house of Tal,
Near Killaloe of Saint Flannan;<note>Saint Flannan is the patron saint of the diocese of Killaloe.</note>
Pleasant is its wood, generous its soil,
Extending from there westward to the Shannon.

The district of Limerick around the noble Shannon,
Has two chieftains on one side:
O'Kealy and the swift O'Malley,
Beautiful heroes of the two estuaries.

The O'Amrits, a land of hospitality,
Are the rightful due of the tribe of O'Dwyer;
Their acquisition extends far beyond Cliach,
A branch in every ancient ford.

O'Keary of the pure heart
Is over the cantred of Caladh;<note>The "Caladh" refers to a marshy meadow or ferry-landing, here a district in the barony of Bunratty.</note>
The people of Cloon of Tal choose him;
O'Keary has a beautiful plain.

The people of the three plains, where every land is smooth,
Is the sheltered territory of O'Gunning;
A bright-watered plain by our noble stream,
Alongside which is Craobh Cumhraidhe.<note>Now Cratloe.</note>

To depart from the race of Cormac Cas of the house of Tal
In a manly way is necessary;
It is now my duty to approach the Uaithni,<note>The territories of Owney-beg in Limerick and Owney-and-Arra in Tipperary.</note>
For their fame and their defense are well known.

<summary>This page lists the chieftains and territories near the Shannon and Killaloe, including the O'Duracks, O'Turloughs, and O'Gunnings. It concludes the section on the Dalcassian tribes before moving toward the Uaithni.</summary>

<meta>This page continues O’Huidhrin’s topographical poem, describing the territories and chieftains of the Dalcassian tribes near the Shannon and Killaloe before moving on to the Uaithnes.</meta>

# O'HUIDHRIN.

The O'Duracks<note>Muintir-Diubhraic.</note> of Dun-Braine,
Are chieftains of Tuath-O' gConghaile;<note>The district of O'Connolly.</note>
Their forts are around the good Borumha;<note>The ancient seat of Brian Boru near Killaloe.</note>
Locks of hair like gold are upon them.

The O'Turloughs<note>Ui Toirdhealbhaigh.</note> of the house of Tal,
Near to Saint Flannan's Killaloe;<note>Cilldalua.</note>
Delightful are its woods, generous its lands,
Extending from there westward to the Shannon.

The district of Limerick<note>Tuath-Luimnigh.</note> around the noble Shannon,
Two chiefs are over it on one side:
O'Kealy<note>O'Cadhla.</note> and O'Malley, the swift,
Beautiful heroes of the two estuaries.<note>Inbhers.</note>

Ui-Aimrit, a land of hospitality,
Is hereditary to the clan of the O'Dwyers;<note>O'Duibhidhirs.</note>
Their territory extends far over Cliach;
They are a branch in every ford.

O'Keavy,<note>O'Cedfadha.</note> of the pure heart,
Is over the cantred of the Caladh;<note>A marshy meadow or ferry-landing, here a district in the barony of Bunratty.</note>
The people of Cloon,<note>Cluain.</note> chosen by Tal,
The beautiful plain of O'Keavy.

Aos-tri-muighe,<note>The people of the three plains.</note> the smoothest of plains,
Is the grassy territory of O'Gunning;<note>O'Conaing.</note>
A bright watered plain, of the noblest appearance,
By the meadowy side of Craobh Cumhraidhe.<note>Now Cratloe.</note>

From the race of Cormac Cas, of the house of Tal,
We must henceforth depart;
To approach the Uaithnes<note>The territories of Owney-beg in Limerick and Owney-and-Arra in Tipperary.</note> is proper for us,
Noble is their fame and their defense.

--- Page 208 ---
<meta>This page continues O’Huidhrin’s topographical poem, moving from the Dalcassian tribes of Clare and Limerick to the territories of the Uaitne and the kingdom of Ely (O'Carroll) in modern-day Tipperary and Offaly.</meta>

# O’HUIDHRIN.

Over Uaitne of the land of fruit,<note>Uaitne-Tire, now the barony of Owney, in Tipperary.</note>
Is Mac Keogh,<note>Mag Ceoch.</note> who loved many a path;
The O'Lynchs,<note>Muintir Loingsigh.</note> people of the feasts,
In that wood against the breast of foreigners.

Uaitne Cliach<note>Now the barony of Owneybeg, in the east of County Limerick.</note> under the bright-white sun,
Is the territory of O'Heffernan;<note>O' h-Ifearnan.</note>
A pure land by the side of every hill,
Pleasantly did O'Cahalan<note>O'Cathalain.</note> love it.

The region of Aradh<note>Now the barony of Owney and Arra, in County Tipperary.</note> over every tribe,
Belongs to O'Donnagan<note>O'Donnagain.</note> of the firm countenance;
The land poured out heavy fruit
For the king of Aradh; it is not light.

A land around Crota Cliach<note>The Galtee Mountains.</note> was taken
By Mac Longahan,<note>Mag Longachain.</note> the fair-gray hero;
Lord of the populous plains here,
And of Ui Cuanach<note>Now the barony of Coonagh, County Limerick.</note> on the white, soft land.

Of this same race, as is right,
Are the O'Dwyers<note>Muintir Duibidir.</note> of the white teeth;
The plain of the beautiful, ditched Seachtmadh,<note>A district in the barony of Owney and Arra.</note>
Under the noble, warlike flock.

Lords to whom the nut-trees bend,
The O'Carrolls<note>Muintir Cearbhaill.</note> of the plain of Birr;<note>Biorra.</note>
King of Ely<note>Ele.</note> as far as sweet Slieve Bloom,<note>Bladhma.</note>
The most hospitable residence in Ireland.

Eight territories and eight chiefs in the east,
Under the king of Ely of the land of cattle;
Brave are the spoils in foretelling raids,
The yellow-haired, fringed host.

<summary>This page identifies the chieftains of the Uaitne, Aradh, and Coonagh regions, including the families of Mac Keogh, O'Heffernan, and O'Dwyer. It then introduces the O'Carrolls of Ely, describing their vast, hospitable territory stretching toward Slieve Bloom.</summary>

# O'HUIDHRIN.

Over Uaitne-tire,<note>Now the barony of Owney, in Tipperary.</note> a land of fruit,
Is Mac Keogh,<note>Mag Ceoch.</note> who loved great projects;
The O'Lynchs,<note>Muintir Loingsigh.</note> people of the lands,
In this wood facing the strangers.

Uaitne-Cliach,<note>Now the barony of Owneybeg, in the east of County Limerick.</note> of bright green land,
Is the country of O'Heffernan;<note>O' h-Ifearnain.</note>
Fine land at the side of each hillock,
Beautiful and loved by O'Cahalan.<note>O'Cathalain.</note>

Chief king of Aradh<note>Now the barony of Owney and Arra, in County Tipperary.</note> over every tribe,
Is O'Donnagan<note>O'Donnagain.</note> of the noble aspect;
The territory yielded heavy produce
For the king of Aradh; it is not insignificant.

A territory around Crota Cliach<note>The Galtee Mountains.</note> was acquired
By Mac Longahan,<note>Mag-Longachain.</note> a fair, gray hero;
Lord of a populous plain is he here,
Of Ui Cuanach<note>Now the barony of Coonagh, County Limerick.</note> on the green, soft land.

Of this same race, and this is right,
Are the O'Dwyers,<note>Muintir-Duibhidhir.</note> of white teeth;
The plain of Seachtmadh,<note>A district in the barony of Owney and Arra.</note> of fair fences,
Is under the noble tribe in turn.

Lords to whom the nut-trees bend,
Are the O'Carrolls<note>Muintir-Cearbhaill.</note> of Birr's plain,<note>Biorra.</note>
King of Ely<note>Eile.</note> to sweet Slieve Bloom,<note>Bladhma.</note>
The most hospitable mansion in Ireland.

Eight <term>cantreds</term>, eight chieftains in the east,
Under the king of Ely, of the land of cattle;
Brave is the host gathering spoils—
The host of yellow, curling hair.

# O'HUIDHRIN.

O'Flanagan, flaming is his hand,
Over the whole of Cenel Arga,<note>Cinel-Arga, now the barony of Ballybritt, County Offaly.</note>
Of the seed of Tadhg, son of Cian Crionda,
From the generous Fir-thic of Oilill.

Clann Ruainne of the flowery roads,
A sweet land of smooth little streams;
Mac Corcoran is of the cultivated district,
Of the white-breasted, fair-surfaced slope.

O'Hegan of the race of Cian,
Over Clann Ionmainen, the very smooth;
A district that yields feasts in every land,
With the gush of honey on every cluster.

The great district of the swift Clann Maonaigh
Belongs to O'Dooley; that land is his;
Brave is the leadership of that family,
At the edge of Slieve Bloom<note>Bladhma.</note> of the bright drops.

A chieftain for whom the trees are white with nuts
Over Clann Coinnleccain of the heavy harvest;
A branch of Birr<note>Biorra.</note> from the people of Ely
Is Mac Gilfoyle of the bright-white seat.

Ui Deci, the good hilly district,
The wide land of fair mansions;
A fruitful territory that has united them,
The ancestral home of O'Banan.

The O'Meaghers have strongly filled the land,
The territory of Ikerrin;<note>Ui-Cairin, now the barony of Ikerrin in County Tipperary.</note>
A people who were at the foot of Bearnan Ele;<note>Now the Devil's Bit Mountain.</note>
It is no shame to record their triumphs.

# O'HUIDHRIN.

O'Flanagan, valiant is his hand,
Over the whole of Cenel Arga,<note>Cinel-Arga, now the barony of Ballybritt, County Offaly.</note>
Of the race of Tadhg, son of Cian of Crinna,

The Clann Ruainne of the flowery roads,
A sweet, clear, smooth-streamed territory;
Mac Corcoran is of this well-populated district,
Of the white-breasted border of banquets.

O'Hegan of Crich Cein
Over the smooth-turfed Clann Ionmainen,
A district that spread banquets over every land,
With honeyed dew upon every pod.

That country is hereditary to O'Dooley;
The tribe is a fine group of leaders,
At the foot of the clear-streamed Slieve Bloom.<note>Bladhma.</note>

A chieftain for whom the trees yield fine nuts
Is over Clann Coinnleccain, of heavy fruit,
Scion of Birr<note>Biorra.</note> of the race of Ely,
Mac Gilfoyle of the fair seat.

Ui Deci, the good hilly district,
The extensive land of fair mansions,
A land of fruit, strengthened by them,
Is the patrimony of O'Banan.

Powerfully they have filled the land,
The O'Meaghers—the territory of Ikerrin,<note>Ui-Cairin, now the barony of Ikerrin in County Tipperary.</note>
A tribe at the foot of Bearnan Eile;<note>Now the Devil's Bit Mountain.</note>
It is no shame to celebrate their triumph.

<meta>This page provides explanatory notes for O’Dubhagain’s Topographical Poem, following the previous section's focus on Irish tribal territories. It shifts from the poetic verses of O'Huidhrin to historical and linguistic annotations regarding the tribes of Tuilen, the ancient names of Ireland, and the significance of Tara.</meta>

# NOTES
->TO<-
## O’DUBHAGAIN’S TOPOGRAPHICAL POEM.

1 *The three septs of Tuilen.*—The language here is defective; it should read as follows: “and the three septs of Tuilen, namely, the Ui-Maine, the Cinel-Eochain, and the Britons; O’Muirchertaigh is Lord of Ui-Maine, O’Modhairn is over the Cinel-Eochain, and O’Domhnaill is over the Britons.”

2 *Fodhla*, one of the most ancient names for Ireland, being borrowed, according to the Bardic historians, from a Tuatha De Danann queen of 그 name who was living at the time of the Milesian or Scotic invasion. See *Ogygia*, part iii. chapter xv. Dr. Lynch translates this line:
->“O Friends, let us visit the borders of beautiful Ireland.”<-

3 *Let the nobles of Erin proceed.*—Ireland was called *Eire* after a Tuatha De Danann queen who was, according to the Bardic accounts, a contemporary of Fodhla (mentioned in the preceding note) and the reigning queen when the sons of Milesius arrived from Spain to conquer the island. O’Dubhagain here imagines himself summoning a royal convention of the men of Ireland to Teamhair, or Tara, for the purpose of being described in his poem. His language is rather abrupt and obscure, but it may be paraphrased thus: “Let us proceed first of all to Tara; let the princes and chieftains meet us there so that we may weave their names into our poem, and thus pass them down to the furthest posterity. ‘No man shall be without a patrimony,’ that is, every man’s inheritance shall be declared and made known in our verses. And when they assemble there, face to face, they will each request that we notice their families and celebrate their nobility.”

4 *Teamhair*, now Tara. It was the palace of the monarchs of Ireland from the earliest dawn of Irish history down to the reign of Diarmaid, son of Fergus Cearbheoil, when it was deserted. See Petrie’s *Antiquities of Tara Hill* (*Transactions of the Royal Irish Academy*, vol. xviii., p. 108).

5 *Has not melted away.*—That is, which has not withered away.

<summary>This page contains historical and geographical notes on O’Dubhagain’s Topographical Poem, detailing the septs of Tuilen and explaining the mythological origins of Ireland's names, Fodhla and Eire.</summary>
<keywords>O’Dubhagain, Tuilen, Ui-Maine, Cinel-Eochain, Fodhla, Eire, Tuatha De Danann, Milesian, Tara, Teamhair, Irish history, topography</keywords>

--- Page 216 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanatory notes for O’Dubhagain’s Topographical Poem, detailing the decline and displacement of specific Irish families like the O'Melaghlins, Harts, Regans, and O'Kellys following the English invasion and subsequent conflicts.</meta>

<page-num>ii</page-num>
<header>O'DUBHAGAIN.</header>

From this it is clear that O'Dubhagain believed many ancient Irish families had dwindled into insignificance at the time he was writing. Many of them had revived in his time—since Bruce's invasion of 1315—but they had been removed from their original territories.

6 *O'Maeileachlainn*, anglicized *O'Melaghlin*, and now corrupted to *Mac Loughlin*. This family, which was the head of the south Hy-Niall race, derived its name and lineage from Maelseachlainn, or Malachy II, monarch of Ireland, who died in the year 1022. The name Maelseachlainn signifies servant of Seachlann, or St. Secundinus, who was a nephew of St. Patrick and the patron saint of this great family. The present head of this family is unknown. The late Con Mac Loughlin of Dublin was of the race, but his pedigree was never made out. His relatives still live near Mullingar, in the county of Westmeath.

7 *O h-Airt*, now *Hart*. After the English invasion this family was banished from Tara and settled in the barony of Carbury, in the county of Sligo.

8 *O'Riagain*, now *O'Regan*, and more frequently *Regan*, without the prefix *O'*. This race was banished from Tara at the English invasion and is now found widely dispersed throughout Ireland.

9 *O'Ceallaigh*, or *O'Kelly*, now usually *Kelly*, without the prefix *O'*. This family was descended from Aedh Slaine, monarch of Ireland; its last representative was Congalach O'Kelly, lord of Bregia, who died A.D. 1292. The race was so dispersed and reduced in the seventeenth century that they could not be distinguished from the O'Kellys of other territories and lineage. Connell Mageoghegan, chief of the sept of Lismoyny, who translated the Annals of Clonmacnoise in 1627, gives the following account of them under A.D. 778: 

> "Dermott Mac Kervell, king of Ireland, had issue Hugh Slane, Colman More, and Colman Beg. To the race of Hugh was allotted Moy-Brey, extending from Dublin to Bealaghbrick, westerly of Kells; and from the hill of Howth to the mountain of Slieve Fwayde, in Ulster. There reigned of King Hugh’s race, as monarchs of this kingdom, nine kings, etc. There were many princes of Moy-Brey besides the said kings, who behaved themselves as became them; and because they were nearer the invasions <note>that is, the rallying point of the invaders</note> of the land than other septs, they were sooner banished and brought lower than others. The O'Kelly of Brey was the chief of that race, though it has many other minor branches, which for brevity's sake I omit to detail. They are brought so low nowadays that the best chroniclers in the

It specifically tracks the lineage of the O'Kellys of Bregia back to the monarch Aedh Slaine and notes their status by the 17th century.</summary>

<header>->### TRIBES OF MEATH.<-</header>

kingdom are ignorant of their ancestry, although the O’Kellys are so common everywhere that it is unknown whether the dispersed parties of them in Ireland are of the families of the O’Kellys of Connaught or Brey; indeed, scarcely one of the same family knows the name of his own great-grandfather, and they have become mere churls and poor laboring men, so that there are few parishes in the kingdom that do not have one or another of these Kellys—I mean of Brey,” or Bregia.

10 *O’Conghalaigh*, now Conolly. A branch of this family remained in Meath and in the present county of Monaghan, where the head of the name became notorious in the year 1641.

11 *Breagh*, a large plain or level territory in East Meath, comprising five cantreds. According to an old poem, quoted by Keating, it extended northwards as far as the Casan, now the Annagassan stream, near Dundalk, in the county of Louth.

12 *O’Ruaidhri.*—This name (which is to be distinguished from Mac Ruaidhri, anglicized Mac Rory and Rogers) is now unknown, as are the name and situation of the territory of Fionnfochla.

13 *Crich na gCedach*, that is, the territory of the Cedachs, a sept descended from Oilioll Cedach, son of Cathaoir Mór, monarch of Ireland in the second century. This territory was formerly in Meath, but is now included in the King’s County. In the Black Book of the Exchequer of Ireland, and in several Pipe Rolls in the reign of Edward III, it appears that this territory, which in these records is called Crynagedagh, was charged with royal services as lying within the county of Meath. It comprised the present parish of Castlejordan, in the barony of Warrenstown, King’s County, adjoining the counties of Meath and Westmeath. See Inquisition taken at Philipstown, 9th January, 1629, and Harris’s Edition of Ware’s Antiquities, chapter v; Annals of Four Masters, A.D. 1124, 1142, 1406, 1484. The O’Follamhains of this race are to be distinguished from the O’Fallamhains or O’Fallons of Clann Uadach, in the county of Roscommon, with which Colgan confuses it in his *Acta SS.*, pp. 138, 142.

Dr. Reeves has supplied the editor with the following notices of the church of Crich na gCedach:—

> “*Ecclesia de Kirnegedach, valet x. mar. per an.*”—Taxatio circ. 1300.
> “*Rectoria de Grenegedah alias Kernekedah. Haec parochia ita denominatur a quodam Kedah O’Connor, qui olim erat dominus illius territorii. Unam tantum habet ecclesiam vocatam ecclesiam de Gortantemple. Ecclesia impropriata erat Priori S. Trinitatis de Ballybogan.*”—Bp. A. Dopping, Account of Meath Diocese (MS. Marsh’s Library). See “

of Crenegedgagh," Patent Rolls, James I, p. 221 *b*. Also Leinster Inquisition, Commission of the King No. 18, James I (1623); Book of Rights, p. 200; Battle of Magh Rath, p. 243.

14 *Laeghaire*, otherwise *Ui-Laeghaire*. This territory comprised the region around the town of Trim, in the county of Meath. It embraced the greater part of the baronies of Upper and Lower Navan. O'Coindealbhain, its chief, was the lineal descendant of Laeghaire, monarch of Ireland in St. Patrick's time. The name is now anglicized as Kindellan, Quinlan, and sometimes Conlan. See *Miscellany of the Irish Archaeological Society*, p. 143; *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1171. The townland of Tullyard, in the barony of Upper Navan, and about two miles to the northeast of Trim, was in this territory and contained the chief residence of O'Coindealbhain.

Dr. Reeves has communicated the following note:
> The Book of Armagh distinguishes between Laoghaire of Bregha and Laoghaire of Meath: "*Vadum Truim* [Ath-Trym, now Trim] *in finibus Loiguiri Breg, Imgae in finibus Loiguiri Midi*," fol. 16 *bb*. See *Vita Tripartita Sancti Patricii*, ii. 3, in *Trias Thaumaturga* p. 129 *b*.

Castletown-Kindalen, or Vastina, is a parish in the barony of Moycashel, Westmeath.

15 *Luighne*, now called in Irish Luibhne, and anglicized as Lune, a barony in the west of the county of Meath. The O'Braoins [O'Breens] of this territory disappeared from history at an early period; the last mentioned in the *Annals of the Four Masters* died in the year 1201. They are to be distinguished from the O'Breens of Breaghmhaine in Westmeath.

16 *Ui-Macuais of Breagh*.—This <term>sept</term> was situated to the southwest of Tara and occupied the barony of Moyfenrath in East Meath. The family name of O'h-Aenghusa is now anglicized as Hennessy. All traditions of the ancient power of this family in Meath have long since died out.

17 *Odhbha*, a territory near Navan in East Meath, which appears to have comprised the present barony of Skreen. The family of O'h-Aedha has been scattered widely over East Meath and Monaghan. The name is now usually anglicized as Hughes. This <term>sept</term> is to be distinguished from the O'Heas of Ui-Fiachrach of Ardsratha, in Ulster.

18 *Cnodhbha*, now anglicized as Knowth. The territory so called appears to have been comprised in the barony of Upper Slane, in East Meath. The name is now applied to a very ancient mound in the parish of Monknewtown. The family name of O'Dubhain is now anglicized as O'Duane, Dwan, Divan, and Downes.

<meta>This page continues the scholarly annotations regarding the tribes of Meath, moving from the baronies of Navan and Lune to the territories of Feara-Bile and Saithni.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>V</page-num>
<header>TRIBES OF MEATH.</header>

<sup>19</sup> *O'h-Ainbheith*, now anglicized as Hanvey and Hanafy, without the O' prefix. Feara-Bile, now Farbill, is a barony in the county of Westmeath. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, 1021, 1095. This family was dispossessed by Sir Hugh De Lacy. The poet takes a great leap here from Cnodhbha on the Boyne to Farbill in Westmeath, simply for the sake of the rhyme.

<sup>20</sup> *Saithni*.—This tribe descended from Glasradh, the second son of Cormac Gaileng, son of Tadhg, son of Cian, son of Oilioll Olum, who was king of Munster in the third century. They were a sub-section of the people called Cianachta Breagh and were located in Fingal, in the east of Bregia, to the north of the city of Dublin. See *O'Flaherty’s Ogygia*, part iii. c. 69. The O'Cathasaigh, now Casey, of this territory, was dispossessed by Sir Hugh De Lacy, who sold his lands. See *Hibernia Expugnata*, book ii. c. 24, and *Leabhar na gCeart*, p. 187, note <sup>s</sup>. It appears from *Alan’s Register* that this territory covered the same area as the barony of Balrothery West, in the county of Dublin. The O'Caseys of this race are to be distinguished from those of Liscannon, near Bruff, in the county of Limerick; surprisingly, Edmond Henry Casey, Esq., of Donahies, is located in a neighboring barony to this very territory. The Caseys of Saithni are, however, numerous in Meath, in Drogheda, and in the city of Dublin.

Dr. Reeves has contributed the following note on this family:—

For the descent of O'Cathasaigh see M'Firbis, Geneal. MS. pp. 348, 353.

> “Among the very beginnings of his works, the lands which Hugh de Lacy had alienated—namely, the land of O'Cathasaigh and many others—he recalled with all diligence to the royal table,” that is, *Phil. Wigorniensis. Gir. Cambr. Hib. Expug.* ii. 24 (p. 799, ed. Camd.).

The extent of O'Casey's country is determined from a legal agreement between John, Archbishop of Dublin, and Geoffrey, Prior of Lanthony (*Registrum Alani*, folio 110 *a*), concerning the ecclesiastical rights of the *land of O'Kadesi*, in which a division is made, and the Archbishop grants to the Prior the churches of Villa Ogari <note>Garristown</note> *with the chapel* of Palmerstown; *of the Holy Wood in Fingal* <note>Holywood</note>; *the chapel of the land of Regredus* otherwise Riredus, namely *Grallagh* <note>the Grallagh</note>; and the *Church of Villa Stephani de Cruys* or Nalle <note>the Naul</note>.

While the Prior granted to the Archbishop the churches of Villa Macdun <note>Ballymadun</note>, of the land of Roger Waspaile <note>Westpalstown</note>, of Villa Radulphi Paslewe <note>Balscaddan</note>, and the chapel of Richard de la Felde. Thus Ui Cathasaigh embraced Garristown, Palmerstown or Clonmethan, Holywood, the Grallagh, Naul, Ballymadun, Westpalstown, and Balscad-

dan, which comprised all of Balrothery West, except for Ballyboghil, which had been disposed of in another way. Therefore, we may say that <term>Saithne</term> (that is, <term>Ui Cathasaigh</term> or Ocadesi) equals Balrothery West. This division between the two clergymen arose from their shared right to all the tithes of the territory; for King John, and after him Edward III, granted and confirmed to the Archbishop a "half of the tithes of the land of Okadesi of Fingal," while the other "half" was reserved for the Priory of Lanthony, near Gloucester.

<sup>21</sup> *O'Leochain*, now anglicized as Loughan, and incorrectly translated as "Duck." The name of <term>Gaileanga Mora</term> or Great Gaileanga, of which O'Leochain was chief, is still preserved in the barony of Morgallion in the north of County Meath; but the ancient territory was more extensive than the barony. We learn from a <gloss>commentary on the *Feiliré Aenghuis*</gloss> for October 13th that the mountainous district of Sliabh Guaire [Slieve Gorey], now part of the barony of Clankee in County Cavan, originally belonged to Gaileanga.

<sup>22</sup> *Teallach-Modharain.*—This tribe was located in East Meath, likely in the barony of South Moyfenrath. The name O'Donnchadha is anglicized as O'Donoghy or Dunphy, but the family bearing it is now obscure. The O'Donnchadhas (or O'Donoghoes) of Kerry are from a different lineage, as are the Dunphys of Ossory.

<sup>23</sup> *Corca Raeidhe*, now the barony of Corcaree in County Westmeath. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1185, p. 66, note n. See also the note on *Korkureti* by Adamnan in Reeves's edition of the *Vita S. Columbæ*, p. 89. The name O'hIonnradhain is now anglicized as Henrion. This family descends from Fiacha Raoidhe, grandson of Feidhlimidh Rechtmhar. See *Ogygia*, part iii. p. 69, and Mac Firbis’s *Genealogical MS.*, p. 106.

<sup>24</sup> *Feara-Ceall*, <note>meaning "Men of the cells," or rather "of the churches."</note> This name was long preserved in Fircal, a barony in King's County, now known as Eglish. However, there is ample evidence to prove that <term>Feara-ceall</term> included not only the modern barony of Eglish but also the baronies of Ballycowan and Ballyboy in the same county. See *Leabhar na gCeart*, p. 180. The current chief is unknown to the editor. The head of the O'Maolmhuaidhs (anglicized as O'Molloys) in 1585 was Connell, son of Cahir, whose grandson was chief in 1677. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1585, p. 1838. Daniel Molloy, Esq., of Clonbela near Birr in King's County, is traditionally considered the current senior representative of the family, though the editor does not know his lineage.

<meta>This page continues the scholarly annotations regarding the territories and tribes of Meath, specifically detailing the lineages and historical geography of the Feara-Tulach and various branches of the Dealbhna.</meta>

<page-num>vii</page-num>
<header>TRIBES OF MEATH.</header>

<sup>25</sup> *Feara-Tulach*, that is, *Viri collium*, now the barony of Fartullagh in the southeast of County Westmeath. The family of O’Dubhlaidh, now Dooley, was driven from this territory by the Irish family of O’Melaghlin before the English invasion of Ireland; they settled in Ely O’Carroll, in the present King’s County, where they are very numerous to this day. See the *Annals of the Four Masters* for the years 978, 1021, 1144, and 1367. The English family of Tyrrell obtained possession of Fartullagh soon after the English invasion.

<sup>26</sup> *Dealbhna-mor*, that is, the Great Delvin, now the barony of Delvin in the east of County Westmeath. The Dealbhna were descended from Lughaidh Dealbhaedh, son of Cas, who was the ancestor of the Dal Cais of Thomond. The descendants of this Lughaidh acquired seven territories contiguous to each other and beyond the limits of Thomond, in Meath and Connaught: namely, Dealbhna Mor (the territory referred to here), Dealbhna Beg (that is, the Small), Dealbhna Eathra, and Dealbhna Teannmaighe in Meath; and Dealbhna Nuadhat (between the rivers Suck and Shannon), Dealbhna Cuilefabhair, and Dealbhna Feadha in Connaught. Sigdy, the great-grandson of this Lughaidh, had two sons: Treon, the ancestor of Mac Coghlan, chief of Dealbhna Eathra; and Lughaidh, the ancestor of O’Finnallain, now Fenelon. The last member of this family who had possession of Dealbhna-mor was Ceallach O’Finnallain, who is mentioned in the *Annals of the Four Masters* in the year 1174. They were dispossessed soon afterward by Sir Hugh de Lacy, who granted their territory to Gilbert Nugent, ancestor of the Marquis of Westmeath, and the family has been in obscurity and poverty for many centuries. See *Ogygia*, part iii, chapter 82, and the *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1174, note w.

<sup>27</sup> *The Brugh.*—This was Brugh na Boinne, on the River Boyne near Stackallan. Dr. Lynch identifies O’Maollughach of this place as the same as the family called O’Mulledy in his time, but this is clearly an error.

<sup>28</sup> *Dealbhna Eathra.*—This territory comprised the entirety of the present barony of Garrycastle in King’s County, except for the parish of Lusmagh, which belonged to Sil-Anamchy. The family of Mag Cochlain retained their territory until they became extinct at the beginning of this century, when they were succeeded by the O’Dalys and Armstrongs, who were descended from female branches. The last chief of the name was locally called the *Maw*, that word being a diastole or lengthening of the prefix *Mac*. See the *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1178, 1572, and 1601; and also De Burgo’s *Hibernia Dominicana*, p. 305; and *Ogygia*, part iii, chapter 82.

<meta>This page continues the scholarly annotations regarding the territories and tribes of Meath, specifically detailing the transition of territories from Irish clans to Anglo-Norman families and the geographical boundaries of the Mageoghegan family's lands.</meta>

<page-num>viii</page-num>
<header>O'DUBHAGAIN.</header>

^29 *Cuircne*, now the barony of Kilkenny West, in the county of Westmeath; but the name of O’Tolairg is now unknown. Shortly after the English invasion, this territory was taken from the O’Tolairgs and became the ancestral inheritance of the Anglo-Norman family of Dillon, for whom the Irish bards attempted to create an Irish pedigree, which is given in all the modern peerages.

^30 *Cinel-Fiachach*, usually anglicized Kenaliaghe in Anglo-Irish documents. The territory of the Cinel-Fiachach, Mageoghegan’s country, originally extended from Birr, in the present King’s county, to the hill of Uisnech, in Westmeath; but subsequently the family of O’Molloy, who were a junior branch of the Cinel-Fiachach, became independent of the Mac Eochagains; and the original territory of the Cinel-Fiachach was divided into two parts, of which O’Molloy retained the southern portion, and Macgeoghegan the northern, which preserved the original name of the clan and was considered to be of the same extent as the barony of Moycashel, in the county of Westmeath. In an old map made in the year 1567, published with the Third Part of the State Papers, the situation of Mageoghegan’s country is described as follows:—

> “Mc Eochagan’s country, called Kenaliaghe, contains in length 12 miles, and in breadth 7 miles. It lies midway between the fort of Faly (i.e. Philipstown) and Athlone, five miles distant from either of them, and also five miles distant from Mullingar, which lies northward of it. The said Mac Eochagan’s country is of the county of Westmeath, situated in the upper end thereof bending towards the south part of the said county; and on the other side, southward of it, is O’Molloy’s country. And on the south-east of it lies Offaly; and on the east side joins Tyrrell’s country, also known as Fartullagh. On the north side lies Dalton’s country, and O’Melaghlin’s country on the west side, between it and Athlone, where a corner of it joins with Dillon’s country.”

The late Sir Richard Nagle inherited the property of the last chieftain of this family, from whom he was maternally descended. Another branch of this family, who later changed the name to O’Neill, was removed by Cromwell to the castle of Bunowen, in the west of the county of Galway, where they possessed a considerable tract of property, which was lately sold under the Incumbered Estates’ Court. The name is now usually written Geoghegan without the Mac, and sometimes Gahagan and Gegan. Richard Mageoghegan, who defended the castle of Dunboy in 1602; Connell Mageoghegan, of Lismoyny, who translated the *Annals of Clonmacnoise*—

It includes a 1567 map description of "Kenaliaghe" and notes on the family's later history, including their relocation to Galway and the evolution of their surname.</summary>

³¹ *Sept of Enda.*—*Cinel-Enda* was a small territory near the hill of Uisnech, in Westmeath. *Ogygia*, part iii., c. 85. Mag Ruaire, the chief of this territory, was descended from Enna Finn, the youngest son of Niall of the Nine Hostages, monarch of Ireland at the beginning of the fifth century. The name Mag Ruaire is now unknown. The various families now called Rourke, without the prefix of O or Mac, are believed to be of the O'Rourkes of Breffney, but some of them may be of this family of Kinel-Enda. See Miscellany of the Irish Archaeological Society, pp. 234, 287.

³² *Tuath-Buadha.*—The situation of this territory is now unknown. The family name, O'Cairbre, still exists and is anglicized Carbery.

³³ *Cinel-Aenghusa.*—The exact situation of this tribe is unknown. The name O'h-Eochadha is now anglicized Hoey and Hoy. In this form, it is very common in East Meath.

³⁴ *Dealbhna Beg*, that is, Little Delvin. This is supposed to be the barony of Demi-Fore in East Meath. See *Ogygia*, part iii., c. 82. The surname O'Maolcallann is now anglicized Mulholland. See *Leabhar na gCeart*, p. 183.

³⁵ *Teathbha.*—The name of this territory is generally Latinized as Teffia, and anglicized as Taffa, Teffa, and Teffa-land, by Connell Mageoghegan in his translation of the *Annals of Clonmacnoise*. In St. Patrick's time, Teffia was a large territory extending into the present counties of Westmeath and Longford, and divided by the river *Eithne*, now the Inny, into two parts, north and south; the former including the greater part of the present county of Longford, and the latter the western half of the county of Westmeath. Its chief lord, O'Cartharnaigh, descended from Maine, the fourth son of Niall of the Nine Hostages, afterwards took the name of Sinnach or Fox, which is still retained. For many centuries, however, the country of the O'Caharneys or Foxes was confined to one small barony, namely, the district of Muinter-Tadhgain, which was formed into the barony of Kilcoursey and placed in the King's County. See Patent Roll of Chancery, 42 Eliz., and the Covenant between Mageoghegan and Fox, printed in the Miscellany of the Irish Archaeological Society, p. 185. Darcy Fox, Esq., of Foxville, in the county of Meath, is believed to be the head of this family. The Foxes of Foxhall, in the county of Longford, are also of this family.

family, and descend from Sir Patrick Fox, who, as appears from the State Papers, was Clerk to the Privy Council of Dublin from 1588 to 1610, and one of the Commissioners for Inquiring into Defective Titles in 1607. His son, Nathaniel Fox, is the ancestor of the family of Foxhall. See the *Miscellany of the Irish Archaeological Society*, etc., pp. 188, 189, and *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1207, note *z*, p. 156.

36 *Mag Cuinn*, more usually O'Cuinn, anglicized O'Quin. This family is to be distinguished from O'Quin of Muinter-Iffernan, in Thomond. The territory of Muintir-Gilgain was distributed among the baronies of Ardagh, Moydow, and Shrule, in the county of Longford. The townlands of which it consisted are specified in an inquisition taken at Ardagh on the 4th of April, in the tenth year of the reign of James I, which found that thirty-five small cartrons of Montergalgan then belonged to O'Ferrall Bane, and seventeen and a half cartrons of the same measure to O'Ferrall Boy's part of the county of Longford. The O'Quins, now Quins, of this territory, have been living for many centuries in poverty and obscurity in their native territory, and have lost all traditions of their former greatness.

37 *O'Confiacla.*—This name is now obsolete, and no anglicized form of it has yet been identified.

38 *O'Lachtnain*, now usually Loughnan; but some families have changed it to Loftus, while others have made it O'Loughlin and MacLoughlin. This family has been in obscurity for several centuries.

39 *O'Muireagan*, now usually anglicized Morgan. The family sank into obscurity soon after the English invasion.

40 *Well have they ordained the seasons*, that is, by their righteous governments. It was the belief among the ancient Irish that when righteous princes reigned, the seasons were genial and the fruits of the earth grew in great abundance. See *Battle of Magh Rath*, p. 100, note *c*, and *Transactions of the Gaelic Society of Dublin*, vol. i, pp. 12, 13.

41 *Comar*, that is, the confluence. This was probably the territory around the Comar of Clonard, where the stream called the Blackwater falls into the river Boyne. The O'Flannagans of this territory, who were sometimes lords of all Teffia, are now unknown. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1034, 1153. They are to be distinguished from various other O'Flannagans seated in different parts of Ireland.

42 *Breaghmhaine*, now the barony of Brawney, in the west of the county of Westmeath. The O'Breens of this territory are still respectable, but they have lately changed the anglicized form of the name to O'Brien.

<page-num>xi</page-num>
<header>TRIBES OF MEATH.</header>

43 *Mac Conmeadha*, now anglicized Mac Namee; but the family has been long dispersed. The situation of the territory of Muinter-Laedhacain is not determined.

44 *Mag Aedha*, now anglicized Magee. The exact situation of Muinter-Tlamain has not been ascertained.

45 *Mac Taidhg*, now usually anglicized Mac Teige, and changed by some to Montague. The exact situation of Muinter-Siorthachain remains to be found out.

46 *Calraighe*, anglicized Calry. This name is still retained and applied to a territory coextensive with the parish of Ballyloughloe, in the barony of Clonlonan, county of Westmeath. Ballyloughloe was for many centuries the chief seat of Magawley, chief of Calry-an-chala. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, p. 1095. The lands belonging to the different members of this family in the seventeenth century are described in an inquisition taken at Mullingar on the 14th of April, 1635, and in another taken on the 14th of May, in the 27th year of Charles II. The editor examined this territory in the year 1837, and took notes of the following particulars, which are perhaps worth preserving:—

1. The lake from which Ballyloughloe derived its name, now nearly dried up. 2. Magawley’s Castle, of which only one vault remains. 3. Dun-Egan Castle, a mere ruin, situated to the east of the village of Ballylong. 4. The site of a small abbey, near Magawley’s Castle. 5. Ruins of a small chapel, near the modern church. 6. A conspicuous green moat of great antiquity. 7. The castle of Carn. 8. The castle of Creeve. 9. The castle of Cloghmareschall. 10. The castle of Moydrum.

The Magawleys of this district are to be distinguished from the Mac Awleys of Fermanagh, and also from those of the county of Cork, who are of a totally different race and even name from those of Calry. The late Count Magawley of Frankford, in the King’s County, was the last chief of this family who lived in Ireland.

47 *Muinter-Maoilsionna.*—O’Flaherty places this tribe in the territory of Cuirenia, now the barony of Kilkenny West. See *Ogygia*, part iii., c. 85. Their ancestor was called Maeltsinna, that is, chief of the Shannon, from the situation of his territory near the river Shannon. The name Mac Carrghamhna is now usually anglicized Mac Carron or Mac Carroon. Connell Mageoghegan renders it Mac Carhon in his translation of the *Annals of Clonmacnoise*. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1182, note *k*.

48 *Corca-Adhamh*, sometimes called Corca Adain. This territory

<meta>This page continues the detailed genealogical and topographical annotations for the tribes of Meath, specifically following the description of the Magawley family's territories and transitioning into the history of the O'Dalaigh (Daly), O'Muireadhaigh (Murray), and O'Scolaighe (Scully) families.</meta>

<page-num>xii</page-num>
<header>O'DUBHAGAIN.</header>

adjoined the barony of Corcaree in County Westmeath and is included in the present barony of Magheradernon. The name O'Dalaigh is now anglicized as O'Daly, but more generally Daly. The family is of the lineage of Maine, son of Niall of the Nine Hostages. Shortly after the English invasion, this family, who followed the poetic or bardic profession, became dispersed and settled in several parts of Ireland. See *Tribes of Ireland*, pp. 1 to 15. Mr. Owen Daly of Mornington, in the barony of Corkaree, was believed to be the senior member of the O'Dalys of Westmeath. Of this family was the famous poet, Donough Mor O'Daly of Finnyvara, in the barony of Burren and County Clare. His descendants moved to Hy Many in the latter part of the fifteenth century, where they acquired considerable property after the Revolution of 1688. Even before that period, the head of this branch of Hy Many, Denis Daly of Carrownekelly, Esq., in County Galway, was second Justice of the Court of Common Pleas and a Privy Councillor in the reign of James II. "He continued," says Lodge, "to fill this position during the Revolution with such impartiality and integrity (in those difficult times) that it added luster to his judicial character." The representative of this gentleman at the end of the eighteenth century was the Right Honorable Denis Daly, for many years a Member of Parliament for County Galway in the Irish Parliament, distinguished for his eloquence and ability, and characterized by Grattan as "one of the best and brightest characters Ireland ever produced." His eldest son, James, after also representing County Galway for many years in Parliament, was raised to the Peerage of Ireland on June 6th, 1845, with the title of Baron Dunsandle and Clanconall. Of this family also was Denis Bowes Daly, Esq., who succeeded as one of the joint heirs of the last head of the Mac Coghlans. He was one of the most polished, refined, and elegant gentlemen that ever came from the Irish race; he once received an income of £20,000 per year but died a pauper in the early part of the nineteenth century.

51 *<term>Ui Mac-Uais</term>*, a tribe of the race of Colla Uais, monarch of Ireland in the fourth century, now the barony of Moygoish, in the county of Westmeath. The name of O’Comraidhe is still extant, but for many centuries has been reduced to obscurity and poverty. In the sixteenth century, it was anglicized as Cowry. It is now more usually Corry and Curry, but this form is to be distinguished from O’Corra of Ulster, as well as from O’Comhraidhe of Thomond, and O’Comhraidhe of Corca-Laighe, in the south of the county of Cork, all of which are usually anglicized as Curry.

52 *O’h-Aedha*.—This name still exists in Meath, but is always anglicized as Hughes; Aedh and Hugh being generally considered the same name. The boundaries of Eastern Tir-Teathfa cannot now be determined.

53 *O’Cearbhail*, now O’Carroll. This family cannot now be distinguished from the O’Carrolls of Ely, in the King’s county, or from the O’Carrolls of Oriel. Their history is unknown; they sank into obscurity at an early period.

54 *O’Duinn*.—This family has also been totally unknown for centuries. It cannot be distinguished from the O’Duinns, or Dunns, of Iregan.

55 *Southern Breagh*.—The family of MacGillaseachlainn, which is to be distinguished from the more royal family of O’Maelseachlainn, is now, and has been for centuries, totally unknown. They sank into obscurity and poverty shortly after the English invasion. They are mentioned by the *Four Masters*, A.D. 1121, 1160, and 1171.

56 *Cairbre Gabhrain*.—Cairbre Gabhra was the ancient name of the barony of Granard, in the north of the county of Longford. See *Four Masters*, A.D. 1405, note ˢ. O’Ronain of this territory is now unknown. See the *Miscellany of the Archæological Society*, vol. i. pp. 145, 146.

57 *Lesser Gailenga of Breagh*.—This territory was in Bregia, and north of the River Liffey. The church of Glas Naidhin, now Glasnevin, was in it. The name O’h-Aenghusa is now anglicized as Hennessy. See *Four Masters*, for the years 825, 937, 1003, 1145. This family sank into obscurity at an early period, and is now unknown.

58 *Fine Gall*, that is, the district of the Galls, or foreigners, now Fingal, a territory comprising that portion of the county of Dublin lying to the north of the River Liffey. The family of MacGillamocholmog, so famous in the history of Leinster, and particularly in that of Dublin, where they achieved complete mastery over the Danes, was of the same race as the O’Byrnes

<summary>This page provides historical and genealogical annotations for several Irish tribes, tracing their modern anglicized names—such as Hughes, O’Carroll, and Hennessy—and noting the historical territories they occupied across Meath, Westmeath, and Dublin.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the genealogical notes regarding the descendants of the Leinster tribes, specifically the MacGillamocholmog family, and begins detailed historical notes on the district of Tuilen and the saint Cairnech.</meta>

<page-num>xiv</page-num>
<header>xiv O'DUBHAGAIN.</header>

and O'Tooles of Leinster. They descended from Dunchadh, the brother of Faelan, who was the ancestor of the O'Byrnes of Leinster. The progenitor from whom they derived their hereditary surname was Gillamocholmog, son of Dunchadh, son of Lorcan, son of Faelan, son of Murcadhach, son of Bran, son of Faelan, son of Dunchadh, (*a quo* <note>from whom</note> the tribe of the Ui Dunchadha <note>descends</note>), son of Murchadh, son of Bran Mut. This family was originally settled in that part of County Dublin through which the River Dothair or Dodder flows, but after they gained control over the Danes of Dublin, their influence extended over the Danish territory of Fingal. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1044, and Gilbert's *History of Dublin*, vol. i. p. 403–408.

^59^ *O'Dunchadha.*—This was the tribe name of the family of MacGillamocholmog, as mentioned in the previous note. Here O'Dugan, who had no local knowledge of the district of Fingal, has converted the tribe name of Ui Dunchadha into a separate family name; however, there never was any such hereditary surname in this territory. Dr. Reeves has provided the following note on the district of Ui Dunchadha. Within it were located the following churches: Cill Cele Christ (Irish Calendar, March 3); Cill Mochritoc, on the banks of the Dodder, that is, Achadh Finnich (May 11); Cill-namanach, now Killnamanagh, in the parish of Tallaght (Dec. 31). MacGillamocholmog's land extended southwards to Glen Umerim (or Glanunder, now Ballyman), on the borders of the counties of Dublin and Wicklow. For a notice of the family of MacGillamocholmog, see *History of the City of Dublin*, by J. T. Gilbert, vol. i. pp. 230, 403.

^60^ *Tuilen*, now Dulane, an old church and parish near the town of Kells, in County Meath. The west end of the present remains of Dulane church is exceptionally ancient and notable for its doorway, built of huge unhewn stones topped by an enormous lintel. The three septs mentioned here, called the Congregation of Cairnech, are now completely unknown. St. Cairnech, who is still remembered as the patron saint of Tuilen, was not a native of Ireland, but of Cornwall; and Colgan supposes him to be the same as St. Cernach or Carantach, whose feast day in the Calendar of the British Church is the 16th of May, and who lived about a century before the other St. Cairnech, having been, as it is said, a contemporary of St. Patrick. See Colgan's *Trias Thaum.*, p. 231, and *Acta Sanctorum*, p. 783. St. Cairnech's day, in the Irish Calendar, is the 16th of May, as it is in the British Calendar. His life, written in Latin, which makes frequent and very interesting mention of his connection with Ireland, is preserved in the British Museum (MS.

Cotton, Vesp. See Reverend Rice Rees’ *Essay on the Welsh Saints*, pages 209-211; also, Dr. Todd’s edition of the *Historia Britonum*, page cxi. The following extracts from his Life will explain the title of St. Cairnech to the place he holds in the Irish Calendar:—

> “Deinde perrexit ad Hiberniam insulam, Patricio antecedente. . . .
> “Perrexit Carantocus ad dexteram partem, Patricius autem ad sinistram, et dixerunt ut convenirent una vice in anno.

> “Beati Cernachi opera leguntur in Hibernia, per totam patriam, sicut leguntur in Roma beati Petri apostoli prodigia.

> “Et ille solus perrexit ad Hiberniam insulam, et sepultus est 17 Kl. Junii (May 16), in civitate sua præclara, et optima præ omnibus civitatibus suis, quæ vocatur *Civitas Chernach*.”

The only family of the three septs of Tuilen now remaining is O’Muirchertaigh, which is probably the name now anglicized as Murtagh, and is very common in the counties of Meath and Monaghan.

---

^61^ *Uladh* is used here to denote the province of Ulster, though for many centuries before the English invasion *Uladh* was applied to that part of the province of Ulster situated to the east of Glen Righe and of the Lower Bann and Loch Neagh, now represented by the counties of Down and Antrim—a territory into which the ancient Ulla were driven by the three Collas in A.D. 333.

^62^ *Tailltin*, now Teltown, in the county of Meath, nearly midway between the towns of Kells and Navan, celebrated in ancient Irish history for its fairs and public games. See Reeves’ *Adamnan*, page 194.

^63^ *Breaghmhagh.*—This is a transposed form of the name Magh Breagh, a famous plain in East Meath.

^64^ *Oileach*, now Greenan-Ely, near Lough Swilly, in the barony of Inishowen, county of Donegal. It was one of the ancient seats of the kings of Ulster. See the ruins of this fort described in the Ordnance Memoir of the parish of Templemore.

^65^ *Race of Eoghan*, that is, the descendants of Eoghan, son of Niall of the Nine Hostages, monarch of Ireland, who died A.D. 406.

It also includes topographical notes on ancient Ulster, Tailltin, and the seat of the northern kings at Oileach.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the historical and genealogical annotations of O'Dugan's work, providing detailed commentary on the O'Neill, MacLachlainn, and O'Hagan families and their ancestral territories in Ulster.</meta>

<page-num>xvi</page-num>
<header>O'DUBHAGAIN.</header>

⁶⁶ *Royal O’Neill.*—The O’Neills were the most powerful family in Ulster in O’Dugan’s time; but at the period of the English invasion, and for a century or two later, the MacLachlainns were more powerful. A branch of this latter family moved with the O’Donnells to the county of Mayo around the year 1679, where they still hold the rank of gentlemen.

⁶⁷ *Two tribes of the sovereignty.*—Muircheartach MacLachlainn, who founded the abbey of Newry around the year 1160, was one of the last monarchs of Ireland, *with resistance*, after Brian Borumha assumed the Irish monarchy. None of the O’Neill family have been kings of Ireland since his time. See *Dublin Penny Journal*, vol. i., p. 102.

⁶⁸ *Ten sons of Eoghan.*—See Mac Firbis’s *Genealogies of the Cinel Eoghain*.

⁶⁹ *Cianachta*, now the barony of Keenaght, in the county of Londonderry. This territory belonged to the O’Conors of Gleann Geimhin, who were of the race of Cian, son of Oilioll Olum, King of Munster in the third century; but they were dispossessed by the O’Cathains (or O’Kanes), of the race of Eoghan, a short time before the English invasion.

⁷⁰ *O'Conchobhair*, now anglicized as O’Conor. There are families of this name and race still living in the barony of Keenaght. The late Reverend Hugh O’Conor, Parish Priest of Culdaff in Inishowen, and Hugh O’Conor of Belfast, were of this family.

⁷¹ *O’Duibhdhiorma*, now anglicized as Diarmid, and sometimes changed to MacDermott. The name still exists in the county of Donegal.

⁷² *Bredach*, a territory comprising about the eastern half of the barony of Inishowen, in the county of Donegal. The name is still preserved in that of a glen and a small river which flows into Lough Foyle. “Bredach is a small river of the Inishowen peninsula, which discharges into the bay of Lough Foyle at Maghbile <note>Moville</note>.” — *Trias Thaum.*, p. 145, 185. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1122. The family of O’Duibhdhiorma disappeared from history about A.D. 1454.

⁷³ *Tulach Og*, meaning *Hill of the youths*, now Tullaghoge, a small village in the parish of Desertcreat, barony of Dungannon, and county of Tyrone. This is the place where the O’Neill was inaugurated. In the year 1602, the Lord Deputy Mountjoy remained here for five days and “broke down the chair in which the O’Neills used to be inaugurated, which was made of stone and set in the open field.” See Fynes Moryson, *Rebellion of Hugh Earl of Tyrone*, book iii. c. 1; and *Dublin Penny Journal*, vol. i., p. 208.

⁷⁴ *Oh-Ogain*, now O’Hagan. The site of the ancient residence of O’Hagan is to be seen on a gentle hill a short distance to the east of the village

<meta>This page continues the detailed annotations on the tribes of Ulster, focusing on the O’Hogan, O’Gormley, O’Donnellan, and MacRory families, among others, and their historical territories.</meta>

<page-num>xvii</page-num>
<header>TRIBES OF ULSTER.</header>

of Tullaghoge. It is a large circular fort, surrounded by deep trenches and earthworks.

75 *Another O'Hogan.*—This family is unknown. There are some families of this name in the counties of Tyrone and Londonderry, supposed to be different from the O'Hagans, but their history is forgotten.

76 *O'Gairmleadhaigh*, now O'Gormleys. This family has remained in obscurity since the Plantation of Ulster in 1609. They were originally located in the present barony of Raphoe, County Donegal, but being driven from there at an early period by the O'Donnells, they established themselves on the east side of the River Foyle, where they retained a considerable territory until 1609. On an old map of Ulster, preserved in the State Paper Office in London, O'Gormley's country is shown as extending from near Derry to Strabane.

77 *Race of Moen.*—Cinel-Moen. This was the tribe name of the O'Gormleys, and also became the name of their country, according to Irish custom.

78 *O'Domhnallains*, anglicized as O'Donnellan. This family sank into obscurity at an early period and can no longer be identified.

79 *O'Donnagains*, now Donegan, without the prefix O'. This name still exists but is obscure. It is to be distinguished from various other families who bore the same name.

80 *MacMurchadha*, now MacMorrow and MacMurray, and some have changed it to Morell. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1172.

81 *MacDuinnchuain*, unknown at present.

82 *MacRuaidhri*, now anglicized as MacRory, and sometimes translated as Rogers, by which the origin of the race is disguised. A branch of this family became <term>herenachs</term> of the parish of Ballynascreen, in the barony of Loughinsholin, County Londonderry, in the old church of which there is a curious monument to the family, with an epitaph and armorial bearings.

83 *Teallach Ainbhith.*—The exact situation is not yet determined.

84 *Muinter-Birn.*—This is still the name of a district in County Tyrone, adjoining the barony of Trough in County Monaghan, and the name is preserved in that of a Presbyterian parish. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1172, note º.

85 *Cinel-Eachach*, written as Corca Each in the prose version. The race of Eochaidh, son of Eoghan, were located in the present barony of Loughinsholin, County Londonderry, where the Muinter Cheallaigh, or O'Kellys, are still numerous; one branch of them lived in the valley of Glenconkeyne.

86 *O'Ciarain*, now anglicized as Kerrins. The name Fearamaighe signifies

<meta>This page continues the detailed annotations on the historical tribes and families of Ulster, specifically focusing on those mentioned in O'Dubhagain’s work, tracing their territories and the modern forms of their names.</meta>

<page-num>xviii</page-num>
<header>O'DUBHAGAIN.</header>

"men of the plain," but their situation is now unknown. The Siol Tighearnaigh, or Tierneys, are now unknown.

87 *Magh Iotha*, that is, the plain of Ith, now the Lagan, a beautiful tract in the barony of Raphoe, containing the church of Donaghmore. See Colgan's *Trias Thaum.*, pp. 144, 181 ; and *Leabhar na gCeart*, p. 124. The families of O'Maiolbreasail and O'Baoighill, of the race of Eoghan, are now unknown in this territory, and must have fallen into obscurity at an early period, as the Irish annalists have preserved no record of them. The O'Baoighills or O'Boyles, of the race of Conall, are a different family, and are still well known.

88 *O'Cuinns*, now Quins, very numerous in Tyrone.

89 *O'Cionaiths*, now Kennys.

90 *Cinel Binnigh.*—These were the descendants of Eochaidh Binnech, son of Eoghan, son of Niall of the Nine Hostages. These three tribes of Cinel Binnigh were located in ancient Tyrone, on the east of the River Foyle, but their exact locations have not yet been determined. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, for the years 1030, 1053, 1068, 1075, 1076, 1078, 1081, 1181. The O'Donnells of the race of Eoghan are now unknown, and seem to have disappeared from history before the English invasion.

91 *O'Duibhduanna*, now unknown.

92 *O'h-Aghmaill*, now anglicized as Hamill, still a common name in Tyrone.

93 *O'h-Eitigein*, now anglicized as Magettigen by a change of O' for Mac, which is not uncommon. The positions of these three tribes cannot now be placed on the map of *Cinel-Eoghain*.

94 *O'Maolfothartaigh*, unknown.

95 *O'Heodhusa*, now Hosey or Hussey, but generally transformed to Oswell, in the county of Fermanagh. This family later became a bardic family and migrated to Fermanagh, where they were poets to the Maguires.

96 *O'Hogains*, now Hogans; but they cannot be distinguished from other families of the same name in Tyrone.

97 *Carraic Brachaidhe*, now *Carrickabraghy*, a territory which included the northwestern portion of the barony of Inishowen, county Donegal. The name is still applied to a castle located at the northwest side of the peninsula of Doagh. The family of O'Maoilfabhail, now anglicized as Mulfaal, and sometimes MacPaul, are still numerous, but the other two families are unknown.

98 *Extended to the wave*, that is, from Lough Swilly to Lough Foyle.

99 *Eanach.*—Location unknown: but somewhere in the barony of Strabane, county of Tyrone.

<page-num>xix</page-num>
<header>TRIBES OF OIRGHIALLA.</header>
<sig>C 2</sig>

<sup>100</sup> *O’Murchadhas*, now Murphys. There are families of this name from various stocks in different parts of Ireland, but they cannot now be distinguished. Don Patricio O’Murphy, the steward of the Duke of Wellington’s estate in Spain, is the only man living who retains the O’ in this name.

<sup>101</sup> *O’Mellains*, now Mellans and Millans. This family was, for a time, the keeper of the bell of St. Patrick called *Clog-an-edachta*. They were located in the parish of Donaghmore, in the territory of Imchlair, near Dungannon, county of Tyrone. See Annals of the Four Masters, A.D. 1356, 1425. Also Tripartite Life of St. Patrick, part ii. c. 142.

<sup>102</sup> *Cinel Feradhaigh.*—This territory comprised the barony of Clogher, in the county of Tyrone, and was the patrimonial inheritance of the family of MacCathmhaoil (MacCawell), descendants of Fergal, son of Muireadhach, son of Eoghan. The MacCawells are famous in Irish history for their learning and the many dignitaries they provided to the church, but they are now very much reduced, and many of the sept seek to hide their ancient origins by anglicizing their name as Caulfield! It is usually Latinized as Cavellus, and some of the clan still retain the form MacCawell; but the greater number of them make it either Camphill, Campbell, Caulfield, or Howell. See Annals of the Four Masters, A.D. 1185. A distinguished branch of this family, who changed the name to Caulfield, settled in the county of Wicklow, where they still retain considerable property: their pedigree is well known. The family O’Fiachra and the other septs of this territory are unknown or disguised under various anglicized forms. The other septs of Cinel-Fearadhaigh cannot now be distinguished.

<sup>103</sup> *Oirghialla.*—This great sept was descended from the three Collas, who conquered the ancient Ultonians and took from them that portion of the province of Ulster lying westward of Glenn Righe, Lough Neagh, and the Lower Bann. The country of this sept originally comprised the greater part of Ulster, but for many centuries it was confined to the present counties of Louth, Armagh, and Monaghan. The descendants of Eoghan, son of the monarch Niall of the Nine Hostages, deprived them of the present counties of Londonderry and Tyrone shortly after the introduction of Christianity.

<sup>104</sup> *And their hostages.*—This is a kind of pun to create a rhyme. The *Oirghialla* are said to have been so called because their hostages were detained in golden fetters.

<sup>105</sup> *O’Cearbhaill.*—This family is still quite numerous in the county of Monaghan; but they now write the name Carroll without the prefix O’. They disappear from history about the year 1193, when they were

<page-num>XX</page-num>
<header>O'DUBHAGAIN.</header>

supplanted by other families of the same race, the MacMahons and Maguires.

106 *O' Duibhdara.*—This family also disappeared from history at an early period, and the name is now either totally unknown or disguised under an anglicized form that cannot be identified. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1076, 1097, 1118, 1128.

107 *Mac Mathghamhna*, now MacMahon. Spenser falsely claims that this family was of English descent, being, according to him, a branch of the English family of Fitz-Ursula; but Dr. Keating, in the preface to his History of Ireland, and O'Flaherty (*Ogygia*, III., c. 76, 77), have shown that they are of ancient Irish descent, specifically of the race of Colla da Chrich, son of Eochaidh Daimhlen, son of Cairbre Liffechair, son of Cormac MacAirt. Mr. Shirley, in his account of the dominion of Farney, pp. 147–150, has given the true pedigree of this family.

108 *Mag Uidhir*, now anglicized as Maguire. This family provided the chiefs of Fermanagh from about the year 1264, when they replaced the older chieftains, and continued in power until the reign of James I. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1264 and 1302.

109 *O'Lairgnen*, now anglicized as Largan.

110 *O'Flaithri*, now anglicized as Flattery. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1071, 1147, 1166.

111 *Ui-Tuirtre.*—These people were located to the west of Lough Neagh, in the present county of Tyrone, during St. Patrick’s time; but for many centuries before the English invasion, they occupied a portion of the present county of Antrim. According to Colgan, they gave their name to a deanery in the diocese of Connor, which included the parishes of Racavan, Ramoan, and Donnagorr, and the old churches of Downkelly and Kilgad, as well as the island of Inistoide in Loughbeg, near Toome Bridge. See *Trias Thaum.*, p. 183; *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1176, note z; and *Leabhar na gCeart*, p. 124, note n. The exact limits of the district are given in *Reeves’s Down and Connor*, pp. 82, 292–297.

112 *O'Flainn*, now rendered as O'Lyn by softening the *F*; however, some very incorrectly change it to Lindsay. The pedigree of this famous family, who were the senior branch of the Oirghialla or Clann Colla, is traced to Colla Uais, Monarch of Ireland around the middle of the fourth century.

113 *O' Domhnallain*, now Donnellan without the *O'*. One member of this family was lord of all Ui-Tuirtre in 1015, but they are now little known. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, 1014, 1015.

114 *Ui-Fiachrach Finn*, otherwise called the Ui-Fiachrach of Ardsratha.

<summary>This page provides historical and genealogical annotations for several Irish families, including the MacMahons, Maguires, and O'Lyns, while identifying their traditional territories and correcting historical misconceptions about their origins.</summary>
