<meta>This page continues the detailed annotations on the geographical and ancestral divisions of the Oirghialla, focusing on the territories within Tyrone, Monaghan, and Armagh.</meta>

<page-num>xxi</page-num>
<header>TRIBES OF OIRGHIALLA.</header>

They were settled along the River Derg, in the northwest of County Tyrone, and their territory comprised the parish of Ardstraw and some adjoining parishes now belonging to the diocese of Derry. Ussher states (*Primordia*, p. 857) that the church of Ardstraw, and many other churches of Opheathrach, were taken from the diocese of Clogher and incorporated with that of Derry. This tribe of the Ui-Fiachrach is to be distinguished from that of Connaught. They were descended from Fiachra, son of Erc, the eldest son of Colla Uais, Monarch of Ireland in the fourth century. See O'Flaherty's *Ogygia*, part iii., c. 76; and *Leabhar na gCeart*, p. 121, note i. The name *O'h-Eirc* is now accurately enough rendered as Ercke, but without the prefix O'.

115 *O'Criodain*, now Cregan, without the prefix O'. The level territory of this family still retains its ancient name, being now called Magheracregan. It is situated to the south of the River Derg, in Tyrone, in the territory anciently called Ui-Fiachrach of Ardstraw.

116 *O'Haedha*, now always anglicized as Hughes, without the prefix O'. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1044, 1069. This name is very common in Ulster.

117 *Fearnmaighe*, now Farney, a barony in the county of Monaghan.

118 *O'Caomhain*, now anglicized as Keevan, without the prefix O'.

119 *Magh Leamhna* was the name of a level district in Tyrone, afterwards called the *Closach*. See Colgan's *Trias Thaum.*, pp. 149, 184. It is shown on an old map of Ulster preserved in the State Paper Office, London, as "the country of Cormocke mac Barone," and the river Blackwater is marked as flowing through it. The fort of Augher and the village of Ballygawley are situated within it, with the town of Clogher on its western boundary and the church of Errigal-Keeroge on its northern boundary.

120 *O'Mochoidhen*, called by the Four Masters *O'Machaidhen*. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 997, 1053, 1062, 1110. The name is now unknown. This family sank into obscurity at an early period.

121 *Mughdhorna*, more usually called Crich Mughdhorn, and Latinized as *Provincia Mugdornorum* and *Regio Mugdornorum*. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1457. The *Mugdorni* were the descendants of Mugdorn Dubh, son of Colla Meann.

122 *Oirtheara*, also called *Crich nan-Airthear*, and translated by Probus, in the second life of St. Patrick published by Colgan, as "*Regio Orientalium.*" The people were called *Oirtheara*, or *Orientales*, because they were located in the east of the country of Oirghialla. The name is still preserved in the two baronies of Orior, in the east of County Armagh.

It traces lineages back to figures such as Colla Uais and Colla Meann while citing historical sources like the Annals of the Four Masters and Ussher's Primordia.</summary>

<page-num>xxii</page-num>
<header>O'DUBHAGAIN.</header>

123 *O'hIr*, now usually anglicized as O'Hare and O'Hayer, and sometimes Hare, without the prefix O'.

124 *O hAnluain*, now O'Hanlon, and sometimes Hanlon, without the O'. This family is very numerous in the baronies of Orior.

125 *O'Cosgraigh*, now usually anglicized as Cosgrave and Cosgrove.

126 *Feara Rois*, that is, the Men of Ross. The territory of this tribe comprised the parishes of Carrickmacross and Clonany, in County Monaghan, and parts of the adjoining counties of Meath and Louth, but its exact limits have not been yet determined. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 322; and *Leabhar na gCeart*, p. 154.

127 *Ui-Meith-Macha.*—This sept descended from Muireadhach Méith (the Fat), son of Imchadh, who was the son of Colla da Chrich. They were seated in the parishes of Tullycorbet, Kilmore, and Tehallon, in the barony and county of Monaghan. See *Leabhar na gCeart*, p. 149, and Colgan's *Trias Thaum.*, pp. 151 and 184, note 16.

128 *O'hInnreachtaigh*, now Hanratty, without the O', a family now very numerous in County Monaghan.

129 *Mac Domhnaill*, now MacDonnell. This family still remains in the east of Fermanagh, and is to be distinguished from the MacDonnells of Scotland.

130 *Clann Ceallaigh*, that is, the race of Ceallach, now Clankelly, a barony in the east of County Fermanagh. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1441, 1466, 1484, 1499, 1501.

131 *OBaoigheallain*, now anglicized as Boylan, without the prefix O'. The family is still numerous.

132 *Dartraighe*, now the barony of Dartry, in the west of County Monaghan. See *Leabhar na gCeart*, p. 153, note i.

133 *Ui-Laeghaire of Loch Lir.*—*Loch Lir* was one of the ancient names of Carlingford Lough, between the counties of Down and Louth, but there must have been another lake of the same name; this tribe would appear to have been seated in County Tyrone, to the east of the barony of Lurg, in County Fermanagh. *O'Taichligh* is now anglicized as Tully and Tilly, without the O'.

134 *Muintir Maoilduin*, that is, the family of O'Maoilduin, now Muldoon and Meldon, without the O'. Lurg is a barony in the north of County Fermanagh, where this family is still numerous.

135 *Clann Fearghaile*, that is, the race of Fergal. Its location is not determined.

136 *Tuathratha*, that is, the district of the fort, a well-known tract comprised in the barony of Magheraboy, in County Fermanagh, and now usually anglicized as Tooraah. The family of O'Flannagain are still nume-

numerous in this district, but reduced to the level of cottiers and farmers. See O’Brien’s Irish Dictionary, *under the word* Flannagan.

<sup>137</sup> *Muinter-Pheodachain*, a well-known district in the county of Fermanagh, extending from the mouth of the Arney river to the western extremity of the Belmore mountains. The MacGillafinnens are still numerous in this territory, but they are disguised at present under the anglicized form of Leonard. Though this family is set down among the Oirghialla, they were of the Kinel Connell, and descended from the same stock as the O’Muldorrys. See *Battle of Magh Rath*, p. 335.

<sup>138</sup> *Ui Conghaile*.—This sept was seated in the barony of Knockninny, county Fermanagh. These two last-mentioned septs were dispossessed in the fifteenth century by two branches of the Maguires called the Clann-Awley and the Clann-Caffrey.

<sup>139</sup> *Muintir Maoilruanaidh*, i.e., the family of O’Maoilruanaidh, now Mulrony. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1057, 1189.

<sup>140</sup> *Ui h-Eignigh*. This is probably the name anglicized as Heeney. This family, as well as the O’Mulronys, fell under the power of the Maguires in the thirteenth century, and are now reduced to obscurity and poverty. Cornelius Heeney, of New York, who had acquired vast wealth, died in 1847, unmarried.

<sup>141</sup> *Slopes of Monach*, i.e., the mountains and undulating hills of Fermanagh.

<sup>142</sup> *Triucha Ched of Cladach*, i.e., the Cantred of Claddach, now the barony of Trough, forming the northern part of the county of Monaghan. The name MacCionaith is now anglicized as MacKenna, and the family are very numerous in this barony and in the city of Dublin. This family is not of the race of the Oirghialla, any more than MacGillafinnen of Fermanagh, but of the Southern Hy-Niall, of Meath.

<sup>143</sup> *O’Corbmaic*, now Cormic.—This sept was seated in the barony of Tirkeeran, in the west of the county of Londonderry, whence they were driven by the O’Kanes and other families of the race of Eoghan, son of the monarch Niall of the Nine Hostages, who gradually displaced the Oirghialla.

<sup>144</sup> *Ui-Breasail of Macha*.—This sept, more usually called Clann-Breasail, were seated in the present barony of Oneilland East, in the county of Armagh. For many centuries the MacCanns, who are of the race of Rochadh, son of Colla-da-chrich, were the chiefs of Clann-Breasail, having dispossessed the O’Garveys at an early period. This territory is

<sup>145</sup> *O'Longain*, now anglicized as Langan and Long, without the prefix O'.
<sup>146</sup> *O'Dubheamhna*, now Devany and Devenny.
<sup>147</sup> *O'Conchobhairs*, now Connors.
<sup>148</sup> *Ui-Lorcain*, now Larkin. The boundary line between these two septs cannot now be drawn.
<sup>149</sup> *O'h-Eighnighs*, now O'Heaneys.
<sup>150</sup> *Ui-Eathach*, that is, descendants of Eochaidh. These were not the people of Iveagh, in the county of Down, but a sept of the Oirghialla, descended from Eochaidh, son of Fiachra Casan, son of Colla da Chrich, who were seated in the district of Tuath-Eathach, which comprised the present barony of Armagh. This district is shown on the old map of Ulster just referred to as Tuaghie, and as the country of Owen mac Hugh mic Neale mic Art O'Neill. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1498. The Clann Ruadhagain, or O'Rogans, of this district are still extant, but the Clann-Cearnaigh and O'Domhnaills, or O'Donnells, are unknown, and perhaps extinct.
<sup>151</sup> *Clann-Daimhin*, that is, the family of O'Daimhin, now Devin and Devine, without the prefix O'. A family of this name is mentioned in the *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1427, as chief in Tirkennedy, in Fermanagh.
<sup>152</sup> *Ui-Maoilcraoibhe*.—A family of this name occupied the west side of Knockbreda, near Belfast, in the county of Down; but it is highly probable that this was not their original situation, but that they were driven from a more westerly position by the increasing power of the race of Eoghan. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1490; Stuart's *Armagh*, p. 630; and Reeves's *Down and Connor*, p. 348.
<sup>153</sup> *Little Modharn*.—This territory formed the northern portion of Meath, where it adjoins the county Monaghan. It was otherwise called Mughdhorn Breagh, as being a part of the plain of Magh-Breagh. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 807, 836, 867, 880.
<sup>154</sup> *Ui-Seaain*, correctly Ui Seghain, a people situated to the north of Ardbraccan, in the county of Meath. See the *Tripartite Life of St. Patrick*, part iii., c. xiv., *Trias Thaum.*, p. 152. O'hAinbheth is now anglicized as Hanvey and Hanafey, without the O'.
<sup>155</sup> *Mag-Uidhir*.—Now Maguire. He was head chieftain of Feara Monach, now Fermanagh, in O'Dugan's time, as already remarked, but not before the fourteenth century.

<page-num>XXV</page-num>
<header>TRIBES OF CRAOBH-RUADH.</header>

156 *Ui MacCarthainn*, now the barony of Tirkeeran, in the county of Londonderry. The family of *O'Conaill* of this district is now called *Connell*, without the *O'*; and the family of *O'Colgan* is written *MacColgan*, by a substitution of *Mac* for *O'*. This latter family, upon being dispossessed by the dominant race of *Eoghan*, son of *Niall of the Nine Hostages*, became herenachs of the church lands of Donaghmore, in Inishowen, where, at the foot of Slieve Snaght, the celebrated John Colgan, author of the *Acta Sanctorum Hiberniæ*, was born.

157 *Very great chieftains*.—These two families would appear to have sunk very low by our author's time; for, notwithstanding these high terms applied to them, they are not even once mentioned in the Irish annals as invading territories, fighting battles, founding churches, or doing anything that indicated possessions, power, or dignity. It is strange that he should mention them in such unqualified terms, while he does not even name the distinguished family of *O'Brolchain*.

158 *Craobh Ruadh*, i.e., the *Red Branch*. This was the name of an ancient fortress of the race of *Rudhraighe*; and the ancient *Ulta* continued to be called after this place by the Irish poets for ages after they had been driven from it by the *Oirghialla*.

159 *Kings of Uladh*, i.e., the chieftains of that portion of the ancient province of *Uladh* or *Ulster*, which remained in the possession of the *Clanna Rury*, or ancient *Ultonians*. Their country comprised only that part of the province lying east of Glenree, Lough Neagh, and the Lower Bann.

160 *O' Duinnsleibhe*, otherwise called *MacDuinnsleibhe*, and now anglicized *Donlevy*, without either prefix. This family lost its ancient rank shortly after the English invasion, and a branch of them removed to *Tir-connell*, where they became physicians to *O'Donnell*. Some of them passed into Scotland, where they adopted the names *Dunlief* and *Dunlap*, and others have changed it to *Livingston*. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1149, 1178, 1227, 1395, 1586.

161 *O'h-Eochadha*.—This family was of the same race as *O'Duinnsleibhe*, and also lost its ancient dignity about the same time. It is now anglicized *Haughey*, *Haugh*, and *Hoey*, without the O'. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, 1114, 1164, 1172, 1194.

162 *O'h-Aidiths*.—These are mentioned in the *Annals of the Four Masters* at the years 980, 965, 1005, 1046, 1065, 1094, 1119, 1136, as lords of *Ui-Eathach Uladh*, now *Iveagh*, in the county of Down, but no later notice

163 *O'h-Eochagain*.—The only notice of this family contained in the *Annals of the Four Masters* occurs at A.D. 1281, when a member of it was killed in the battle of Desertcreaght, in Tyrone. The name is now anglicized O'Haughian; and a family of this name, who came from the County Down, is living in Ballymena.

164 *O'Labhradhas*.—This name is now anglicized Lavery, without the O'. See the note on *Magh Rath* below.

165 *O'Leathlabhras*, now Lawlers or Lalors.—This family is mentioned in the *Annals of the Four Masters* in the years 904, 912, and 930 as kings of Dalaradia and Ulidia, but no later notices of them occur. Whether they are the same Lawlers who appear to have been seated at an early period at Dysart Enos, in Laoighis, in the Queen's County, and are also of this race, has not yet been determined. See Reeves's *Down and Connor*, p. 343.

166 *O'Loingsighs*.—Many members of this family appear in the Irish annals as kings of Dalaradia, but the last notice of them occurs in the year 1159. The name is now anglicized Linchy and Lynch.

167 *O'Mornas*.—This family, who were of Connaught origin, afterwards took the name of MacGillamuire, now Gilmore. They are seated in the territory of Ui-Ercachein, in the County Down. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1391; and Reeves's *Down and Connor*, etc., pp. 339, 368.

168 *O'Mathghamhna*, usually anglicized O'Mahony, but there is no such name in Ulidia at present, so we may conclude it to be the name usually written Moghan, Mahon, or Mahan. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1108, 1113, 1114, 1124, 1127, 1149. No later notices of them occur in Irish history. For their descent, see Reeves's *Down and Connor*, p. 362.

169 *Ui Eathach Cobha*.—This sept gave its name to the baronies of Iveagh, in the County Down. For their descent and ancient history, see Reeves's *Down and Connor*, pp. 348, 349, 359.

170 *O'Coinne*, now Kenny and Quin. See Reeves's *Down and Connor*, pp. 79, 367.

171 *O'Gairbhiths*, now Garvys.—For a curious account of this family, see *O'Brien's Irish Dictionary*. The townland of Aughnagon, in the parish of Clonallon, near Newry, in the County Down, was part of their ancient patrimony and remained in their possession until about fifty years ago. See Reeves's *Down and Connor*, p. 367.

172 *O'hAinbhith*, now Hanvey and Hannifey. No notice of this family occurs in the *Annals of the Four Masters*, which contains several entries concerning the O'Hannifeys of Oirghialla.

173 *Mag-Aenghusa*, now anglicized Magennis. The exact location of the territory of Clann Aedha has not yet been determined. During the twelfth century, they rose to power and became the chief lords of the entire country of Iveagh. See Reeves's *Down and Connor*, pp. 348-352.

174 *Cinel Faghartaigh*, now Kinelarty, a barony in the county of Down, of which the MacArtans were the hereditary lords. See O'Flaherty's *Ogygia*, p. 371; Harris's *History of the County of Down*, p. 74; *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1130, 1375, 1493; and Reeves's *Down and Connor*, pp. 213, 214, 215.

175 *Mag Duibheamhna*, now Devany. Dr. Reeves conjectures that Cinel Amhalghadha (Kinel-Awley), the tribe name of this family, may have given its name to the parish of Magherally, anciently Magherawly. See Reeves's *Down and Connor*, p. 368.

176 *O'Morna*.—See note 174, above. This family came originally from Connaught.

177 *Mag Duilechains of Clann Breasail*.—In Dubourdieu's *Statistical Account of the County of Antrim*, p. 627, this territory is described from an old manuscript as follows: "Clanbreasel Mac Coolechan <note>that is, Clann Breasail meg Dhuilechain,</note> (so called to distinguish it from another territory of the same name in the county of Armagh); is a very impenetrable country of wood and bog, inhabited by a sept called the O'Kellies, a very savage and barbarous people, who are given altogether to spoils and robberies."

178 *O'Coltarain of Dal Cuirb*.—Dr. Reeves conjectures that the parish of Ballyculter, at Strangford, in the county of Down, derived its name from this family. It seems to be now extinct, as it is not the same as the family of Coulter, which is of English origin.

179 *Leath Chuinn*, meaning Conn's half, which is a name for the northern half of Ireland.

180 *Brughaidh*, a farmer who kept a house of general hospitality.

181 *Eamhain*.—Our author speaks here as if the Clanna Rury, whom he lists, were still the possessors of the palace of Eamhain and Craebh Ruadh, situated two miles west of Armagh; but his poetic license is too extreme, as they had been driven from there by the Oirghialla about the middle of the fourth century. It is curious to remark that he takes no

<meta>This page continues the annotations on Ulster territories, specifically discussing the lands of the Clanna Rury and Oirghialla, and providing geographical identifications for historical sites and battles.</meta>

<page-num>xxviii</page-num>
<header>O'DUBHAGAIN.</header>

...notice of any district in the present county of Armagh as being in the possession of the Clanna Rury: that territory was, in his time and for some centuries earlier, in the possession of the Oirghialla.

182 *Peaky Boirche*, now the Mourne mountains in the south of the county of Down. See *Tighernach An.*, 611; *Annals of the Four Masters*, pp. 735, 1495; and Reeves's *Down and Connor*, p. 369.

183 *Cuailgne*, now Cooley, a mountainous district in the north of the county of Louth, very famous in Irish history. It was originally a part of Uladh, though it is now in the province of Leinster.

184 *Magh Rath*, now Moira, in the county of Down. In the *Book of Lecan*, fol. 96 *b*, it is stated that the church of Lann Ronan Finn is situated in Corca Ruisen in Magh Rath; and it has now been determined that St. Ronan Finn's church is the present Magheralin, in the county of Down. See the *Feilire Aenguis* at May 21st; see also Reeves's *Down and Connor*, pp. 313, 367; his *Adamnan*, p. 201; *Battle of Magh Rath*, p. 277.

185 *O'Labhradha*, now Lavery, a numerous clan in the parish of Moira. From these lines, it appears that O'Dugan considered the present parish of Moira as the site of the great battle fought here in AD 637. In the early part of the seventeenth century, Tirlagh Oge O'Lawry held several townlands in the present parish of Moira and in the adjacent part of Magheralin. See Reeves's *Down and Connor*, p. 369. This fact, combined with the proximity of the church of St. Ronan Finn, who cursed Suibhne Geilt, makes it highly probable that the plain around the present village of Moira was the scene of the great battle. The late Mr. John Rogan, a local antiquary, wrote a letter on this subject to the Editor in 1842, detailing the local traditions remaining of this battle, but his letter arrived too late to be used in the introductory remarks to the *Battle of Magh Rath*, printed for the Irish Archaeological Society in 1842. The probability is that the fort of Dun Adhmainn was situated in Tir Om Breasail, in the southwest of the barony of Orior; and that the idiot Cuanna set out from there to Newry, and then to Moira, for he is described as advancing *from the southwest*. See *Battle of Magh Rath*, pp. 276, 277. The whole of Mr. Hanna's argument (in his paper on Magh Rath, in the *Ulster Journal of Archaeology*) to prove that the present Moira is not the scene of the great battle, rests on the situation of Clann Breasail, which, being on the south side of Lough Neagh, lies due west of Moira; but he overlooks the fact that O'Dugan connects the Magh Rath of the fierce contention with O'Lavery, and that the parish of Moira is still considered the country of the Laverys.

<summary>This page contains scholarly annotations identifying historical Irish locations, such as the Mourne Mountains and Cooley, and discusses the evidence for locating the Battle of Magh Rath (AD 637) at the modern site of Moira.</summary>
<keywords>O'Dugan, Magh Rath, Moira, O'Labhradha, Lavery, Mourne Mountains, Cuailgne, Cooley, Clanna Rury, Oirghialla, Battle of Magh Rath, Irish archaeology</keywords>

--- Page 243 ---
<meta>This page continues the historical and geographical annotations on Ulster, moving from the territories of the Clanna Rury to those of the Eoghanachs and Cinel-Conaill, while addressing the traditions surrounding Downpatrick and Armagh.</meta>

<page-num>xxix</page-num>
<header>TRIBES OF CRAOBH-RUADH.</header>

186 *Dun da-leathghlas*.—This was one of the ancient names of Downpatrick. See Reeves’s *Down and Connor*, pp. 41, 139, 143, 224, 228, 361, 369.

187 *The clay covered Columb*.—It was generally believed at Down, and throughout Ireland, that St. Patrick, St. Bridget, and St. Columbkille were buried in one tomb at Downpatrick; but this seems to be a fabrication of the twelfth century. For although part of their relics may have been deposited there long after their deaths, there is no evidence that their bodies were ever deposited there in a single tomb. See Reeves’s *Adamnan*, pp. 312, 313, 314, 315. It is very clear from the life of St. Bridget, by Cogitosus, that her body was preserved at Kildare. See Colgan’s *Trias Thaum.*, pp. 523, 524. The body of St. Patrick may have been buried there immediately after his death, but even this is very doubtful. The finding of the relics of the Irish *Trias Thaumaturga* (the Wonder-working Three) at Down in 1185 was an invention devised by Sir John De Courcy and his clergy in that year for the purpose of elevating the status of Down, which had then been recently conquered by the English. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 526 and 1293, note f.

188 *Cumber*.—It is difficult to know which place is referred to here: whether Comber, near Loch Cuan; or Magh-Comair, that is, Muckamore, in the county of Antrim.

189 *Eoghanachs*, that is, the race of Eoghan, son of Niall of the Nine Hostages, who took possession of a large portion of the ancient province of Ulster and who, in O’Dugan’s time, were the most powerful race there.

190 *Ard-Macha*, now Armagh, the chief ecclesiastical city of Ireland. The Archbishop of Armagh ranked in dignity with the monarch of all Ireland.

191 *Their knowledge there*.—This alludes to the great school of Armagh, in which, during the middle ages, many distinguished foreigners received their education.

192 *Cinel-Conaill*, that is, the race of Conall, son of Niall of the Nine Hostages. They were settled in Tirconnell, which in later ages was co-extensive with the present county of Donegal.

193 *Rugged is the land*, alluding to the extensive mountains in the north and west of their territory.

194 *The cataract of Aedh*, that is, the waterfall of Eas Aedha, or the cataract of Red Hugh, now the salmon-leap at Ballyshannon, in the county of Donegal. See *Annals of the Four Masters* (Ed., J. O’D.), A.M. 4518, and A.D. 1184 and 1194.

<summary>This page provides historical and geographical annotations regarding Downpatrick, the alleged joint burial site of Ireland's three patron saints, and the territories of the Eoghanachs and Cinel-Conaill in Ulster. It clarifies the locations of historical landmarks like the school of Armagh and the waterfall at Ballyshannon.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the historical and geographical annotations on the tribes of Ulster, shifting focus from the ecclesiastical centers like Armagh to the hereditary territories and families of Tir-Connell, specifically modern-day County Donegal, as mentioned in O’Dugan’s topographical poem.</meta>

<page-num>xxx</page-num>
<header>O'DUBHAGAIN.</header>

195 *The O' Maoildoridhs, if they were living.* This shows that the O'Muldorys and O'Canannans, who were the chieftains of Tir-Connell preceding the O'Donnells, were either extinct or powerless in O'Dugan's time. At present, there is not a single family of either name in the county of Donegal. For their pedigrees, so far as they can be traced, see *Battle of Magh Rath*, p. 335.

196 *The Clann Daly.*—This was the tribe name of the O'Donnells, who were the head chiefs of Tir-Connell in O'Dugan's time. For their pedigree, see *Battle of Magh Rath*, pp. 336-337, and *Annals of Four Masters*, Appendix, pp. 2377 to 2420. They derived their tribe name of Clann Dalaigh from Dalach, the chief lord of Tir-Connell, who died in the year 868; from his grandson, Domhnall, the O'Donnells have derived their hereditary surname. The original territory of this family was situated between the River Dobhar (or Gweedore) and Swilly. See *Battle of Magh Rath*, p. 157.

197 *Clann Chinnfhaelaidh*, a district in the northwest of the county of Donegal, comprising the parishes of Raymunterdony and Tullaghobegly. See the note to *Annals of Four Masters*, A.M. 3330, p. 18.

198 *Tir-Ainmirech*, now the barony of Boylagh, in the west of the county of Donegal. See *Annals of Four Masters*, A.D. 1343, p. 582, note f.

199 *Tir Baghaine*, that is, the territory of Enna Baghaine, the second son of Conal Gulban, now the barony of Banagh, in the west of the county of Donegal. See *Battle of Magh Rath*, p. 156, note p. It extended from the River Eany, at Inver harbour, to the Dobhar, now the Gweedore river.

200 *O' Baoighill*, now O'Boyle, a family remarkable for their ruddy complexions, still very numerous in the west of the county of Donegal.

201 *Magh-Seiridh*, a plain in the north of the barony of Tirhugh. The family name O'Maoilmaghna is now anglicized as Mullany.

202 *Eas Ruaidh*, that is, the <term>Tricha ched</term> of Eas Ruaidh, or of the Salmon Leap, at Ballyshannon. This is described in a poem preserved in the *Book of Fenagh*, folio 47, as extending from the River Erne to the River Eidhneach, now the Edny. See *Battle of Magh Rath*, p. 158. The name O'h-Aedha is now anglicized as Hughes, but this sept is to be distinguished from several others of the same name in Ulster.

203 *O' Taircheirt.*—This name is now unknown in the county of Donegal. The O'Taircherts are mentioned in the *Annals of Four Masters* in the years 1113, 1197, and 1212, as chiefs of Clann Snedhghile, now Clanelly, a territory in the barony of Raphoe, situated to the west of the town of Letterkenny. The pedigree of this family is not preserved in the Irish genealogical books.

<meta>This page continues the detailed annotations on the territories and families belonging to the Cinel Conaill in modern-day County Donegal, identifying historical land divisions and the subsequent rise of the O’Doherty family over the race of Eoghan.</meta>

<page-num>xxxi</page-num>
<header>TRIBES OF CINEL CONAILL.</header>

$^{204}$ *Clann Neachtain*, another name for the Clann Snedhghaile. The pedigree of this clan is not preserved by the O'Clerys or Duald MacFirbis.

$^{205}$ *MacDubhain*, now anglicized as MacGuane.

$^{206}$ *Cinel-Enna*, that is, the race of Enna or Enda, the sixth son of Conall Gulban. The territory of this clan, usually called Tir-Enda, comprised thirty quarters of land, and is situated in the barony of Raphoe, in the county of Donegal, to the south of Inishowen, and between the arms of Lough Foyle and Lough Swilly. See *Battle of Magh Rath*, p. 156, and *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1175.

$^{207}$ *Gleann-Binnigh*, a valley in the parish of Kilteevoge, situated to the west of Stranorlar, in the county of Donegal. The name MacLoingseachain is now anglicized as Lynch, without the prefix Mac.

$^{208}$ *Fanaid.*—This territory is still well known by this name and forms the northeast part of the barony of Kilmacrenan; it extends from Lough Swilly to Mulroy Lough, and from the sea southward to Rathmelton. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1186, p. 70, note $^s$. O'Breslen was driven from this territory, and the family of MacSweeny Fanaid settled there.

$^{209}$ *Ard-Miodhair.*—The limits of this territory have not yet been determined. In the year 1199, O'Dochartaigh, now O'Dogherty or Doherty, was chief of the territory of Cinel-Enna and Ard-Miodhair. Ard-Miodhair extended westward of Cinel-Enna, in the direction of Glenfinn, in the parish of Kilteevoge. Due to the increasing power and population of the descendants of Conall Gulban, O'Doherty, a very prominent family of that race, became lord of Inishowen and expelled or subdued the families of the race of Eoghan, who had been lords of that territory before him. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1199.

$^{210}$ *Mac Gillatsamhais.*—This name is now either unknown or remains hidden under some anglicized form. The most logical anglicized form of it would be MacIltavish.

$^{211}$ *Ros Guill*, now Rossgull, a well-known promontory in the parish of Mevagh, barony of Kilmacrenan, and county of Donegal, lying between Mulroy Lough and Sheephaven.

$^{212}$ *Ros-Irguill.*—The exact limits have not yet been determined. It adjoined Ros Guill on the west.

$^{213}$ *Fionn-Ros.*—This was the original name of the district now called "the Rosses," situated in the barony of Boylagh, and county of Donegal. O'Furadhrain is now rendered as Farran or Forran.

$^{214}$ *Tuath Bladhach*, now Tuath, anglicized as Doe, a well-known district

<meta>This page continues the annotations on the tribes and territories of Tirconnell (County Donegal) and begins the notes on the section relating to Connaught, identifying lineages such as the Mulligans and Wynnes and noting missing historical records in the Book of Lecan.</meta>

...in the north of the barony of Kilmacrenan, situated between the quarters of Cloghineely and Sheephaven. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1515, p. 1332.

215 *O'Cearnachain*, now rendered Kernaghan, without the O'.

216 *O'Dalachain*, now obsolete.

217 *Tir MacCarthainn*, that is, the territory of the race of Caerthann, son of Fergus, son of Conall Gulban. The Abbé MacGeoghegan places this district to the east of Boylagh, but the present editor does not know on what authority. The genealogy of this race is lost. Neither MacFirbis nor Peregrine O'Clery was able to fill the gap in the Books of Lecan and Ballymote. See *Battle of Magh Rath*, p. 156.

218 *Siol Maolagain*, now anglicized as Mulligan, and by some as Molineaux, without the O'. The family seems to have lost its power in *O'Dugan's* time.

219 *Tir Breasail*, that is, Breasal's land or territory. The location of this territory has not been determined; and the genealogies of O'Donnagain and MacGaibhidh have not been preserved, or at least not yet discovered.

220 *O'Maoilgaoithe*, now anglicized as Mulgeehy and Wynne. This family was originally situated in the parish of Clondavaddock, in the territory of Fanaid, from where they were driven by the MacSweenys. Some families of this name still exist. The late Dr. Thomas Wynne, of Croydon, near London, for whom there is a monument in the church of St. Margaret, was of this race, as the editor was informed by that gentleman's brother.

221 *Clann Fearghaile*.—Location and genealogy unknown, due to the gap in the Book of Lecan already referred to. The MacTighernains of this race are to be distinguished from those of Breifne and Sligo.

The territories and tribes of Tirconnell can never be properly illustrated until the gap in the Book of Lecan is filled.

### NOTES TO THE PORTION RELATING TO CONNAUGHT.

222 *Macha*, that is, Armagh, here used to represent Ulster, by a bold figure of speech.

223 *The Drobhaois*, a river which flows out of Lough Melvin, and, following a west-northwest course, falls into the Bay of Donegal. See Colgan's *Trias Thaum.*, p. 180, note 154; Harris's *Ware*, vol. i., p. 18; and *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1420, p. 843.

<meta>This page continues the annotations on the tribes and territories of Connaught, specifically identifying the geographical boundaries of the plain of Cruachan and the lineages of the O'Conors, O'Rourkes, and other descendant families of the Sil Muireadhaigh.</meta>

<page-num>xxxiii</page-num>
<header>TRIBES OF CONNAUGHT.</header>

224 *The plain of Cruachan*, that is, the plain of *Magh Naoi*, or *Machaire Chonnacht*, in the county of Roscommon, in which Cruachan, the ancient palace of the kings of Connaught, was situated. It lies between the towns of Roscommon and Elphin, and Castlereagh and Strokestown.

225 *Clann-Conchobhair*, that is, the Clan-Conor, the family of the O’Conors of Connaught, who derive their name and descent from Conchobhar, king of Connaught, who died in the year 971 [972].

226 *Cill-ard*, that is, high church, now unknown. There is only one Killard in all Ireland, namely, that in the southwest of County Clare.

227 *Tuaim Dreccoin*, that is, Drecon’s mound or tumulus, now Toomregan, on the frontiers of the counties of Cavan and Fermanagh. See the *Feilire Aenguis* at the 5th of September, and the battle of *Magh-Rath*, p. 283.

228 *The Ui-Fiachrach*, that is, the descendants of Fiachra, the father of Dathi, the last Pagan monarch of Ireland, in the beginning of the 5th century. The chiefs of the northern Ui-Fiachrach, after the establishment of surnames, were the O’Dowdas, and of the southern Ui-Fiachrach, the O’Heynes and O’Shaughnessys.

229 *Ath-Slisen*, otherwise called *Bél atha slisen*, now Belaslishen, a ford on the river Uair, within one mile of the town of Elphin, on the road to Strokestown. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1288, p. 446.

230 *The Ui-Ruairc*, that is, the family of the O’Rourkes. Only three of this family were kings of Connaught, of whom the most distinguished was Art, who was slain in 1046. The other chiefs of the family were lords paramount of Breifne, the present county of Leitrim.

231 *The Sil Muireadhaigh*, that is, the descendants of Muireadhach Muilleathan, king of Connaught, who died in the year 701. The people known by this name were the O’Conors of Magh Naoi and their relatives who, after the establishment of hereditary surnames, branched into various families and spread themselves over the neighboring territories; such as the Mac Dermots, Mac Donoughs, O’Beirnes, O’Flannagans, Mageraghtys, and O’Finaghtys. The O’Conors were the most powerful of all these, though the O’Finaghtys and Mac Dermots were senior to them. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 700, p. 301; and A.D. 1189, p. 87.

232 *Duach Teangumha*, that is, Duach of the brazen tongue. He was king of Connaught and died in the year 499 [500]. He was the son of Fergus, son of Muireadhach Mael, son of Eoghan Sreimh, son of Duach Galach, son of Brian, son of Eochaidh Muighmheadhoin, monarch of Ireland. He was the ancestor of the O’Flahertys of West Connaught, but not of the

²³³ The *Sil-Flaithbheartaigh*, that is, the race of Flaithbheartach, now the O’Flaherty family. For their descent, see *Chorographical Description of Iar Connaught*, p. 364.

²³⁴ *Clann-Cosgraigh*, that is, the race of Coscrach, a sept of the Ui-Bruin-Seola, located east of Galway Bay. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1162, note <gloss>a</gloss>.

²³⁵ *Clann-Maoilruana*.—This was the tribe name of the Mac Dermot family of Moylurg, who descend from Maolruana, the eldest son of Tadhg an eich ghil, king of Connaught, A.D. 1014–1036.

²³⁶ *Clann-Conchobhair*, that is, the family of the O’Conors of Connaught.

²³⁷ *Both one tribe*.—They are both descended from the same ancestor and are both essentially O’Conors.

²³⁸ *Clann-Cathail*, that is, the race of Cathal, the second son of Muireadhach Muilleathan, king of Connaught, who died A.D. 701. This was the tribe name of the O’Flanagans of Magh Aoi, hereditary stewards to the kings of Connaught, whose territory originally included the parishes of Kilmacumshy, Kilcorkey, and Shankill, and the majority of the parishes of Creeve and Elphin. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1289, p. 448, note <gloss>s</gloss>.

²³⁹ *O’Maoilmordha, O’Mulmore*. This family fell into obscurity early on and is either extinct or the name is now obsolete.

²⁴⁰ *O’Carthaigh, O’Carry*, now unknown in Clancahill.

²⁴¹ *O’Mughroin*, now O’Moran, or Moran. This name still exists in the vicinity of Elphin.

²⁴² *O’Maoilbhrenainn*, now anglicized as Mulrenin, without the prefix O’. This family, which shares the same descent as the O’Flanagans, was located in the parish of Baslick, near Ballintober, in County Roscommon. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1097, 1193.

²⁴³ *Clann-Foghartaigh*.—See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1151. Their location has not been determined.

²⁴⁴ *Clann-Murthuile*.—The location of this tribe has not been determined. The name O’Maonaigh is now anglicized as O’Meeny and Mooney.

²⁴⁵ *Mag-Oireachtaigh*, now anglicized as MaGeraghty and Geraghty. This family was located in Magh-Naoi before the English invasion; however, in 1585, the head of the family was settled in Hy-Many. See *Tribes and Customs of Hy-Many*, p. 19.

²⁴⁶ *Clann-Conmhaigh*, now locally called Clanconoo. The name is now applied to a territory situated on the west side of the River Suck, in the barony of Ballimoe and County Galway; but it formerly extended to the east of the same river, in what is now County Roscommon. Shortly after the English invasion, this territory came into the possession of a branch of the De Burgos, the head of whom was called Mac David, who was descended on his mother's side from the Finaghtys.

We are informed by Duald Mac Firbis that Conmhach, the ancestor of the Clann-Conmhaigh, was the eldest son of Muiredhach Muillethan, king of Connaught, who died in 701; and that as a result of this seniority, the O'Finaghty family enjoyed considerable privileges under the kings of Connaught. Namely, he was entitled to drink the first cup at all the king's banquets, and all the descendants of the other sons of Muiredhach were to rise up before the senior of the race of Conmhach. He adds that the O'Finaghtys held forty-eight ballys (townlands) lying on both sides of the Suck before the English invasion. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1232, p. 265, note <gloss>r</gloss>.

²⁴⁷ *Clann-Murchadha*.—This was the tribe name of that sept of the Finaghtys located on the east side of the River Suck, in County Roscommon. This territory comprised twenty-four ballys, or ancient Irish townlands. See *Genealogies, Tribes, &c. of Ui-Fiachrach*, p. 108, note <gloss>b</gloss>; and *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1225, p. 237.

²⁴⁸ *Ui-Diarmada*, otherwise *Clann-Diarmada*. This was the tribe name of the O'Concannon family, chiefs of Corcamoe, in the barony of Killian and County Galway. The head of this family had his seat at Kiltullagh, in the parish of Kilkerrin, locally called the parish of Corcamoe. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1382, note <gloss>u</gloss>.

²⁴⁹ *Clann-Tomaltaigh*.—This sept was located in Magh Naoi, but their position has not been determined. The name Mac Murchadha is now obsolete in County Roscommon.

²⁵⁰ *Siol-Fallamhain*, that is, the race of Fallamhan, or the O'Fallon family. Their territory of *Clann-Uadach* comprised the parishes of Camma and Dysart, in the barony of Athlone and County Roscommon. O'Fallon had his chief residence at Milltown, in the parish of Dysart, in 1585. See *Tribes and Customs of Hy-Many*, p. 19.

²⁵¹ *Clann-Maoilruana*.—This was the tribe name of the Mac Dermots of the Sil-Murry race.

²⁵² *Magh-Luirg*, usually anglicized as Moylurg, a territory included in the present barony of Boyle, in County Roscommon.

²⁵³ *Airtech*, a territory comprising the parish of Tibohine, in the present

254 *Tir-Oilella*, that is, the land or territory of Oilell, now Tirerrill, a barony in County Sligo. See *O'Flaherty's Ogygia*, part iii., c. 79.

255 *Tir-Tuathail*, that is, the land or territory of Tuathal, now Tirhuahil, a territory comprising the parish of Kilronan, in the barony of Boyle and County Roscommon.

256 *Fir-Thire*, a people who gave their name to a territory in the barony of Carra, County Mayo. The River of Castlebar flows through it. See *Tribes and Customs of Ui-Fiachrach*, pp. 163, 205.

257 *The Clann-Chuain.*—They were separated from the Fir-Thire by the River Suir, now called the River of Castlebar. These territories originally belonged to the Ui-Fiachrach. See *Tribes, &c. of Ui-Fiachrach*, pp. 163, 205, 213.

258 *Tir-Nechtain and Tir-Enda.*—The positions of these territories are unknown.

259 *Sen-Feargal*, that is, old Fergal O'Ruairc, king of Connaught, who was slain in the year 964.

260 *Breifne.*—This territory comprised the present counties of Leitrim and Cavan.

261 *O'Ruairc*, now O'Rourke and O'Rorke, and sometimes Rourke without the prefix O'.

262 *The tribute of Connacht is due.*—There were only three kings of Connaught from this family, namely, Sen-Feargal, who was slain in 964; Art, who was slain in 1046, and Donnell, son of Tiernan, who was slain in 1102.

263 *MacTighearnain*, now anglicized Mac Kernan, and sometimes Kernan without the prefix Mac. Teallach Dunchadha is now anglicized Tullaghunco and Tullyhunco. It is the name of a barony in the west of County Cavan.

264 *MacSamhradhain*, now anglicized Magauran and MacGovern. *Teallach Eachdhach* is the present barony of Tullaghagh or Tullyhaw, in the north-west of County Cavan, where the Magaurans are still very numerous.

265 *MacConsnamha*, now Mac Kinnawe, and more usually anglicized Forde, being a false translation of Kinnawe, which is supposed to signify "head of the ford;" but this is a mere blunder, because *Cusnamha* (genitive Consnamha) is the name of the ancestor from whom the appellation is derived,

signifies, “dog of swimming.” *Clann-Chionaoith* is more usually called Muintir Cionaoith, and is now anglicized as Munter-Kenny. It is the local or traditional name of a territory in the barony of Dromahaire and county of Leitrim, and lies between Lough Allen and the river Arigna. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A. ²⁶⁶ *MacCagadhain*, now MacCogan, and Cogan without the prefix Mac. *Clann Fearmaighe* is now anglicized as Glanfarne. It adjoins Munter-Kenny, and both territories are comprised in the barony of Dromahaire, in the county of Leitrim. Glanfarne stretches to the east and north-east of Lough Allen, and contains twenty-one quarters of land. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A. D. 1217, note ᵍ.

²⁶⁷ *Mag Dorchaidh*, now Dorcey. The last chief of Cinel-Luachain of this family died in the year 1403. This territory comprised the parish of Oughteragh, situated at the foot of Slieve-an-ierin, in the east of the county of Leitrim. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A. D. 1403, p. 778, note ᵗ.

²⁶⁸ *Dartraighe.*—This is still the local name for the barony of Rossclogher, in the county of Leitrim. It was the territory of the family of Mag-Flannchadha, now anglicized as MacClancy, and more generally Clancy without the prefix Mac.

²⁶⁹ *Calraighe.*—The name of this territory is still retained in that of the parish of Calry, in the barony of Carbury and county of Sligo. See *Tribes and Customs of Ui-Fiachrach*, p. 482. The O’Cearbhaills or Carrolls of this territory are now unknown. The O’Finns are numerous, but have all dropped the O’.

²⁷⁰ *O'Raghallaigh*, now O’Reilly, and more frequently Reilly without the prefix O’. The family of O’Reilly supplied the chiefs to Muintir Maoilmordha, a territory which comprised the entire of the present county of Cavan, except the baronies of Tullyhaw and Tullyhunco, which belonged to O’Rourke. In the reign of Queen Elizabeth, however, these baronies were claimed by Sir John O’Reilly, and were confirmed to him by the government of that day.

²⁷¹ Race of Fergus.—These were the descendants of Fergus MacRoigh, king of Ulster in the first century, who settled in many parts of Ireland; but the people whom our author is going to visit now were the Mag Rannells and O’Ferralls, and their relatives of the counties of Leitrim and Longford, who were the chief families of the race of Fergus, or of the Clanna-Rury, in this part of Ireland.

²⁷² *O'Cuinn*, now Quin without the prefix O’. Their territory of Munter

<meta>This page continues the annotations on the territories and families of Connaught and neighboring regions, detailing the lands of the O'Quins, O'Ferralls, and other tribes across Longford, Westmeath, Leitrim, and Sligo.</meta>

<page-num>xxxviii</page-num>
<header>O'DUBHAGAIN.</header>

Gillagan was distributed among the baronies of Ardagh, Moydow, and Shrule, in the county of Longford. The O'Quins of this race were dispossessed by the O'Ferralls in the fourteenth century. An inquisition, taken at Ardagh in the tenth year of James I, found that thirty-five small cartrons <note>A cartron was a traditional Irish measure of land, often varying in size by region.</note> of Montergalgan then belonged to O'Farrell Bane, and seventeen and a half cartrons to O'Farrell Boy's part of the county of Longford. The O'Quins are still numerous in this territory.

²⁷³ *Magh Breacraighe*, a plain comprising the northern part of the barony of Moygoish, in the county of Westmeath, and extending also into the county of Longford. The name Mag Maoilisa is now obsolete in this district.

²⁷⁴ *Mag-Finnbhairr*, now Maginver, and sometimes anglicized as Gaynor. The territory of Muintir Geradhain, anglicized as Munter-geran, is situated on the west side of Lough Gowna, in the county of Longford. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1080, p. 916, note ˣ.

²⁷⁵ *Mag-Raghnaill*, now Magrannell, and more frequently anglicized as Reynolds. The territory of Muintir-Eolais comprised the southern half of the present county of Leitrim. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1079, p. 51, note ᵇ.

²⁷⁶ *Muintir Maoilmhiadhaigh*, now O'Maoilmhiadhaigh, and anglicized as Mulvey without the prefixed O'. Magh Nisi, otherwise called Muintir-Chearbhallain, from the tribe name of this family, and Upper Muintir-Eolais, was a level district on the east side of the Shannon, in the barony and county of Leitrim. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1243, p. 306, note ¹; and Hardiman's edition of *Iar Connaught*, p. 349.

²⁷⁷ *Muintir-Fearghail*, i.e., the family of O'Ferrall, who for many centuries were chief lords of the entire territory of Anghaile, in the present county of Longford, though the O'Quins of the same race were their seniors in point of genealogy. They had sometimes sovereignty over that subsection of the race of Fergus, on the east side of the Shannon, but never over the whole race of Fergus, who had large territories in Connaught, as well as in Thomond and Kerry.

²⁷⁸ *Cairbre*, now the barony of Carbury, in the north of the county of Sligo. It derived its name from Cairbre, son of Niall of the Nine Hostages, who was chief of this territory in St. Patrick's time.

²⁷⁹ *O' Maoilcluiche*.—This name is still common in the barony of Carbury, but it is always anglicized to Stone, from the idea that *cluiche*, the latter part of it, signifies "stone;" but this is a mere blunder, for Maoilcluiche signi-

...signifies youth of the game; based on this meaning of the name, the author makes a kind of pun. See *Tribes and Customs of Ui-Fiachrach*, p. 275, note b.

²⁸⁰ *Luighne*, now the barony of Leyny, in the county of Sligo.

²⁸¹ *The Clanna-Cein*, that is, the septs descended from Cian (son of Oilioll Olum, king of Munster in the third century), whose grandson, Cormac Gaileng, settled here in the reign of Cormac Mac Airt, monarch of Ireland. See *Ogygia*, part iii., c. 69.

²⁸² *O'h-Eaghra*, now O'Hara, which is extinct in its senior branches but remains numerous in its junior branches, though their pedigrees are unknown.

²⁸³ *O'h-Uathmharain*, now obsolete. It would be anglicized as Hofferan.

²⁸⁴ *The Ui-Cearnachain*.—O'Dugan blunders here; this shows that he derived the materials for his poem from reading, rather than from any actual visit to the territories at the time he composed this poem. O'Cearnachain was lord of Luighne in Meath, and not of any territory in Connaught. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1030, 1056, 1159.

²⁸⁵ *O'Gadhra*, now O'Gara. He was lord of Coolavin and Sliabh Lugha. O'Dugan errs in claiming that O'Gara belonged to the sept of Ui-Cearnachain.

²⁸⁶ *O'Dobhailen*, now Devlin, without the O'. He was lord of Corca-Firtri, in Corran, in the present county of Sligo. See *Ogygia*, part ii., c. 69.

²⁸⁷ *O'Duinncathaigh*.—This name would be anglicized as O'Duncahy, or Duncahy, but it is little known today.

²⁸⁸ *Corann*, now Corran, a barony in the county of Sligo. See *Ogygia*, part iii., c. 69, for the legendary or bardic derivation of this name.

²⁸⁹ *Magh-Luirg*.—Our author has already mentioned this territory in his notice of the Sil-Muireadhaigh (see above, note 252, p. xxxv); however, regarding his second visit to Connaught, he considers it his duty to mention its more ancient chieftains of the Milesian or Scotic race.

²⁹⁰ *Mag Eoch*, would be anglicized as Mageogh, or Keogh, but the name seems obsolete in the territory of Moylurg at present.

²⁹¹ *Mag-Maonaigh*, anglicized as MacMeeny, now unknown in Moylurg.

²⁹² *Mag-Riabhaigh*, anglicized as Magreevy. This name is still in use but little known. There was another family of this name in the territory of Calry, near Sligo.

²⁹³ *Ui-Fiachrach*, that is, the race of Fiachra, son of Eochaidh Muighmheodhain, monarch of Ireland. The territory of the North Ui-Fiachrach comprised the baronies of Carra, Erris, and Tirawley, in the county of Mayo, and the barony of Tireragh, in the county of Sligo, besides that portion of the barony of Carbury lying south of Drumcliff. See *Tribes and Customs of Ui-Fiachrach*

294 *The Codhnach.*—This was the name of a small stream which flows into the bay of Sligo, at the village of Drumcliff, in the barony of Carbury, and county of Sligo. See *Tribes and Customs of Ui-Fiachrach*, pp. 278, 279, 301.

295 *Rodhba*, now the river Robe, flowing through the south of the county of Mayo, and through the town of Ballinrobe, to which it gives its name, and empties into Lough Mask, opposite the island of Inis-Rodhba. See *Hy-Fiachrach*, p. 143, note ˣ.

296 *O'Dubhda*, now anglicized O'Dowda, O'Dowd, and sometimes Doody, without the prefix O'. See *Tribes and Customs of Ui-Fiachrach*, pp. 111, 112.

297 *O'Muireadhaigh*, now anglicized Murry and Murray, a name still common among the peasantry of the barony of Carra. See *Ui-Fiachrach*, p. 187, note ᵈ.

298 *O'Gormog*, now anglicized Gorman, without the prefix O'.

299 *O'Tighearnaigh*, now anglicized Tierney and Tiernan. The name is common among the peasantry of the barony of Carra. See *Ui-Fiachrach*, p. 186, note ᵇ.

300 *Ceara*, now the barony of Carra, in the county of Mayo. The inhabitants of the northern part of this territory had placed themselves under the protection of Mac Dermot of Moylurg, before the English invasion. See *Ui-Fiachrach*, pp. 163, 186, 187, 204, 205, 208.

301 *The three Tuathas.*—These were three territories in the east of the county of Roscommon, which are still well known. They were called Tir-Briuinn-na-Sinna, Cinel-Dobhtha, and Corca-Eachlann, and formed a deanery in the diocese of Elphin. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1189, p. 86, note ᵈ.

302 *Muintir-Birn*, that is, the family of O'Beirne, who were chiefs of Ui-Briuinn-na-Sinna, a beautiful district in the county of Roscommon, situated between Elphin and Jamestown, of which O'Monahan was chief up to the year 1249, but after that period it was the lordship of O'Beirne. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1218, note ʳ.

303 *Corca-Sheachlann*, or Corca-Achlann, a territory in the east of the county of Roscommon, comprising the parishes of Bumlin, Kiltrustan and Cloonfinlough, and the western half of the parish of Lissonuffy. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1256, p. 458, note ˡ. The Clann-Branain, or Mac-Branans of this territory are descended from the noble Druid, Ona, who granted Imleach-Ona, now Elphin, to St. Patrick. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1256, p. 358, note ˡ. The O'Maoilmhichils, or Mulvihils of

This territory would appear to have lost its rank of chieftains at an early period, as only one notice of the family occurs in the *Annals of the Four Masters*, <note>scilicet; that is to say</note> at the year 1189. The MacBrannans still possess a small estate at Bellmont in the original territory.

$^{304}$ *Cinel-Dobhtha*, now locally called Doohy-Hanly, from its chief O’Hanly, senior of the Cinel-Dobhtha family. This territory extended along the river Shannon, from Caranadoe Bridge to Drumdaff in the south of the parish of Kilgefin, and was divided from Corca-Achlann by the ridge of the mountain of Slieve Baune. It comprises the parishes of Kilglass, Termonbarry, Cloontuskert, and the eastern half of the parish of Lossonuffy. See *Annals of Four Masters*, A.D. 1210, pp. 169, 170, *note* e.

$^{305}$ *Cinel-Fechin*.—This would appear from the context to be the generic name for the three septs of the three Tuathas, but it does not appear from their line of descent that they ever had any such appellation.

$^{306}$ *Ciarraighe* of the plain, now called *Clann-Ceithernaigh*, a district comprising the parish of Kilkeevin, in the modern barony of Castlerea in the west of the county of Roscommon. See *Annals of Four Masters*, A.D. 1595, p. 1963, *note* r; and O’Flaherty’s *Ogygia*, part iii., c. 46.

$^{307}$ *MacCeithearnaigh*, now obsolete as a family name.

$^{308}$ *Clann-Cheirin*, i.e., the family of O’Ceirin, now *anglicized* Kerrin, without the prefix O’.

$^{309}$ *Clann-Taidhg*.—This sept was seated in *Iochtar-tire*. Mr. Molloy, of Oakport, in the county of Roscommon, is the present head of this family. The O’Molloys of this territory are to be distinguished from the O’Molloys of Fircall, in the King's County.

$^{310}$ *Siol-Maoilruana*.—This was the tribe name of the O’Flynns of the county of Roscommon, and their territory comprised the entire parish of Kiltullagh, and a part of that of Kilkeevin. The *Ui-Floinn* or O’Flynns are still very numerous in this district.

$^{311}$ *Caille-Fothaidh*.—The limits of this territory have not been determined. The family of O’Rothlain, now Rowley, was seated in the parish of Kilshesnan, barony of Gallen, and county of Mayo. See *Annals of Four Masters*, A.D. 1208, p. 160, *note* n.

$^{312}$ *MacSgaithghil*, now *anglicized* Scahill. The territory of *Corca Mogha* (Corcamoe), which comprised the parish of Kilkerrin, in the county of Galway, originally belonged to O’Scahill before the English invasion, but they were soon after dispossessed by the *Ui-Diarmada* or O’Concannons.

$^{313}$ *Loch Gealgosa*.—This name is now obsolete. It was probably the

name of Urlare Lough, in the barony of Costello and county of Mayo. The O'Braoins, or O'Breens, of this territory are now unknown.

³¹⁴ *Eochaidh*, that is, *Eochaidh Muighmheadhain*, monarch of Ireland and father of Brian, the ancestor of the kings of Connacht. He was also the father of Conall Oiriosan, the ancestor of the O'Malleys, who were chiefs of the two Umhalls, now the baronies of Murresk and Burrishoole in the west of the county of Mayo. See O'Flaherty's *Ogygia*, part iii., c. 79.

³¹⁵ *Clann-Maille*, that is, the family of O'Malley.

³¹⁶ *Prophets of the weather.*—The O'Malleys are celebrated in several Irish poems as most expert seamen. They are called the Manannans, or sea-gods, of the western ocean. Grace O'Malley, the daughter of Owen O'Malley, chief of this territory, was celebrated over all Ireland in the reign of Queen Elizabeth. She visited England and was introduced to Queen Elizabeth by the Lord Deputy of that day by a letter which still exists in the State Paper Office.

³¹⁷ *Conmaicne-Cuile-toladh*, now the barony of Kilmaine, in the south of the county of Mayo. See *Ogygia*, part iii., c. 46. There are a few families of the sept of O'Talcharain still remaining in this barony, but they have descended into the peasantry. They anglicize the name as Tolleran.

³¹⁸ *Conmaicne-mara*, that is, *Conmaicne* of the sea, now Connemara in the barony of Ballynahinch, in the west of the county of Galway. *Ogygia*, part iii., c. 46.

³¹⁹ *O'Cadhla*, now Keely. See Hardiman's edition of *Iar-Connaught*, p. 29, note <sup>w</sup>. This name was anglicized as *Quæleus* by the Roman Catholic Archbishop of Tuam, who was a contemporary of Colgan, the author of the *Acta Sanctorum*.

³²⁰ *Conmaicne of Dunmor*, now the barony of Dunmore, in the north of the county of Galway. *Ogygia*, part iii., c. 46.

³²¹ *Sidhlin to the Shannon.*—This is evidently a mistake.

³²² *Gno-mor*, a territory in the west of the county of Galway; it comprised the northern and larger part of the barony of Moycullen, in the county of Galway. See *Ogygia*, part iii. c. 82. These were of the race of Lughaidh Dealbhaedh—of the Dalcassian race. The family of MacConroi have all anglicized their name to *King*, and their seat of Ballymaconry is now called Kingston! See Hardiman's edition of O'Flaherty's *Iar-Connaught*, pp. 52, 54, 62, 156, 252, 255, 391, 392.

³²³ *Gno-beg.*—This territory comprised the southern and smaller portion of the said barony of Moycullen. See *Iar-Connaught, as cited above*. The name *O'Hadhnaidh* is now anglicized as Heyny, without the prefix O'.

<page-num>xliii</page-num>

# TRIBES OF CONNAUGHT.

<sup>324</sup> *Clann-Choscraigh*. These were a sub-section of the Ui-Bruin-Seola, located on the east side of Lough Corrib, in the barony of Clare and county of Galway. The name, Mac Aodha, is now anglicized as MacHugh.

<sup>325</sup> *The race of Murchadh*, more usually called Muintir-Murchadha, now anglicized as Muntermorroghoe, applied to a district in the barony of Clare and county of Galway. It was the tribe name of the family of O’Flaithbheartaigh, now O’Flaherty. See *Iar Connaught*, 368.

<sup>326</sup> *Aidhne*, a territory in the south of the county of Galway, coextensive with the diocese of Kilmacduagh. See *Tribes and Customs of Hy-Many*, p. 77, and *Hy-Fiachrach*, p. 52, note <sup>1</sup>.

<sup>327</sup> *Ui-Fiachrach*, i.e., evidently of the southern Ui-Fiachrach, located in Aidhne, for he has already treated the northern Ui-Fiachrach in the counties of Mayo and Sligo.

<sup>328</sup> *Mac Gilla-Ceallaigh*, now anglicized as Killykelly and Kilkelly, without the prefix Mac. The name is still present and respected in this district.

<sup>329</sup> *The Ui-Eidhin*, i.e., the O’Heynes, a famous family of this territory, of the race of Guaire Aidhne, surnamed "the Hospitable," king of Connaught in the seventh century. See *Tribes and Customs of Hy-Fiachrach*, pp. 398–406.

<sup>330</sup> *The Ui-Cleirigh*, i.e., the O’Clerys. This family was originally located in Ui-Fiachrach Aidhne, but was dispersed to different parts of Ireland after the English invasion. For a curious account of this family, as preserved by themselves, see *Tribes and Customs of Ui-Fiachrach*, pp. 71 to 91, and 391 to 398.

<sup>331</sup> *Cinel-Cinngamhna*, a <term>sept</term> of the southern Ui-Fiachrach, situated near Kinvara in the south-west of the county of Galway, descended from Seanach Cinngamhna, son of Eoghan Aidhne, and grandson of Dathi, the last pagan monarch of Ireland. O’Duibhghirolla, the name of the chief of this ancient <term>sept</term>, has not been identified.

<sup>332</sup> *Caenraighe*, a <term>sept</term> not of the race of Fiachra, but of the related race of Conn, who had been located here before the Ui-Fiachrach. O’Maghna, their chief, is now unknown. See *Hy-Fiachrach*, p. 53.

<sup>333</sup> *Cinel-Aodha*, i.e., the race of Aodh, son of Cobhthach, son of Goibh-nenn, son of Conall, son of Eoghan Aidhne, son of Eochaidh Breac, son of Dathi, the last pagan monarch of Ireland. This tribe, whose chiefs in later ages were the O’Shaughnessys and O’Cahills, possessed the eastern half of the diocese of Kilmacduagh, in the county of Galway. See *Ui-Fiachrach*, p. 55.

<meta>This page continues the detailed annotations regarding the territories and families of Connaught, specifically focusing on the boundaries and clans of Hy-Many (Ui Maine), including the O'Naghtens and O'Mullallys.</meta>

<page-num>xliv</page-num>
->O'DUBHAGAIN.<-

334 *Echtghe*, the name of a celebrated mountainous district on the borders of the counties of Galway and Clare. It is now generally called *Sliabh Eachtaighe*, or *Slieve Aughty* in English.
335 *Maonmhagh*, usually anglicized as Moinmoy, a rich plain lying around Loughrea in the county of Galway. It was bounded on the east by the territory of Sil-Anmchadha, on the south by the mountain of Sliabh Echtghe, and on the west by the diocese of Kilmacduagh. It included Moyode, Finure, and other places mentioned in the old Irish documents.
336 *Caradh*, now Caradh na d'Tuath (Carranadoo), in the barony of Ballintober East, in the county of Roscommon.
337 *Grian*, that is, the river Grean in the county of Clare, which was originally the southern boundary of Hy-Many. See *Tribes and Territories of Hy-Many*, p. 134.
338 The great third of Connaught, in the territory of Hy-Many, which comprised the third part of the province of Connaught.
339 *Sionainn*, that is, the river Shannon, which was the eastern boundary of Hy-Many, except at one point where Hy-Many extended beyond it—at least for several centuries—for it included the present parish of Lusmagh in King's County.
340 *Meadh-Siul*, now Knockmea, near Castle Hackett, about six miles southeast of Tuam in the county of Galway. This was on the western boundary of Hy-Many.
341 *O'Conaill*. This name is now unknown in Hy-Many. See *Tribes and Territories of Hy-Many*, p. 68.
342 *Grian*. This river is now in the county of Clare, rising on the frontiers of the ancient Hy-Many. By "head of the great plain" is meant here the head of the plain of Maonmhagh.
343 *O’Neachtain*, now anglicized as O'Naghten and, more usually, Naughton and Norton, without the prefix O'. For the descent and present circumstances of this family, see *Tribes and Territories of Hy-Many*, pp. 70, and 176, 177.
344 *O’Maolalaidh*, now usually Mullally and Lally, without the prefix O'. After the English invasion, this family was moved from the plain of Maonmhagh to the parish of Tuam, where their chief resided in the castle of Tullindal. The head of this family moved to France in the seventeenth century, where his descendants acquired European fame. For the pedigree of this family, see *Tribes and Territories of Hy-Many*, p. 71, and 177 to 183.

# TRIBES OF CONNAUGHT.
<page-num>xlv</page-num>

345 *As far as Ui-Fiachrach.*—This line shows that the plain of Maonmhagh extended westward to the country of the Ui-Fiachrach Aidhne, which comprised the entire present diocese of Kilmacduagh.

346 *The six Sodhans.*—From various references, it appears that the territory of these six septs (who were not of the Hy-Many, but of the race of Sodhan Salbhuidhe, son of Fiacha Araidhe, king of Ulster, about the year A.D. 240) was nearly coextensive with the barony of Tiaquin, in the county of Galway. See *Tribes, etc., of Hy-Many*, pp. 72, 73–159, 160–165. The O'Mannins and MacWards were the chief families of this territory; the others were O'Scurry, O'Lennain, O'Casain, O'Gialla, O'Maigin, and O'Duvagan. See *Tribes, etc., of Hy-Many*, p. 159.

347 *Crumhthann.*—This territory still retains its ancient name, which is anglicized as Cruffon. It is a large district in the county of Galway, comprising the barony of Killyan and a considerable portion of the adjoining barony of Ballimoe. The families mentioned in the text are now called Cahill, Moran, and Mulrony, without the prefix O', but no pedigrees of them are preserved.

348 *Caladh.*—This district was nearly coextensive with the barony of Kilconnell, in the county of Galway. The family name of O’Laodhóg is now obsolete, but it is locally believed to be the name now anglicized as Lee, the latter syllable being struck off. See *Tribes and Territories of Hy-Many*, pp. 74, 75.

349 *Sionainn.*—This cannot mean the river Shannon, because the territory of Caladh is very far from that river; either it should be *na Suca* (of the river Suck), or *na Sionna* bears some other meaning. See *Hy-Many*, p. 74.

350 *Ui-Anmchadha*, otherwise *Siol-Anmchadha.* The territory of this sept comprised the barony of Longford in the county of Galway, and the parish of Lusmagh on the east side of the Shannon in the King's County. Although O'Dugan identifies O'h-Uallachain (now MacUllachain, anglicized as Cuolahan) as the chief of this territory, it would appear from the *Irish Annals* that the O'Madden family has been far more celebrated for centuries, and that O'h-Uallachain had no possessions on the west side of the Shannon for many centuries. See *Tribes, etc., of Hy-Many*, pp. 41, and 183 to 188.

351 *MacEitteagain.*—This seems to be a corrupt writing of *MacAedh*-agan, now MacEgan. See *Tribes and Customs of Hy-Many*, pp. 31, 86, 168.

352 *MacGiolla-Fhionnagáin*, now unknown. See *Tribes, etc., of Hy-Many*, pp. 30, 31, 75.

<summary>This page contains historical and geographical notes identifying the territories and ruling families of various septs in Connaught, specifically within the Hy-Many region and its neighbors.</summary>
<keywords>Ui-Fiachrach, Sodhans, Crumhthann, Caladh, Ui-Anmchadha, Hy-Many, Galway, O'Madden, O'h-Uallachain, Irish geography, tribal history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the detailed annotations for O’Dugan’s topographical poem, concluding the section on the territories of Connaught (specifically the Hy-Many region) and beginning the notes on the province of Leinster.</meta>

<page-num>xlvi</page-num>
<header>O'DUBHAGAIN.</header>

353 *Clann-Chionaoth*, otherwise called *Muintir-Chionaoth*. The family name was *O'Cionaoth*, now Kenny.

354 *O'Domhnallain*, now Donolan, without the prefix O'. See *Tribes and Customs of Hy-Many*, pp. 32, 33, 76, 169, 174. The territory of this family, anciently called Clann-Breasail, is situated between the towns of Ballinasloe and Loughrea. For several centuries, the chief mansion of the family has been called Ballydonolan.

355 *Ui-Donnchadha*.—O'Donaghue is now unknown.

356 *Ui-Cormaic*.—See *Tribes, etc., of Hy-Many*, pp. 76, 77.

357 *The Lathach*, i.e., the mire or quagmire. The situation of this territory is unknown to the editor. It is not Lathach Caichtubil, near Athlone.

358 *Inis Duibhghinn*.—It consisted of twelve ballies <note>townlands or land units</note>. This is probably the same name as O'Dubhagain or O'Dugan. See *Tribes and Territories of Hy-Many*, pp. 28, 29, 62, 75, 76, 77. Ballydugan, near Roscrea, is believed to have been the head residence of this little territory.

359 *O'Docamlain*, now unknown, as is their territory of *Rinn na hEighthidhe*. See *Tribes and Territories of Hy-Many*, pp. 13, 76, 77, 85, 87, 90, 91.

360 *Magh-Finn*, i.e., fair plain; a territory in the barony of Athlone, county of Roscommon, containing forty quarters of land, and now commonly called Keogh's Country. The Keoghs or MacKeoghs, a branch of the O'Kellys, have been chiefs of this district for many centuries. The family of O'Maoilbhrighde are now unknown in this neighborhood.

361 *Brighit*, i.e., St. Brighit, or Brigit, of Kildare, to whom this parish was dedicated. See *Hy-Many*, pp. 15, 75, 77, 78, 102, 130, 166, 167.

362 *Bredach*.—This was the old name of *Magh-Finn*.

---

### NOTES TO THE PART OF O'DUGAN'S POEM RELATING TO LEINSTER.

363 *MacMurchadha*, i.e., Murchardides, or descendant of Murchadh, surnamed Maolnambo, king of Leinster, anglicized MacMurrough. The principal branch of this family took the surname of *Caomhanach*, from their ancestor, Domhnall Caomhanach, son (illegitimate, according to Giraldus) of the king of Leinster at the period of the English invasion. This family is now known by the name of Kavanagh and is very numerous in Leinster.

<summary>This page provides historical and genealogical annotations for O'Dugan's poem, identifying the territories and modern surnames of families in the Hy-Many region of Connaught and introducing the MacMurrough and Kavanagh families of Leinster.</summary>
<keywords>O'Dugan, Hy-Many, Clann-Chionaoth, O'Domhnallain, Magh-Finn, Keogh's Country, MacMurrough, Kavanagh, Leinster, Irish genealogies</keywords>

--- Page 261 ---
<meta>This page continues the detailed annotations for the tribes and territories of Leinster, specifically focusing on the descendants of Cathaoir Mor and the geographical shifts following the English invasion.</meta>

<page-num>xlvii</page-num>
<header>TRIBES OF LEINSTER.</header>

364 *Ui n-Enechlais*, more correctly *Ui-Enechglais*. They were the descendants of Breasal Einechghlas, son of Cathaoir Mor, monarch of Ireland in the second century, and they were located in the present barony of Arklow and county of Wicklow. See *Leabhar na gCeart*, p. 195, note g. The name O'Fiachrach is now obsolete.

365 *Plain of Cualann*.—This territory was called Fercuolen <note>Feara Cualann</note> in the reigns of Elizabeth and James I, when it was considered to be coextensive with the manor of Powerscourt, but it was anciently much more extensive. The family of O'Cosgraigh is now unknown. They were dispossessed shortly after the English invasion by the families of O'Toole and O'Byrne.

366 *Ui-Drona*, now the barony of Idrone, in the county of Carlow. The O'Ryans, or Ryans, of this race are still very numerous, but they are to be distinguished from the O'Mulryans of the county of Tipperary, who also shorten their name to Ryan. Both, however, are of the race of Cathaoir Mor, king of Leinster in the second century.

367 *The Ui-Muireadhaigh*.—This was the tribe name of the family of O'Tuathail, now anglicized as O'Toole, and was also applied to their territory, which comprised about the southern half of the county of Kildare. Shortly after the English invasion, the O'Tooles were driven from this level district, and they settled in the territory of Imaile, and soon after in that of Fercuolen. See *Leabhar na gCeart*, p. 210, note i.

368 *Ui-Faolain*.—This was the tribe name of the Mac Eochaidhs (Keoghs) and O'Byrnes, and was also applied to their territory, which comprised about the northern half of the county of Kildare. They were driven from there shortly after the English invasion, when they settled in the east of the present county of Wicklow.

369 *Ui-Bairche*.—This tribe, which gave its name to the territory where they were located, derived their name from Daire Barrach, second son of Cathaoir Mor, king of Leinster and monarch of Ireland in the second century. They were located between the Ui-Drona and the Ui-Muireadhaigh, and possessed the whole of the present barony of Slievermargy, and some of the adjoining districts of the county of Carlow. The Mac Gormans were driven from this territory after the English invasion, and their chief settled in the barony of Ibrickan, in the west of Thomond, in the present county of Clare.

370 *Ui-Failghe*, i.e., the descendants of Rossa Failghe, the eldest son of Cathaoir Mor. The country of this tribe originally comprised the baro-

baronies of East and West Offaly, in the county of Kildare, those of Portnahinch and Tinnahinch in the Queen's County, and that portion of the King's County comprised in the diocese of Kildare and Leighlin. See *Leabhar na gCeart*, pp. 193, 216.

371 *O'Brogarbhain*, now unknown in Offaly.

372 *Clann-Chionaith*, now Kenny, obscure and little known in this territory.

373 *Clann-Chonchobhair*, that is, the family of O'Conchobhair or O'Conor, who were the head chiefs of this territory until the reign of Philip and Mary, when they were dispossessed. After this, O'Dempsey became the most powerful family of the Ui-Failghe, and remained so until the Revolution of 1688.

374 *O'Duinns*, that is, the family of O'Doyne, or Dunn, of Iregan, now the barony of Portnahinch in the Queen's County. Colonel Francis Dunne, <term>M.P.</term>, is the present head of this family.

375 *O'Diomasaigh*, now O'Dempsey, or Dempsey, without the prefix. The Viscount Clanmalier was the head of this family at the time of the Revolution of 1688. They are now obscure, and have sunk into poverty and degradation.

376 *O'Aenghusa*, now Hennessy, without the O'. The name is still common in the Queen's County, but is confined to the lower classes.

377 *O'Aimirgin*, now anglicized as Mergin and Bergin, a name very common around Geashill in the King's County.

378 *Clann-Murchadhain*, that is, the family of O'Murchadhain, now called Morachain; but they are little known, and the name is sometimes anglicized as Moran and Morrin.

379 *Cairbre*, now the barony of Carbury, in the northwest of the county of Kildare. The family name, O'Ciardha, is now anglicized as Keary and Carey, and the name is common, but is found only among the lower orders. See *Hy-Fiachrach*, pp. 276, 277.

380 *Osraighe*, an ancient territory in Leinster, coextensive with the present diocese of Ossory.

381 *Clann-Cearbhaill*, that is, the race of Cearbhall, a celebrated chief of Ossory in the middle of the ninth century.

382 *Clann-Donnchadha*, that is, the family of O'Dunchadha, now anglicized as Dunphy.

383 *Mac Giollaphatraic*, now anglicized as Fitzpatrick.

384 *O'Bruadair*, now anglicized as Brothers and Broderick.

385 *Mac Braoin*, now Breen, without the Mac.

<header>TRIBES OF CONNAUGHT.</header>

386 **O'Braonains**, anglicized as O'Brennan and Brennan, the name of a numerous clan in Ossory.

387 **Three tribes of Munstermen.** — These tribes were of the lineage of the kings of Leinster.

388 **The Comar, or Confluence.** — This was the old name of Castlecomer, in the county of Kilkenny.

389 **Ui-Eirc**, now the barony of Iverk, in the south of the county of Kilkenny.

## NOTES TO O'HUIDHRIN.

390 *Who is not old.* — Compare the observations in the poem of Dubhthach Mac Ui Lughair. *Leabhar na gCeart*, page 237.

391 *Boinn*, the Bubinda of Ptolemy, now the river Boyne, the country to the south of which O'Huidhrin undertook to describe.

392 *Fodhla*, one of the old names of Ireland. See note 2, above.

393 *Which Conn divided.* — This alludes to the division made of Ireland, in the second century, into two equal parts, between Conn of the Hundred Battles and Eoghan Mogh Nuadhat; a line of low gravel hills extending from Dublin to Clarinbridge, near Galway, forming the boundary between them.

394 *O'Dubhagain*, that is, John Mor O'Dubhagain, O'Duvegan, or O'Dugan, the author of the former part of this poem. Notwithstanding the evidence of this statement, Dr. Lynch attributes the whole of the two parts of the poem to O'Duveganus, which is incorrect. Both copies of the poems still in existence are older than Lynch's time.

395 *Daoil.* — This was, and still is, the name of several rivers in Ireland. O'Huidhrin seems to have in view here the river Daoil (Deel), which rises in the mountains near Charleville, county Cork, flows through Rathkeale, in the county of Limerick, and pays its tribute to the Shannon. The Barrow, or the Slaney, would be more appropriately mentioned in connection with the race of Cathaoir.

396 *Race of Cathaoir.* — This race comprised the principal families of Leinster.

397 *From ancient books.* — This is the fact; for he mentions many families who had declined in his time.

398 *Leath-Mhogha*, the southern half of Ireland. According to the

<page-num>1</page-num>
<header>O'HUIDHRIN.</header>

bardic History, Eibher Finn, eldest son of Milesius, was the ancestor of the principal Milesian families of the south of Ireland. 

**399** ***Luimneach.***—This name, though now generally believed to be the name of the city of Limerick, was in ancient times applied to the lower Shannon only. 

**400** ***The Gaoidhil, that is,*** the *Scoti*, or Milesian Irish. 

**401** ***Province of the race of Cathaoir,*** that is, the province of Laighin, now called Leinster. 

**402** ***Sabhrann.***—This was an old name of the river Lee, in the county of Cork. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1163, p. 1151. 

**403** ***The Dun of Dubhlinn, that is,*** the fort of Dublin. 

**404** ***Boirinn,*** that is, Burren, a rocky barony in the north of the present county of Clare. 

**405** ***Mac Murchadha***, usually anglicized as Mac Murrough. The primary family of this race took the surname Kavanagh, and the current head of the name is Arthur Kavanagh, Esq., of Borris, in the county of Carlow, who inherits a very considerable portion of the territory of his ancestors. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1193, p. 97, note *f*. Naas, now the town of Naas in the county of Kildare, was one of the chief seats of the kings of Leinster from the most remote period, but it does not appear to have ever been occupied by the Mac Murrough family. It was instead the seat of the ancestors of the O'Byrnes, whose progenitors had been the earlier kings of Leinster. 

**406** ***Ui Failghe***, usually anglicized as Offaly, Ophaly, etc., a large territory in Leinster. It comprised the baronies of East and West Offaly in the county of Kildare, those of Portnahinch and Tinnahinch in the Queen's County, and that portion of the King's County included in the dioceses of Kildare and Leighlin. The Ui-Failghe were the descendants of Ross Failghe, the eldest son of Cathaoir Mor, monarch of Ireland in the second century. 

**407** ***O'Conchobhair***, now anglicized as O'Conor. The O'Conors of this race are to be distinguished from those of Silmurray in Connaught and various other families who bore the same name but were of totally different stocks, such as O'Conor of Kerry, O'Conor Corcomroe, O'Conor of Glengevin, etc. 

**408** ***Of the plain.***—The territory of this race is a perfect plain, as there is hardly any elevation in its whole extent from the hill of Croghan to Slieve Bloom. 

**409** ***Cruachan***, originally called Cruachan Bri-Eile, now Croghan, a prominent hill in the barony of Lower Philipstown, in the north of the
