410 *Sub-Chiefs*, that is, the chieftains subject to O'Conor Faly, who was the head chief or king of this territory.

411 *Ui-Riagain*, now anglicized as Oregan, Iregan, and Dooregan. This territory still locally retains its ancient name and is co-extensive with the barony of Tinnahinch, in the northwest of the Queen's County. The present representative of O'Duinn of this territory is Colonel Francis Dunne, <term>M.P.</term> For his pedigree, see *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1448, p. 968, and 1585, p. 1840.

412 *O'h-Aenghusa*, now Hennessy, without the prefix O'. The family is still very numerous in Offaly but reduced to obscurity. Their territory of Clan-Colgain, which adjoined the hill of Croghan, is comprised in the barony of Lower Philipstown, in the King's County. For the descent of this family, see *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1414, pp. 819, 820.

413 *Tuath-da-mhuighe*, that is, the cantred of the two plains, called Tuomoy on an old map of Leix and Offaly, made in the reign of Philip and Mary; and in other documents, Tethmoy. It appears from this map that Tuomoy Nether and Upper comprised the baronies of Warrenstown and Coolestown, in the King's County. The family name, O'Maoilchein, is now unknown in this territory.

414 *Cantred of Geisill*, now the barony of Geshill, in the King's County.

415 *Border of Leinster.*—He was in Leinster, and on the borders of ancient Meath.

416 *O'h-Aimirgin*, now locally anglicized as Bergin, but more correctly made Mergin in other parts of Leinster. This family is still very numerous.

417 *Magh Aoife*, a district in the barony of East Offaly, adjoining Tethmoy. O'Murchadhain is now shortened to Moran and Morrin, but the ancient Irish form is preserved by those who speak Irish.

418 *Fidh Gaibhle.*—This was the name of a celebrated wood of Leinster, in which St. Berchan erected the Church of Clonsasta. It is now locally called Fee-Goille or Fee-guile, and is situated in the parish of Cloonsast, barony of Coolestown, and King's County. See *Leabhar na gCeart*, p. 214, note °.

419 *Clann Maoilughra*, usually anglicized as Clanmaliere, a territory extending, on both sides of the river Barrow, into the King's and Queen's Counties. It contained the barony of Portnahinch, in the Queen's County,

420 *Leghe.*—This territory comprised the barony of Western Offaly and a small portion of the northern part of the barony of Portnahinch, in which the great castle of Leighe, now Ley or Lea, is situated.

421 *Laoighis*, usually Anglicized as Leix and Latinized as *Lagisia*. This territory comprised the eastern and southern baronies of the present Queen's County. The present baronies of Upper Ossory, Portnahinch, and Tinnahinch, in the Queen's County, never formed any part of Leix.

422 *Laoighis-Reata.*—This was the most distinguished of the seven divisions of Laoighis, containing the fort of Rath-Bacain and the rock of Lec-Reda. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.M. 3529, and A.D. 958, note a. The name O'Mordha is usually Anglicized as O'More, but it is sometimes rendered as Moore, without the prefix O'.

423 *Dun-Masc*, now Dunamase, in the barony of East Maryborough, Queen's County. It is said to have derived this name from Masc, son of Augen Urgnuidh, the fourth son of Sedna Siothbhaic, ancestor of the people of Leinster. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 843, note a. It is a lofty isolated rock, on which formerly stood an earthen fort or stone <term>Cathair</term>, but which is now crowned by the ruins of a strong castle. See *Leabhar na gCeart*, p. 216, note q.

424 *O'Duibh.*—This is probably the name now Anglicized as Deevy and sometimes Devoy. Their territory of Cinel Crimthainn, extending around the fortress of Dun-Masc, is comprised in the barony of East Maryborough, in the Queen's County. See *Leabhar na gCeart*, p. 216, note q.

425 *Muintir-Fiodhbhuidhe.*—The location of this clan has not yet been determined.

426 *Magh Druchtain.*—This territory is still locally known and is considered the best district in the whole of the Queen's County, extending from the ford of Ath-baiteoige to the ford of Ath-fuiseoige, near Luggacurran. It is shown on an old map of "Leax and Ophaly," made in the reign of Queen Mary, under the name of FERAN O'KELLY, as extending from Ballymaddock southwards to the hills of Slewmargie, and as comprising Ballymaddock and the Park, near Stradbally; and the churches of Grange and Oghteoge, and the castle of Coragh are shown as in this

See *Leabhar na gCeart*, pp. 213, 214, note <sup>m</sup>.

<sup>434</sup> *Dinn-righ*, that is, the Hill of the Kings. This was the most ancient palace of the kings of Leinster. Its ruins are identified in the townland of Ballyknockan, on the west side of the River Barrow, about a quarter of a mile to the south of Leighlin Bridge, in the county of Carlow. See *Leabhar na gCeart*, pp. 14, 15, note <sup>o</sup>.

<page-num>liv</page-num>
<header>O'HUIDHRIN.</header>

about the southern half of the county of Kildare. See *Leabhar na gCeart*, p. 210, note i, and *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1180, p. 51, note e.

<sup>437</sup> *Almhain*, now Allen, a celebrated hill in the county of Kildare, situated about five miles north of the town of Kildare. See *Leabhar na gCeart*, p. 14, note 1.

<sup>438</sup> *Mac Giolla Mocholmog*.—This family has been extinct for many centuries. They were chiefs of the territory of *Ui-Dunchadha*, comprising that portion of the present county of Dublin through which the River Dodder flows. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, for the years 956, 995, 1032, 1044, 1155; and *Leabhar na gCeart*, p. 12, note f. For their pedigree, see Gilbert’s *History of Dublin*, vol. i.; Appendix, No. 1, pp. 403–408.

<sup>439</sup> *Feara-Cualann*, anglicized as Fercuolen, an ancient territory, nearly coextensive with the half barony of Rathdown, in the north of the county of Wicklow. See *Leabhar na gCeart*, p. 13, note b, and Ussher’s *Primordia*, p. 846.

<sup>440</sup> *The plain of the Life*, otherwise called *Magh-Life*. This was the name of a level plain in the county of Kildare, through which the River Liffey winds its course. The churches of Cill-Ausaille and Cill-Cuillinn (Killossy and Kilcullen) are mentioned as being in this plain. See Colgan’s *Trias Thaum.*, p. 152, and Lanigan’s *Ecclesiastical History of Ireland*, vol. i., pp. 273, 276.

<sup>441</sup> *West beyond Teamhair*.—This must be a mistake.

<sup>442</sup> *O’Gealbrain*.—This name does not occur in the *Annals of the Four Masters*, and seems to be obsolete at present.

<sup>443</sup> *O’Taidhg*.—This name would be anglicized as O’Teige; but it seems to be obsolete, unless it is one of the several old Irish names now anglicized as Tighe.

<sup>444</sup> *Ui-Mail*, now Imail, a well-known territory in the barony of Upper Talbotstown, in the county of Wicklow. The O’Tooles were driven into this territory shortly after the English Invasion.

<sup>445</sup> *Ui-Teigh*.—This was the tribe name of the Ui-Ceallaigh Cualann in the north of the present county of Wicklow. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 713, note h; 765, note x; 915, note i.

<sup>446</sup> *Cairbre of Leinster*, now the barony of Carbury, in the county of Kildare. See note 413, above, and *Tribes and Customs of Ui-Fiachrach*, pp. 276, 277.

<sup>447</sup> *O’Ciardha*, now anglicized as Keary and Carey, a rather numerous name in the counties of Meath and Kildare.

<header>TRIBES OF LEINSTER.</header>

448 *Almhain*, now the Hill of Allen, in the county of Kildare. From this it appears that Cairbre Ua-Ciardha must have originally extended to this hill.

449 *Cruachan*, now the conspicuous Hill of Croghan, in the barony of Lower Philipstown, in the north of the King’s County. See note 409, above.

450 *Fortuatha of Leinster.*—This territory comprised the Glen of Imail and Glendalough, in the present county of Wicklow. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 707, 774, 1039, and *Leabhar na gCeart*, p. 207, note d. O’Fearghaile was the chief of this territory.

451 *From the Boinn.*—This seems to indicate that the Fortuatha of Leinster were from the neighborhood of the River Boyne, and were of the race of Colla and Conn of the Hundred Battles.

452 *Ui-Inechrais.*—This is a mistake for Ui-Einechghlais, a tribe descended from Breasal Einechghlais <note>Breasal of the Green Face</note>, son of Cathaoir Mor, monarch of Ireland in the second century. This tribe was seated in the present barony of Arklow, in the south-east of the county of Wicklow. See *Leabhar na gCeart*, p. 195, note g; p. 207, note e.

453 *O’Fiachra.*—This name is now unknown in the county of Wicklow. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1154, 1170.

454 *Almhuin.*—This is some place, now unknown, in the barony of Arklow, as it cannot be the Hill of Almhain or Allen, in the county of Kildare.

455 *O’h-Aodha*, now anglicized as Hay and Hughes.

456 *Ui-Deaghaidh.*—This name is still preserved and is that of a rural deanery in the diocese of Ferns, which is nearly coextensive with the barony of Gorey, in the county of Wexford.

457 *Ui-Muirte.*—This name is now forgotten in Leinster. The locations of the territories of Cinel-Flaitheamhvain and Ui-Mealla are now unknown.

458 *O’Finntighearn*, now anglicized as Finneran.

459 *O’Murchadha*, anciently anglicized as O’Murchoe, but now generally Murphy, without the prefix O’. See observations on this name at note 100, above, and in the Introduction to the present volume.

460 *Ui-Felme*, that is, descendants of Felim, son of Enna Censellagh, king of Leinster in the fifth century. This was the tribe name of the O’Murchoes, and it was also applied, as was usual among the old Irish, to their territory, which comprised the barony of Ballaghkeen, in the east of the county of

<meta>This page continues the detailed topographical and genealogical annotations to O’Huidhrin’s poem, focusing on territories and family names within the modern counties of Wexford and Carlow.</meta>

<page-num>lvi</page-num>
<header>O'HUIDHRIN.</header>

Wexford, still called the Murroes territory. Connell O'Murchoe, the head of this family, lived at Toberlumnich, in the Murroes, in 1634. There was another respectable branch of the family at Oulartleigh, who possessed a considerable estate down to our own times. O'Murchadha, which is now anglicized as Murphy, is the most prevalent name in the province of Leinster. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1381, p. 684, note m; also the Annuary of the Kilkenny Archaeological Society for the year 1858, vol. i., p. 1, p. 24, *et seq.*

^461 *Ui-Felme the northern.*—The territory of this sept was situated in the present county of Carlow (and comprised the present parish of Tullowphelim, in the barony of Rathvilly, county of Carlow), which retains the name. *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1381, note m, and *Leabhar-na-gCeart*, p. 208, note f.

^462 *O'Gairbhidh*, now anglicized as Garvey, without the prefix O'.

^463 *Tulach*, now the town of Tullow (in the parish of Tullowphelim), which was the residence of the chieftain of this territory.

^464 *Siol-Brain*, now the barony of Shelburne, in the southwest of the county of Wexford.

^465 *Dubhthoire.*—This name would be anglicized as Duffry, which is now the name of a district near Mount Leinster, in the county of Wexford; but the place here referred to must be placed farther to the southwest.

^466 *From the Bearbha to the Slaine*, i.e., from the River Barrow to the River Slaney.

^467 *Beanntraighe*, now the Barony of Bantry, in the county of Wexford, lying between these rivers. The Clann-Coscraigh are now unknown.

^468 *Fearann-deisceartach*, i.e., the southern land. This is probably the present barony of Bargy. The family name O'Duibhginn is still very common in Leinster, and is anglicized as Deegin and Duggan. It is to be distinguished from O'Dubhagain.

^469 *Fothart of the Carn*, so called from Carnsore point, its eastern extremity, now the barony of Forth, in the southeast of the county of Wexford. The people called Fotharta were, according to the Irish genealogists, the descendants of Eochaidh Finn Fothairt, brother of Conn of the Hundred Battles.

^470 *O'Lorcain*, now always anglicized as Larkin, without the prefix O'. This name is very common in Leinster, but the pedigree has not been preserved, as the family had sunk into obscurity at an early period.

^471 *Crioch-na-gCenel*, also called Fearann na gCenel.—Fernegenall was

granted by the Earl Richard Strongbow to Maurice de Prendergast. See Harris's *Hibernica*, p. 41. This territory seems to have comprised the district around Artramont and is included in the barony of Shelmaliere East. It was separated from the town of Wexford by the River Slaney. The exact location of this territory is described as follows by Giraldus, *Topographia Hib.*, Dist. ii., c. 32, where it is corruptly called Fernigenan:—

> "Concerning the rats expelled from Fernigenan by Saint Ivor. There is a certain province in Leinster called Fernigenan <note>Fernigenal</note>, which is separated from Wexford only by the waters of the Slaney. From there, the larger mice, commonly called *Rats*, were entirely expelled by the curse of Saint Ivor the Bishop (whose books they had perhaps gnawed); afterwards, they could neither be born there nor live there if brought in."

472 O'h-Artghoile, now Hartley or Hartilly, without the prefix O'. This name still exists in south Leinster, where Irish speakers pronounce it O'h-Airtialla.

473 O'Riaghain, now Ryan, a name still found in County Carlow. It should be distinguished from O'Mulryan of County Tipperary, which is now usually shortened to Ryan without the "O'" or "Mul."

474 *Ui-Drona.*—This tribe, descendants of Drona (the fourth in the line from Cathaoir Mor, monarch of Ireland in the second century), gave its name to the barony of Idrone in County Carlow. See *Leabhar-na-gCeart*, p. 212, note k.

475 O'Nuallain, now anglicized as Nolan, without the prefix O'.

476 *Fotharta*, generally called Fotharta-Fea, now the barony of Forth in County Carlow. O'Flaherty states in his *Ogygia*, part iii. c. 64, that the descendants of Eochaidh Finn Fothart remained chiefs of this territory until the death of O'Nuallain, the last proprietor, who died shortly before O'Flaherty wrote. See *Leabhar na gCeart*, p. 211.

477 *Magh-da-chon*, plain of the two hounds. This name is now anglicized as Moyacomb, a parish in the barony of Rathvilly, County Carlow, extending into the barony of Shillelagh in County Wicklow. It is sometimes called Farron O'Neale. The O'Neill family of this territory is now unknown. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1088, p. 930, note l.

478 *Siol-Elaigh*, i.e., the lineage of Elach, now the barony of Shillelagh in the southwest of County Wicklow. The Commissioners appointed to form the county of Wicklow on January 10, 1605, described the territorial situation of this barony as follows:—

<page-num>lviii</page-num>
<header>O'HUIDHRIN.</header>

> "And having viewed and surveyed the Irish territory called Shillelagh, <note>they say and present</note> that it is bounded on the south side by the territory of Kilteile, also known as MacMorishe's country in County Wexford; on the west by the country of Farren O'Neale and the lordship of Tully <note>Offelimy</note> in County Carlow; on the north and east by the lordship of Clonemore and the territory of Cosha." See *Erck's Repertory of the Chancery Enrolments*.

479 *O'Gaoithin*, now anglicized Geehan, Gihon, and Gahan, without the prefix O'. This name is still common in the barony of Shillelagh and throughout Leinster, but it is now obscure and reduced, with very few exceptions.

480 *O'Dunlaing*, now anglicized Dowling, without the prefix O'. This family would appear from our text to have been situated on the east side of the Barrow; but the old map of Leix <note>Leax</note> and Offaly <note>Ophaly</note>, already referred to, places O'Dowling's country on the west side of the Barrow, in the present Queen's County <note>County Laois</note>.

481 *Across the Bearbha.*—From this it is quite clear that the last-mentioned territory, namely, the Lagan (O'Dunlaing's territory), was on the east side of the Barrow, for the author next proceeds <note>westward</note> across that river into Ossory.

482 *Mac Giollaphatraic*, now anglicized Fitzpatrick. Ancient Ossory comprised the barony of Upper Ossory in Queen's County <note>County Laois</note> and nearly the entire county of Kilkenny. It is stated by Keating and others that Ossory originally extended from the Barrow westward to the Suir; but there is sufficient evidence to show that since the introduction of Christianity, its limits never extended beyond those of the present Diocese of Ossory. See *Leabhar na gCeart*, pp. 17, 18, note a.

483 *Bladhma*, now Slieve Bloom, on the frontiers of King's County <note>County Offaly</note> and Queen's County <note>County Laois</note>.

484 *To the sea*, that is, to the estuary called the Meeting of the Three Waters, near Waterford.

485 *Liathdruim.*—This is one of the ancient names of Tara and is incorrectly applied here. There are several places of the name in Ireland, but none in Ossory.

486 *To the plain of Munster*, that is, from the Barrow to the plain of Magh Feimhin in the county of Tipperary.

487 *Coill Uachtorach*, now the barony of Upperwoods, the present legal name of a subdivision of the old barony of Upper Ossory, at the foot of Slieve Bloom in Queen's County <note>County Laois</note>.

<header>TRIBES OF LEINSTER.</header>

488 *O' Dubhshlaine*, now anglicized Delany, without the prefix O'. This family is still very numerous in this territory.

489 *Mountain of most beauteous rivers.*—This is Slieve Bloom, in which the three sister rivers—the Suir, Nore, and Barrow—have their sources.

490 *O' Cearbhaill*, now Carroll. He was a descendant of the celebrated Cearbhall, chief lord of Ossory from 845 to 885. He is to be distinguished from O' Carroll of Ely O'Carroll, who was seated on the other side of Slieve Bloom and was of a different race.

491 *O' Donnchadha*, now anglicized throughout the diocese of Ossory as Dunphy. The chief of this family, Donogh O'Donoghue, was the founder of the abbey of Jerpoint in 1180, in which he was buried in the year 1185. See *Archdall's Monasticon*, county Kilkenny, Jerpoint.

492 *Gabhran*, now the barony of Gowran, in the county of Kilkenny.

493 *Cill Chainnigh*, i.e., the cell or church of St. Canice, now Kilkenny. "Of the limestones" is a particularly characteristic description, and it is to be wished that our author had given us more geological notes of this nature.

494 *Sliabh gCaithle.*—This name is now forgotten, nor has any authority been found to fix the limits of the territory of O'Carroll of Ossory. It probably extended from the church of Kilkenny to the mountains of Fasaghdineen, comprising all the rich lands between the present town and these mountains.

495 *The sea is smooth*, i.e., whenever he goes on the sea it becomes calm and smooth, as a consequence of his justice and righteousness.

496 *Ui-Duach of Osraighe*—For several centuries this territory has been considered coextensive with the barony of Fassadinin, in the county of Kilkenny; but it was anciently more extensive, as appears from the words of our author, who calls it "the extensive plain of the Feoir," i.e., of the river Nore. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 850, note ᵉ, p. 484, and p. 26, note ᵍ.

496 *O' Braonain*, now always anglicized Brennan, without the O'. The noted Dr. John Brennan of Dublin, commonly called "The Wrestling Doctor," was the last recognized chief of this sept. Families named Brennan are still exceedingly numerous in their original territory, but their pedigrees are unknown.

498 *Mac Braoin*, now Breen, without the prefix Mac. The situation of this family is uncertain.

499 *O' Broithe.*—This family is still called this by those who speak Irish, and was anglicized O'Broghie in the Patent Rolls of the first and fourth year of

<meta>This page continues the annotations on the tribes and territories of Ossory, specifically focusing on the lineages and geographical boundaries of families within modern County Kilkenny and the neighboring areas.</meta>

<page-num>lx</page-num>
<header>O'HUIDHRIN.</header>

James I; but the name is now usually pronounced Brophy. Their territory comprised the level portion of the barony of Galmoy, in the county of Kilkenny. This family is still numerous throughout ancient Ossory and in the neighboring districts of the county of Tipperary. They were driven from the plain of Magh Sedna into Upper Ossory after the English Invasion, and their chief settled at Ballybrophy, near Borris-in-Ossory, in Queen’s County. In 1603, Patrick O’Broghie was of Reo, in this county. The name of *Sedna*, now anglicized as *Shade*, is still hereditary in this family.

500 *Magh Lacha*, a plain in the barony of Kells, county of Kilkenny.

501 *O’Faolain*, now Phelan and Whelan, without the prefix O’. This family is very numerous throughout ancient Ossory. The Phelans of this race are to be distinguished from those of the Decies of Munster.

502 *Magh-Airbh*, a plain in the barony of Crannagh, in the county of Kilkenny.

503 *O’Caibhdeanaigh*, now anglicized as Keveny, and by some as Gaffney; but the name O’Gamhna is the one usually anglicized throughout ancient Ossory.

504 *Coill O gCathasaigh*, i.e., the wood of the Ui-Cathasaigh.

505 *O’Gloiairn*. This name was anglicized as Glory, but it is now obsolete. See the *Annuary of the Kilkenny, etc., Archaeological Society*, vol. 1, part 2, p. 101, note b.

506 *The Callann*, now the King’s River, on which the town of Callan stands. It has its source in the hills near Tullaroan and, flowing through Callan and past Kells and Stonyford, joins the Suir at Annamult.

507 *Ui-Berchon*, anglicized as Ibercon, an ancient barony in the county of Kilkenny, forming the northern portion of the present barony of Ida, which comprises three ancient baronies, namely, Ida, Igrine, and Ibercon.

508 *O’Caolluidhe*, now always anglicized as Kelly, which is incorrect. It should be Cayley, or at least Keally, with the first syllable long.

509 *Bright-flowing Bearbha.*—Ros-Ua-Berchon, now Rosbercon, which formed the eastern extremity of this territory, is on the west bank of the River Barrow.

510 *Ui-Eirc*, now Iverk, a barony forming the southern portion of the county of Kilkenny. It is watered by the Suir and the River Graney, which frequently flood some adjacent districts and cover them with sand.

511 *O’Bruadair*, anglicized as Broder and Broderic, a name now reduced to obscurity in this territory.

--- Page 275 ---
<meta>This page continues the topographical and historical annotations of Irish territories, moving from the borders of Ossory into the tribes and landmarks of Munster.</meta>

<page-num>lxi</page-num>
## TRIBES OF MUNSTER.

512 *Maonmhagh.*—This was the name of a plain (Moinmoy), extending around Loughrea, in the county of Galway. The territory of Iverk is, however, currently far richer, more beautiful, and better cultivated.

513 *The Siuir, that is, the sister,* now the river Suir. It rises at the eastern base of *Sliabh Ailduin*, now the Devil’s Bit mountain, in the county of Tipperary, and passes through the towns of Thurles, Cahir, Clonmel, and Carrick, from where it forms the boundary between the counties of Kilkenny and Waterford until it joins the Barrow.

514 *Magh Feimhin.*—The ancient name of a plain comprising that portion of the present county of Tipperary which belongs to the diocese of Lismore. It is described as extending from the river Suir northwards to Corca-Eathrach, from which it is clear that it comprised the whole of the barony of Iffa and Offa East. See Colgan’s *Trias Thaum.*, p. 201, and Lanigan’s *Ecclesiastical History of Ireland*, vol. i. p. 282.

515 *Caisel of the kings, that is,* the stone fort of the kings, now the town of Cashel, in the county of Tipperary, the seat of the kings of Munster from the beginning of the fifth century until the English Invasion. See *Leabhar na gCeart*, p. 28, note ᵃ.

516 *Corc.*—He was king of Munster early in the fifth century, but the authentic Irish annals contain no notice of his death. His grandson, Aenghus MacNadfraich, who is said to have been the first Christian king of Munster, was slain in the year 489. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 489, and *Leabhar na gCeart*, p. 28, note ᶜ.

517 *Corca-Athrach.*—O’Flaherty describes this territory as extending in length from Tiprait-farran, near the abbey of the Holy Cross, called Huachtar-lamhann, to Dunandreas, and the northern part of Knockgraffan. *Ogygia*, part iii. c. 81.

518 *Plain of Tál.*—Tal was a nickname of Cormac Cais, ancestor of the Dal-gCais.

519 *Brian, that is,* Brian Borumha, ancestor of the O’Briens of Thomond, who became monarch of Ireland in 1002 and was killed at Clontarf in the year 1014. He was called Borumha because he renewed the cow tribute of Leinster, which had been remitted by Finachta, monarch of Ireland, in the seventh century. *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 690, p. 299, note ˣ.

520 *Tailgenn.*—This was the name by which St. Patrick was called by the Druids. It is explained as *circulo tonsus in capite* <note>shaven in a circle on the head</note> by Colgan, but as *Asciciput* <note>adze-head</note> in the *Book of Armagh*, fol. 2, p. b, col. 1; and as *artis caput* <note>head of art</note> by Probus. See *Trias Thaum.*, p. 5, col. 2, p. 49, col. 1, and p. 123, col. 2.

<vocab>Maonmhagh, Siuir, Suir, Magh Feimhin, Caisel, Cashel, Corc, Corca-Athrach, Brian Borumha, Tailgenn, St. Patrick, Munster, Tipperary, Galway, Sliabh Ailduin, Leabhar na gCeart, Dal-gCais</vocab>

<page-num>lxii</page-num>
<header>O'HUIDHRIN.</header>

521 *Eoghan*, son of *Oilioll*, that is, Eoghan, eldest son of Oilioll Olum, king of Munster in the third century.

522 *MacCarthaigh*, now anglicized Mac Carthy. This family was driven from Cashel shortly after the English Invasion, and they settled in what are now the counties of Kerry and Cork, where, in course of time, they became very numerous.

523 *Eoghanacht of Caisel*.—This was the original tribe name of the O'Donoghues, who were anciently located in Magh-Feimhin, now the barony of Iffa and Offa East, in the southeast of the county of Tipperary. They were driven from this territory shortly after the English Invasion, when they settled in the present barony of Magunihy, in the county of Kerry, to which they gave the name of Eoghanacht Ui Donnchadha, anglicized Onaght-O'Donoghue.

524 *Sliabh Ardachaidh*, now the barony of Slieveardagh, in the county of Tipperary.

525 *O'Deaghaidh*, now anglicized Day, without the prefix O'. This family is to be distinguished from the O'Deaghaidhs, or O'Deas, of Cinel-Fearmaic, in Thomond, who are of the Dalcassian race.

526 *O' h-Oilella*.—This name is now obsolete, as is every derivative in Ireland formed from Oilioll, whether belonging to a person or place, except Tir-Oilella, in the county of Sligo, which has been corrupted to Tirerrill. According to this analogy, O' h-Oilella might be anglicized O' Herrill, or Herrill; but there is no such surname now in Ireland.

527 *O'Brachain*, now Brahan.

528 *Deisi*.—This tribe descended from Fiacha Suighdhe, the elder brother of the monarch Conn of the Hundred Battles, and were originally located in *Deisi Teamhrach*, the present barony of Deece, to the south of Tara, in Meath; but they were expelled from there in the third century by their relative, King Cormac, grandson of Conn, and after having attempted to obtain a footing in various parts of the south of Ireland, they ultimately settled in Munster, and subdued that part of the country extending from the river Suir to the sea, and from Lismore to Credan Head, the eastern extremity of the present county of Waterford. In the fifth century, Aenghus Mac Nadfraeich, king of Munster, granted them the plain of Magh Feimhin, in the present county of Tipperary; but they were driven from there by the Eoghanachts. See Keating's *History of Ireland*; and *Ogygia*, Part iii., c. 69.

529 *O'Bric*, now Brick, without the prefix O'. This family originally

possessed the southern Desies, comprised in the present county of Waterford, but they had fallen under the O’Faelains or O’Phelans, who were originally located in the northern Desies, in the present county of Tipperary, some time before the English Invasion.

<sup>530</sup> **O’Faelain**, now called Phelan in the anglicized form of the name, without the O' prefix; and by some, Whelan.

<sup>531</sup> **O’Mearadhaigh**, now O’Meara, or O’Mara, a name still numerous in the county of Tipperary. Many people drop the prefix.

<sup>532</sup> **Ui-Fathaidh**, now the barony of Iffa and Offa West, in the county of Tipperary.

<sup>533</sup> **Ui-Eoghain Finn**.—The territory of this tribe was in northern Deisi, in the present county of Tipperary, and bordering Iverk on the west side. See the *Miscellany of the Archaeological Society*, vol. i., p. 205. The O’Neills of this race, the head of whom was an esquire in 1753, were afterwards located at Mount Neill, in the barony of Iverk, county Kilkenny.

<sup>534</sup> **Uachtar-tire**, now the barony of Upperthird, in the northwest of the county of Waterford. The O’Flanagans of this race were dispossessed shortly after the English Invasion by the Anglo-Norman family of Poer, now Power, who still possess a large portion of this territory.

<sup>535</sup> **Ui-Aithele**.—The name of this tribe and territory, clearly located between the barony of Upperthird and the sea, is now obsolete. The O’Breslens of this race are also unknown.

<sup>536</sup> **O’ Fodhladha**, now Foley, without the O' prefix. This family is very numerous in the county of Waterford.

<sup>537</sup> **O’Cein**, now Kean, a name still existing in the county of Waterford, and to be distinguished from the family of O’Cathain, now anglicized as Kane, without the O' prefix. The two great tragedians of world-wide fame are of this race.

<sup>538</sup> **Machuin**, now the river Mahon, which rises near Kilmacthomas, and falls into the sea at the village of *Bun Machuine* (Bunmahon).

<sup>539</sup> **Ui Eachach**.—This was the tribe name of the O’Bricks.

<sup>540</sup> **Inis-Fail**, one of the ancient names of Ireland.

<sup>541</sup> **O’ Bric**, now Brick, without the O' prefix.

<sup>542</sup> **Lec Logha**.—This was probably the ancient name of the remarkable rock now called *Clochlobhrais*, located about midway between Kilmacthomas and Dungarvan, in the county of Waterford.

<sup>543</sup> **Liathdruim**, meaning gray ridge, now Leitrim, on the borders of the counties of Waterford and Cork.

<sup>544</sup> **Feara Muighe**.—This name is now preserved in Fermoy, a beautiful

It traces the lineage and modern names of families like the O'Phelans, O'Mearas, O'Flanagans, and O'Foleys.</summary>

<page-num>lxiv</page-num>
<header>O'HUIDHRIN.</header>

and fertile barony in the north of the county of Cork; but the ancient Feara Maighe comprised the modern baronies of Fermoy, and Condons and Clangibbon. See *Leabhar na gCeart*, pp. 78, 82, 261.

<sup>545</sup> **O'Dubhagain**, now O'Dugan, and more usually Duggan. This family descends from the Druid Mogh Ruith, *Magus Rotæ*, who was of the race of Rudhraighe, King of Ulster. See *Leabhar na gCeart*, p. 82.

<sup>546</sup> **Dun Manann**.—This name is now obsolete; it was evidently that of the chief residence of O'Dubhagain, who possessed approximately the northern half of the territory of Feara Maighe Feine, being situated between O'Keeffe and the Ui-Fidhgeinte.

<sup>547</sup> **O'Caoimh**, now anglicized as O'Keeffe, and by many as Keeffe, without the prefix O'. O'Keeffe originally possessed the district now called Roche's Country, which formed the southern half of the ancient Feara Maighe. The family is of the race of Oilioll Olum, and had a king of Munster, namely Fionguine, son of Gorman, who died in the year 902; since that period, the line of MacCarthy has been far more powerful. The O'Keeffes were driven from Fermoy shortly after the English Invasion, when they settled in the present barony of Duhallow.

<sup>548</sup> **Gleannomhain**, or Gleann Amhnach, now Glanworth, in Roche's Country, in the north of the county of Cork. This was the original seat of O'Keeffe. See Smith, *Natural and Civil History of Cork*, book ii., chap. 7, and *Leabhar na gCeart*, p. 90, note s.

<sup>549</sup> **Ui Liathain**.—This tribe derived its name and origin from Eochaidh Liathanach, son of Daire Cearba, ancestor of the Ui-Fidhgeinte. Their territory was nearly coextensive with the present barony of Barrymore, in the county of Cork. See *Hibernia Expugnata*, lib. ii., c. 18, 19, and *Leabhar na gCeart*, pp. 73, 74.

<sup>550</sup> **O'Anamchadhas**.—This name is obsolete, or changed into some anglicized form not now recognizable.

<sup>551</sup> **Ui-Mac Caille**, now the barony of Imokilly, in the county of Cork.

<sup>552</sup> **O'Breaghdha**.—This name is now unknown. It might be anglicized as Bray.

<sup>553</sup> **O'Glaisin**, now unknown. It is not the name anglicized as Gleason or Gleeson.

<sup>554</sup> **Ciarraighe Chuirche**, now Kerrycurrihy, a barony in the county of Cork.

<sup>555</sup> **Race of Torna**, that is, the sept of Ui Torna. The hereditary family name was O'Cuirre, which is now little known.

<sup>556</sup> **Cinel-Aedha**, that is, the race of Aedh (father of Failbhe Flann, King of Munster, A.D. 636), now the barony of Kinelea, in the county of Cork.

It details their lineages, original seats of power, and how many were displaced following the English Invasion.</summary>

<header>TRIBES OF MUNSTER.</header>

⁵⁵⁷ **O'Ceallachain**, now O'Callaghan. This family, which is of the same race as the MacCarthys, was moved from the barony of Kinelea shortly after the English Invasion, when they settled in the barony of Duhallow, where they possessed the parishes of Kilshannick and Clonmeen. See Harris's edition of Ware's Works, vol. ii., p. 72, and Smith's History of Cork, book ii., chap. 6. The senior branch of this family was transplanted to the county of Clare by Cromwell, where it became extinct in the male line early in the nineteenth century. Lord Lismore is the present head of this family in Ireland. See *Circuit of Murchertach MacNeill*, p. 64, for the descent of O'Callaghan and Mac Carthy.

⁵⁵⁸ *Plain of Bearra.*—This seems to be a mistake, as O'Callaghan never had any connection with the territory of Bearra.

⁵⁵⁹ **Cinel m-Bece**, i.e., the race of Bece, from Bece, son of Fergus, who was the son of Felimy, king of Desmond, A.D. 584, and ancestor of O'Mahony. See *Battle of Magh-Rath*, Genealogical Table p. 340, and Payne's *Description of Ireland*, edited by Dr. Aquilla Smith, p. 23.

⁵⁶⁰ **Bandain**, now the river Bandon in the county of Cork.

⁵⁶¹ *Rapid Muaidh* seems to be the name of a river, but the name is now unknown in the county of Cork.

⁵⁶² **O'Mathghamhna**, now anglicized as O'Mahony, and sometimes Mahony, without the prefix O'. The senior member of this family is probably in France. O'Mahony of Dunloe, in Kerry, is believed to be the present head of the family in Ireland.

⁵⁶³ *Race of Lughaidh.*—These were the O'Driscolls who, according to the Irish genealogists, descend from Lughaidh Mac Ithu, the uncle of Milesius of Spain. See the *Miscellany of the Celtic Society*, 1849, pp. 56, 57.

⁵⁶⁴ **O'h-Eidirscoeil**, now O'Driscoll, and sometimes Driscoll, without the prefix O'. *Ibid*, p. 56, and 384–400.

⁵⁶⁵ **Corca Laighdhe.**—This, which was the tribe name of the O'Driscolls, was also applied to their territory, which originally comprised all the south-west part of the present county of Cork, namely, the baronies of Carbery, Beare, and Bantry; but shortly after the English Invasion they were encroached upon by the O'Donovans, O'Mahonys, and O'Sullivans, and more recently by the MacCarthy Reaghs, who reduced their principality—comprising the parishes of Myross, Glanbarahane [Castlehaven], Tullagh, Creagh, Kilcoe, Aghadown, and Clear—to much narrower limits. See *Miscellany of the Celtic Society*, p. 48–57, and p. 148.

⁵⁶⁶ *Harbour of Clear*, i.e., the bay between Cape Clear and Mizen Head, in the south of the county of Cork.

567 *O’Floinns of Arda, i.e., O’Flynn of Ardagh.* The chief of this family resided at Ardagh Castle, situated nearly midway between Skibbereen and Baltimore, in the barony of West Carbery, and county of Cork. See O’Brien’s Irish Dictionary, under *Flann* and *Cobhthach*, and the *Miscellany for the Celtic Society*, pp. 9, 10, 36.

568 *Ui-Baghamhna,* now the barony of Ibawn, in the south of the county of Cork, *ibid.,* p. 36.

569 *Tricha chéd medhonach, i.e.,* the central cantred. This was the old name of the present barony of Barryroe, in the county of Cork. See O’Brien’s Irish Dictionary, *under the word* COBHTHACH. Dr. O’Brien, who knew this part of Ireland well, speaking of the families of O’Cowhig and O’Floinn Arda about the middle of the last century, has the following observation which nearly holds true at the present day:

> “But the melancholy remark which remains to be made is, that, of the two families first mentioned, there is not, to my knowledge, one individual now living who may be considered a gentleman, they having all been dispossessed long ago of their very ancient and large properties; which, indeed, is the case with many other Irish families, no less illustrious in former times, who are now quite extinct or reduced to a state of perfect obscurity, for the reason now mentioned.”

570 *O’Cobhthaigh.*—Dr. O’Brien anglicizes this name as O’Cowhig, which seems to have been the form of the name in use, in his time, among this sept in the county of Cork; but in other more northern parts of Ireland, it is anglicized as Coffey, without the prefix O’. Dr. Smith, in his “Natural and Civil History of Cork,” book ii. c. 3, writes of this family:

> “Almost on every headland of this barony, castles were erected by the Irish, seven of which belonged to the sept of O’Cowhig, namely Dundeedy, Dunowen, Dunore, Duneen, Dunocowhig, Dunworley, and Dungorley.”

571 *Land of Cliodhna, i. e.,* bordering on Tonn Cliodhna, a loud surge in the bay of Glandore, much celebrated by Irish poets.

572 *Muintir-Bhaire,* now Muntervary, a peninsula in the barony of West Carbery in the south-west of the county of Cork, extending from Bantry to Sheepshead, and containing the parish of Kilcrohane. See *Corca Laidhe,* in the *Miscellany of the Celtic Society*, p. 5, and *Tribes of Ireland,* pp. 11 to 15.

573 *Warlike Fothadh.*—He was the third son of Lughaidh Maccon, king of Ireland, in the second century. See *Corca Laidhe,* p. 5, note ^n.

574 *O’Baire.*—This name is now obsolete, unless it has been assimilated to the Anglo-Norman name of Barry. This territory belonged to the

bardic family of O'Daly for several centuries. See *Tribes of Ireland*, p. 14; and *Pacata Hibernia*, book iii.

575 *Plain of Manainn.*—This seems to refer to the plain lying around the fort of Dun-Manann, which, however, was the seat of O'Dubhagain in Fear-Maighe or Fermoy. It must be admitted that Muinter Bhaire cannot compare to it in terms of fertility, though it may compete with it in picturesque beauty.

576 *O'h-Eidersceoil of Bearra*, i.e., O'Driscoll of Beare, a barony in the south-west of the county of Cork, which was held by the Driscolls until they were dispossessed by a branch of the O'Sullivans sometime after the English invasion.

577 *The harbour of Baoi*, now Bantry Bay. The island of *Baoi Bheirre* in this bay is now called Beare Island.

578 *The race of Lughaidh*, i.e., the O'Driscolls and their relatives.

579 *The land of Ith*, a bardic name for the O'Driscoll territory.

580 *Clann t-Sealbaigh*, i.e., the race of Sealbhach. This was the tribe name of the O'Donoghues of the county of Kerry. O'Domhnaill (O'Donnell) was one of the ancient chiefs of this race, but the name has been long obsolete.

581 *O'Donnchadha of Loch Lein*, i.e., O'Donoghue of Ross, at Lough Leane or Killarney, county of Kerry.

582 *O'Donnchadha of the Flesc*, i.e., O'Donoghue of Glenflesk, which is the valley of the river Flesk in Kerry. The present O'Donoghue is the head of this family. See *Tribes of Ireland*, p. 71.

583 *On Munster.*—Dubhdabhoirenn, ancestor of O'Donoghue, was king of Munster in 957, and his son Domhnall, who was killed at the battle of Clontarf, A. D. 1014, was king of Desmond.

584 *Ui-Floinn of Lua*, i.e., the territory of Muscraighe Ui-Fhloinn, or Muskerrylin, which contains fifteen parishes and is accurately described as being around the far-extending Lee and Lua, now Lough Lua, in the barony of Muskerry, through which the river Lee flows. See *Leabhar na g-Ceart*, p. 44.

585 *O'Bece.*—This name, which would be anglicized as Beck, has long since sunk into oblivion.

586 *Beanntraighe*, now the barony of Bantry, in the county of Cork.

587 *Fergus of Uladh*, i.e., Fergus Mac Roigh, the exiled king of Ulster in the first century, from whom O'Conor Kerry, O'Conor of Corcumroe, O'Loughlin, and many other families in Munster are descended.

<header>->O'HUIDHRIN.<-</header>

588 *Ui-Eachach*, that is, the descendants of Eochaidh, son of Cas, son of Core, king of Munster. The *Ui-Mathghamhna*, or O'Mahonys, were the chief family of this race. They were first seated in the barony of Kinel-meaky, in the county of Cork, but they later encroached on the Corca-Laighe and became masters of the district called Fonn-Iartharach, that is, the western land. The name Ui-Eachach is usually anglicized as Ivahagh and is shown on several maps of Munster made during the reigns of Elizabeth I and James I. According to the *Liber Regalis Visitationis* of 1615, it comprised the parishes of Kilmoe, Sscol, Kilcrohane, Durris, Kilmoconnoge, and Caheragh, in the south-west of the county of Cork.

589 *O'Mathghamhna*, now O'Mahony. See the note on Cinel m-Bece, above.

590 *Aos Aísde*.—This name is now forgotten; but as it was the tribe name of the family of O'Muircheartaigh, now O'Moriarty (or more usually Moriarty, without the prefix O'), we must conclude that it was the name of a territory along the river Mang in Kerry.

591 *O'h-Imhasbhain*. This name is now unknown in Munster.

592 *Race of Conaire*, that is, of Conaire II, of the Deagads of Munster, monarch of Ireland in the year 212. See O'Flaherty's *Ogygia*, part iii. c. 63.

593 *Tulach-an-trir*, that is, the hill of the three persons. This was one of the most ancient names of Tara. See *Ogygia*, part iii., c. 17.

594 *Corca Duibhne*.—These were of the race of Conaire I, monarch of Ireland at the beginning of the first century (*Ogygia*, part iii., c. 45). After the establishment of surnames, they branched into the families of O'Falvey, O'Shea, and O'Conghaile (O'Connell). Shortly before the English Invasion, O'Falvey possessed the barony of Corcaguiny, O'Shea that of Iveragh, and O'Conghaile (O'Connell) that of Magunihy; but about the middle of the eleventh century, the O'Donoghues settled in Magunihy and drove the O'Conghailes westwards into Iveragh, where they were seated at Ballycarbery as castellans to Mac Carthy More. The territories of this race of Conaire extended to the Suir, in the county of Tipperary.

595 *O'Seagha*, now anglicized as O'Shea. A branch of this family moved to the city of Kilkenny around the end of the fourteenth century, where they became wealthy and highly respectable.

596 *O'Conghaile*, now corrupted to O'Conaill and anglicized as O'Connell. The head of this family was transplanted by Cromwell to Brenter, near Callan Hill in the county of Clare.

597 *Magh O'gCoinchinn*, now Mugunihy, forming the eastern portion of the county of Kerry. The O'Conghailes were driven from this territory

# TRIBES OF MUNSTER.

$^{598}$ *O'Failbhe*, Anglicized as O'Falvy and Falvy, without the prefix O'. Their territory originally extended from the river Maing to Finntraigh, now Ventry, in the west of the county of Kerry.

$^{599}$ *Ui-Rathach*. This was the tribe name of the O'Sheas. It is now Anglicized as Iveragh, which is a well-known barony in the west of the county of Kerry.

$^{600}$ *Muscraighe*. According to all our Irish genealogical manuscripts, the Muscraighe were the descendants of Cairbre Musc, son of Conaire Mor, monarch of Ireland at the beginning of the third century. *Ogygia*, part iii., chapter 63.

$^{601}$ *Mairtine of Munster*, an ancient tribe of the Firbolgs, of whose territory Emly, in the county of Tipperary, was the center and capital.

$^{602}$ *Muscraighe-Mitine*, otherwise called Muscraighe Ui-Fhloinn. This was the ancient name of the barony of West Muskerry, in the county of Cork. The family of O'Maoilfhabhaill of this race is now unknown. It is quite clear that our author is compiling his list of chiefs and territories from written sources, and not from the families existing in his own time. See note 583, above, on the territory of O'Floinn of Lua, which is evidently the one referred to here, but belonging to a different era. Neither of these families was dominant here in our author's time.

$^{603}$ *O'h-Aodha*, now Anglicized as O'Hea and Hayes, but the O'h-Aodhas of this race are quite obscure.

$^{604}$ *Muscraighe Luachra*, near the *Abhainn mhor*, a territory extending on both sides of the Blackwater, near its source, in the north-west of the county of Cork. See *Leabhar na gCeart*, page 44.

$^{605}$ *Muscraighe of the Three Plains*. This territory was granted by King John to William de Barry, under the name of Muskerry Donegan. The family of O'Donegan is still extant in this territory, but reduced to poverty and obscurity. Dr. James Donegan, the author of the Greek-English Dictionary and a native of Charleville, was one of them. "The church of *Fiort Sceithe*, which is placed by the Calendars of Marianus and the Four Masters (September 6th) in Muscraighe-tri-maighe, is now known by the name of Ardskeagh, which is a small parish in that part of the barony of Fermoy bordering on the barony of Orrery and Kilmore. In the ancient taxations of the diocese of Cloyne, we find a rural deanery called *Muscrydonnegan*, containing the parishes now included in the barony of Orrery and Kilmore, with small adjacent portions of Duhallow and Fermoy. Among the churches in this deanery, Orwery <note>that is, Orbraidhe, or Orrery</note>

->O'HUIDHRIN.<-

and *Fersketh* <note>that is, Feart Skeithe, called *Ardskagh* in 1615, now Ardskeagh</note> are two. Thus the identity of *Muscraighe-tri-maighe* and the barony of Orrery is proved to a demonstration, and O'Brien's statement on the subject (*Irish Dictionary, under the word Muscraighe*) is fully established." — *Note communicated by Dr. Reeves.*

$^{606}$ *Iarann.* — This was evidently the name of a river, but it is now obsolete.

$^{607}$ *Tuath-Saxon*, that is, the <term>cantred</term> of the Saxons. This is the ancient name of the district containing the parish of Tullylease in the north-west of the county of Cork, of which St. Berichert, a Saxon, is the patron. It is probable that this saint established a Saxon colony here in the eighth century, in the same way as St. Cairnech had established a colony of Britons at Tuilen, near Kells, in Meath. The family of O'h-Ionmhainen, now Anglicized as Noonan, were the <term>herenachs</term> <note>hereditary wardens of church lands</note> of the church of Tullylease. See Dr. Reeves's paper on St. Berichert in the *Ulster Journal of Archaeology*, vol. vi., p. 267.

$^{608}$ *Race of Conaire*, that is, of Conaire II. See note 591, above.

$^{609}$ *Muscraighe Treithirne*, also called Muscraighe Brogain and Muscraighe Chuirc. This territory is now comprised in the barony of Clanwilliam, in the south-west of the county of Tipperary. The family name, O'Cuirc, is now Anglicized as Quirk, without the prefix O'.

$^{610}$ *O'Maoilbhloghain.* — This name is still extant and Anglicized as Malone or Mullowne; but this family is to be distinguished from that of O'Maoileoin, which is Anglicized in a similar way.

$^{611}$ *O'Carthaigh.* — This name, which is to be distinguished from that of MacCarthaigh, is still extant and Anglicized as Carty, without the prefix O'.

$^{612}$ *Muscraighe of the west of Feimhen*, so-called because it lies to the west of Magh Feimhen, in the county of Tipperary.

$^{613}$ *Muscraighe-tire.* — This territory comprises the present barony of Lower Ormond, and a part of Upper Ormond, in the county of Tipperary. Its extent is defined by Sir Charles O'Carroll in a letter to the Lord Deputy in 1585, in which he calls it Muschryhyry and states that the Earl of Ormonde lately called it by the false name of Lower Ormonde—a designation which it had never borne before, because it was always considered a part of Thomond.

$^{614}$ *O'Donghalaigh*, now Anglicized as Donnelly, without the prefix O'. This family is to be distinguished from the O'Donnellys of Tyrone, who are of a totally different race.

$^{615}$ *O'Fuirg*, now obsolete.

<summary>This page provides historical and geographic annotations for various Irish territories and families, identifying the ancient districts of the Muscraighe and documenting the Anglicization of family names like Quirk, Malone, and Donnelly.</summary>

<page-num>lxxi</page-num>

### TRIBES OF MUNSTER.

616 *Corca-Baiscinns.*—Our author here follows the lineage and proceeds from the east side of the Shannon to the west of Thomond. These were the descendants of Cairbre Baschaoin, the brother of Cairbre Musc, already mentioned. The two Corca-Baiscinns originally comprised the baronies of Clonderalaw, Moyarta, and Ibrickan, in the west of the county of Clare.

617 *Muintir Domhnaill*, that is, the family of O’Domhnaill, now Anglicized as O’Donnell.

618 *O’Baiscinn*, now Anglicized as Baskin, without the prefix O’. These two families of the race of Cairbre Baschaoin were dispossessed by the MacMahons, a branch of the O’Briens, early in the fourteenth century.

619 *Tree over the Boinn*, so called because his ancestor was king of Tara and Meath, through which the River Boyne flows.

620 *Ui-Bracain*, now the barony of Ibrickan, in the west of the county of Clare. After the expulsion of the MacGormans from Leinster <note>see note on *Ui-Bairrche*, above</note>, shortly after the English Invasion, they were settled in this territory by O’Brien.

621 *O’Maolcorcra*.—This name is now unknown in the barony of Ibrickan. This family would appear to have sunk into insignificance when the MacGormans were planted in their territory by O’Brien.

622 *The two Invers*, that is, Liscannor Bay and Dunbeg Bay, at the extremities of the territory of Ibrickan.

623 *Fochla*, that is, the north, alluding to Ibrickan being the most northern portion of the country of the Corca-Baiscinn, of the race of Conaire II, in North Munster.

624 *O’Ceallaigh*.—The king of Cashel was bound to defend O’Ceallaigh. Was O’Ceallaigh of Hy-Many bound to protect the race of Cairbre Baschaoin, son of Conaire II? This is obscure.

625 *Conaire of Cliach*, alluding to the battle of Cliach, where Conaire I defeated Nuada Finn, king of Leinster.

626 *Ernai*, that is, the Ernaans of Munster, who descended from Ederscel, the father of Conaire I, monarch of Ireland, A.M. 3944. See *Ogygia*, part iii., chapter 44.

627 *Race of Fergus*, ex-king of Ulster in the first century. This Fergus, surnamed Mac Roigh, had three sons by Meadhbh, queen of Connaught, namely, Ciar, ancestor of all the Ciarraighe; Corc, ancestor of the Corcomroe of Thomond; and Conmac, ancestor of all the Conmaicni of Connaught and Moy-Rein. See O’Flaherty’s *Ogygia*, part iii., chapters 42 and 46.

<summary>This page provides historical and genealogical annotations for the tribes of West Thomond (modern County Clare), specifically the Corca-Baiscinns and the descendants of Fergus Mac Roigh. It details the displacement of original families like the O'Bashkins by the MacMahons and the settlement of the MacGormans in Ibrickan.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the annotations on the tribes of Munster, focusing on the Ciarraighe (Kerry) families and the Corcumruadh of Clare, specifically the descendants of Fergus of Ulster.</meta>

<page-num>lxxii</page-num>

<header>O'HUIDHRIN.</header>

628 *Ciarraighe*, now anglicized as Kerry.

629 *O'Conchobhair*, now O'Conor, or O'Connor Kerry.

630 *From the Strand*, that is, the country of the Ciarraighe which extended from the strand of the harbor of Tralee to the River Sinaim, now the Shannon, and comprised about the northern third part of the present county of Kerry.

631 *O'Laoghain*, now anglicized as Lane, without the prefix O'.

632 *Ui-Fearba*.—Location not proven.

633 *O'Caithneannaigh*.—This name is now unknown in Kerry.

634 *Battle-peaks of Cualann*.—Cualann is a mountainous territory in Leinster; but it is probable that the name is here intended for the mountains of Sliabh Mis, Cathair Conroi, etc., in the barony of Trughanackmy, and county of Kerry.

635 *O'Duibhdain*.—This family is now unknown in Kerry, as is the name and location of their territory of *Ui-Flannain*.

636 *Alltraighe*.—This clan was situated around the river of Tralee, as we learn from the Latin *Lives of St. Brendan*, in which it is stated that the saint, who was of the Alltraighe, was born at *Littus Ly (Lighe)*, now Tralee.

637 *O'Neidhe*.—This name is still extant in Kerry, but by a whim of custom anglicized to Neville! A branch of this family was situated at Knockpatrick, in the county of Limerick, where they were hereditary keepers of Saint Patrick's Bell. John Neville, Esq., M.R.I.A., engineer for the county of Louth, is of this family.

638 *Clann Conaire*, that is, the family of O'Conaire, now anglicized as Connery, without the prefix O'.

639 *Corcumruadh*, otherwise called Corc Modhruadh, that is, the descendants of Corc Modhruadh, third son of Fergus, the dethroned king of Ulster in the first century. The country of the Corcumruadh was originally coextensive with the diocese of Kilfenora, and comprised the present baronies of Corcomroe and Burrin, in the northwest of the county of Clare. The bard here, following the tribes genealogically, jumps from Kerry to Clare to describe the territories of the race of Fergus of Ulster. The families of O'Dicholla, O'Maoileitigh, and O'Draighnen, of Sliabh-Eise, are now unknown in this territory. The name O'Draighnen is extant in other parts of Ireland, and anglicized as Drinan. Sliabh-Eise may be the present Sliabh Eilbhe, on the borders of the baronies of Burrin and Corcomroe.

640 *Feara-Arda*, that is, men of the point. This was another name for the Corcumruadh. The island of Inis-caerach, now Mutton Island, near Kilmurry Ibrickan, was in the territory of Feara-Arda.

<summary>This page provides historical and geographical annotations for various clans and territories in Kerry and Clare, tracing the lineages of families like O’Connor Kerry and the descendants of Fergus of Ulster.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the annotations on the tribes of Munster, focusing on the O'Conor and O'Loughlin families of Corcomroe and Burren, as well as the territories of Luachair and the lineage of Fergus.</meta>

<page-num>lxxiii</page-num>

<header>TRIBES OF MUNSTER.</header>

641 *O'Conchobhair*, now O'Conor. This family had significant possessions in the barony of Corcomroe in the year 1584, and for some time after; but today, there is not a man of the race above the rank of cottier or small farmer.

642 *Conach*—This was probably the old name of the river Farsett, which rises in Binn Formaoile and falls into Lisconor Bay, near Duagh Castle.

643 *O'Lochlainn*, now O'Loughlin.—This family has been somewhat more fortunate than their relations the O'Conors, for there are some respectable gentlemen of the name, such as O'Loughlin of Newtown and Sir Colman O'Loghlen; but their pedigrees have not been established with any certainty.

644 *Boirinn*, that is, the rocky district, now the barony of Burren in the north of the county of Clare. It was originally considered a part of Corcomroe and called East Corcomroe; it is interesting to note that the abbey of Corcomroe is situated in Burren.

645 *Tealach-Chuire*—This was the tribe-name of the O'Loughlins.

646 *Dal Meadhruaidh*.—This was another name for the Corca Modhruadh. They are called the Host of Macha because they came from Ulster, where Eamhain Macha was the name of their original palace.

647 *Race of musical Ciar*, that is, the Ciarraighe.

648 *The Race of Tál*, that is, the people of Corcomroe, so called from their ancestor Tál, son of Broc, who was the eleventh in descent from Modhruadh. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1573, p. 1669, note ᵘ.

649 *Turn we westwards*.—This is a mistake, because the province of the race of Maicniadh, by which Desmond is meant here, is nearly due south of the race of Tál, or the people of Corcomroe.

650 *Prevailed over Cruachan*, that is, whose ancestor Fergus had possession of Cruachan when he seduced Meadhbh, queen of Connacht.

651 *Old Luachair*.—This was the name of a territory of great extent situated to the south of the country of the Ciarraighe, and extending into the present counties of Cork and Limerick.

652 *Plain of Luachair*.—This was the name of the level portion of the present barony of Magunihy, in the southeast of the present county of Kerry; but it formed no part of the country of the ancient Ciarraighe. It comprised the territories of O'Keeffe, O'Callaghan, O'Donoghue, and MacAuliffe.

653 *O'Dunadhaigh*, now anglicized as Doney and Denny, without the prefix O'.

654 *O'Donnchadha*, now anglicized as O'Donoghue. O'Donoghue of Loch

# O'HUIDHRIN.

Lein, or the Lakes of Killarney, is now unknown. He had his residence at Ross Castle, near Killarney, and was the head chieftain over the entire territory of Eoghanacht Ui Donnchadha (anglicized as Onaght-Idonoghue), which, during the reign of Queen Elizabeth, was considered to be as extensive as the present barony of Magunihy. O'Donoghue of Glenflesk is the only known representative of this family.

<margin>655</margin> *O'Cearbhaill*, in English O'Carroll. There was a family of this name in Magunihy preceding the O'Donoghues; but they sank into poverty and obscurity many centuries ago and are now unknown.

<margin>656</margin> *O'Caoimh*, now O'Keeffe. The position of Urluachair is marked by the Crown lands of Pobble O'Keeffe, situated in the barony of Duhallow, on the borders of the counties of Cork, Limerick, and Kerry, and containing about 9,000 statute acres; but this territory was originally much more extensive, for we learn from ancient authorities that the two Paps of Danann, now the Pap Mountains, were in it. See *Cormac's Glossary* under the entry Anann; and Keating's *History of Ireland*, Haliday's edition, p. 104; also *Leabhar na gCeart*, p. 75.

<margin>657</margin> *O'Ceallachain*.—The O'Callachains, now O'Callaghans, are descended from Ceallachan, son of Domhnall, son of Murchadh, son of Donnchadh, son of Ceallachan, king of Cashel, or Munster, who died in the year 954. See *Circuit of Muircheartach Mac Neill*, p. 64. Before the English Invasion the O'Callaghans were located in the barony of Cinel-Aedha, now Kinelea, in the south of the county of Cork; but being driven from there by Robert Fitzstephen and Milo de Cogan, they settled in the barony of Duhallow, in the north of the same county, where the chief of the family, Conor O'Callaghan, resided at the Castle of Drumaneen, on the Blackwater, in 1594, and then held extensive territorial possessions, comprising the parishes of Kilshannig and Clonmeen, as appears from a legal inquiry taken at Mallow before Sir Thomas Norris, Vice-President of Munster, on the 25th of October, 1594. The head of this family was forcibly relocated by Cromwell to the county of Clare. Lord Lismore is the present chief of the name in Ireland.

<margin>658</margin> The river *Ella*, now the Allo or Allow, which rises from the slopes of the Use mountains, in the northwest of the county of Cork, and flows into the Blackwater ten miles below Kanturk.

<margin>659</margin> *Gleann Salchain*, a valley extending northwest of Newmarket, in the barony of Duhallow, and county Cork.

<margin>660</margin> *Mac Amhlaoibh*, now Mac Auliffe. The chief residence of Mac Auliffe was Castle Mac Auliffe, near Newmarket; and his territory,

<page-num>lxxv</page-num>

### TRIBES OF MUNSTER.

...with that of Aes-Ella, or the people of the river Allo, comprised all that wild, mountainous, and heath-covered district lying between Newmarket and the boundaries of the counties of Limerick and Kerry, where the rivers Feale, Allo, and Blackwater have their sources. The head of this family, who had been born to a substantial estate, was the weighmaster in the market-house at Kenmare in 1840, when the editor had a long conversation with him about the traditions of this wild district of Aes-Ealla.

<margin>661</margin> *O'Tedgamhna*.—This name is now obsolete, and the family seems to have been overshadowed by the Mac Auliffes and O'Keeffes at an early period.

<margin>662</margin> *Dun Durlais*.—If this was a proper name, it is now obsolete. It might simply mean "of the strong fort." Durlis and Derlish, as well as Thurles, commonly occur as names of townlands and earthen forts.

<margin>663</margin> *Across Luachair*.—This shows that Luachair shared a boundary with the territory of Claonghlais, now Clonlish, a wild district in the barony of Upper Connello in the county of Limerick, and on the borders of the counties of Cork and Kerry. Luachair evidently comprised the territories of O'Donoghue, O'Keeffe, O'Callaghan, and Mac Auliffe, or the barony of Magunihy in the county of Kerry and that of Duhallow in the county of Cork.

<margin>664</margin> *Ui-Conaill*, now the baronies of Upper and Lower Connello in the county of Limerick.

<margin>665</margin> *O'Coilens*, now Collins, without the prefix O'. The head of this family was later driven from this territory and settled in the barony of Carbery, County Cork. The family is still numerous in the original territory.

<margin>666</margin> *O'Billraidhe*.—This name is now obsolete. This family, after being expelled from Ui-Conaill Gabhra, settled at Cnocan Ui-Bhillraidhe, now Watergrass Hill, in the county of Cork.

<margin>667</margin> *Mac Innerigh*, now Mac Eniry.

<margin>668</margin> *Corca-Muicheat*.—This name is still preserved and is another name for Castletown Mac Eniry in the barony of Upper Connello in the county of Limerick. Mac Eniry descends from Sedna, the fourth son of Cairbre Aebhdha, ancestor of the Ui Cairbre; and although his territory is now a part of the barony of Upper Connello, it was originally a portion of the territory of the Ui-Cairbre Aebhdha. The Mac Enirys were never driven from this territory and held considerable estates here until the period of the Revolution; however, they are all currently reduced to poverty and obscurity.

<margin>669</margin> *Corca-Oiche*.—The exact location of this territory is unknown. The

<page-num>lxxvi</page-num>

# O'HUIDHRIN.

The family of O'Macasa is still in existence and anglicized as Macassey and Maxey, without the prefix O'. The name is more numerous in County Tipperary than in County Limerick at the present day.

<margin>670</margin> *Ui-Rossa*, now Iveross, or Iveruss, is a parish on the Shannon in the barony of Kenry, County Limerick. The name O'Bearga, which might be anglicized as O'Bargie or Bargie, is now extinct.

<margin>671</margin> *Caonraighe* is now the barony of Kenry, in the north of County Limerick.

<margin>672</margin> *O'Maolcallann*: This name is now extinct in this part of Ireland. In other places, it is anglicized as Mulholland or Mulhollan, without the prefix O'.

<margin>673</margin> *Dal-Cairbre-Ebha*, otherwise Ui Cairbre Aebhdha. The territory of this tribe comprised the present barony of Coshma in County Limerick, and the plains extending from there down to the Shannon.

<margin>674</margin> *O'Cleirchin*, now O'Clerchain, and anglicized as Clerkan and Cleary. The name still exists in this territory, but the family has been reduced to poverty and obscurity.

<margin>675</margin> *O'Donnabhain* is now anglicized as O'Donovan, and more frequently Donovan, without the prefix O'. This family, from the senior line of Oilioll Olum, was expelled from this territory shortly after the English Invasion by the O'Briens and Fitzgeralds, and they settled in O'Driscoll's country in County Cork. Their principal seats had been at Bruree and Croom in the present County Limerick.

<margin>676</margin> *Dun Cuirc*, meaning the fort of Corc. This is a bardic name for Bruree, the seat of O'Donovan.

<margin>677</margin> *The Maigh*, meaning the river of the plain, is now the Maigue, which rises near Charleville and, passing through Croom and Adare, flows into the Shannon nine miles below Limerick.

<margin>678</margin> *Down to the Sionainn*, meaning down or northwards to the Shannon.

<margin>679</margin> *Eoghanacht-Aine* is the name of a tribe and territory lying around Knockany, in the barony of Small County, County Limerick.

<margin>680</margin> *O'Ciarmhaic* is now anglicized as Kerwick, but more generally changed to Kirby.

<margin>681</margin> *Ui-Enda* is now Heney, without the O'. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1205, 1215.

<margin>682</margin> *Aine-Aulum*; its location has not been determined.

<margin>683</margin> *O'Suilleabhain*, now O'Sullivan. This family was originally seated at Knockraffon, in the barony of Middlethird, County Tipperary, but they

were driven from there shortly after the English Invasion by the family of De Burgo, when they settled in the present counties of Cork and Kerry.

<margin>684</margin> *Eoghanacht-Aradh.*—This appears to be the same as Eoghanacht-Caille-na-manach, which is the present barony of Kilnamanagh in County Tipperary. *O'Cuile* is probably the name now anglicized as Quill. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1046.

<margin>685</margin> *Aolmhagh*, meaning "limestone plain." The exact location of this territory, which was in County Tipperary, is now unknown. The name O'Caollaighe is now anglicized as Kelly. It seems to be a mistake for O'Caella, a name still numerous and anglicized as Kyley.

<margin>686</margin> *Eoghanacht of Crich-Cathbhuidh.*—This territory is also in the present County Tipperary, extending, according to the *Book of Lismore*, folio 208, from *Fert Moraidh* to *Sliabh Eibhlinne*. The *Abhainn Ua gCathbhadha*, now the River Nenagh, flows through this district. It rises at the boundary of the barony of Kilnamanagh and Upper Ormond and flows westward through the latter for several miles, then winding northwest through Lower Ormond and passing close to the town of Nenagh, falls into *Loch Dergdheirc* (Lough Derg) at Drumneen, five miles northwest of Nenagh, after a course of about thirty-five miles. See *Book of Leinster*, folio 105.

<margin>687</margin> *O'Duineachair*, now anglicized as Donaher, without the prefix O'.

<margin>688</margin> *Eoghanacht of Rosarguid.*—This was a territory in the barony of Upper Ormond in the present County Tipperary. O'Mergdha, or O'Meara, of this race, had his seat at Toomyvara in this barony.

<margin>689</margin> *Carn-Mughaine*, that is, the cairn or burial mound of Mughain, a woman's name. This cairn is still to be seen near Toomyvara.

<margin>690</margin> *Siol-Maoilduin.*—This sept of the Eoghanachts and their seat of <term>DungCais</term> are now unknown. From the reference to the water, it is probable that they were located on the east side of *Loch Dergdheirc*, to the northwest of Nenagh.

<margin>691</margin> *Eachdhruim*, now unknown. The name is usually anglicized as Aughrim.

<margin>692</margin> *Eoghanacht of Gabhra.*—This is another name for Ui-Conaill-Gabhra, now the barony of Connello in County Limerick.

<margin>693</margin> *O'Cinnfhaeladh*, now anglicized as Kinealy, without the prefix O'. This family is now reduced to poverty and obscurity, but the name is still common among the peasantry.

<margin>694</margin> *Aes-Greine.*—This territory is comprised within the present barony of Clanwilliam in County Limerick. See *Aes-tri-maighe* below.

<margin>695</margin> *O'Conaing.*—This name is now anglicized as Gunning. Their chief seat was at Caislen-Ui-Chonaing, now corruptly anglicized as Castleconnell, but they have long since sunk into poverty and obscurity, having been dispossessed by the Burkes shortly after the English Invasion.

<margin>696</margin> *Saingil*, now Singland, near Limerick, originally included in O'Conaing's territory.

<margin>697</margin> *Grian*, now Pallis-grean, which originally belonged to this territory of Aes-Greine, though now included in the barony of Coonagh.

<margin>698</margin> *The Race of Cormac Cas.*—These were the O'Briens of Thomond and their relatives, who were, in O'Huidhrin's time, principally seated to the north of the river Shannon, but they had a considerable territory to the south of it in the present County Limerick. See a curious genealogical account of the people of Dal-Cais of Thomond in O'Flaherty's *Ogygia*, part iii. chapters 81, 82.

<margin>699</margin> *Lorc of the lamp.*—This was Lorcan, grandfather of Brian Boruumha.

<margin>700</margin> *Deis-beg.*—This was the ancient name of the present barony of Small County in the county of Limerick. The town of Bruff was the chief seat of this territory and is still called Brugh na Deise by all the Irish-speaking people of the counties of Tipperary, Waterford, and Kilkenny.

<margin>701</margin> *Claire.*—This was the ancient name of a hill near Duntryleague, in the barony of Small County. Oilíoll Olum, the great ancestor of the kings of Munster, was buried in this hill, and a remarkable cromlech was raised over him, which still remains in good preservation.

<margin>702</margin> *O'Luain*, now Loane, and sometimes anglicized as Lamb.

<margin>703</margin> *Ui-Duibhrosa*, now unknown. The name would be anglicized as Duross.

<margin>704</margin> *O'Faircheallaigh*, anglicized as Farrelly in other parts of Ireland; but the name is unknown at Duntryleague, and there is scarcely one of the name in the barony of Small County.

<margin>705</margin> *Martine*, an old sept of the Firbolgs, of whose territory Emly, in this neighborhood, was the seat and centre.

<margin>706</margin> *Collan*, now Slieve Collane, or the Callan mountain, about five miles to the east of Milltown Malbay, in the barony of Ibrickan and county of Clare, celebrated for its Ogham inscription. The western and southwestern part of the county of Clare, as we have already seen, originally belonged to the Corca-Bhaiscinn.

<margin>707</margin> *Upper Cantred.*—This was included in the present barony of Inchiquin in the county of Clare. The baronies of Corcomroe and Burren ori-

<summary>This page contains historical and topographical annotations for O'Huidhrin's work, detailing the territories and family lineages of the O'Conaings, the O'Briens of Thomond, and various ancient septs in Limerick and Clare.</summary>
<keywords>O'Conaing, Castleconnell, Cormac Cas, Dal-Cais, Deis-beg, Small County, Claire, Slieve Collane, Ogham inscription, County Limerick, County Clare</keywords>

--- Page 293 ---
<meta>This page continues the annotations regarding the territories and families of the Dalcassian race in County Clare, following the previous discussion on the families of Limerick and the lineage of Cormac Cas.</meta>

<page-num>lxxix</page-num>
<header>TRIBES OF MUNSTER.</header>

It originally belonged, as we have already seen, to the race of Fergus MacRoigh, king of Ulster—that is, the O'Conors and O'Loughlins—so that the country of the O'Deas was the Upper Cantred of Dal Cais.

708 *O'Deadhaigh*, now anglicized as O'Dea. The "O'" is generally retained in this territory, but in other parts of Ireland, it is anglicized as Day, without the prefix "O'." This family was called *Aes Iar Forgas* by Irish genealogists, due to their location on the west side of the River Fergus. They had seats at Tully O'Dea and Disert Tola. The O'Deas derive their surname from Deaghaidh, the twentieth in descent from Cormac Cas, from whom the Dal Cais descend.

709 *Tealach*, now Tullyodea, in the parish of Ruan, barony of Inchiquin, County Clare, and about three miles to the north of the church of Dysert.

710 *O'Cuinn*, now anglicized as Quin, without the prefix "O'."

711 *Muinter-Ifearnain*.—This was the tribe name of the O'Quins in County Clare, whose territory extended around *Coradh-Finne*, now Corofin, in the barony of Inchiquin, County Clare. Inchiquin was the original seat of this family, but they were driven from there by the O'Briens in the fourteenth century. The O'Quins derived this tribe name, Muinter-Iffernain, from Iffernan, son of Corc, the fifteenth in descent from Cormac Cas, the ancestor of all the Dalcassian septs. See the Genealogical Table in *Battle of Magh Rath*, opposite page 340.

712 *Ui-Flaithri*.—The location of this territory, as well as that of Finnchoradh, is now unknown.

713 *O'Cathail*, now anglicized as Cahill, without the prefix "O'"; but the name has sunk into poverty and obscurity.

714 *Brentir*, now Breintre, a district comprising seven townlands lying northeast of Sliabh Collain in County Clare. The tribe name Cinel-Baith is now obsolete.

715 *Eidhneach*, now the Inagh, a small river near Milltown Malbay in the west of County Clare. It is also the name of a Roman Catholic parish through which this river flows. *O'Maoilmeadha* would now be anglicized as O'Mulvey or Mulvey, but it is obsolete in this district.

716 *Ui-Corbmaic*.—This name is still locally remembered and is now applied to a district comprising the parish of Kilmaley in County Clare; but it can be proved from various authorities that it originally comprised all the barony of Islands, except the parish of Clondagad, which was a part of East Corca-Vaskin. *O'Haichir*, now anglicized as O'Hehir and Hare, was of the sept of the Ui-Fidhgeinte, of the race of Eoghan, son of Oiloll Olum, and not of the race of Cormac Cas; but no account has yet been discovered of when or by what means they established a settlement in Thomond.

<summary>This page provides genealogical and geographical annotations for several prominent families in County Clare, including the O'Deas, O'Quins, and O'Hehirs. It details their ancestral lineages from Cormac Cas and Fergus MacRoigh, as well as the modern anglicization of their names and the locations of their ancient territories.</summary>

--- Page 294 ---
<meta>This page continues the detailed annotations on the territories and septs of the Dalcassian race in County Clare, transitioning from the lands of the O'Deas and O'Quins to the territories of the O'Gradys and the Mac Namaras.</meta>

<page-num>lxxx</page-num>
<header>O'HUIDHRIN.</header>

717 *Ui-Flannchadha.*—Situation unknown; but it is probable that it adjoined the last-mentioned territory.

718 *O'Duibhginn.*—This seems to be a mistake for *O'Griobhtha,* now anglicized as Griffy and Griffin. See the *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1483, 1588.

719 *Muintir Connlochaigh,* otherwise called Cinel Cuallachta. According to the *Caithreim Thoirrdhealbaigh*, this territory comprised the southeastern part of the barony of Inchiquin, County Clare. The castles of Ballygriffy and Mogowna were located in it.

720 *O'Grada,* now anglicized as O'Grady.

721 *Cinel-Dunghaile.*—This was the tribe name of the O'Gradys, and it became, as was common, the name of their territory. Since the year 1318, this district has comprised the parishes of Tomgraney, Inishcaltra, and Clonrush, of which the latter two are now included in County Galway, though they belong to the diocese of Killaloe.

722 *Mac Conmara,* now anglicized as Mac Namara. This family derives its name from its ancestor Cumara, son of Domhnall, who was the twenty-second in descent from Cormac Cas. His son Domhnall died in 1099.

723 *Magh Adhair,* a level district lying between the towns of Ennis and Tullagh, in County Clare. See the *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 981, 1099, and 1599. It would appear that the family of the O'Hehirs were settled here before the Mac Namaras.

724 *Ui-gCaisin.*—The name and exact extent of this territory are preserved in the deanery of Ogashin, which comprises the parishes of Quin, Tullagh, Clooney, Doora, Kilraghtis, Kiltalagh, Templemaley, Inchicronan, and Kilmurry-na-Gall, in the eastern part of County Clare. However, in the year 1318, after the defeat of De Clare and the expulsion of his allies—the Ui-Bloid—O'Brien gave the Mac Namaras possession of a more extensive territory than Ogashin, lying between the rivers Fergus and Shannon. The exact limits of this area in 1584 are defined in a manuscript account of Thomond, preserved in the library of Trinity College, Dublin, E., 2, 14.

725 *Muinter-Lideadha,* that is, the O'Liddys, who still exist but are reduced in status.

726 *Clann-Dealbhaith.*—This was the tribe name of the O'Neills of Tradry, a fertile territory in County Clare. Its extent is preserved in the deanery of Tradry, which contains the parishes of Tomfinlough, Killnasoolagh, Kilmaleery, Kilconry, Clonloghan, Drumline, Feenagh, Bunratty, Killaneen, and the Island of Inis-da-drom, in the south of County Clare. After the defeat of De Clare and his adherents, O'Brien gave the Mac Namaras the whole of this territory, which is the richest in all Thomond.

<summary>This page provides historical and geographical annotations on various septs in County Clare, including the O'Gradys, Mac Namaras, and O'Neills, detailing their ancestral lands and shifts in territory following the 14th-century defeat of De Clare.</summary>
<keywords>Ui-Flannchadha, O'Grady, Mac Namara, O'Neill, Thomond, County Clare, Inchiquin, Magh Adhair, Ogashin, Tradry, Irish genealogy, Dalcassian septs</keywords>
