<meta>This page continues the annotations on the territories and septs of the Dalcassian race in County Clare, transitioning from the Mac Namara lands to the territories of the O'Kennedys, O'Shanahans, and O'Aherns, specifically focusing on their displacement after the year 1318.</meta>

<page-num>lxxxi</page-num>
->TRIBES OF MUNSTER.<-

727 *Fionnluaraigh.*—This name is now unknown. It seems to have been the name of the residence of the ancient chiefs of Tradry. The O'Neills of this race still exist, but they are reduced to obscurity and poverty. If tradition can be relied upon, the family of Creagh is a branch of them.

728 *O'm-Bloid.*—This name is still preserved in the deanery of Omulloid, in the east of County Clare. The chief families of this territory were the O'Kennedys, O'Shanahans, O'Duracks, and O'Aherns, who were all driven out of it in 1318 by Turlogh O'Brien because of the assistance they had given to De Clare.

729 *Ui-Cearnaigh.*—This was the tribe name of the O'Echtigherns, now O'Aherns, and was, as usual, applied to their territory. It comprised the parish of Kilfinaghty and a considerable portion of the district lying between it and the city of Limerick. The name of this territory is still locally preserved in that of the Ogarney river, which intersects the small town of Six-mile-bridge and unites with the Shannon near Bunratty. This river flows through the middle of the territory of Ui-Cearnaigh, from near the castle of Enaghofline to that of Rosmanagher; after passing which, it forms the boundary between Hy-Cearnaigh and Tradry. It was the ancestor of O'Ahern who granted the island of Inis-Sibtonn, now King's Island, in the city of Limerick, to St. Munchin, which suggests that he held a larger territory than that which remained in the possession of his descendants.

730 *Maicniadh's land.*—This was a bardic name for Munster.

731 *Ui-Ronghaile*, the country of *O'Seanchain.*—This territory is frequently mentioned in the *Caithreim Thoirdhealbaigh* as the country of O'Shanahan, a chieftain of the Ui-Bloid who joined De Clare. He was driven out in the year 1318, and his country was given to his enemies, the Mac Namaras. Hy-Ronghaile comprised the parishes of Kilnoe and Killuran, and some of the adjoining districts; however, its exact limits cannot now be determined.

732 *Gleann Omra*, now Glenomra, the country of *O'Cinneidigh*, now O'Kennedy. This territory shares the same boundaries as the parish of Killokennedy. The O'Kennedys were driven out of this territory during the struggles between the descendants of Turlogh and Brian Roe O'Brien, and they settled on the east side of the Shannon. Some of the race, however, remained behind, and their descendants are still living in Glenomra and its vicinity as small farmers and <term>cottiers</term>.

733 *Race of Donnchuan.*—The O'Kennedys are the descendants of Donn-

<header>->O'HUIDHRIN.<-</header>

chuan, brother of the famous Brian Borumha, who was monarch of Ireland from A.D. 1002 to 1014.

734 *Muinter Diubhthraic*, i.e., the family of *O'Dubhraic*, now anglicized as Durack, without the prefix O'. *Dun-Braine*, the name of their seat, is now unknown.

735 *Tuath O'g-Conghaile*, i.e., the territory of the Ui-Conghaile. This territory is probably co-extensive with the parish of Ogonnelloe, also known as *Aglis-Sinnell*, which preserves its name.

736 *Borumha*, now Bealboroo, a fort near Killaloe, in the southeast of the county of Clare.

737 *Ui Toirdhealbhhaigh*.—The territory of this sept was bounded on the north by Ui Conghaile; on the east by the Shannon; on the south and southwest by the river Shannon; and on the west by *Glenomra*, the territory of *O'Kennedy*. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1192.

738 *Flannan's Cill Dalua*, i.e., Killaloe, of which St. Flannan is the patron saint.

739 *Tuath Luimnigh*.—A district bordering on the city of Limerick. *O'Cadhla* is now anglicized as Kealy, and *O'Maille* as O'Malley.

740 *Ui Aimrit* or *Ur Aimrit*.—The location of this sept is unknown. O'Duibhidhir, now *O'Dwyer*, was seated in the present barony of *Kilnamanagh*, in the county of Tipperary; but this appears to be a different family.

741 *Caladh* is on the north side of the river Shannon, near the city of Limerick, and extends from the Shannon to the southern boundary of the parish of *Kilmurry-na-Gaul*. *O'Ceadfadha* is now anglicized as *Keating*, but the true form would be *O'Keaty*.

742 *Aos-tri-muighe*, i.e., the people of the three plains. This territory comprised the whole of the present barony of *Clanwilliam* and a considerable part of what is now called the county of the city of Limerick. *O'Conaing* was seated at *Caislen Ui-Chonaing*, now *Castleconnell*, and his territory extended from *Cnoc-Greine*, near *Pallas-Grean*, to the city of Limerick. He was dispossessed by a branch of the *Burkes* shortly after the English Invasion. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1597, p. 2041, note ².

743 *Craobh Cumhraidhe*, i.e., the sweet or fragrant branch, now Crecora, the name of a parish near the city of Limerick.

744 *Uaithnes*, now the baronies of *Owney*, in the counties of Limerick and Tipperary.

745 *Uaithne-tire*, now the barony of *Owney*, in the county of Tipperary.

<page-num>lxxxiii</page-num>
<header>TRIBES OF MUNSTER.</header>

746 *Mag-Ceoch*, now Mac Keogh, and Keogh. This family was seated at Ballymakeogh, near the river Mulkern, not far from the city of Limerick. The Rev. John Keogh, author of the “Irish Herbal and Irish Zoology” and of “Vindication of the Antiquities of Ireland,” was of this sept, as he himself informs us in the last-mentioned work, p. 142, where he states that he was the son of the Rev. John Keogh, of Strokestown, in county Roscommon, the son of Denis, son of John, who was the son of Anthony Keogh, of Cloonclieve, near the river Mulkern, within two miles of Limerick, where his ancestors enjoyed a very plentiful estate on both sides of the Shannon and Mulkern rivers. Compare *Tribes and Customs of Hy-Many*, p. 167, and correct the error in the notice of this John which is given there.

747 *Muinter Loingsigh*, that is, the family of O’Loingsigh, now anglicized as Lynch in this territory, though the same name is in other parts of Ireland anglicized as Linchy and Linskey. William Lynch, Esq., author of the “Feudal Dignities,” was of this family.

748 *Uaithne-Cliach*, now the barony of Owneybeg, in the east of the county of Limerick.

749 *O’h-Ifearnan.*—This name is now anglicized as Heffernan, without the prefix O’. The name is rather common in the counties of Limerick and Tipperary.

750 *O’Cathalain*, now Cahallan, and more generally shortened to Callan. These three families were dispossessed by the O’Mulryans, now Ryans, a Leinster family of the race of Cathaoir Mor.

751 *Ara*, now the barony of Ara or Duhara, in the northwest of the county of Tipperary. The people of Ara are of the Ulster race of Rudh-raighe, being, according to the Irish genealogists, descended from Feart-lachta, the son of Fergus Mac Roigh, king of Ulster in the first century. See *Ogygia*, Part III., chapter 46. There was another territory of this name called Ara Cliach, situated in the county of Limerick.

752 *O’Donnagain*, now Donegan without the O’. There are families of this name still extant in Tipperary, but among a very humble class. There are various other families of the name in Ireland of totally different races.

753 *Crota Cliach.*—This was the ancient name of the Galtee mountains in the county of Tipperary.

754 *Mag Longachain.*—This name is now obsolete, unless it is that anglicized as Lanigan, which is pronounced O’Lonnagain and O’Luinegain among the Irish-speaking people.

755 *Ui-Cuanach.*—This name is preserved in the now barony of Coonagh,

<sig>G 2</sig>

<summary>This page provides genealogical and geographical annotations for several Munster families, including the Keoghs, Lynches, and Heffernans, and identifies the ancient territories of Ara and Ui-Cuanach with their modern locations in Limerick and Tipperary.</summary>

<page-num>lxxxiv</page-num>
<header>O'HUIDHRIN.</header>

in the east of the county of Limerick; but, from its connection with Crota Cliach, it would appear that it was originally far more extensive. It was a portion of Ara Cliach.

756 *Muintir-Duibhidhir*, now anglicized as O'Dwyer and Dwyer. This family was seated in the present barony of Kilnamanagh, in the county of Tipperary. They seem to be different from the O'Duibhidhirs of Ui-Aimrit, already referred to.

757 *Muinter Cearbhaill*, that is, the family of O'Cearbhaill, now anglicized as O'Carroll, and more frequently Carroll without the prefix O'.

758 *Biorra's plain*, that is, the level district lying around Birr, now Parsonstown, in King's County.

759 *Eile.*—This was the name of a tribe which was, as usual among the ancient Irish, applied to a territory. It was derived from Eile, the seventh in descent from Cian, son of Oilill Olum, king of Munster in the third century. It contained the whole of Ely O'Carroll, which belonged originally to Munster but is now assigned to King's County and contains the baronies of Clonlisk and Ballybritt; in it were also included the baronies of Ikerrin and Eliogarty, now in the county of Tipperary. The boundary between Ely O'Carroll and the ancient Meath is determined by that between the diocese of Killaloe and the diocese of Meath; for that portion of King's County which belongs to the diocese of Killaloe was Ely O'Carroll and originally belonged to Munster. The other portions of the original Ely, such as Ikerrin and Eliogarty, were withdrawn from O'Carroll shortly after the English Invasion and added to the Earl of Ormond's country; however, the native chieftains, O'Meagher and O'Fogarty, were left in possession but were tributary to the Earl of Ormond. See *Leabhar na gCeart*, pages 78, 79, note ⁱ.

760 *Cinel-Farga*, Kinelarga, a territory in Ely-O'Carroll, nearly, if not exactly, coextensive with the present barony of Ballybritt, in King's County. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1548, page 1,509, note ᶠ. The O'Flanagans of this race are still extant, but all are reduced to poverty and obscurity. The O'Flanagans of the line of Tadhg of the Battle of Crinna are to be distinguished from those of Clancahill in the county of Roscommon and of Tooraah in the county of Fermanagh, who have been much more famous in Irish history.

761 *Race of Tadhg, son of Cian of Crinna.*—This has reference to Tadhg (the ancestor of the O'Flanagans of this race, and also of O'Carroll), who assisted Cormac Mac Art in the battle of Crinna in the third century, in

a reward for which King Cormac granted him the territory of Cianachta, in the east of ancient Meath. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 226, and O'Flaherty's *Ogygia*, Part III., c. 68.

762 *Lec-Oilella.*—This place, which was the seat of O'Flanagan, has not been identified.

763 *Clann-Ruainne*, and *Mag Corcrain.*—The exact location of this territory has not yet been determined; Donogh Mac Corcrane was one of O'Carroll's freeholders in 1576, when O'Carroll made his submission to Queen Elizabeth. The name MacCorcrain still exists, but is anglicized as Corcoran and Corkran, without the prefix Mac.

764 *O h-Aedhagain*, now anglicized as Egan. This name is to be distinguished from MacEgan, with which it is now confused. Teige O'Hegan was one of O'Carroll's freeholders in 1576. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1576, p. 1690, note <sup>e</sup>.

765 *Crich Cein.*—This is only a bardic name for *Ely-O'Carroll*.

766 *Clann Ionmainen.*—This was the tribe name of the O'Hegans, but it is now forgotten, and the exact location of O'Hegan is unknown to tradition.

767 *Clann Maenaigh.*—This was the tribe name of the O'Doolys, who were settled on the western face of Slieve Bloom, in Ely-O'Carroll. But this family had been originally chiefs of Fertullagh, in Westmeath, from where they were banished before the English Invasion by the O'Melaghlins. Donogh Oge O'Dowlye was one of O'Carroll's freeholders in 1576.

768 *Bladhma*, now Slieve Bloom, on the western face of which this family was located.

769 *Clann-Coinlegain.*—This was the tribe name of the family of Mac Giolla-Phoil, now MacGilfoyle. This family had their seat at Suidhe-an-roin, now Shinrone, in 1576, when Sir William O'Carroll, chief of Ely, made his submission to the Lord Deputy, Sir Henry Sidney.

770 *Hui-Deci.*—This was the tribe name of the family of O'Banain, now Banan, originally settled at *Leim Ui-Bhanain*, now Leap Castle, in the barony of Clonlisk, near Roscrea. William O'Banane was one of O'Carroll's freeholders in 1576. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, 1514, 1516, 1576.

771 *The O'Meachairs.*—The name of this family is now anglicized as O'Meagher, but more generally Meagher or Maher, without the prefix O'. Their territory of Ui-Cairin is now called Ikerrin, and is a barony in the north of the present county of Tipperary.

772 *Bearnan-Eile*, that is, the gapped mountain of Ely, now called in English the Devil's Bit Mountain.

It details their tribal names, geographical locations such as Slieve Bloom and Leap Castle, and their historical interactions with the English crown in the late 16th century.</summary>

<page-num>lxxxvi</page-num>
<header>O'HUIDHRIN.</header>

773 *Tuatha-Faralt*.—This name is now obsolete. *O'h-Ailche* is now anglicized Halley.

774 *Corca Thine*, anglicized as Corkehenny. This is still the ecclesiastical name of the parish of Templemore, in the county of Tipperary. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1580, p. 1749, note ᵃ.

775 *Druim-sailech*, i.e., *dorsum salicum* <note>ridge of the willows</note>, a conspicuous ridge in the barony of Ikerrin, about five miles to the south of Roscrea. The castle of *Moy-drum* stands upon it. See *Annals of the Four Masters*, A.D. 1601, p. 2276.

776 *O'Cathail*, now Cahill, without the prefix O'.

777 *The Southern Eile*.—This is Eliogarty, i.e. *Eile Ui-Fhogartaigh*, or O'Fogarty's Ely, a barony in the county of Tipperary.

778 *Eochaidh Baillderg*.—He was the son of Caerthann Fionn, king of Thomond, in St. Patrick's time. See *Ogygia*, Part III., c. 82. According to this, O'Fogarty was not of the Elian race, but of the Dal-Cais of Thomond.

779 *O'Fogarta*, now anglicized as Fogarty, without the prefix O'. This family became extinct, in the senior line, in the last century, and was succeeded by the Lanigans of Castlefogarty.

780 *Corca-Aela*.—The exact location has not been discovered. The families mentioned here are now totally unknown.

781 *Ui-Lughdhach*, otherwise called *Ui-Luighdheach*, or Ileagh, was formerly a separate barony and shown as such in the Down Survey, but is now included in the barony of Eliogarty.

782 *O'Spealain*.—This name is now anglicized as Spillan and Spollan, without the prefix O'.

<summary>This page provides historical and geographical annotations for the territories and families of Tipperary, specifically focusing on the lineages of O'Fogarty and O'Cahill within the baronies of Eliogarty and Ikerrin.</summary>
<keywords>Tuatha-Faralt, Corca Thine, Templemore, Tipperary, Druim-sailech, Ikerrin, O'Cathail, Eliogarty, O'Fogarty, Eochaidh Baillderg, Thomond, Ui-Lughdhach, O'Spealain</keywords>

> Iomḋa triaṫ is taoiseaċ teann
> ’San tir-si O ḃFiaċraċ; airḃeaṁ
> Taoiseaċ gaċa tuaite ḋi,
> Agus bruġaiḋ gaċ baile.

Many a chief and strong sub-chief
In this territory of the <term>Uí Fiachrach</term>; I record
The sub-chief of every district in it,
And the <term>brughaidh</term> <note>a wealthy landowner or hospitaller</note> of each townland.

# VARIOUS READINGS.

M. adds the following memorandum at the end:

Ar ſlioċt Iollainn, mic Seaain 1 Maoilconaire, ro ſcriobuſ an duan ſin, 7 an taḋbar a tá roimpe ; 7 ar ſlioċt . . . . ro ſcriobuſ triallam timceall na Fodla, 7 a haḋbar i cCorcaig 3 Iulii, 1629. Tabraḋ gaċ aon dia ffoirgéna, 7 dia n-eiſtfe a beannaċt ar anmuin an tí ro ſcriobh.

> “Following the version of Iollann, son of Shane O’Maelchonaire <note>O’Mulconry</note>, I have written this poem <note>of O’h-Uidhrin</note>, and the argument <note>the prose abstract</note> which precedes it; and following the version of . . . . . . . I have copied <note>O’Dubhagain’s poem beginning</note> *Triallam timcheall na Fodhla*, and its argument, at Cork, on the 3rd of July, 1629. Let everyone to whom they may be useful, or who shall hear them, give his blessing on the soul of him who transcribed them.”

<margin>☛ The prose abstract mentioned here by Michael O’Clery, which he prefixed to O’h-Uidhrin’s poem, is considered by the Editor to be too defective and inaccurate to be included in the present publication.</margin>

<summary>This page presents a concluding memorandum by Michael O’Clery, dated July 3, 1629, in which he identifies his source manuscripts and asks for a blessing on his soul. The editor notes the exclusion of O'Clery's prose abstract from this edition due to its inaccuracies.</summary>

# The Irish Archaeological and Celtic Society,

->FOR THE<-
->PUBLICATION OF THE MATERIALS FOR IRISH HISTORY.<-

->1862.<-

---

### President:
->HIS GRACE THE DUKE OF LEINSTER.<-

### Vice-Presidents:
->THE MOST NOBLE THE MARQUESS OF KILDARE, M.R.I.A.<-
->THE RIGHT HON. THE EARL OF DUNRAVEN, M.R.I.A.<-
->THE RIGHT HON. LORD TALBOT DE MALAHIDE, M.R.I.A.<-
->VERY REV. CHARLES W. RUSSELL, D.D., President of Maynooth College.<-

### Council:
->VERY REV. CHARLES GRAVES, D.D., President of the Royal Irish Academy.<-
->REV. JAMES GRAVES, A.B., M.R.I.A.<-
->SIR THOMAS A. LARCOM, K.C.B., M.R.I.A.<-
->JOHN C. O'CALLAGHAN, ESQ.<-
->EUGENE O'CURRY, M.R.I.A.<-
->GEORGE PETRIE, LL.D., M.R.I.A.<-
->REV. WILLIAM REEVES, D.D., Secretary of the Royal Irish Academy.<-
->AQUILLA SMITH, M.D., M.R.I.A.<-
->W. R. WILDE, M.D., Vice-President of the Royal Irish Academy.<-

### Secretaries:
->REV. J. H. TODD, D.D., Senior Fellow of Trinity College, Dublin.<-
->J. T. GILBERT, M.R.I.A., Librarian of the Royal Irish Academy.<-

### Treasurer:
->THE BANK OF IRELAND.<-

---

THE existing materials for Irish history have so far been accessible to the student only to a limited extent. The published authorities have been so thoroughly exhausted, and the works compiled from them are so inadequate, that the expectation of any reliable history of Ireland has been generally delayed, under the conviction

<summary>This page identifies the leadership and mission of the Irish Archaeological and Celtic Society in 1862, listing its president, council, and secretaries. It begins an introduction explaining the need for publishing new primary sources to create a reliable history of Ireland.</summary>
<keywords>Irish Archaeological and Celtic Society, Irish history, Duke of Leinster, historical materials, scholarly society, 1862, academic leadership, historical sources</keywords>

--- Page 354 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the Irish Archaeological and Celtic Society, explaining the necessity of new source material for a complete history of Ireland and outlining the Society's membership structure.</meta>

that, before a work of that nature can be produced, great additions must be made to the sources of information currently available in print.

The immediate object of this Society is to print in the original language, with accurate English translations and annotations, the unpublished documents that illustrate Irish history, especially those in the ancient and obsolete language of the country. Many of these can be faithfully translated and explained only by scholars who have been long engaged in the study of the Celtic remains of Ireland. The publication of these manuscripts will make many most important literary monuments accessible, not only for historical inquiry, but also for the purposes of comparative <term>philology</term>.

The production of twenty-one volumes related to Irish history has been accomplished by the <term>Irish Archæological Society</term>, founded in 1840, and the <term>Celtic Society</term>, established in 1845. The present Society has been formed by the union of these two bodies, under the name of the “Irish Archæological and Celtic Society,” for the preservation of the monuments that illustrate Irish history, and for the publication of the historic, bardic, ecclesiastical, and topographical remains of Irish literature, especially those that exist in the Irish language.

The books printed by the Society are to be obtained only by its subscribers, who are divided into two classes: Members, who pay a three-pound admission fee and one pound per year; and Associates, who pay an annual subscription of one pound without any entrance fee. Members may settle all future annual subscriptions by a single payment of ten pounds, which includes the subscription for the current year. Members alone are eligible for the Council, and only they can vote at the general meetings of the Society. The works published separately by the Irish Archæological and Celtic Societies may be obtained by and through Members at the charges specified in the joint Catalogue; copies of the catalogue can be obtained at all times free of expense on application to the Honorary Secretaries at 19 Dawson Street, Dublin.

<summary>This page explains the mission of the Irish Archæological and Celtic Society to translate and publish ancient Irish manuscripts and details the membership fees and benefits for subscribers.</summary>
<keywords>Irish Archæological and Celtic Society, Irish history, manuscripts, Irish language, comparative philology, membership fees, Dublin, historical documents</keywords>

# PUBLICATIONS

**Liber Hymnorum**: The Book of Hymns of the Ancient Church of Ireland; from the original manuscript in the Library of Trinity College, Dublin. Edited by the Reverend **James Henthorn Todd**, D.D., President of the R.I.A. <note>Royal Irish Academy</note>, Senior Fellow of Trinity College. Part I. Containing the following Latin Hymns, with Irish <term>scholia</term> (marginal notes) and glosses:—
1. The Alphabetical Hymn of St. Sechnall, or Secundinus, in praise of St. Patrick. 2. The Alphabetical Hymn in praise of St. Brigid, attributed to St. Ultan, Bishop of Ardbreccan. 3. The Hymn of St. Cummain Fota. 4. The Hymn or Prayer of St. Mugint.

**The Life of St. Columba**, by **Adamnan**, Ninth Abbot of Hy <note>or Iona</note>. The Latin text taken from a manuscript of the early part of the eighth century, preserved at Schaffhausen; accompanied by various readings from six other manuscripts found in different parts of Europe; and illustrated by extensive notes and dissertations. By the Reverend **William Reeves**, D.D., M.B., Vice-President of the R.I.A. <note>Royal Irish Academy</note>. With maps and colored facsimiles of the manuscripts.
The two parts are bound in one volume for the convenience of members.

**Irish Glosses**: A medieval tract on Latin declension, with examples explained in Irish. From a manuscript in the Library of Trinity College, Dublin. Together with the *Lorica* of Gildas, and the Middle Irish gloss on it, from the Leabhar Breac. Edited, with a commentary, notes, and <term>indices verborum</term> (word indexes), by **Whitley Stokes**, A.B.

**Three Fragments of Ancient Irish Annals**, previously unpublished. Edited from a manuscript in the Burgundian Library, Brussels, with a translation and notes by **John O'Donovan**, LL.D., Member of the R.I.A. <note>Royal Irish Academy</note>, Professor of Celtic Languages at the Queen's College, Belfast.

**Liber Hymnorum**: The Book of Hymns of the Ancient Church of Ireland; from the original manuscript in the Library of Trinity College, Dublin. Edited by the Reverend **James Henthorn Todd**, D.D., Senior Fellow of Trinity College. Part II. *In the press.*

**The Topographical Poems of Seaan O'Dubhagain and Gilla-na-naomh O'Huidhrin**, listing the principal families and territories of Ireland and their chiefs in the fourteenth century. The Irish text edited with a translation and extensive illustrative notes by **John O'Donovan**, LL.D.; with the following introductory essays by the editor:—
1. On the poems of O'Dubhagain and O'Huidhrin. 2. On the ancient names of tribes and territories in Ireland. 3. On ancient Irish surnames and <term>agnomina</term> (nicknames or additional names). 4. On the Irish names anciently assumed by the English in Ireland. 5. On the assumption of English

->1853.<-

**CAMBRENSIS EVERSUS**; or, Refutation of the Authority of Giraldus Cambrensis on the History of Ireland, by Dr. JOHN LYNCH (1662), with some account of the affairs of that kingdom during his own and former times. Edited, with a translation and extensive notes, by the **REV. MATTHEW KELLY**. Three volumes. Price £1 10s.

---

# PUBLICATIONS OF THE CELTIC SOCIETY.

## FOUNDED 1845.

---

->1847.<-

**Leabar na g-Ceart**, or, The Book of Rights; a treatise on the rights and privileges of the ancient Kings of Ireland, now for the first time edited, with a translation and notes, by **JOHN O'DONOVAN, LL. D., M. R. I. A.** Price 10s.

->1848-50-51-52.<-

**CAMBRENSIS EVERSUS**, etc. as above. Three volumes.
<note>Given to Members of the Celtic Society for 1848, 1850–52; and to Members or Associates of the United Society for 1853.</note>

->1849.<-

**MISCELLANY OF THE CELTIC SOCIETY**, containing:
A treatise from the **Book of Lecan** on O'h-Eidirseceoil's (O'Driscoll's) country, in the County of Cork.
A historical poem on the Battle of Dun (Downpatrick), A.D. 1260.
Sir Richard Bingham's account of his proceedings in Connacht during the reign of Elizabeth.
A narration of Sir Henry Docwra's services in Ulster, written A.D. 1614; together with other original documents and letters illustrating Irish history.
Edited by **JOHN O'DONOVAN, Esq., LL. D., M. R. I. A.** Price 10s.

->1853.<-

**Cath Muighe Lena**: The Battle of Magh Lena; an ancient historical tale, edited by **EUGENE CURRY, Esq., M. R. I. A.**, from original manuscripts. Price 10s.

Complete set of Irish Archaeological Society's publications, 15 volumes, price £9.
Complete set of Celtic Society's publications, 6 volumes...
